In another hour his spirit had taken its flight; and it was said by those present that his face lighted up into a smile as the last breath escaped him, and that smile he carried into his grave. Almost his last words were: “Won’t Barnum open his eyes when he finds I have humbugged him by being buried in his new hunting-dress?” That dress was indeed the shroud in which he was entombed.
And that was the last on Earth of “Old Grizzly Adams.”

===Chinese===

他闻此噩耗，神情泰然自若，显出一副浑然不以为意的模样；随后，他面带微笑转向妻子，嘱咐她务必让自己穿着那套新猎装下葬。
“因为，”他说道，“巴纳姆白纸黑字答应过把这身衣服留给我，直到我再也用不上为止，而我这回打定主意要将他一军。他休想再见着那套衣服了。”
妻子向他保证定会遵照他的遗愿办理。随后他派人请来牧师，二人促膝深谈了数小时之久。
亚当斯向牧师坦言，关于他的那些熊，他确实吹过不少牛皮，但在人与人的交往中，他向来力求光明磊落、不欺不瞒。“过去六年间，我每天都在听道，连礼拜天也不例外，”他说道，“有时给我讲道的是老灰熊，有时是美洲豹；更多的则是内华达山脉群峰之巅或落基山脉幽谷之中的雷鸣电闪、狂风暴雨或飓风；可无论向我布道的是什么，它总能令我领悟造物主的威严，并向我启示天上仁慈天父那永垂不朽、恒久不变的大爱。我虽然是个相当粗野难缠的家伙，”这位弥留之际的汉子接着说道，“但我自问心肠还算摆在正道上，我满怀信心地仰望受颂的救主，期盼获得我所急需、却在尘世从未享得过的安息。”随后他请牧师与他一同祷告，祷告完毕，他紧握住牧师的手，感谢他的仁厚恩情，并与之作别。
又过了一个小时，他的灵魂飘然离世；据在场之人说，当他呼出最后一口气时，脸上蓦然绽放出笑意，而他便带着那抹微笑长眠于坟茔之中。他弥留之际最后的遗言几乎便是这句：“等巴纳姆发现我穿着他的新猎装下了葬，反倒把他给蒙骗了一回，他岂不是要大吃一惊吗？”那套猎装最终确实成了包裹他入殓下葬的寿衣。
这便是“老灰熊亚当斯”在人世间的最后结局。

### V / 第五章

===Original===

The golden pigeons﻿—​Grizzly Adams﻿—German chemist﻿—Happy family﻿—​French naturalist.
“Old Grizzly Adams” was quite candid when, in his last hours, he confessed to the clergyman that he had “told some pretty large stories about his bears.” In fact, these “large stories” were Adam’s “besetting sin.” To hear him talk, one would suppose that he had seen and handled everything ever read or heard of. In fact, according to his story, California contained specimens of all things, animate and inanimate, to be found in any part of the globe. He talked glibly about California lions, California tigers, California leopards, California hyenas, California camels, and California hippopotami. He furthermore declared he had, on one occasion, seen a California elephant, “at a great distance,” but it was “very shy,” and he would not permit himself to doubt that California giraffes existed somewhere in the neighborhood of the “tall trees.”
I was anxious to get a chance of exposing to Adams his weak point, and of showing him the absurdity of telling such ridiculous stories. A fit occasion soon presented itself. One day, while engaged in my office at the Museum, a man with marked Teutonic features and accent approached the door and asked if I would like to buy a pair of living golden pigeons.
“Yes,” I replied, “I would like a *flock* of ‘golden pigeons,’ if I could buy them for their weight in *silver*; for there are no ‘*golden*’ pigeons in existence, unless they are made from the pure metal.”
“You shall see some golden pigeons alive,” he replied, at the same time entering my office and closing the door after him. He then removed the lid from a small basket which he carried in his hand, and sure enough there were snugly ensconced a pair of beautiful living ruff-necked pigeons, as yellow as saffron and as bright as a double eagle fresh from the mint.

===Chinese===

金鸽——格里兹利·亚当斯——德国化学家——“快乐一家”——法国博物学家。

“老格里兹利·亚当斯”（Old Grizzly Adams）在弥留之际向牧师忏悔，坦言自己“关于那些熊着实吹过不少大牛”，这话倒也十分坦诚。事实上，这些“漫天大话”正是亚当斯“改不掉的老毛病”。听他开口闲聊，旁人准会以为世上凡是书里写过、耳边听过的稀罕物，他无一不曾亲眼见过、亲手摸过。依他所言，世界任何一个角落能找到的飞禽走兽与死物奇珍，加利福尼亚州应有尽有。他口若悬河地大谈加州狮子、加州老虎、加州豹子、加州鬣狗、加州骆驼以及加州河马。他甚至信誓旦旦地说，有一次自己“在极远处”瞧见过一头加州大象，只可惜它“性子极害羞”；他还坚信不疑，在“高大红杉”一带的某处必然生活着加州长颈鹿。

我一直巴望着能寻个机会当众戳穿亚当斯的这处软肋，叫他明白编造这等荒唐瞎话有多么可笑。没过多久，一个绝佳的契机便送上了门。某天，我正在博物馆办公室里埋头理政，一位相貌与口音都带着浓重德意志特征的男子走到门口，问我愿不愿意买下一对活生生的金鸽。

“愿意啊，”我答道，“要是能按*白银*的重量论斤买，我倒真想买下*一整群*‘金鸽’；因为世上根本不存在什么‘*金*’鸽子，除非是用纯金铸出来的。”

“您这就亲眼瞧瞧活的金鸽吧，”他一面说着，一面走进我的办公室并随手掩上房门。接着，他揭开手里拎着的小篮盖子——果不其然，里面舒舒服服地窝着一对鲜活漂亮的皱领鸽，毛色黄如藏红花，亮得宛若刚从造币厂铸出的双鹰金币。

===Original===

I confess I was somewhat staggered at this sight, and quickly asked the man where those birds came from.
A dull, lazy smile crawled over the sober face of my German visitor, as he replied in a slow, guttural tone of voice:
“What you think yourself?”
Catching his meaning, I quickly answered:
“I think it is a humbug?”
“Of course, I know you will say so; because you ‘forstha’ such things better as any man living, so I shall not try to humbug you. I have color them myself.”
On further inquiry, I learned that this German was a chemist, and that he possessed the art of coloring birds any hue desired, and yet retain a natural gloss on the feathers, which gave every shade the appearance of reality.
“I can paint a green pigeon or a blue pigeon, a gray pigeon or a black pigeon, a brown pigeon or a pigeon half blue and half green,” said the German; “and if you prefer it, I can paint them pink or purple, or give you a little of each color, and make you a rainbow pigeon.”

===Chinese===

我承认自己当时着实被眼前这幕景象震了一下，连忙追问那人这两只鸟究竟产自何处。

一抹迟缓而慵懒的笑意爬上了这位德国访客严肃的面庞，他用低沉而浑浊的喉音慢悠悠地答道：

“您自己个儿觉得呢？”

领会了他的弦外之音，我立刻接话道：

“我看这又是场欺罔骗局（humbug）吧？”

“那是自然，我就知道您会这么说；因为您‘懂行’（forstha）得很，比世上任何人都门儿清，所以我可不敢拿这种把戏来糊弄您。这鸟是我亲手染的颜色。”

经进一步打听，我得知这名德国人是位化学家，掌握着一种独门手艺，能把鸟羽染成任意想要的色调，同时还能保留羽毛天然的光泽，让每一种色泽都显得浑然天成。

“我能染出绿鸽子或蓝鸽子，灰鸽子或黑鸽子，棕鸽子或者一半蓝一半绿的鸽子，”德国人说道，“您要是喜欢，我也能把它们染成粉色或紫色，或者每种颜色都来上一点，给您造出一只彩虹鸽。”

===Original===

The “rainbow pigeon” did not strike me as particularly desirable; but, thinking here was a good chance to catch “Grizzly Adams,” I bought the pair of golden pigeons for ten dollars, and sent them up to the “Happy Family,” marked “Golden Pigeons from California.” Mr. Taylor the great pacificator, who has charge of the Happy Family, soon came down in a state of perspiration.

“Really, Mr. Barnum,” said he, “I could not think of putting those elegant golden pigeons into the Happy Family﻿—they are too valuable a bird﻿—they might get injured﻿—they are by far the most beautiful pigeons I ever saw; and as they are so rare, I would not jeopardize their lives for anything.”

“Well,” I replied, “you may put them in a separate cage, properly labeled.”

Monsieur Guillaudeu, the naturalist and taxidermist of the Museum, has been attached to that establishment since the year it was founded, 1810. He is a Frenchman, and has read everything upon Natural History that was ever published in his own or in the English language. He is now seventy-five years old, but is lively as a cricket, and takes as much interest in Natural History as he ever did. When he saw the “golden pigeons from California,” he was considerably astonished! He examined them with great delight for half an hour, expatiating upon their beautiful color, and the near resemblance which every feature bore to the American ruff-neck pigeon. He soon came to my office and said:

“Mr. B., these golden pigeons are superb, but they cannot be from California. Audubon mentions no such bird in his work upon American Ornithology.”

I told him he had better take Audubon home with him that night, and perhaps by studying him attentively he would see occasion to change his mind.

===Chinese===

“彩虹鸽”我倒没觉得有多稀罕；不过转念一想，这正是套住“格里兹利·亚当斯”的好机会，于是我花十美元买下了这对金鸽，并打上“产自加利福尼亚的金鸽”标签，派人送到楼上的“快乐一家”（Happy Family，即巴纳姆博物馆著名的动物混养展笼）。负责照料“快乐一家”的调解大师泰勒先生（Mr. Taylor）没过多久便满头大汗地跑下楼来。

“说真的，巴纳姆先生，”他急切地说道，“我实在不敢把那对雅致的金鸽放进‘快乐一家’里——这鸟太金贵了——万一受了伤可怎么好——它们是我平生见过最漂亮的鸽子；既然如此珍稀，我无论如何也不敢拿它们的性命去冒这个险。”

“好吧，”我答道，“你可以把它们单独关进一只笼子里，贴好恰当的标签。”

纪尧多先生（Monsieur Guillaudeu）是本博物馆的博物学家兼动物标本剥制师，自博物馆于 1810 年创立起便在此任职。他是位法国人，凡是以法语或英语出版的博物学著作，他无一不曾涉猎。如今他已七十五岁高龄，却依然像只蟋蟀般精神矍铄，对博物学的热忱不减当年。当他瞧见那对“产自加利福尼亚的金鸽”时，着实大吃了一惊！他兴致勃勃地端详了整整半小时，对其艳丽的羽色以及与美洲皱领鸽极为相似的各项特征赞不绝口。不久，他来到我的办公室说道：

“巴纳姆先生，这对金鸽固然妙绝，但它们绝不可能产自加利福尼亚。奥杜邦（Audubon）在他的《美洲鸟类学》一书中从未提及过这种鸟类。”

我劝他今晚不妨把奥杜邦的著作带回家去，或许潜心研读一番之后，便会找出改变看法的理由。

===Original===

The next day, the old naturalist called at my office and remarked:
“Mr. B., those pigeons are a more rare bird than you imagine. They are not mentioned by Linnaeus, Cuvier, Goldsmith, or any other writer on Natural History, so far as I have been able to discover. I expect they must have come from some unexplored portion of Australia.”
“Never mind,” I replied, “we may get more light on the subject, perhaps, before long. We will continue to label them ‘California Pigeons’ until we can fix their nativity elsewhere.”
The next, morning, “Old Grizzly Adams,” whose exhibition of bears was then open in Fourteenth street, happened to be passing through the Museum, when his eyes fell on the “Golden California Pigeons.” He looked a moment and doubtless admired. He soon after came to my office.
“Mr. B.,” said he, “you must let me have those California pigeons.”
“I can’t spare them,” I replied.
“But you *must* spare them. All the birds and animals from California ought to be together. You own half of my California menagerie, and you must lend me those pigeons.”

===Chinese===

次日，老博物学家又来到我的办公室，感慨道：

“巴纳姆先生，这对鸽子比您想象的还要珍稀得多。据我迄今所能查考的范围，林奈（Linnaeus）、居维叶（Cuvier）、戈德史密斯（Goldsmith），以及任何其他博物学作家都未曾记载过它们。我料想它们必定来自澳大利亚某片尚未被探索的地域。”

“不要紧，”我回答说，“或许过不了多久，我们就能对这事儿多几分眉目。在确切查明它们的原产地之前，我们还是继续把它们标为‘加州鸽’吧。”

第二天早上，“老格里兹利·亚当斯”——他那场黑熊展览当时正在第十四街开张——恰巧路过博物馆，目光一下子落在了“加州金鸽”身上。他驻足端详了片刻，心头想必也是好生赞叹。没过多久，他便走进了我的办公室。

“巴纳姆先生，”他开口道，“您一定得把那对加州鸽子让给我。”

“我可抽不出手让给您，”我回绝道。

“可您*非得*让出来不可。所有来自加利福尼亚的飞禽走兽本就该摆在一块儿。我的加州珍禽异兽展有一半归您所有，您必须把那对鸽子借给我。”

===Original===

“Mr. Adams, they are too rare and valuable a bird to be hawked about in that manner; besides, I expect they will attract considerable attention here.”
“Oh, don’t be a fool,” replied Adams. “Rare bird, indeed! Why, they are just as common in California as any other pigeon! I could have brought a hundred of them from San Francisco, if I had thought of it.”
“But why did you not think of it?” I asked, with a suppressed smile.
“Because they are *so common* there,” said Adams. “I did not think they would be any curiosity here. I have eaten them in pigeon-pies hundreds of times, and shot them by the thousand!”
I was ready to burst with laughter to see how readily Adams swallowed the bait, but maintaining the most rigid gravity, I replied:
“Oh well, Mr. Adams, if they are really so common in California, you had probably better take them, and you may write over and have half a dozen pairs sent to me for the Museum.”
“All right,” said Adams; “I will send over to a friend in San Francisco, and you shall have them here in a couple of months.”

===Chinese===

“亚当斯先生，这鸟太过珍稀贵重，经不起这么搬来搬去四处招摇；再者说，我还指望着它们在这儿招揽不少目光呢。”

“哎呀，别犯傻了，”亚当斯答道，“还珍稀鸟类呢！得了吧，这玩意儿在加州就跟寻常鸽子一样随处可见！我要是早想到，从旧金山带一百只回来都不成问题。”

“可您当时怎么就没想到呢？”我强忍着笑意问道。

“就因为它们在那儿*太常见了*，”亚当斯说，“我压根儿没料到这鸟运到这儿还能算个稀罕物。我用它们做的鸽肉馅饼都吃过几百回了，开枪打死过成千上万只！”

眼看亚当斯如此痛快地一口咬下了鱼饵，我险些放声大笑出来，但仍极力绷住脸，摆出一副无比庄重的神情答道：

“哦，那好吧，亚当斯先生，既然它们在加州果真如此寻常，您还是把它们带去展出吧；劳您写信过去，再替博物馆捎半打对回来。”

“没问题，”亚当斯满口应承，“我这就托旧金山的朋友寄过来，一两个月之内准能送到您这儿。”

===Original===

I told Adams that, for certain reasons, I would prefer to change the label so as to have it read: “Golden Pigeons from Australia.”
“Well, call them what you like,” replied Adams; “I suppose they are probably about as plenty in Australia as they are in California.”
I fancied I could discover a sly smile lurking in the eye of the old bear-hunter as he made this reply.
The pigeons were labeled as I suggested, and this is how it happened that the Bridgeport non-believing lady, mentioned in the next chapter, was so much attracted as to solicit some of their eggs in order to perpetuate the species in old Connecticut.
Six or eight weeks after this incident, I was in the California Menagerie, and noticed that the “Golden Pigeons” had assumed a frightfully mottled appearance. Their feathers had grown out, and they were half white. Adams had been so busy with his bears that he had not noticed the change. I called him up to the pigeon cage, and remarked:
“Mr. Adams, I fear you will lose your Golden Pigeons; they must be very sick; I observe they are turning quite pale!”

===Chinese===

我告诉亚当斯，出于某些缘故，我更倾向于把标签改一下，写成：“产自澳大利亚的金鸽”。

“随您爱怎么叫就怎么叫吧，”亚当斯答道，“我猜这鸟在澳大利亚大概也跟在加州一样多得是。”

他作此回答时，我隐约觉得这位老猎熊人眼角藏着一抹狡黠的笑意。

鸽子照我的提议换上了新标签。正因如此，下一章将要提到的那位来自布里奇波特、素来疑心重重的女士，才会对此鸟倾倒备至，甚至央求讨几枚鸽蛋，好在老家康涅狄格州把这物种繁衍下来。

此事过去六到八周之后，我去加州珍禽异兽展转了转，却见那对“金鸽”已变得斑驳陆离、惨不忍睹。它们的新羽毛长了出来，浑身一半都露出了白色。亚当斯整日忙着伺候他的熊，压根没留意到这番变化。我把他唤到鸽笼前，开口道：

“亚当斯先生，我真担心您要折了这对金鸽；它们准是病得不轻；您瞧，脸色都变得惨白了！”

===Original===

Adams looked at them a moment with astonishment; then turning to me, and seeing that I could not suppress a smile, he indignantly exclaimed:
“Blast the Golden Pigeons! You had better take them back to the Museum. You can’t humbug me with your painted pigeons!”
This was too much, and “I laughed till I cried” to witness the mixed look of astonishment and vexation which marked the “grizzly” features of old Adams.
“These Golden Pigeons,” I remarked, “are very common in California, I think I heard you say? When do you expect my half-dozen pairs will arrive?”
“You go to thunder, you old humbug!” replied Adams, as he marched off indignantly, and soon disappeared behind the cages of his grizzly bears.
From that time, Adams seemed to be more careful about telling his large stories. Perhaps he was not cured altogether of his habit, but he took particular pains when making marvelous statements to have them of such a nature that they could not be disproved so easily as was that regarding the “Golden California Pigeons.”

### VI / 第六章

===Original===

The whale, the angel fish, and the golden pigeon.
If the fact could be definitely determined, I think it would be discovered that in this “wide awake” country there are more persons humbugged by believing too little than too much. Many persons have such a horror of being taken in, or such an elevated opinion of their own acuteness, that they believe everything to be a sham, and in this way are continually humbugging themselves.
Several years since, I purchased a living white whale, captured near Labrador, and succeeded in placing it, “in good condition,” in a large tank, fifty feet long, and supplied with salt water, in the basement of the American Museum. I was obliged to light the basement with gas, and that frightened the sea-monster to such an extent that he kept at the bottom of the tank, except when he was compelled to stick his nose above the surface in order to breathe or “blow,” and then down he would go again as quick as possible. Visitors would sometimes stand for half an hour, watching in vain to get a look at the whale; for, although he could remain under water only about two minutes at a time, he would happen to appear in some unlooked for quarter of the huge tank, and before they could all get a chance to see him, he would be out of sight again. Some impatient and incredulous persons after waiting ten minutes, which seemed to them an hour, would sometimes exclaim:
“Oh, humbug! I don’t believe there is a whale here at all!”
This incredulity often put me out of patience, and I would say:
“Ladies and gentlemen, there is a living whale in the tank. He is frightened by the gaslight and by visitors; but he is obliged to come to the surface every two minutes, and if you will watch sharply, you will see him. I am sorry we can’t make him dance a hornpipe and do all sorts of wonderful things at the word of command; but if you will exercise your patience a few minutes longer, I assure you the whale will be seen at considerably less trouble than it would be to go to Labrador expressly for that purpose.”

===Chinese===

白鲸、神仙鱼与金鸽。

倘若事实真能查明，我想人们定会发现，在这个“精明警醒”的国度里，因信得太少而自欺欺人者，远多于因信得太多而上当受骗者。许多人唯恐吃亏受骗，或是对自己的敏锐眼力自视过高，以至于把世间万物都视作假货，结果反倒在日复一日中把自己给糊弄了。

几年前，我买下一头在拉布拉多（Labrador）附近捕获的活白鲸，并设法将其“完好无损地”安置在美洲博物馆（American Museum）地下室一只五十英尺长、注满海水的大水箱里。我不得不在地下室点起煤气灯，这把那头海怪吓得够呛，它总是死死伏在水箱底部，除非逼不得已要将吻部探出水面换气或“喷水”，随后便会以最快速度一头扎回水底。游客有时会站在那里等上半小时，却连白鲸的影子也瞧不见；因为尽管它每次在水下最多只能待两分钟左右，可它总偏偏挑在巨大水箱里某处意想不到的角落冒头，不等众人来得及看清，它早已又潜得无影无踪。有些性急又多疑的人等上十分钟——在他们觉得仿佛过了一个钟头——有时便会嚷道：

“哦，骗局（humbug）！我看这里根本就没有什么鲸鱼！”

这种多疑常常让我按捺不住性子，我便会说道：

“诸位女士、诸位先生，水箱里确确实实有一头活鲸鱼。它是被煤气灯和游客吓着了；不过它每隔两分钟就必须浮出水面一次，只要诸位留神盯紧些，定能看见它。很抱歉，我们没法让它听从口令跳水手舞、表演各色奇技；但只要诸位再多耐着性子等上几分钟，我向大家保证，在这里瞧见白鲸，可比专程跑一趟拉布拉多省事多了。”

===Original===

This would usually put my patrons in good humor; but I was myself often vexed at the persistent stubbornness of the whale in not calmly floating on the surface for the gratification of my visitors.
One day, a sharp Yankee lady and her daughter, from Connecticut, called at the Museum. I knew them well; and in answer to their inquiry for the locality of the whale, I directed them to the basement. Half an hour afterward, they called at my office, and the acute mother, in a half-confidential, seriocomic whisper, said:
“Mr. B., it’s astonishing to what a number of purposes the ingenuity of us Yankees has applied India-rubber.”
I asked her meaning, and was soon informed that she was perfectly convinced that it was an India-rubber whale, worked by steam and machinery, by means of which he was made to rise to the surface at short intervals, and puff with the regularity of a pair of bellows. From her earnest, confident manner, I saw it would be useless to attempt to disabuse her mind on the subject. I therefore very candidly acknowledged that she was quite too sharp for me, and I must plead guilty to the imposition; but I begged her not to expose me, for I assured her that she was the only person who had discovered the trick.
It was worth more than a dollar to see with what a smile of satisfaction she received the assurance that nobody else was as shrewd as herself; and the patronizing manner in which she bade me be perfectly tranquil, for the secret should be considered by her as “strictly confidential,” was decidedly rich. She evidently received double her money’s worth in the happy reflection that she could not be humbugged, and that I was terribly humiliated in being detected through her marvelous powers of discrimination! I occasionally meet the good lady, and always try to look a little sheepish, but she invariably assures me that she has never divulged my secret and never will!
On another occasion, a lady equally shrewd, who lives neighbor to me in Connecticut, after regarding for a few minutes the “Golden Angel Fish” swimming in one of the Aquaria, abruptly addressed me with:

===Chinese===

这番话通常能让主顾们重展笑颜；不过我本人也常常为白鲸那副倔脾气感到恼火——它偏不肯安安静静地浮在水面上供我的游客观赏。

某天，一位来自康涅狄格州、精明泼辣的扬基太太带着女儿来到博物馆。我与她们颇为熟稔；她们问起白鲸在何处，我便指点她们去地下室。半小时后，她们来到我的办公室，那位精明过人的母亲压低嗓音，用一种半是私密、半带诙谐的严肃腔调说道：

“巴纳姆先生，咱们扬基人的巧思妙想把印度橡胶用在了这么多门道上，真是叫人叹服啊。”

我问她此话何意，她随即告诉我，她敢百分之百断定那是一头印度橡胶做的假鲸鱼，由蒸汽和机械驱动，靠着机关每隔一小会儿就浮出水面，像风箱一般规律地喷气。看她那副言之凿凿、笃信不疑的神情，我知道试图纠正她的念头纯属徒劳。于是我十分坦白地承认，她实在是太精明，远非我所能比拟，这桩把戏我只得认栽；但我恳求她千万别拆穿我，并向她保证，她是唯一识破这一机关的人。

瞧着她听闻世上再无他人如她这般精明时脸上泛起的那抹得意微笑，光这一幕就值回不止一美元的票价；而她那副居高临下、宽慰我尽管把心放回肚子里——因为她会把这秘密视作“绝对机密”——的派头，更是妙趣横生。显然，她在自己“绝不会被骗倒”、而我则因她惊人的鉴别力而“当场露馅、无地自容”的快意遐想中，收获了双倍于门票的价值！此后我偶尔还会碰见这位好心的太太，每次都刻意做出几分羞愧难当的模样，而她总是不忘向我打包票，说自己从未泄密，今后也绝不会泄露半个字！

还有一回，另一位同样精明、住在康涅狄格州我家附近的女士，在水族箱前端详了片刻游来游去的“金色神仙鱼”后，突然转头对我说道：

===Original===

“You can’t humbug me, Mr. Barnum; that fish is painted!”
“Nonsense!” said I, with a laugh; “the thing is impossible!”
“I don’t care, I know it is painted; it is as plain as can be.”
“But, my dear Mrs. H., paint would not adhere to a fish while in the water; and if it would, it would kill him. Besides,” I added, with an extra serious air, “we never allow humbugging here!”
“Oh, here is just the place to look for such things,” she replied with a smile; “and I must say I more than half believe that Angel Fish is painted.”
She was finally nearly convinced of her error, and left. In the afternoon of the same day, I met her in Old Adams’ California Menagerie. She knew that I was part-proprietor of that establishment, and seeing me in conversation with “Grizzly Adams,” she came up to me in some haste, and with her eyes glistening with excitement, she said:

===Chinese===

“您可糊弄不了我，巴纳姆先生；那条鱼是染过色的！”

“胡说！”我笑着答道，“这根本不可能！”

“我不管，我就知道它是染色的；这再明白不过了。”

“可是，我亲爱的 H. 夫人，鱼在水里游，颜料根本附着不住啊；就算附着得住，也会把鱼毒死的。况且，”我又换上一副格外郑重的神色补充道，“我们这儿向来不允许弄虚作假！”

“得了吧，这儿正是找这种假把戏的好地方呢，”她笑着回敬道，“我非说不可，我十有八九相信那条神仙鱼就是染过色的。”

她总算勉强信了大半，转身离去。当天下午，我在老亚当斯的加州珍禽异兽展又碰见了她。她知道我是这家展馆的合伙老板之一，看见我正与“格里兹利·亚当斯”攀谈，便急匆匆地走上前，双眼因兴奋而闪闪发光，开口说道：

===Original===

“O, Mr. B., I never saw anything so beautiful as those elegant ‘Golden Pigeons’ from Australia. I want you to secure some of their eggs for me, and let my pigeons hatch them at home. I should prize them beyond all measure.”
“Oh, you don’t want ‘Golden Australian Pigeons,’ ” I replied; “they are painted.”
“No, they are not painted,” said she, with a laugh, “but I half think the Angel Fish is.”
I could not control myself at the curious coincidence, and I roared with laughter while I replied:
“Now, Mrs. H., I never let a good joke be spoiled, even if it serves to expose my own secrets. I assure you, upon honor, that the Golden Australian Pigeons, as they are labeled, are really painted; and that in their natural state they are nothing more nor less than the common ruff-necked white American pigeons!”
And it was a fact. How they happened to be exhibited under that auriferous disguise was owing to an amusing circumstance, explained in another chapter.
Suffice it at present to say, that Mrs. H. to this day “blushes to her eyebrows” whenever an allusion is made to “Angel Fish” or “Golden Pigeons.”

### VII / 第七章

===Original===

Pease’s hoarhound candy﻿—​the Dorr rebellion﻿—The Philadelphia aldermen.

In the year 1842, a new style of advertising appeared in the newspapers and in handbills which arrested public attention at once on account of its novelty. The thing advertised was an article called “Pease’s Hoarhound Candy;” a very good specific for coughs and colds. It was put up in twenty-five cent packages, and was eventually sold wholesale and retail in enormous quantities. Mr. Pease’s system of advertising was one which, I believe, originated with him in this country, although many have practiced it since, but of course, with less success﻿—for imitations seldom succeed. Mr. Pease’s plan was to seize upon the most prominent topic of interest and general conversation, and discourse eloquently upon that topic in fifty to a hundred lines of a newspaper-column, then glide off gradually into a panegyric of “Pease’s Hoarhound Candy.” The consequence was, every reader was misled by the caption and commencement of his article, and thousands of persons had “Pease’s Hoarhound Candy” in their mouths long before they had seen it! In fact, it was next to impossible to take up a newspaper and attempt to read the legitimate news of the day without stumbling upon a package of “Pease’s Hoarhound Candy.” The reader would often feel vexed to find that, after reading a quarter of a column of interesting news upon the subject uppermost in his mind, he was trapped into the perusal of one of Pease’s hoarhound candy advertisements. Although inclined sometimes to throw down the newspaper in disgust, he would generally laugh at the talent displayed by Mr. Pease in thus captivating and capturing the reader. The result of all this would generally be, a trial of the candy on the first premonitory symptoms of a cough or influenza. The degree to which this system of advertising has since been carried has rendered it a bore and a nuisance. The usual result of almost any great and original achievement is, the production of a shoal of brainless imitators, who are “neither useful nor ornamental.”

In the same year that Pease’s hoarhound candy appeared upon the commercial and newspaper horizon, the “Governor Dorr Rebellion” occurred in Rhode Island. As many will remember, this rebellion caused a great excitement throughout the country. Citizens of Rhode Island took up arms against each other, and it was feared by some that a bloody civil war would ensue.

At about this time a municipal election was to come off in the city of Philadelphia. The two political parties were pretty equally divided there, and there were some special causes why this was regarded as an unusually important election. Its near approach caused more excitement in the “Quaker City” than had been witnessed there since the preceding Presidential election. The party-leaders began to lay their plans early, and the wire-pullers on both sides were unusually busy in their vocation. At the head of the rabble upon which one of the parties depended for many votes, was a drunken and profane fellow, whom we will call Tom Simmons. Tom was great at electioneering and stump-spouting in barrooms and rum-caucuses, and his party always looked to him, at each election, to stir up the subterraneans “with a long pole”﻿—and a whiskey-jug at the end of it.

The exciting election which was now to come off for Mayor and Aldermen of the good city of Brotherly Love soon brought several of the “ring” to Tom.

===Chinese===

皮斯夏至草糖——多尔叛乱——费城市议员。

1842 年，报刊与传单上出现了一种新奇的广告体例，甫一露面便因别出心裁而吸引了公众的目光。所宣传的商品名为“皮斯夏至草糖”（Pease’s Hoarhound Candy），乃是一种治咳嗽与伤风的良药。它以二十五美分一包的价格装袋出售，最终在批发与零售渠道创下了惊人的销量。皮斯先生的这套广告手法，我相信在国内是由他首创的；此后虽有不少人效仿，但自然远不及他成功——毕竟东施效颦向来难成气候。皮斯先生的策略，是抓住当下最受瞩目、街头巷尾热议的话题，在报纸专栏里洋洋洒洒地用五十到一百行篇幅就此侃侃而谈，随后便不着痕迹地滑入对“皮斯夏至草糖”的盛赞之中。结果便是，每一位读者都被文章的标题和开头引入歧途，成千上万的人甚至在亲眼见到这糖果之前，嘴里早已念叨着“皮斯夏至草糖”了！事实上，当时只要拿起一份报纸想读读当日的正经新闻，几乎不可能不迎头撞上一包“皮斯夏至草糖”。读者往往刚津津有味地读完四分之一栏自己最关心的时事新闻，却发觉自己中了圈套，竟在读皮斯夏至草糖的广告，心头难免恼火。虽说有时气得真想把报纸一把摔在地上，可转念一想，又大多会为皮斯先生这般牢牢套住并俘获读者的才智失笑。最终的结果往往是：只要刚有一点儿咳嗽或流感的苗头，人们便会买一包糖来尝尝。后来这种广告手法被滥用到极致，反倒成了一种令人生厌的滋扰。大凡任何伟大的独创之举，到头来通常都会催生出一大群既“无用又无趣”的无脑模仿者。

就在皮斯夏至草糖登上商界与报界舞台的同一年，罗得岛州爆发了“多尔州长叛乱”（Governor Dorr Rebellion）。正如许多人所记得的那样，这场叛乱在全国上下掀起了轩然大波。罗得岛的公民彼此兵戎相见，甚至有人担忧一场血腥的内战即将随之而来。

大致就在同一时期，费城（Philadelphia）即将举行一场市政选举。当地两大政党势力旗鼓相当，加之另有几桩特殊缘由，此次选举被视为非同寻常的重要角逐。选战临近之际，这座“贵格之城”所呈现出的狂热气氛，甚至超过了上一届总统大选。两党领袖早早便开始排兵布阵，双方的幕后操盘手更是比往常更加忙碌地奔走钻营。其中一个政党指望用来拉拢大量选票的乌合之众，其头目是个嗜酒如命、满口粗话的家伙，我们姑且叫他汤姆·西蒙斯（Tom Simmons）。汤姆最擅长在酒吧间和朗姆酒党团会议上拉票鼓噪、发表街头演说，每逢大选，他的政党总是指望他“拿根长竹竿”去搅动底层选民——当然，竹竿顶端还得挂上一壶威士忌。

眼下这场争夺友爱之城（费城）市长与市议员席位的激烈选战，很快便引得“核心圈”的几位人物找上了汤姆。

===Original===

“Now, Tom,” said the head wire-puller, “this is going to be a close election, and we want you to spare neither talent nor liquor in arousing up and bringing to the polls every voter within your influence.”

“Well, Squire,” replied Tom carelessly, “I’ve concluded I won’t bother myself with this ’lection﻿—it don’t pay!”

“Don’t pay!” exclaimed the frightened politician. “Why, Tom, are you not a true friend to your party? Haven’t you always been on hand at the primary meetings, knocked down interlopers, and squelched every man who talked about conscience, or who refused to support regular nominations, and vote the entire clean ticket straight through? And as for ‘pay,’ haven’t you always been supplied with money enough to treat all doubtful voters, and in fact to float them up to the polls in an ocean of whiskey? I confess Tom, I am almost petrified with astonishment at witnessing your present indifference to the alarming crisis in which our country and our party are involved, and which nothing on Earth can avert, except our success at the coming election.”

“Oh, tell that to the marines,” said Tom. “We never yet had an election that there wasn’t a ‘crisis,’ and yet, whichever party gained, we somehow managed to live through it, crisis or no crisis. In fact, my curiosity has got a little excited, and I would like to see this ‘crisis’ that is such a bugaboo at every election; so trot out your crisis﻿—let us see how it looks. Besides, talking of pay, I acknowledge the whiskey, and that is all. While I and my companions lifted you and your companions into fat offices that enabled you to roll in your carriages, and live on the fat of the land, we got nothing﻿—or, at least, next to nothing﻿—all we got was﻿—well﻿—we got drunk! Now, Squire, I will go for the other party this ’lection if you don’t give me an office.”

“Give you an office!” exclaimed the “Squire,” raising his hands and rolling his eyes in utter amazement; “why, Tom, what office do you want?”

“I want to be Alderman!” replied Tom, “and I can control votes enough to turn the ’lection either way; and if our party don’t gratefully remember my past services and give me my reward, t’other party will be glad to run me on their ticket, and over I go.”

The gentleman of the “ring” saw by Tom’s firmness and clenched teeth that he was immovable; that his principles, like those of too many others, consisted of “loaves and fishes;” they therefore consented to put Tom’s name on the municipal ticket; and the worst part of the story is, he was elected.

===Chinese===

“听着，汤姆，”头号幕后操盘手说道，“这回选情将会十分胶着，我们要你拿出浑身解数，也别吝惜酒水，把你影响力所及的每一位选民都鼓动起来，拉到投票站去。”

“得了，乡绅老爷，”汤姆漫不经心地答道，“我琢磨着，这回选举我就不掺和了——不划算！”

“不划算！”吓坏了的政客失声叫道，“我说汤姆，你难道不是咱们党忠诚的朋友吗？历次预选会你哪回不是准时到场，把不速之客揍趴下，把那些满嘴良心、或者不肯支持正式提名、不肯原封不动全票投给本党名单的家伙统统压下去？至于说到‘报酬’，哪回不是给你备足了银钱，好让你请所有摇摆不定的选民喝酒，简直是拿威士忌的汪洋大海把他们一路漂送到投票箱前？老实说，汤姆，眼看着咱们的国家与政党正陷入如此骇人的危机——除了在即将到来的大选中获胜，世上再无任何法子能够挽救——你竟表现得如此冷漠，我简直惊得目瞪口呆。”

“得了吧，这话骗傻子去吧，”汤姆说，“咱们哪回选举不喊着有‘危机’？可不管哪一党赢了，管它危机不危机，咱们不照样活得好好的。说实话，我倒真有点儿好奇了，很想亲眼见识见识这个每次选举都要拿出来吓唬人的‘危机’到底长啥样；赶紧把你的危机牵出来溜溜——让大伙儿开开眼。再说了，既说到报酬，威士忌我认领了，可也就仅此而已。我跟我的弟兄们把你和你的同伙扶上了肥缺，让你们坐着马车招摇过市、吃香喝辣，而我们却什么也没捞着——或者说，几乎什么也没捞着——我们唯一捞到的不过是——嗯——喝得酩酊大醉！现在听好了，乡绅老爷，这回选举你们要是不给我个一官半职，我就投靠对家去。”

“给你个官职！”那位“乡绅”惊得举起双手、直翻白眼，“天哪，汤姆，你想要个什么官儿？”

“我要当市议员（Alderman）！”汤姆答道，“我手里攥着的选票足够左右这回选举的胜负；要是咱们党不懂得感恩念旧，不肯酬谢我过去的汗马功劳，对家可乐意把我写进他们的候选人名单呢，那我就直接倒戈过去。”

“核心圈”的几位先生见汤姆态度坚决、咬紧牙关，知道他是铁了心不可动摇；他们看出他的政治原则与世上太多其他人一样，无非是“五饼二鱼”的实惠利益；于是只得同意把汤姆的名字列上市政候选人名单；而这桩故事最糟糕的结局在于——他还真就当选了。

===Original===

In a very short time, Tom was duly installed into the Aldermanic chair, and, opening his office on a prominent corner, he was soon doing a thriving business. He was generally occupied throughout the day in sitting as a judge in cases of book debt and promissory notes which were brought before him, for various small sums ranging from two to five, six, eight, and ten dollars. He would frequently dispose of thirty or forty of these cases in a day, and as imprisonment for debt was permitted at that time, the poor defendants would “shin” around and make any sacrifice almost, rather than go to jail. The enormous “costs” went into the capacious pocket of the Alderman; and this dignitary, as a natural sequence, “waxed fat” and saucy, exemplifying the truth of the adage “Put a beggar on horseback,” etc.

As the Alderman grew rich, he became overbearing, headstrong, and dictatorial. He began to fancy that he monopolized the concentrated wisdom of his party, and that his word should be law. Not a party-caucus or a political meeting could be held without witnessing the vulgar and profane harangues of the self-conceited Alderman, Tom Simmons. As he was one of the “ring,” his fingers were in all the “pickings and stealings;” he kept his family-coach, and in his general swagger exhibited all the peculiarities of “high life below stairs.”

But after Tom had disgraced his office for two years, a State election took place and the other party were successful. Among the first laws which they passed after the convening of the Legislature, was one declaring that from that date imprisonment for debt should not be permitted in the State of Pennsylvania for any sum less than ten dollars.

This enactment, of course, knocked away the chief prop which sustained the Alderman, and when the news of its passage reached Philadelphia, Tom was the most indignant man that had been seen there for some years.

Standing in front of his office the next morning, surrounded by several of his political chums, Tom exclaimed:

“Do you see what them infernal tories have done down there at Harrisburg? They have been and passed an outrageous, oppressive, barbarous, and unconstitutional law! A pretty idea, indeed, if a man can’t put a debtor in jail for a less sum than ten dollars! How am I going to support my family, I should like to know, if this law is allowed to stand? I tell you, gentlemen, this law is unconstitutional, and you will see blood running in our streets, if them tory scoundrels try to carry it out!”

===Chinese===

没过多久，汤姆便正式坐上了市议员的宝座。他在一处显眼的街角挂牌办公，很快便做得风生水起。他平日里整天都在升堂断案，审理送到他面前的各类账簿欠款与期票纠纷，涉案金额大多零星琐碎，从两美元到五美元、六美元、八美元乃至十美元不等。他常常一天就能打发掉三四十起这类案件；由于当时法律尚允许因欠债而入狱，可怜的被告们为了免遭牢狱之灾，只得四处奔走筹钱，几乎不惜任何代价。巨额的“诉讼费”源源不断地落入了市议员肥硕的口袋；顺理成章地，这位显贵便日渐“腰缠万贯”、趾高气扬起来，活脱脱印证了那句俗谚——“乞丐骑上马，得意忘形”，等等。

市议员腰包鼓起来之后，变得愈发盛气凌人、刚愎自用、独断专行。他开始自以为独占了本党智慧的结晶，觉得自己金口一开便是金科玉律。凡是召开党团会议或政治集会，必定少不了自负至极的市议员汤姆·西蒙斯那番粗鄙亵渎的高谈阔论。身为“核心圈”的一员，大大小小的“油水外快”他无不插手染指；他养起了自家马车，平日里摇摇摆摆的做派，把“下人学主子作威作福”的习气展露得淋漓尽致。

然而，在汤姆玷污这一公职整整两年之后，州议会选举举行，对立政党大获全胜。新议会召集之后率先通过的几部法律中，便有一条明文规定：自即日起，在宾夕法尼亚州境内，凡欠款金额不足十美元者，概不得因债监禁。

这项法案自然抽掉了维系市议员生计的顶梁柱。法案通过的消息传到费城时，汤姆简直成了这座城市好些年来最怒不可遏的人。

次日清晨，汤姆站在自己办公室门前，周围簇拥着几个政坛狐朋狗友，他破口大骂道：

“你们瞧瞧哈里斯堡（Harrisburg）那帮该死的保守派干的好事！他们居然通过了一项骇人听闻、欺压百姓、野蛮透顶而又违宪的恶法！真是荒唐透顶，欠债不到十美元难道就不能把欠债人关进大牢了吗？我倒要问问，要是任由这破法条生效，我拿什么养活一家老小？诸位，我告诉你们，这部法律是违宪的，那帮保守派混蛋要是敢强行推行，你们就等着瞧大街上血流成河吧！”

===Original===

His friends laughed, for they saw that Tom was reasoning from his pocket instead of his head; and, as he almost foamed at the mouth in his impotent wrath they could not suppress a smile.

“Oh, you may laugh, gentlemen﻿—you may laugh; but you will see it. Our party will never disgrace itself a permitting the tories to rob them of their rights by passing unconstitutional laws; and I say, the sooner we come to blood, the better!”

At this moment, a gentleman stepped up, and addressing the Alderman, said:

“Alderman, I want to bring a case of book debt before you this morning.”

“How much is your claim?” asked Tom.

“Four dollars,” replied the rumseller﻿—for such he proved to be﻿—and his debt was for drinks chalked up against one of his “customers.”

“You can’t have your four dollars, Sir,” replied the excited Alderman. “You are robbed of your four dollars, Sir. Them legislative tories at Harrisburg, Sir, have cheated you out of your four dollars, Sir. I undertake to say, Sir, that fifty thousand honest men in Philadelphia have been robbed of their four dollars by these bloody tories and their cursed unconstitutional law! Ah, gentlemen, you will see blood running in our streets before you are a month older. (A laugh.) Oh, you may laugh; but you will see it﻿—see if you don’t!”

===Chinese===

他的朋友们哄笑起来，因为他们一眼便看出汤姆是拿钱包而非脑袋在讲道理；瞧着他在无能狂怒中险些口吐白沫，众人实在忍俊不禁。

“哦，你们尽管笑吧，先生们——你们尽管笑；可你们很快就会亲眼见识到。咱们党绝不会蒙受奇耻大辱，任凭保守派靠通过违宪恶法来剥夺咱们的权利；我把话撂这儿，流血的日子来得越早越好！”

就在这时，一位先生走上前来，对着市议员开口道：

“市议员大人，今早我想在您这儿递一桩账簿欠款的案子。”

“你要讨多少钱？”汤姆问道。

“四美元，”那位酒馆老板答道——后来查明他确是卖酒的——这笔欠款是记在一位“老主顾”账上的酒钱。

“先生，您这四美元拿不回来了，”情绪激动的市议员答道，“您的四美元被人抢走了，先生。哈里斯堡议会里那帮保守派，先生，生生坑走了您的四美元，先生。我敢打包票，先生，费城有五万名老实人被这帮该死的保守派和他们那套遭天谴的违宪恶法抢走了四美元！啊，先生们，不出一个月，你们就会看到大街上血流成河。（一阵笑声。）哦，你们尽管笑；可你们等着瞧吧——瞧着到底会不会发生！”

===Original===

A newsboy was just passing by.

“Here, boy, give me the *Morning Ledger*,” said the Alderman, at the same time taking the paper and handing the boy a penny. “Let us see what them blasted cowboys are doing down at Harrisburg now. Ah!﻿—what is this?” (Reading:) “ ‘Blood, blood, blood!’ Aha! laugh, will you, gentlemen? Here it is.” Reads:

> “ ‘Blood, blood, blood! The Dorrites have got possession of Providence. The military are called out. Father is arrayed against father, and son against son. Blood is already running in our streets.’

“Now laugh, will you, gentlemen? Blood is running in the streets of Providence; blood will be running in the streets of Philadelphia before you are a fortnight older! The tories of Providence and the tories of Harrisburg must answer for this blood, for they and their unconstitutional proceedings are the cause of its flowing! Let us see the rest of this tragic scene.” Reads:

> “ ‘Is there any remedy for this dreadful state of things?’ ”

Alderman.﻿—“Of course not, except to hang every rascal of them for trampling on our g-l-orious Constitution.” Reads:

> “ ‘Is there any remedy for this dreadful state of things? Yes, there is.’ ”

Alderman.﻿—“Oh, there is, is there? What is it? Let me see.” Reads:

> “ ‘Buy two packages of Pease’s hoarhound candy.’ ”

“Blast the infernal *Ledger*!” exclaimed the now doubly incensed and indignant Alderman, throwing the paper upon the pavement with the most ineffable disgust, amid the shouts and hurrahs of a score of men who by this time had gathered around the excited Alderman Tom Simmons.

As I before remarked, the “candy” was a very good article for the purposes for which it was made; and as Pease was an indefatigable man, as well as a good advertiser, he soon acquired a fortune. Mr. Pease, Junior, is now living in affluence in Brooklyn, and is bringing up a “happy family” to enjoy the fruits of his industry, probity, good habits, and genius.

The “humbug” in this transaction, of course consisted solely in the manner of advertising. There was no humbug or deception about the article manufactured.

### VIII / 第八章

===Original===

Brandreth’s pills﻿—​Magnificent advertising﻿—Power of imagination.
In the year 1834, Dr. Benjamin Brandreth commenced advertising in the city of New York, “Brandreth’s Pills specially recommended to purify the blood.” His office consisted of a room about ten feet square, located in what was then known as the *Sun* building, an edifice ten by forty feet, situated at the corner of Spruce and Nassau Streets, where the *Tribune* is now published. His “factory” was at his residence in Hudson Street. He put up a large gilt sign over the *Sun* office, five or six feet wide by the length of the building, which attracted much attention, as at that time it was probably the largest sign in New York. Dr. Brandreth had great faith in his pills, and I believe not without reason; for multitudes of persons soon became convinced of the truth of his assertions, that “all diseases arise from impurity or imperfect circulation of the blood, and by purgation with Brandreth’s Pills all disease may be cured.”
But great and reasonable as might have been the faith of Dr. Brandreth in the efficacy of his pills, his faith in the potency of advertising them was equally strong. Hence he commenced advertising largely in the *Sun* newspaper﻿—paying at least $5,000 to that paper alone, for his first year’s advertisements. That may not seem a large sum in these days, when parties have been known to pay more than five thousand dollar for a single day’s advertising in the leading journals; but, at the time Brandreth started, his was considered the most liberal newspaper-advertising of the day.
Advertising is to a genuine article what manure is to land﻿—it largely increases the product. Thousands of persons may be reading your advertisement while you are eating, or sleeping, or attending to your business; hence public attention is attracted, new customers come to you, and, if you render them a satisfactory equivalent for their money, they continue to patronize you and recommend you to their friends.

===Chinese===

布兰德雷斯药丸——气魄宏大的广告——想象力的威力。

1834 年，本杰明·布兰德雷斯医生（Dr. Benjamin Brandreth）开始在纽约市刊登广告，宣传“布兰德雷斯药丸，专效清血”。他的办公室不过是一间十英尺见方的小屋，设在当时人称“《太阳报》大楼”的建筑内——那是一座十乘四十英尺的房子，坐落在斯普鲁斯街与拿骚街交界处，即如今《论坛报》出版的地方。他的“工厂”就设在哈得孙街的寓所里。他在《太阳报》社上方悬起一块巨大的鎏金招牌，宽五六英尺，长度与整座大楼齐平，极为引人注目，因为在当时这大概是全纽约最大的一块招牌。布兰德雷斯医生对自己的药丸笃信不疑，我想也并非全无道理；因为没过多久，成群结队的人便深信他的断言：“百病皆因血液不洁或循环不畅而起，借布兰德雷斯药丸清泻体内污浊，诸疾皆可痊愈。”

然而，尽管布兰德雷斯医生对自家药丸的疗效信心十足且言之有理，他对广告威力的信仰却丝毫不逊分毫。于是，他开始在《太阳报》上大举投放广告——仅头一年付给该报的广告费便至少达 5,000 美元。在如今这个有人动辄为各大报刊单日广告豪掷逾五千美元的时代，这笔钱或许算不得什么大数目；但在布兰德雷斯起步的年代，他这番手笔已被公认为当时最慷慨阔绰的报刊广告投入。

广告对于货真价实的商品，正如同肥料对于土地——它能极大提升收成。当你用餐、安睡或忙于日常事务之际，成千上万的人或许正在阅读你的广告；由此公众的注意力被吸引过来，新主顾登门光顾，而只要你回馈给他们物有所值的商品，他们便会持续惠顾，并将你推荐给亲朋好友。

===Original===

At the commencement of his career, Dr. Brandreth was indebted to Mr. Moses Y. Beach, proprietor of the *New York Sun*, for encouragement and means of advertising. But this very advertising soon caused his receipts to be enormous. Although the pills were but twenty-five cents per box, they were soon sold to such a great extent, that tons of huge cases filled with the “purely vegetable pill” were sent from the new and extensive manufactory every week. As his business increased, so in the same ratio did he extend his advertising. The doctor engaged at one time a literary gentleman to attend, under the supervision of himself, solely to the advertising department. Column upon column of advertisements appeared in the newspapers, in the shape of learned and scientific pathological dissertations, the very reading of which would tempt a poor mortal to rush for a box of Brandreth’s Pills; so evident was it (according to the advertisement) that nobody ever had or ever would have “pure blood,” until from one to a dozen boxes of the pills had been taken as “purifiers.” The ingenuity displayed in concocting these advertisements was superb, and was probably hardly equaled by that required to concoct the pills.
No pain, ache, twinge, or other sensation, good, bad, or indifferent, ever experienced by a member of the human family, but was a most irrefragable evidence of the impurity of the blood; and it would have been blasphemy to have denied the “self-evident” theory, that “all diseases arise from impurity or imperfect circulation of the blood, and that by purgation with Brandreth’s Pills all disease may be cured.”
The doctor claims that his grandfather first manufactured the pills in 1751. I suppose this may be true; at all events, no *living* man will be apt to testify to the contrary. Here is an extract from one of Dr. Brandreth’s early advertisements, which will give an idea of his style:

> “ ‘What has been longest known has been most considered, and what has been most considered is best understood.
> “ ‘The life of the flesh is in the blood.’ —﻿Leviticus 22:2
> “Bleeding reduces the vital powers; Brandreth’s Pills increase them. So in sickness never be bled, especially in Dizziness and Apoplexy, but always use Brandreth’s Pills.
> “The laws of life are written upon the face of Nature. The Tempest, Whirlwind, and Thunderstorm bring health from the Solitudes of God. The Tides are the daily agitators and purifiers of the Mighty World of Waters.
> “What these Providential means are as purifiers of the Atmosphere or Air, Brandreth’s Pills are to man.”

===Chinese===

在事业起步之初，布兰德雷斯医生曾蒙《纽约太阳报》社主摩西·Y. 比奇先生（Mr. Moses Y. Beach）多方鼓励并给予登报之便。然而恰恰是这番广告攻势，很快便令他的进账暴增至惊人地步。尽管这药丸每盒仅售二十五美分，销量却迅速飙升，以至于每周都有成吨装满这种“纯植物药丸”的大木箱从他那座新建的宽敞制药厂发往各地。随着生意越做越大，他也按同等比例不断扩大广告规模。医生一度专门聘请了一位文人，在他本人的督导下专职打理广告部门。报纸上连篇累牍地刊登出一栏又一栏的广告，行文犹如渊博深奥、富有科学依据的病理学论文，单是读上几段，便足以诱使可怜的凡夫俗子急忙冲去买上一盒布兰德雷斯药丸；因为依广告所言，道理再明显不过：若不服下一盒乃至一打药丸作为“清血剂”，世人便从未拥有过、也永远不可能拥有“洁净的血液”。炮制这些广告所展现出的巧思妙想可谓登峰造极，恐怕连配制那药丸本身所需的匠心也望尘莫及。

凡是人类成员所经历过的任何疼痛、酸楚、刺痛或其他感受——无论好坏与否——无一不是血液不洁的铁证；若有人胆敢否认那套“不言自明”的理论，即“百病皆因血液不洁或循环不畅而起，借布兰德雷斯药丸清泻，诸疾皆可痊愈”，那简直形同亵渎神明。

医生宣称他的祖父早于 1751 年便首次制出了此药丸。我想这或许不假；无论如何，眼下*活在世上*的人里，怕是没谁能作证反驳。以下摘录自布兰德雷斯医生早期的一则广告，读者由此可窥见其行文风格之一斑：

> “‘知之愈久者，思之愈深；思之愈深者，明之愈彻。
> “‘活物的生命是在血中。’——《利未记》22:2
> “放血削弱生命元气；布兰德雷斯药丸则增进元气。故患病时切勿放血，尤以眩晕与中风为忌，务须常服布兰德雷斯药丸。
> “生命之律已铭刻于自然容颜之上。暴风、旋风与雷霆自上帝的幽寂之境带来生机与健康。潮汐乃是浩瀚水域每日的搅动者与净化者。
> “此等天佑之力于大气或空气之净化何如，布兰德雷斯药丸于人身亦复如是。”

===Original===

This splendid system of advertising, and the almost reckless outlay which was required to keep it up, challenged the admiration of the business community. In the course of a few years, his office was enlarged; and still being too small, he took the store 241 Broadway, and also opened a branch at 187 Hudson Street. The doctor continued to let his advertising keep pace with his patronage; and he was finally, in the year 1836, compelled to remove his manufactory to Sing Sing, where such perfectly incredible quantities of Brandreth’s Pills have been manufactured and sold that it would hardly be safe to give the statistics. Suffice it to say, that the only “humbug” which I suspect in connection with the pills was, the very harmless and unobjectionable yet novel method of advertising them; and as the doctor amassed a great fortune by their manufacture, this very fact is prima facie evidence that the pill was a valuable purgative.
A funny incident occurred to me in connection with this great pill. In the year 1836, while I was travelling through the States of Alabama, Mississippi, and Louisiana, I became convinced by reading Doctor Brandreth’s advertisements that I needed his pills. Indeed, I there read the proof that every symptom that I experienced, either in imagination or in reality, rendered their extensive consumption absolutely necessary to preserve my life. I purchased a box of Brandreth’s Pills in Columbus, MS The effect was miraculous! Of course, it was just what the advertisement told me it would be. In Tuscaloosa, Alabama, I purchased half a dozen boxes. They were all used up before my perambulating show reached Vicksburg, MS, and I was a confirmed disciple of the blood theory. There I laid in a dozen boxes. In Natchez, I made a similar purchase. In New Orleans, where I remained several months, I was a profitable customer, and had become thoroughly convinced that the only real “greenhorns” in the world were those who preferred meat or bread to Brandreth’s Pills. I took them morning, noon, and night. In fact, the advertisements announced that one could not take too many; for if one box was sufficient to purify the blood, eleven extra boxes would have no injurious effect.
I arrived in New York in June 1838, and by that time I had become such a firm believer in the efficacy of Brandreth’s Pills, that I hardly stopped long enough to speak with my family, before I hastened to the “principal office” of Doctor Brandreth to congratulate him on being the greatest public benefactor of the age.
I found the doctor “at home,” and introduced myself without ceremony. I told him my experiences. He was delighted. I next heartily endorsed every word stated in his advertisements. He was not surprised, for he knew the effects of his pills were such as I described. Still he was elated in having another witness whose extensive experiments with his pills were so eminently satisfactory. The doctor and myself were both happy﻿—he in being able to do so much good to mankind; I in being the recipient of such untold benefits through his valuable discovery.

===Chinese===

这套辉煌的广告体系，以及维持它运转所需的近乎孤注一掷的巨额开销，赢得了商界同行的钦佩。短短数年间，他的办公室便一再扩建；依旧嫌窄小后，他又租下了百老汇大道 241 号的铺面，并在哈得孙街 187 号开设了分店。医生始终让广告投放与生意兴隆同步扩张；终于在 1836 年，他不得不将制药厂迁往辛辛（Sing Sing），在那里生产与售出的布兰德雷斯药丸数量之庞大简直令人难以置信，若要报出具体统计数字，怕是反倒显得不甚妥当。总而言之，依我揣测，围绕这药丸唯一算得上“欺罔”（humbug）的，不过是它那既毫无恶意、无可非议却又别出心裁的广告手法；而医生借制造此药积聚起万贯家财，这一事实本身便是证明该药丸确为一剂良效泻药的初步确证。

围绕这颗神奇药丸，我自己还亲历过一桩趣事。1836 年，我正辗转亚拉巴马州、密西西比州与路易斯安那州巡回演出，读了布兰德雷斯医生的广告后，便深信自己急需服用他的药丸。诚然，我在广告里读到的种种论据表明，无论是我臆想出来的还是实打实感受到的每一种症状，都决定了唯有大量吞服此药方能保住性命。我在密西西比州哥伦布市买下一盒布兰德雷斯药丸。药效简直如奇迹一般！这自然完全应验了广告里的说法。到了亚拉巴马州塔斯卡卢萨，我又买下半打。不等我的巡回马戏团抵达密西西比州维克斯堡，那半打药丸便已吞食一空，而我也成了“血液学说”的忠实信徒。我在维克斯堡又囤了一打。到了纳奇兹，我再次如法炮制。在新奥尔良逗留的几个月里，我成了药店的大主顾，彻底坚信世上真正的“傻瓜”，莫过于那些宁吃鱼肉面包而不吃布兰德雷斯药丸的人。我早中晚一日三顿按时吞服。事实上，广告里也宣称这药多多益善；因为倘若一盒便足以净化血液，那么再多吃上十一盒也绝不会有任何害处。

1838 年 6 月我回到纽约。彼时我已对布兰德雷斯药丸的奇效深信不疑，甚至顾不上在家中与亲人多叙几句，便火急火燎地赶往布兰德雷斯医生的“总办事处”，当面祝贺他成为当代最伟大的公众恩人。

我见医生“正在府上”，便不拘礼节地上前自报家门，向他细述自己的服药经历。他听后喜出望外。接着我又由衷赞同他广告中一字一句的陈述。他对此毫不意外，因为他深知自家药丸的效用本就如我所述；不过，能再添一位亲身试药数月而效果极其圆满的见证人，仍令他振奋不已。医生与我俱感欣慰——他为自己能造福苍生而高兴，我则为自己蒙受他这项宝贵发明的莫大恩泽而庆幸。

===Original===

At last, the doctor chanced to say that he wondered how I happened to get his pills in Natchez, “for,” said he, “I have no agent there as yet.”
“Oh!” I replied, “I always bought my pills at the drug stores.”
“Good Heavens!” exclaimed the doctor, “then they are were all counterfeits! vile impositions! poisonous compounds! I never sell a pill to a druggist﻿—I never permit an apothecary to handle one of my pills. But they counterfeit them by the bushel; the unprincipled, heartless, murderous impostors!”
I need not say I was surprised. Was it possible, then, that my imagination had done all this business, and that I had been cured by poisons which I supposed were Brandreth’s Pill? I confess I laughed heartily; and told the doctor that, after all, it seemed the counterfeits were as good as the real pills, provided the patient had sufficient faith.
The doctor was puzzled as well as vexed, but an idea struck him that soon enabled him to recover his usual equanimity.
“I’ll tell you what it is,” said he, “those Southern druggists have undoubtedly obtained the pills from me under false pretences. They have pretended to be planters, and have purchased pills from me in large quantities for use on the plantations, and then they have retailed the pills from their drug-shops.”
I laughed at this shrewd suggestion, and remarked: “This may be so, but I guess my imagination did the business!”
The doctor was uneasy, but he asked me as a favor to bring him one of the empty pill boxes which I had brought from the South. The next day, I complied with his request, and I will do the doctor justice to say that, on comparison, it proved as he had suspected; the pills were genuine, and although he had advertised that no druggist should sell them, they were so popular that druggists found it necessary to get them “by hook or by crook;” and the consequence was, I had the pleasure of a glorious laugh, and Doctor Brandreth experienced “a great scare.”
The doctor “made his pile” long ago, although he still devotes his personal attention to the “entirely vegetable and innocent pills, whose life-giving power no pen can describe.”
In 1849, the doctor was elected President of the Village of Sing Sing, N.Y. (where he still resides,) and was reelected to the same office for seven consecutive years. In the same year, he was elected to the New York State Senate, and in 1859 was again elected.
Dr. Brandreth is a liberal man and a pleasant, entertaining, and edifying companion. He deserves all the success he has ever received. “Long may he wave!”

===Chinese===

末了，医生随口说起，他颇纳闷我在纳奇兹究竟是怎么买到他家药丸的，“因为，”他说道，“我在那里至今还没设代理商呢。”

“哦！”我答道，“我向来都是在药店里买的。”

“天哪！”医生失声叫道，“那你买的全是假药！卑劣的冒牌货！有毒的混合物！我从来不把哪怕一颗药丸卖给药店老板——我绝不允许药剂师经手我的药丸。可他们竟成蒲式耳地造假；这帮毫无操守、丧尽天良、草菅人命的骗子！”

不用说，我当时大吃一惊。莫非先前这一切奇效全凭我自己的想象力撑腰，我竟是靠着那些自以为是布兰德雷斯药丸的毒药治好了自己？我承认自己忍不住放声大笑，并对医生说，看来只要病人心怀足够的信仰，假药竟也与真药一般灵验。

医生听罢既困惑又恼火，不过灵光一闪，一个念头很快令他恢复了惯常的镇定。

“我告诉你到底是怎么回事，”他说道，“南方那些药店老板无疑是冒名骗过了我，才弄到了这批药丸。他们伪装成种植园主，以庄园自用为名从我这儿大宗买走药丸，回头却在自家药铺里零售。”

我听了这番机敏的推测不禁失笑，答道：“或许果真如此，但我猜还是我的想象力起了决定作用！”

医生心头仍觉不安，便恳请我帮个忙，把从南方带回来的空药盒拿一只给他瞧瞧。次日我照办了，我也得替医生说句公道话：经比对查验，果如他所料——那药丸确系正品。尽管他曾在广告中声明绝不许药店经销，但此药实在太过抢手，药店老板们只得“不择手段”地设法进货；结果便是，我痛痛快快地大笑了一场，而布兰德雷斯医生则着实“吓出了一身冷汗”。

医生早已“赚得盆满钵满”，尽管他至今仍亲力亲为地照料着他那“纯植物配制、绝无害处、笔墨难以形容其回春之力的药丸”。

1849 年，医生当选为纽约州辛辛村（他至今仍居住于此）村长，并连续七年蝉联该职。同年，他还当选为纽约州参议员，并于 1859 年再度当选。

布兰德雷斯医生为人慷慨大度，且谈吐风趣、引人入胜、颇具教益，是个极好的伴侣。他迄今所赢得的一切成功，皆乃实至名归。“愿他长久风风光光！”

## Part / 部

===Original===

II The Spiritualists

===Chinese===

第二部 招魂术士与灵媒

### IX / 第九章

===Original===

The Davenport brothers, their rise and progress﻿—​Spiritual rope-tying﻿—​Music playing﻿—​Cabinet secrets﻿—​“They choose darkness rather than light,” etc.﻿—​The spiritual hand﻿—​How the thing is done﻿—​Dr. W. F. Van Vleck.
The Davenport Brothers are natives of Buffalo, N.Y., and in that city commenced their career as “mediums” about twelve years ago. They were then mere lads. For some time, their operations were confined to their own place, where, having obtained considerable notoriety through the press, they were visited by people from all parts of the country. But, in 1855, they were induced by John F. Coles, a very worthy spiritualist of New York City, to visit that metropolis, and there exhibit their powers. Under the management of Mr. Coles, they held “circles” afternoon and evening, for several days, in a small hall at 195 Bowery. The audience were seated next the walls, the principal space being required for the use of “the spirits.” The “manifestations” mostly consisted in the thrumming and seemingly rapid movement about the hall of several stringed instruments, the room having been made entirely dark, while the boys were supposed or asserted to be quietly seated at the table in the center. Two guitars, with sometimes a banjo, were the instruments used, and the noise made by “the spirits” was about equal to the united honking of a large flock of wild geese. The manifestations were stunning as well as astonishing; for not only was the sense of hearing smitten by the dreadful sounds, but, sometimes, a member of the circle would get a “striking demonstration” over his head!
At the request of the “controlling spirit,” made through a horn, the hall was lighted at intervals during the entertainment, at which times the mediums could be seen seated at the table, looking very innocent and demure, as if they had never once thought of deceiving anybody. On one of these occasions, however, a policeman suddenly lighted the hall by means of a dark lantern, without having been specially called upon to do so; and the boys were clearly seen with instruments in their hands. They dropped them as soon as they could, and resumed their seats at the table. Satisfied that the thing was a humbug, the audience left in disgust; and the policeman was about to march the boys to the station-house on the charge of swindling, when he was prevailed upon to remain and farther test the matter. Left alone with them, and the three seated together at the table on which the instruments had been placed, he laid, at their request, a hand on each medium’s head; they then clasped both his arms with their hands. While they remained thus situated (as he supposed,) the room being dark, one of the instruments, with an infernal twanging of its strings, rose from the table and hit the policeman several times on the head; then a strange voice through the trumpet advised him not to interfere with the work of the spirits by persecuting the mediums! Considerably astonished, if not positively scared, he took his hat and left, fully persuaded that there was “something in it!”
The boys produced the manifestations by grasping the neck of the instrument, swinging it around, and thrusting it into different parts of the open space of the room, at the same time vibrating the strings with the forefinger. The faster the finger passed over the strings, the more rapidly the instrument seemed to move. Two hands could thus use as many instruments.
When sitting with a person at the table, as they did with the policeman, one hand could be taken off the investigator’s arm without his knowing it, by gently increasing, at the same time, the pressure of the other hand. It was an easy matter then to raise and thrum the instrument or talk through the horn.

===Chinese===

达文波特兄弟及其发迹与演变——通灵捆绳——奏乐——降神柜的秘密——“他们宁要黑暗不要光明”，等等——幽灵之手——戏法究竟如何拆穿——W. F. 范弗莱克医生。

达文波特兄弟（Davenport Brothers）是纽约州布法罗的本地人，大约十二年前就在那座城市开启了他们的“灵媒”（mediums）生涯。当时他们不过是两个毛头小子。有好一阵子，他们的活动范围仅限于家乡一带，在那里靠着报刊宣传赚足了名声，引得全国各地的人纷纷前来拜访。然而到了 1855 年，纽约市一位颇有声望的招魂论者约翰·F. 科尔斯（John F. Coles）说动了他们前往那座大都会，当众展示他们的神通。在科尔斯先生的安排下，他们连续数日在包厘街 195 号的一间小礼堂里，每天下午和晚上各举办一场“降神会”。观众沿墙根落座，场地中央大片空间则须留给“亡灵”施展。所谓“显灵”大多表现为几件弦乐器在完全漆黑的屋子里叮咚作响，似乎还在大厅里飞快地四处穿梭，而众人则被告知或理所当然地以为，那两个男孩正安安静静地坐在屋子中央的桌旁。所用乐器为两把吉他，有时外加一把班卓琴，而“亡灵”制造出的噪音，差不多相当于一大群野雁齐声嘶鸣。这番显灵既令人震耳欲聋，又让人惊愕不已；因为不仅听觉遭到可怕声响的狂轰滥炸，有时圈子里某位观众的脑袋还会挨上一记“直击灵魂的实证”！

按照“主控亡灵”通过一只号角发出的指示，表演期间礼堂会每隔一阵亮起灯光，每逢此时，便能看见两位灵媒端坐在桌旁，神情纯洁无辜、庄重安详，仿佛压根儿没动过欺骗任何人的念头。然而有一次，一名警察未等亡灵发话，便突然拧亮随身携带的遮光提灯照亮大厅；众人清清楚楚地看见那两个男孩手里正攥着乐器。他们手忙脚乱地丢下乐器，重新坐回桌旁。观众确信这纯属欺罔（humbug），愤然离席；那名警察也正准备以诈骗罪名将两个男孩押往警局，却被人劝住，留下来进一步查验虚实。屋里只剩下他们三人，一同坐在摆放乐器的桌旁，警察应他们之请，将一只手分别按在两位灵媒头顶；他们则用双手握住警察的两条胳膊。就在他们（依警察以为）保持这般姿势之际，屋里漆黑一片，其中一件乐器竟伴着刺耳的拨弦声从桌上腾空而起，接连在警察头上敲了好几下；紧接着，号角里传出一个诡异的声音，劝告他休要迫害灵媒、妨碍亡灵行事！这警察即便没被吓破胆，也着实大吃一惊，随即戴上帽子扬长而去，彻底相信这事儿里头“确有门道！”

那两个男孩制造显灵的法子，其实是攥住乐器的琴颈，将其挥舞着伸向屋子空旷处的不同方位，同时用食指拨动琴弦。手指拨弦越快，乐器听起来就仿佛移动得越迅疾。两只手便能同时操弄两件乐器。

当他们像对付那名警察一样与旁人同坐桌旁时，只需在轻轻加重另一只手按压力度的同时，便可神不知鬼不觉地把一只手从查验者的胳膊上抽走。接下来，举起乐器拨弦或是对着号角说话，自然易如反掌。

===Original===

About a dozen gentlemen﻿—several of whom were members of the press﻿—had a private séance with the boys one afternoon, on which occasion “the spirits” ventured upon an extra “manifestation.” All took seats at one side of a long, high table﻿—the position of the mediums being midway of the row. This time, a little, dim, ghostly gaslight was allowed in the room. What seemed to be a hand soon appeared, partly above the edge of the vacant side of the table, and opposite the “mediums.” One excited spiritualist present said he could see the fingernails.
John F. Coles﻿—who had for several days, suspected the innocence of the boys﻿—sprang from his seat, turned up the gaslight, and pounced on the elder boy, who was found to have a nicely stuffed glove drawn partly on to the toe of his boot. That, then, was the spirit-hand! The nails that the imaginative spiritualist thought he saw were not on the fingers. The boy alleged that the spirits made him attempt the deception.
The father of these boys, who had accompanied them to New York, took them home immediately after that exposure. In Buffalo, they continued to hold “circles,” hoping to retrieve their lost reputation as good mediums﻿—by being, not more honest, but more cautious. To prevent anyone getting hold of them while operating, they hit upon the plan of passing a rope through a buttonhole of each gentleman’s coat, the ends to be held by a trusty person﻿—assigning, as a reason for that arrangement, that it would then be known no one in the circle could assist in producing the manifestations. The plan did not always work well, however; for a skeptic would sometimes cut the rope, and then pounce upon “the spirit”﻿—that is, if he didn’t happen to miss that individual, on account of the darkness and while trying to avoid a collision with the instruments.
To secure greater immunity from detection, and to enable them to exhibit in large halls which could not easily be darkened, the boys finally fixed upon a “cabinet” as the best thing in which to work. They had, some time before, made the “rope-test” a feature of their exhibitions; and in their cabinet-show they depended for success in deceiving entirely upon the presumption of the audience that their hands were so secured with ropes as to prevent their playing upon the musical instruments, or doing whatever else the spirits were assumed to do.
Their cabinet is about six feet high, six feet long, and two and a half feet deep, the front consisting of three doors, opening outward. In each end is a seat, with holes through which the ropes can be passed in securing the mediums. In the upper part of the middle door is a lozenge-shaped aperture, curtained on the inside with black muslin or oilcloth. The bolts are on the inside of the doors.

===Chinese===

一天下午，约莫一打绅士——其中几位是报界人士——与这两个男孩举行了一场私人降神会（séance），那回“亡灵”大着胆子来了场额外“显灵”。众人全都在一张高大长桌的同一侧落座，两位灵媒坐在那排人的正当中。这一次，屋里获准留了一盏微弱、幽暗、如鬼火般的煤气灯。很快，一只看似人手的物事从桌子空着的那一侧边缘探出半截，正对着“灵媒”。在场一位亢奋的招魂论者声称自己甚至能看清手指甲。

约翰·F. 科尔斯——他好几天来早已对这两个男孩的清白生疑——猛地从座位上弹起，拧亮煤气灯，一把扑向年长的那个男孩，当场抓获他靴子尖上正套着半只塞得鼓鼓囊囊的手套。原来这就是那只“幽灵之手”！那位富于想象力的招魂论者以为自己瞧见的指甲，压根儿不在手指头上。那男孩却狡辩称，是亡灵逼着他搞这场骗局的。

那次穿帮之后，一直陪着兄弟俩来到纽约的父亲立刻把他们带回了家。回到布法罗后，他们继续举办“降神会”，指望挽回自己作为优秀灵媒的受损声誉——靠的不是变得更诚实，而是变得更谨慎。为防止施法时被人一把抓住，他们想出个主意：拿一根绳子穿过每位男士外套的扣眼，两端由一位可靠之人攥着——对外宣称这样安排是为了证明圈内绝无他人协助制造显灵。不过这招并不总是灵验；因为有时会有怀疑论者干脆剪断绳子，随后猛扑向“亡灵”——当然，前提是他在漆黑中躲避乐器撞击时没扑个空。

为了更大程度地免遭识破，也为了能在不易彻底遮黑的大礼堂里登台献艺，兄弟俩最终选定了“降神柜”（cabinet）作为最佳行骗道具。此前不久，他们已将“捆绳测试”打造成演出的招牌项目；而在降神柜表演中，他们行骗得手全赖观众先入为主的假定——以为他们的双手已被绳索捆得结结实实，绝无可能弹奏乐器或做出其他被归功于亡灵的举动。

他们的降神柜高约六英尺、长六英尺、深两英尺半，正面由三扇向外开启的门构成。柜子两端各设一只座位，座上凿有孔洞，供捆绑灵媒时穿绳之用。中间那扇门的上部开有一个菱形小窗，内侧垂挂着黑色细棉布或油布帘子。门栓则装在柜门内侧。

===Original===

The mediums are generally first tied by a committee of two gentlemen appointed from the audience. The doors of the cabinet are then closed, those at the ends first, and then the middle one, the bolt of which is reached by the manager through the aperture.
By the time the end doors are closed and bolted, the Davenports, in many instances, have succeeded in loosening the knots next their wrists, and in slipping their hands out, the latter being then exhibited at the aperture. Lest the hands should be recognized as belonging to the mediums, they are kept in a constant shaking motion while in view; and to make the hands look large or small, they spread or press together the fingers. With that peculiar rapid motion imparted to them, four hands in the aperture will appear to be half-a-dozen. A lady’s flesh colored kid glove, nicely stuffed with cotton, is sometimes exhibited as a female hand﻿—a critical observation of it never being allowed. It does not take the medium long to draw the knots close to their wrists again. They are then ready to be inspected by the Committee, who report them tied as they were left. Supposing them to have been securely bound all the while, those who witness the show are very naturally astonished.
Sometimes, after being tied by a committee, the mediums cannot readily extricate their hands and get them back as they were; in which case they release themselves entirely from the ropes before the doors are again opened, concluding to wait till after “the spirits” have bound them, before showing hands or making music.
It is a common thing for these impostors to give the rope between their hands a twist while those limbs are being bound; and that movement, if dexterously made, while the attention of the committeemen is momentarily diverted, is not likely to be detected. Reversing that movement will let the hand out.
The great point with the Davenports in tying themselves is, to have a knot next their wrists that looks solid, “fair and square,” at the same time that they can slip it and get their hands out in a moment. There are several ways of forming such a knot, one of which I will attempt to describe. In the middle of a rope a square knot is tied, loosely at first, so that the ends of the rope can be tucked through, in opposite directions, below the knot, and the latter is then drawn tight. There are then two loops﻿—which should be made small﻿—through which the hands are to pass after the rest of the tying is done. Just sufficient slack is left to admit of the hands passing through the loops, which, lastly, are drawn close to the wrists, the knot coming between the latter. No one, from the appearance of such a knot, would suspect it could be slipped. The mediums thus tied can, immediately after the committee have inspected the knots, and closed the doors, show hands or play upon musical instruments, and in a few seconds be, to all appearance, firmly tied again.

===Chinese===

通常先由观众推选出的两名绅士组成委员会，将灵媒捆绑起来。随后关上降神柜的门，先关两端，再关中间那扇，由经理伸手穿过菱形小窗将中间的门栓插上。

往往等两端的柜门刚一关好插栓，达文波特兄弟便已设法松开了手腕处的绳结，把手抽了出来，随后在小窗处晃动展示。唯恐别人认出那是灵媒自己的手，他们在众人视线内会让手保持不停抖动；为了显得手大或手小，他们时而张开手指，时而并拢手指。借着那种特有的急速晃动，小窗里的四只手看上去就像有半打之多。有时他们还会把一只塞满棉花的女士肉色小羊皮手套伸出来充作女鬼的手——当然，绝不允许观众凑近细看。灵媒不用费多少工夫，便能将绳结重新拉紧贴回手腕。接着他们便可请委员会前来查验，委员会只得如实报告说捆绑状态与先前一模一样。观众以为他们自始至终都被绑得严严实实，亲眼目睹此景，自然惊叹不已。

有时在被委员会捆好之后，灵媒没法轻易把手抽出来再原样塞回去；遇到这种情况，他们便在柜门重新打开前干脆彻底挣脱所有绳索，宁可先按兵不动，等到“亡灵”重新把他们绑好之后，再伸手显灵或奏响乐器。

这帮骗子在被人捆绑双臂时，惯用的伎俩是把两手之间的绳子暗中拧上一股；只要手法利落，趁着委员们注意力短暂分散的一瞬下手，极难被人察觉。事后反向拧转一下，手便能滑脱出来。

达文波特兄弟自己捆绑自己的高明之处，在于让贴近手腕的绳结看起来结实稳固、“规规矩矩”，同时又能瞬间滑开并抽出双手。打这种结有好几种法子，我试着说明其中一种：在一条绳子正中先松松地打一个平结，让绳子两端能从结的下方沿相反方向穿过去，然后再把结拉紧。这样便形成两个绳圈——应当留得小些——待身体其余部位捆好后，双手再穿入这两个绳圈。绳圈只留出刚好容手掌穿过的余量，最后拉紧贴住手腕，绳结恰好卡在两腕之间。单从这种绳结的外观来看，任谁也猜不到它竟能轻易滑脱。如此捆绑的灵媒，在委员会查验过绳结、关上柜门后，立即可在小窗伸手或演奏乐器，并在几秒钟内又恢复成表面上绑得牢不可破的模样。

===Original===

If flour has been placed in their hands, it makes no difference as to their getting those members out of or into the ropes; but, to show hands at the aperture, or to make a noise on the musical instruments, it is necessary that they should get the flour out of one hand into the other. The moisture of the hand and squeezing, packs the flour into a lump, which can be laid into the other hand and returned without losing any. The little flour that adheres to the empty hand can be wiped off in the pantaloons pocket. The mediums seldom if ever take flour in their hands while they are in the bonds put upon them by the committee. The principal part of the show is after the tying has been done in their own way. Wm. Fay, who accompanies the Davenports, is thus fixed when the hypothetical spirits take the coat off his back.
As I before remarked, there are several ways in which the mediums tie themselves. They always do it, however, in such a manner that, though the tying looks secure, they can immediately get one or both hands out. Let committees insist upon untying the knots of the spirits, whether the mediums are willing or not. A little critical observation will enable them to learn the trick.
To make this subject of tying clearer, I will repeat that the Davenports always untie themselves by using their hands; as they are able in ninety-nine cases out of a hundred, however impossible it may seem, to release their hands by loosening the knots next their wrists. Sometimes they do this by twisting the rope between their wrists; sometimes it is by keeping their muscles as tense as possible during the tying, so that when relaxed there shall be some slack. Most “committees” know so little about tying, that anybody, by a little pulling, slipping, and wriggling, could slip his hands out of their knots.
A violin, bell, and tambourine, with perhaps a guitar and drum, are the instruments used by the Davenports in the cabinet. The one who plays the violin holds the bell in his hand with the bow. The other chap beats the tambourine on his knee, and has a hand for something else.
The “mediums” frequently allow a person to remain with them, providing he will let his hands be tied to their knees, the operators having previously been tied by “the spirits.” The party who ventures upon that experiment is apt to be considerably “mussed up,” as “the spirits” are not very gentle in their manipulations.

===Chinese===

即便在他们手里撒上面粉，也丝毫不妨碍他们把手从绳索里抽出或套回；不过，要想在小窗处伸手或用乐器制造声响，他们必须把一只手里的面粉倒腾到另一只手里。手心的潮气加上用力攥捏，会把面粉团成一坨面块，可以稳稳放进另一只手再原样拿回来，丝毫不会撒漏。空出来的那只手上沾的一点儿浮粉，只需在裤兜里擦掉即可。当灵媒被委员会捆绑时，他们极少、几乎从不在手里攥面粉。整场表演的重头戏，全在按照他们自己的法子捆好之后。随同达文波特兄弟演出的威廉·费伊（Wm. Fay），在所谓的“亡灵”脱下他身上外套时，用的正是这套手法。

正如我先前所言，灵媒自己绑自己的手法有好几种。但无论用哪一种，虽说看上去绑得死死的，他们却总能立刻抽出一只或两只手。各路查验委员会只需不管灵媒乐意与否，坚持亲手解开那些所谓“亡灵”打的结。只要稍加审慎观察，便能识破其中玄机。

为了把捆绳这事儿说得更透彻，我再重申一遍：达文波特兄弟向来都是靠自己的双手替自己解绑的；尽管听上去不可思议，他们在百分之九十九的情况下，都能通过松开腕部绳结来抽手脱身。有时他们靠拧转两腕间的绳索做到这一点；有时则在被捆时极力绷紧肌肉，待肌肉放松下来便能腾出松动余地。绝大多数“委员会”成员对绳结一窍不通，换作任何一个人，只要稍微拉扯、滑动、扭动几下，都能从他们打的结里把手抽出来。

达文波特兄弟在降神柜里用到的乐器，是一把小提琴、一只铃铛和一面铃鼓，有时外加一把吉他和一面鼓。拉小提琴的那位拿着琴弓的手里同时攥着铃铛；另一位哥们儿则把铃鼓磕在膝盖上敲打，腾出一只手还能干点别的勾当。

这帮“灵媒”还时常允许一名观众留在柜中陪坐，条件是此人必须同意让自己的双手绑在他们的膝盖上，而两位施法者此前已由“亡灵”捆好。敢于尝试这番实验的倒霉蛋，往往会被折腾得“狼狈不堪”，因为“亡灵”动手向来可不怎么温柔。

===Original===

To expose all the tricks of these impostors would require more space than I can afford at present. They have exhibited throughout the Northern States and the Canadas; but never succeeded very well pecuniarily until about two years ago, when they employed an agent, who advertised them in such a way as to attract public attention. In September last, they went to England, where they have since created considerable excitement.
If the hands of these boys were tied close against the side of their cabinet, the ropes passing through holes and fastened on the outside, I think “the spirits” would always fail to work.
Dr. W. F. Van Vleck, of Ohio, to whom I am indebted for some of the facts contained in this chapter, can beat the Davenport brothers at their own game. In order that he might the better learn the various methods pursued by the professed “mediums” in deceiving the public, Dr. Van Vleck entered into the medium-business himself, and by establishing confidential relations with those of the profession whose acquaintance he made, he became duly qualified to expose them.
He was accepted and endorsed by leading spiritualists in different parts of the country, as a good medium, who performed the most remarkable spiritual wonders. As the worthy doctor practiced this innocent deception on the professed mediums solely in order that he might thus be able to expose their blasphemous impositions, the public will scarcely dispute that in this case the end justified the means. I suppose it is not possible for any professed medium to puzzle or deceive the doctor. He is up to all their “dodges,” because he has learned in their school. Mediums always insist upon certain conditions, and those conditions are just such as will best enable them to deceive the senses and pervert the judgment.
Anderson “the Wizard of the North,” and other conjurers in England, gave the Davenports battle, but the “prestidigitators” did not reap many laurels. Conjurers are no more likely to understand the tricks of the mediums than any other person is. Before a trick can be exposed it must be learned. Dr. Van Vleck, having learned “the ropes,” is competent to expose them; and he is doing it in many interesting public lectures and illustrations.
If the Davenports were exhibiting simply as jugglers, I might admire their dexterity, and have nothing to say against them; but when they presumptuously pretend to deal in “things spiritual,” I consider it my duty, while treating of humbugs, to do this much at least in exposing them.

===Chinese===

要将这伙骗子的全部伎俩一一拆穿，所需的篇幅远非我眼下所能负担。他们在北方各州与加拿大各地巡回演出过，但在经济上一直算不上十分成功，直到约莫两年前，他们聘请了一位经纪人，以极其吸睛的方式替他们大肆宣传，才终于红火起来。去年九月，他们前往英格兰，自那以来在当地掀起了不小的轰动。

倘若把这两个男孩的双手紧贴着降神柜侧壁绑死，绳子穿过孔洞系在柜体外部，我想“亡灵”多半次次都要失灵罢工。

俄亥俄州的 W. F. 范弗莱克医生（Dr. W. F. Van Vleck）——本章部分事实正是承蒙他见告——在达文波特兄弟自己的行当里足以把他们打得落花流水。为了摸清那些自封“灵媒”的人欺骗公众的种种门道，范弗莱克医生亲自投身灵媒行当，通过与结识的同行建立私交与互信，终于具备了彻底揭穿他们的真本事。

全国各地的招魂论领袖们一致接纳并力挺他，视其为一位能施展最非凡通灵奇迹的优秀灵媒。鉴于这位可敬的医生对职业灵媒施展这番善意的欺瞒，纯粹是为了能够借此揭露他们亵渎神明的骗局，公众想必不会质疑此举确属“目的正当则手段亦正当”。我想，世上大概没有哪位职业灵媒能难倒或蒙蔽这位医生。他们的一切“花招”他都门儿清，因为他正是在他们的行当里拜师学艺的。灵媒们向来坚持要满足某些特定条件，而那些条件恰恰是最方便他们欺瞒感官、误导判断的障眼法。

“北方巫师”安德森（Anderson）以及英格兰的其他魔术师曾向达文波特兄弟发起挑战，但这些“戏法大师”却未能赢回多少荣光。魔术师并不比寻常人更懂得灵媒的机关。在拆穿一个戏法之前，必须先学会它。范弗莱克医生深谙此中“门道”，因而完全有能力将其揭露；他眼下正通过一场场引人入胜的公开演讲与现场演示将之公之于众。

倘若达文波特兄弟仅仅以杂耍魔术师的身份登台献艺，我或许还会钦佩他们的灵巧手法，绝不会有半句非议；但当他们狂妄地自称是在操持“通灵圣事”时，我便认为自己在论述欺罔（humbug）之际，至少有责任尽到这番揭露之责。

### X / 第十章

===Original===

The spirit-rapping and medium humbugs﻿—​Their origin﻿—​How the thing is done﻿—​$500 reward.
The “spirit-rapping” humbug was started in Hydesville, New York, about seventeen years ago, by several daughters of a Mr. Fox, living in that place. These girls discovered that certain exercises of their anatomy would produce mysterious sounds﻿—mysterious to those who heard them, simply because the means of their production were not apparent. Reports of this wonder soon went abroad, and the Fox family were daily visited by people from different sections of the country﻿—all having a greed for the marvelous. Not long after the strange sounds were first heard, someone suggested that they were, perhaps, produced by spirits; and a request was made for a certain number of raps, if that suggestion was correct. The specified number were immediately heard. A plan was then proposed by means of which communications might be received from “the spirits.” An investigator would repeat the alphabet, writing down whatever letters were designated by the “raps.” Sentences were thus formed﻿—the orthography, however, being decidedly bad.
What purported to be the spirit of a murdered peddler, gave an account of his “taking off.” He said that his body was buried beneath that very house, in a corner of the cellar; that he had been killed by a former occupant of the premises. A peddler really had disappeared, somewhat mysteriously, from that part of the country some time before; and ready credence was given the statements thus spelled out through the “raps.” Digging to the depth of eight feet in the cellar did not disclose any “dead corpus,” or even the remains of one. Soon after that, the missing peddler reappeared in Hydesville, still “clothed with mortality,” and having a new assortment of wares to sell.
That the “raps” were produced by disembodied spirits many firmly believed. False communications were attributed to evil spirits. The answers to questions were as often wrong as right; and only right when the answer could be easily guessed, or inferred from the nature of the question itself.
The Fox family moved to Rochester, New York, soon after the rapping-humbug was started; and it was there that their first public effort was made. A committee was appointed to investigate the matter, most of whom reported adversely to the claims of the “mediums;” though all of them were puzzled to know how the thing was done. In Buffalo, where the Foxes subsequently let their spirits flow, a committee of doctors reported that these loosely-constructed girls produced the “raps” by snapping their toe and knee joints. That theory, though very much ridiculed by the spiritualists then and since, was correct, as further developments proved.

===Chinese===

扶乩敲击与灵媒欺罔——其起源——把戏是如何做到的——500 美元悬赏。

“扶乩敲击”（spirit-rapping）这一欺罔行径，大约十七年前发端于纽约州海兹维尔（Hydesville, New York），始作俑者是住在当地的一位福克斯先生（Mr. Fox）的几个女儿。这几个女孩发现，身体某些部位的特定动作能发出诡异的声响——在旁听者耳中之所以诡异，纯粹是因为发声的门道隐而不显。这一奇闻的消息很快传遍四方，全国各地对奇闻异事趋之若鹜的人们每天都蜂拥而至，登门拜访福克斯一家。怪声初现不久，便有人猜想这兴许是亡灵所为；于是当场请求：若此猜测属实，请敲击特定次数。话音刚落，指定次数的敲击声应声而响。随后又有人想出了一套借此与“亡灵”通灵交流的法子：查验者依次背诵字母表，每当“敲击声”响起便记下对应的字母。语句就这样拼凑出来了——只不过拼写实在不敢恭维。

一个自称是遇害货郎亡灵的家伙，讲述了自己“惨遭毒手”的经过。他说自己的尸身就埋在这座屋子底下、地窖的一角，凶手是此处房产的前任住户。此前不久，当地确实有一名货郎颇为蹊跷地失踪了；因此，通过“敲击声”拼写出来的这番说辞立刻赢得了众人的轻信。可人们在地窖里往下掘了八英尺深，既没挖出什么“死尸”，连半块残骸也不见踪影。没过多久，那位失踪的货郎竟大摇大摆地重新出现在海兹维尔，依旧“活在凡胎肉身之中”，还带来了一批新货兜售。

许多人仍坚信“敲击声”乃无形亡灵所发。至于那些出错的通灵讯息，则统统归咎于恶灵作祟。亡灵对问题的回答对错参半；唯有当答案显而易见，或者能从问题本身的性质轻易推断出来时，才偶尔能答对。

扶乩敲击的把戏开张不久，福克斯一家便搬到了纽约州罗切斯特，并在那里首次公开登台献艺。当地成立了一个调查委员会查究此事，多数委员提交了不利于“灵媒”说法的报告；不过他们全都百思不得其解，这声响究竟是怎么弄出来的。后来福克斯姐妹移师布法罗继续大展“通灵神迹”，当地一个由医生组成的委员会报告指出，这几个关节松弛的女孩是靠弹响脚趾关节与膝关节发出“敲击声”的。这一论断在当时及此后虽遭招魂论者大加嘲讽，却千真万确，随后的种种事态发展已予以证实。

===Original===

Mrs. Culver, a relative of the Fox girls, made a solemn deposition before a magistrate, to the effect that one of the girls had instructed her how to produce the “raps,” on condition that she (Mrs. C.) should not communicate a knowledge of the matter to anyone. Mrs. Culver was a good Christian woman, and she felt it her duty﻿—as the deception had been carried so far﻿—to expose the matter. She actually produced the “raps,” in presence of the magistrate, and explained the manner of making them.
Doctor Von Vleck﻿—to whom I referred in connection with my exposition of the Davenport imposture﻿—produces very loud “raps” before his audiences, and so modulates them that they will seem to be at any desired point in his vicinity; yet not a movement of his body betrays the fact that the sounds are caused by him.
The Fox family found that the rapping business would be made to pay; and so they continued it, with varying success, for a number of years, making New York city their place of residence and principal field of operation. I believe that none of them are now in the “spiritual line.” Margaret Fox, the youngest of the rappers, has for some time been a member of the Roman Catholic Church.
From the very commencement of spiritualism, there has been a constantly increasing demand for “spiritual” wonders, to meet which numerous “mediums” have been “developed.”
Many, who otherwise would not be in the least distinguished, have become “mediums” in order to obtain notoriety, if nothing more.
Communicating by “raps” was a slow process; so some of the mediums took to writing spasmodically; others talked in a “trance”﻿—all under the influence of spirits!
Mediumship has come to be a profession steadily pursued by quite a number of persons, who get their living by it.
There are various classes of “mediums,” the operations of each class being confined to a particular department of “spiritual” humbuggery.

===Chinese===

福克斯姐妹的一位亲戚卡尔弗夫人（Mrs. Culver），曾在治安法官面前郑重宣誓作证：其中一个女孩曾亲手教过她如何发出这种“敲击声”，条件是她（卡尔弗夫人）绝不可将此事诀窍泄露给任何人。卡尔弗夫人是个虔诚善良的基督徒，眼见这场骗局愈演愈烈，她觉得自己有责任将真相公之于众。她当着治安法官的面亲手发出了同样的“敲击声”，并详尽解释了制造声响的手法。

范弗莱克医生（Doctor Von Vleck）——我在揭露达文波特兄弟骗局时曾提到过他——能在观众面前发出极为响亮的“敲击声”，且能随心所欲地调节音色与方位，让声音听起来仿佛发自他身旁任意指定的位置；而他全身上下却没有一丝一毫的动作会暴露出这声音正是由他本人发出的。

福克斯一家发现敲击通灵这门营生有利可图，便接着干了好些年，其间成败起伏不一，并将纽约市作为常住地与主要营业地盘。据我所知，她们一家如今已无人再吃“通灵这碗饭”。姐妹中年纪最小的玛格丽特·福克斯（Margaret Fox），早已皈依了罗马天主教会。

自招魂术兴起伊始，世人对“通灵奇迹”的需求便与日俱增，为迎合这一需求，形形色色的“灵媒”如雨后春笋般被“培养”了出来。

许多原本籍籍无名、毫无过人之处的人，也纷纷摇身一变成为“灵媒”，哪怕只为博取一点儿虚名也在所不惜。

靠“敲击”通灵实在太慢，于是有些灵媒改成了抽搐般地自动书写；另一些则进入“出神恍惚”状态开口代亡灵传话——统统宣称是受亡灵驱使！

充当灵媒已然演变成一门正经职业，相当一批人以此为常业，赖以谋生。

“灵媒”分为三六九等，每一类灵媒的业务都局限于“通灵”骗术的某一个特定门类。

===Original===

Some call themselves “test mediums;” and, by insisting upon certain formulas, they succeed in astonishing, if they don’t convince most of them who visit them. It is by this class that the public is most likely to be deceived.
There is a person by the name of J. V. Mansfield, who has been called by spiritualists the “Great Spirit Postmaster,” his specialty being the answering of sealed letters addressed to spirits. The letters are returned﻿—some of them at least﻿—to the writers without appearing to have been opened, accompanied by answers purporting to be written through Mansfield by the spirits addressed. Such of these letters as are sealed with gum-arabic merely, can be steamed open, and the envelopes resealed and reglazed as they were before. If sealing-wax has been used, a sharp, thin blade will enable the medium to nicely cut off the seal by splitting the paper under it; and then, after a knowledge of the contents of the letter is arrived at, the seal can be replaced in its original position, and made fast with gum-arabic. Not more than one out of a hundred would be likely to observe that the seal had ever been tampered with. The investigator opens the envelope, when returned to him, at the end, preserving the sealed part intact, in order to show his friends that the letter was answered without being opened!
Another method of the medium is, to slit open the envelope at the end with a sharp knife, and afterward stick it together again with gum, rubbing the edge slightly as soon as the gum is dry. If the job is nicely done, a close observer would hardly perceive it.
Mr. Mansfield does not engage to answer all letters; those unanswered being too securely sealed for him to open without detection. To secure the services of the “Great Spirit-Postmaster,” a fee of five dollars must accompany your letter to the spirits; and the money is retained whether an answer is returned or not.
Rather high postage that!

===Chinese===

有些人自诩为“实证灵媒”（test mediums）；他们靠着死抠一套固定程式，即便不能让登门拜访者尽数信服，也总能叫大多数人惊愕不已。公众最容易受骗上当的，正是这一类灵媒。

有一位名叫 J. V. 曼斯菲尔德（J. V. Mansfield）的人，被招魂论者尊为“大亡灵邮政局长”，他的拿手绝活是回复寄给亡灵的密封信件。信件——至少其中一部分——在寄还给写信人时，表面上看去完好无损、从未拆封，随信还附有一封号称是由收信亡灵借曼斯菲尔德之手写就的回信。倘若信封仅仅是用阿拉伯树胶封口的，只需用水蒸气蒸开，事后再重新粘合抛光，便可恢复原貌；若是用了火漆封口，灵媒只需用一把薄而锋利的刀片剖开火漆底下的纸层，将整块火漆完整切下，待窥知信中内容后，再把火漆放回原位，用阿拉伯树胶粘牢。一百个人里头，怕是找不出一个能察觉出封口曾动过手脚。查验者收到退回的信件时，往往从信封一端拆开，特意保留火漆封口处完好无损，好拿去向朋友们炫耀：信未拆封便已得了亡灵回复！

灵媒的另一套手法，是用利刃从信封侧端划开一条缝，事后再用树胶粘合，等胶水干透便把边缘轻轻揉摩一番。只要手艺够精细，哪怕凑近细看也极难发现破绽。

曼斯菲尔德先生并不包办回复所有来信；那些石沉大海的信，多半是封得太严实，他没把握在不留痕迹的情况下拆开。要想请动这位“大亡灵邮政局长”效劳，寄往阴曹地府的信中必须随附五美元酬金；至于回信与否，钱是一概照收不退的。

这邮资可真够贵的！

===Original===

Several years since, a gentleman living in Buffalo, N.Y., addressed some questions to one of his spirit-friends, and enclosed them, together with a single hair and a grain of sand, in an envelope, which he sealed so closely that no part of the contents could escape while being transmitted by mail. The questions were sent to Mr. Mansfield and answers requested through his “mediumship.” The envelope containing the questions was soon returned, with answers to the letter. The former did not appear to have been opened. Spreading a large sheet of blank paper on a table before him, the gentleman opened the envelope and placed its contents on the table. The hair and grain of sand were not there.
Time and again has Mansfield been convicted of imposture, yet he still prosecutes his nefarious business.
The “Spirit-Postmaster” fails to get answers to such questions as these:
“Where did you die?”
“When?”
“Who attended you in your last illness?”
“What were your last words?”
“How many were present at your death?”
But if the questions are of such a nature as the following, answers are generally obtained:
“Are you happy?”
“Are you often near me?”
“And can you influence me?”
“Have you changed your religious notions since entering the spirit-world?”
It is to be observed that the questions which the “Spirit-Postmaster” can answer *require no knowledge of facts about the applicant*, while those which he cannot answer, do require it.
Address, for instance, your spirit-father without mentioning his name, and the name will not be given in connection with the reply purporting to come from him﻿—unless the medium knows your family.
I will write a series of questions addressed to one of my spirit-friends, enclose them in an envelope, and if Mr. Mansfield or any other professed medium will answer those questions pertinently in my presence, and without touching the envelope, I will give to such party five hundred dollars, and think I have got the worth of my money.

===Chinese===
