There was a whole code of business regulations made to meet the peculiar needs of the tulip business, besides, and in every town were to be found “tulip-notaries,” to conduct the legal part of the business, take acknowledgments of deeds, note protests, etc.
To say that the tulips were worth their weight in gold would be a very small story. It would not be a very great exaggeration to say that they were worth their size in diamonds. The most valuable species of all was named *Semper Augustus*, and a bulb of it which weighed 200 perits, or less than half an ounce avoirdupois, was thought cheap at 5,500 florins. A florin may be called about 40 cents; so that the little brown root was worth $2,200, or 220 gold eagles, which would weigh, by a rough estimate, eight pounds four ounces, or 132 ounces avoirdupois. Thus this half ounce *Semper Augustus* was worth﻿—I mean he would bring﻿—two hundred and sixty-four times his weight in gold!
There were many cases where people invested whole fortunes equal to $40,000 or $50,000 in collections of forty or fifty tulip roots. Once there happened to be only two *Semper Augustus*es in all Holland, one in Haarlem and one in Amsterdam. The Haarlem one was sold for twelve acres of building lots, and the Amsterdam one for a sum equal to $1,840.00, together with a new carriage, span of grey horses and double harness, complete.
Here is the list of merchandise and estimated prices given for one root of the Viceroy tulip. It is interesting as showing what real merchandise was worth in those days by a cash standard, aside from its exhibition of tremendous speculative bedlamism:
160 bushels wheat
$179.20
320 bushels rye
223.20
Four fat oxen
192.00
Eight fat hogs
96.00
Twelve fat sheep
48.00
Two hogsheads wine
28.00
Four tuns beer
12.80
Two tuns butter
76.80
1000 lbs. cheese
48.00
A bed all complete
40.00
One suit clothes
32.00
A silver drinking cup
24.00
Total exactly
$1,000.00

===Chinese===

在 1634 — 1635 年大约一年之内，此前已以相当快速度升温了十五到二十年的郁金香狂热抵达顶峰，毒害了整个荷兰民族。在这短短时期结束时，价格已从高昂涨至离谱，再从离谱涨至疯癫。上至伯爵、市长，下至商人、店主、仆役、擦鞋童，贵贱上下人人发疯般买卖郁金香。为了让这种当红商品人人都买得起，人们甚至发明了一种新的重量单位，叫做“佩里特”（perit），小到一常衡磅约合八千佩里特；一颗重半盎司到一盎司的郁金香球茎，便含有 200 到 400 佩里特。于是，买不起一整颗郁金香的人，大可买上一两佩里特，在一颗球茎上拥有法学家所谓的“未分割权益”。这种持股方式恰恰暴露出所谓价值是何等虚幻不实。试想若有个小股东企图挖出属于自己的几佩里特揣进兜里，他在球茎上挖个小洞，多半会弄死这颗花根，自己得到的也必定是一小块毫无价值的烂泥，分文不值。此外，人们还专门制定了一整套商业法规来满足郁金香交易的特殊需要，每座城镇都能找着“郁金香公证人”，负责经办相关法律事务、办理契据公证、登记拒付抗议书等等。

若说郁金香“值同等重量的黄金”，那简直太小看它了；哪怕说它“值同等体积的钻石”，也算不得多夸张。最名贵的品种名为“永远的奥古斯都”（*Semper Augustus*），一颗重 200 佩里特（不到半常衡盎司）的球茎，卖 5,500 弗罗林（florins）还算便宜。一弗罗林大致相当于 40 美分；也就是说，这颗褐扑扑的小花根值 2,200 美元，即 220 枚鹰洋金币——粗略估算重达八磅四盎司，合 132 常衡盎司。换言之，这颗半盎司重的“永远的奥古斯都”，其身价——我是说它能卖出的价钱——竟相当于自身重量黄金的两百六十四倍！

更有不少实例，人们把相当于 40,000 或 50,000 美元的毕生家产全投进四五十颗郁金香球茎的收藏中。有一回，全荷兰恰巧只有两颗“永远的奥古斯都”，一颗在哈勒姆，一颗在阿姆斯特丹。哈勒姆那颗换了十二英亩建筑用地；阿姆斯特丹那颗则卖出了相当于 1,840.00 美元的价钱，外加一辆新车、一对灰色骏马及全套双马拉具。

下面是为了换取一颗“总督”（Viceroy）郁金香球茎而付出的一揽子实物及其折算价目表。这份清单除了展示惊世骇俗的投机疯人院景象外，还能让我们按现金标准一窥当年实物商品的真实价值，颇具趣味：

===Original===

In 1636, regular tulip exchanges were established in the nine Dutch towns where the largest tulip business was done, and while the gambling was at its intensest, the matter was managed exactly as stock gambling is managed in Wall Street today. You went out into “the street” without owning a tulip or a perit of a tulip in the world, and met another fellow with just as many tulips as yourself. You talk and “banter” with him, and finally (we will suppose) you “sell short” ten *Semper Augustus*es, “seller three,” for $2,000 each, in all $20,000. This means in ordinary English, that without having any tulips (i.e., short,) you promise to deliver the ten roots as above in three days from date. Now when the three days are up, if *Semper Augustus*es are worth in the market only $1,500, you could, if this were a real transaction, buy ten of them for $15,000, and deliver them to the other gambler for $20,000, thus winning from him the difference of $5,000. But if the roots have risen and are worth $2,500 each, then if the transactions were real you would have to pay $25,000 for the ten roots and could only get $20,000 from the other gambler, and he, turning round and selling them at the market price, would win from you this difference of $5,000. But in fact the transaction was not real, it was a stock gambling one; neither party owned tulips or meant to, or expected the other to; and the whole was a pure game of chance or skill, to see which should win and which should lose that $5,000 at the end of three days. When the time came, the affair was settled, still without any tulips, by the loser paying the difference to the winner, exactly as one loses what the other wins at a game of poker or faro. Of course if you can set afloat a smart lie after making your bargain, such as will send prices up or down as your profit requires, you make money by it, just as stock gamblers do every day in New York, London, Paris, and other Christian commercial cities.
While this monstrous Dutch gambling fury lasted, money was plenty, everybody felt rich and Holland was in a whiz of windy delight. After about three years of fool’s paradise, people began to reflect that the shuttlecock could not be knocked about in the air forever, and that when it came down somebody would be hurt. So first one and then another began quietly to sell out and quit the game, without buying in again. This cautious infection quickly spread like a pestilence, as it always does in such cases, and became a perfect panic or fright. All at once, as it were, rich people all over Holland found themselves with nothing in the world except a pocket full or a garden-bed full of flower roots that nobody would buy and that were not good to eat, and would not have made more than one tureen of soup if they were.
Of course this state of things caused innumerable bankruptcies, quarrels, and refusals to complete bargains, everywhere. The government and the courts were appealed to, but with Dutch good sense they refused to enforce gambling transactions, and though the cure was very severe because very sudden, they preferred to let “the bottom drop out” of the whole affair at once. So it did. Almost everybody was either ruined or impoverished. The very few who had kept any or all of their gains by selling out in season, remained so far rich. And the vast actual business interests of Holland received a damaging check, from which it took many years to recover.
There were some curious incidents in the course of the tulipomania. They have been told before, but they are worth telling again, as the poet says, “To point the moral or adorn the tale.”

===Chinese===

1636 年，在郁金香交易最兴旺的九座荷兰城镇里，正规的郁金香交易所纷纷设立；在赌风最炽烈之际，其运作方式与今日华尔街上的股票赌博如出一辙。你两手空空地走上“大街”，世上既不拥有一颗郁金香也不拥有一佩里特郁金香，迎面碰上另一个手里同样一棵花也没有的家伙。你们寒暄一番、互相“逗价”，最后（姑且这么假设）你按每颗 2,000 美元、总计 20,000 美元的价格“卖空”十颗“永远的奥古斯都”，约定“卖方三日内交割”。用通俗的话讲，就是你手里明明一颗郁金香也没有（即“做空”），却承诺从成交之日起三天内交付上述十颗花根。等到三天期满，如果市场上“永远的奥古斯都”只值 1,500 美元，倘若这是一笔真实交易，你便可花 15,000 美元买进十颗，按 20,000 美元交付给另一名赌徒，从而净赢 5,000 美元差价。反之，如果花根涨到了每颗 2,500 美元，在真实交易中你便得花 25,000 美元买进十颗，却只能从对方手里收回 20,000 美元；而对方转手按市价卖出，便从你身上赢走了这 5,000 美元差价。可实际上交易根本不是真的，纯粹是场股票赌博；双方手里都没有郁金香，既不打算交货，也不指望对方真交货；整件事纯粹是一场凭运气或手腕的赌局，只看三天期满谁赢走或输掉那 5,000 美元。到期时，依然用不着半颗郁金香，输家直接把差价赔给赢家便算了结，正如扑克牌或法罗赌局里一方输掉的正是另一方赢走的一样。当然，你若能在成交之后放出一则机灵的假消息，按你获利的需要把行情抬上去或砸下来，便能大赚一笔——正如纽约、伦敦、巴黎及其他基督教商业都市的股票赌徒天天都在干的那样。

在这场骇人的荷兰赌博狂潮持续期间，市面银根充裕，人人都觉得自己富得流油，整个荷兰飘飘然沉浸在虚幻的狂喜之中。过了大约三年傻瓜乐园般的日子后，人们开始琢磨：羽毛球不可能永远在半空飞来飞去，等它掉下来时总有人要吃苦头。于是先是一个、接着又一个，人们开始悄悄抛售离场、洗手不干，再也不肯接盘。这种谨慎情绪如同瘟疫般迅速蔓延——这类事向来如此——转眼便演变成一场彻头彻尾的恐慌与惊悸。仿佛一夜之间，全荷兰的富人们发觉自己除了一兜子或一花圃没人肯买、既不能吃（就算能吃也熬不出超过一汤碗）的花根之外，竟已一无所有。

不用说，这种局面在各地引发了数不清的破产、争吵与毁约拒付。人们纷纷诉诸政府与法庭，然而当局凭着荷兰人特有的务实理智，拒绝强制执行赌博合约；虽说这剂猛药因来得太骤而极度痛苦，但他们宁可让整场泡沫“立刻彻底崩盘”。泡沫果真就此破灭。几乎人人都倾家荡产或沦为赤贫。唯独极少数在恰当时机及时套现、守住部分或全部收益的人，依然保住了财富。而荷兰庞大的实体工商业亦遭受重创，历经多年方才恢复元气。

郁金香狂热期间还发生过几桩奇闻逸事。虽说前人早已讲过，却仍值得一讲再讲，正如诗人所言：“既可点明寓意，又能点缀故事。”

===Original===

A sailor brought to a rich Dutch merchant news of the safe arrival of a very valuable cargo from the Levant. The old hunks rewarded the mariner for his good tidings with one red herring for breakfast. Now Ben Bolt (if that was his name﻿—perhaps as he was a Dutchman it was something like Benje Boltje) was very fond of onions, and spying one on the counter as he went out of the store, he slipped it into his pocket, and strolling back to the wharf, sat down to an odoriferous breakfast of onions and herring. He munched away without finding anything unusual in the flavor, until just as he was through, down came Mr. Merchant, tearing along like a madman at the head of an excited procession of clerks, and flying upon the luckless son of Neptune, demanded what he had carried off besides his herring?
“An onion that I found on the counter.”
“Where is it? Give it back instantly!”
“Just ate it up with my herring, mynheer.”
Wretched merchant! In a fury of useless grief he apprised the sailor that his sacrilegious back teeth had demolished a *Semper Augustus* valuable enough, explained the unhappy old fellow, to have feasted the Prince of Orange and the Stadtholder’s whole court. “Thieves!” he cried out﻿—“Seize the rascal!” So they did seize him, and he was actually tried, condemned and imprisoned for some months, all of which however did not bring back the tulip root. It is a question after all in my mind, whether that sailor was really as green as he pretended, and whether he did not know very well what he was taking. It would have been just like a reckless seaman’s trick to eat up the old miser’s twelve hundred dollar root, to teach him not to give such stingy gifts next time.
An English traveller, very fond of botany, was one day in the conservatory of a rich Dutchman, when he saw a strange bulb lying on a shelf. With that extreme coolness and selfishness which too many travellers have exercised, what does he do but take out his penknife and carefully dissect it, peeling off the outer coats, and quartering the innermost part, making all the time a great many wise observations on the phenomena of the strange new root. In came the Dutchman all at once, and seeing what was going on, he asked the Englishman, with rage in his eyes, but with a low bow and that sort of restrained formal civility which sometimes covers the most furious anger, if he knew what he was about?
“Peeling a very curious onion,” answered Mr. Traveller, as calmly as if one had a perfect right to destroy other people’s property to gratify his own curiosity.
“One hundred thousand devils!” burst out the Dutchman, expressing the extent of his anger by the number of evil spirits he invoked﻿—“It is an Admiral van der Eyck!”
“Indeed?” remarked the scientific traveller, “thank you. Are there a good many of these admirals in your country?” and he drew forth his note book to write down the little fact.
“Death and the devil!” swore the enraged Dutchman again﻿—“come before the Syndic and you shall find out all about it!” So he collared the astounded onion-peeler, and despite all he could say, dragged him straightway before the magistrate, where his scientific zeal suffered a dreadful quencher in the shape of an affidavit that the “onion” was worth four thousand florins﻿—about $1,600﻿—and in the immediate judgment of the Court, which “considered” that the prisoner be forthwith clapt into jail until he should give security for the amount. He had to do so accordingly, and doubtless all his life retained a distaste for Dutchmen and Dutch onions.
These stories about such monstrous valuations of flower roots recall to my mind another anecdote which I shall tell, not because it has anything to do with tulips, but because it is about a Dutchman, and shows in striking contrast an equally low valuation of human life. It is this. Once, in time of peace, an English and a Dutch Admiral met at sea, each in his flag ship, and for some reason or other exchanged complimentary salutes. By accident, one of the Englishman’s guns was shotted and misdirected, and killed one of the Dutch crew. On hearing the fact the Englishman at once manned a boat and went to apologize, to inquire about the poor fellow’s family and to send them some money, provide for the funeral, etc., etc., as a kind hearted man would naturally do. But the Dutch commander, on meeting him at the quarterdeck, and learning his errand, at once put all his kindly intentions completely one side, saying in imperfect English:
“It’sh no matter, it’sh no matter﻿—*dere’s blaanty more Tutchmen in Holland*!”

===Chinese===

一名水手向一位富有的荷兰商人报信，说一艘满载黎凡特（Levant）贵重货物的船只已平安抵港。那老抠门儿赏了水手一条红鲱鱼当早饭，作为报喜的酬劳。恰巧这名叫本·博尔特（Ben Bolt，姑且算他叫这名——既是个荷兰人，兴许叫本耶·博尔特耶之类）的水手素爱吃洋葱，走出铺子时瞥见柜台上摆着一颗，便顺手塞进衣兜，溜达着回到码头，坐下来美美享用了一顿香气扑鼻的洋葱配鲱鱼早餐。他大快朵颐，丝毫没觉得滋味有何异样，直到刚吃完最后一口，只见商人先生领着一长串激动万分的店员疯了似的狂奔而来，一把扑向这位倒霉的海神之子，厉声喝问他除了鲱鱼外还顺手拿走了什么？

“我在柜台上捡的一颗洋葱。”

“它在哪儿？立刻还给我！”

“刚跟我的鲱鱼一块儿吃进肚里了，老爷。”

可怜的商人！他陷入一阵徒劳无益的悲愤狂怒，告知水手说他那几颗亵渎神明的后槽牙竟嚼碎了一颗“永远的奥古斯都”——依这不幸老头儿的解释，那玩意儿值钱得足以宴请奥兰治亲王以及执政官的全套宫廷朝臣！“抓贼啊！”他失声嚎叫，“抓住这恶棍！”众人果真扭住了他，这水手还真被押上法庭受审、定罪，坐了好几个月大牢，可这一切终究没能把那颗郁金香球茎变回来。说到底，我心里倒一直存疑：那水手真像他装出来的那么傻乎乎吗，还是他心里门儿清自己拿的是什么？一口吞掉老吝啬鬼那颗值一千两百美元的花根，好教训他下回别再出手这么寒酸，这倒真像极了天不怕地不怕的水手干得出来的恶作剧。

还有一位酷爱植物学的英国旅行家，某天在一位荷兰富翁的温室里溜达，瞧见架子上躺着一颗奇特的球茎。他拿出了太多旅行家惯常展露的那副极度冷静而又自私的派头，二话不说掏出随身小折刀，小心翼翼地将它解剖开来——先剥去外层鳞衣，再把最里头的心儿切成四瓣，嘴里还不忘就这颗奇异新物种的植物学现象发表一通高论。恰在此时，荷兰主人走了进来，见此情景怒火中烧，却仍深鞠一躬，用那种有时恰恰掩盖着最狂暴怒火的克制礼貌问道：阁下可晓得自己在干什么？

“剥一颗极稀奇的洋葱呢，”旅行家先生镇定自若地答道，仿佛任何人都有权为了满足一己好奇而毁坏他人财物似的。

“十万个魔鬼！”荷兰人再也按捺不住，一口气召唤出十万个恶灵来丈量自己的怒火，“这是一颗‘范德艾克海军上将’（Admiral van der Eyck）！”

“当真？”讲究科学的旅行家淡淡应道，“多谢指教。贵国像这样的‘海军上将’很多吗？”说着便掏出笔记本打算记下这桩小知识。

“见鬼去吧！”暴怒的荷兰人再次破口大骂，“跟我上治安法官那儿去，你很快就会全弄明白！”说罢他一把揪住吓傻了的剥洋葱专家，不管他如何辩白，径直拖到治安法官面前；在那里，他的科学热忱挨了一记迎头冷水——一份宣誓书作证这颗“洋葱”价值四千弗罗林（约合 1,600 美元），而法庭当即判决“裁定”将人犯立刻收监，直至他缴足等额担保金为止。他只得乖乖照办，想必这辈子都不会再对荷兰人和荷兰洋葱有任何好感了。

这些关于花根天价估值的故事，令我想起另一则轶事——我讲出来并非因为它跟郁金香有什么干系，而是因为它同样关乎荷兰人，却以鲜明反差展现出对人命同样低贱的估价。事情是这样的：从前在和平时期，一艘英国旗舰与一艘荷兰旗舰在海上相遇，不知何故双方互相鸣炮致礼。阴差阳错，英国船上一门礼炮里竟装了实弹且炮口偏斜，当场打死了荷兰船上的一名水手。英国海军上将闻讯，立刻派人划小艇登船致歉，打听那位可怜水手的家眷情况，并打算送去一笔抚恤金、操办丧事等等——正如任何仁厚之人自然会做的那样。然而荷兰舰长在后甲板上迎见他、听明来意后，却瞬间把对方的一片好意撇在一边，用蹩脚的英语摆手说道：

“没关系，没关系——*荷兰多的是荷兰人*！”

### XXVI / 第二十六章

===Original===

John Bull’s great money humbug﻿—​The south sea bubble in 1720.
The “South Sea Bubble” is one of the most startling lessons which history gives us of the ease with which the most monstrous, and absurd, and wicked humbugs can be crammed down the throat of poor human nature. It ought also to be a useful warning of the folly of mere “speculation,” as compared with real “business undertakings.” The history of the South Sea Bubble has been told, before, but it is too prominent a case to be entirely passed over. It occupied a period of about eight months, from February 1, 1720, to the end of the following September. It was an unreasonable expansion of the value of the stock of the “South Sea Company.” This Company was formed in 1711; its stock was at first about $30,000,000, subscribed by the public and handed over by the corporators to Government to meet certain troublesome public debts. In return, Government guaranteed the stockholders a dividend of six percent, and gave the Company sundry permanent important duties and a monopoly of all trade to the South Pacific, or “South Sea.” This matter went on with fair success as a money enterprise, until the birth of the “Bubble,” which was as follows:﻿—In the end of January, 1720, probably in consequence of catching infection from “Law’s Mississippi Scheme” in France, the South Sea Company and the Bank of England made competing propositions to the English Government, to repeat the original South Sea Company financiering plan on a larger scale. The proposition of the Company, which was accepted by Government, was: to assume as before the whole public debt, now amounting to over one hundred and fifty millions of dollars; and to be guaranteed at first a five percent dividend, and afterward a four percent one, to thestockholders by Government. For this privilege, the Company agreed to pay outright a bonus of more than seventeen million dollars. This plan is said to have been originated and principally carried through by Sir John Blunt, one of the Company’s directors. Parliament adopted it after two months’ discussion﻿—the Bubble having, however, been swelling monstrously all the time.

===Chinese===

约翰牛的惊天金融骗局——1720 年的南海泡沫。

“南海泡沫”（South Sea Bubble）是历史留给我们的最令人惊骇的教训之一，它证明了世上最荒诞不经、丧心病狂的弥天骗局，竟能如此轻易地被硬塞进可怜的人性喉咙里。它也理应成为一记有益的警钟，昭示与脚踏实地的“实业经营”相比，纯粹“投机”是何等愚蠢。南海泡沫的历史固然早有人讲述过，但此案太过显赫，实在不容略过。它前后持续了约八个月，自 1720 年 2 月 1 日起至同年 9 月底止，本质上是“南海公司”股票价值的疯狂虚胖。该公司成立于 1711 年，最初股本约 30,000,000 美元（三千万美元），由公众认购后经发起人移交给政府，用以清偿若干棘手的国债。作为回报，政府向股东担保百分之六的年息，并赋予公司若干永久性重要特权以及通往南太平洋（即“南海”）全部贸易的垄断权。作为一桩金融买卖，它原本运转得颇为顺遂，直到“泡沫”诞生，其经过如下：1720 年 1 月底，大概是染上了法国“约翰·劳密西西比计划”的传染病，南海公司与英格兰银行竞相向英国政府递交方案，提议以更大规模重演当初南海公司的融资戏法。政府最终采纳了南海公司的提案：照旧承接全部国债——此时总额已逾一亿五千万美元——由政府向股东担保最初百分之五、随后百分之四的股息。为换取这项特权，公司同意一次性向政府支付超过一千七百万美元的酬金。据称该计划是由公司董事之一约翰·布朗特爵士（Sir John Blunt）首倡并一手力推的。议会经过两个月的辩论后通过了该法案——而在此期间，那只泡沫早已惊人地越吹越大。

===Original===

It must be remembered that the wonderful profits expected from the Company were to come from their monopoly of the South Sea trade. Tremendous stories were told by Blunt and his friends, who can hardly have believed more than one half of their own talk, about a free trade with all the Spanish Pacific colonies, the importation of silver and gold from Peru and Mexico in return for dry goods, etc., etc.; all which fine things were going to produce two or three times the amount of the Company’s stock every year. When the bill authorizing the arrangement passed, South Sea stock had already reached a price of four hundred percent. The bill was stoutly opposed in Parliament by Mr.﻿—afterwards Sir﻿—Robert Walpole, and a few others but in vain. Under the operation of the beautiful stories of the speculative Blunt and his friends, South Sea stock, after a short lull in April, began to rise again, and the bubble swelled and swelled to a size so monstrous, and with colors so gay, that it filled the whole horizon of poor foolish John Bull:﻿—perfectly turned his bullheaded brain, and made him for the time absolutely crazy. The directors opened books on April 12th for £5,000,000 new stock, charging, however, £300 for each share of £100, or three hundred percent to begin with. Double the amount was subscribed in a few days; that is, John Bull subscribed thirty million dollars for ten millions of stock, where only five millions were to be had. In a few days more, these subscribers were selling at double what they paid. April 21st, a ten percent dividend was voted for midsummer. In a day or two, another five million subscription was opened at four hundred percent to begin with. The whole, and half as much more, was taken in a few hours. In the end of May, South Sea stock was worth five hundred to one. On the 28th, it was five hundred and fifty. In four days more, for some reason or other, it jumped up to eight hundred and ninety. The speculating Blunt kept all this time blowing and blowing at his bubble. All summer, he and his friends blew and blew; and all summer the bubble swelled and floated, and shone; and high and low, men and women, lords and ladies, clergymen, princesses and duchesses, merchants, gamblers, tradesmen, dressmakers, footmen, bought and sold. In the beginning of August, South Sea stock stood at one thousand percent! It was really worth about twenty-five percent. The crowding in Exchange Alley, the Wall Street of the day, was tremendous. So noisy, and unmanageable and excited was this mob of greedy fools, that the very same stock was sometimes selling ten percent higher at one end of the Alley than at the other.

===Chinese===

必须记住，众人指望公司赚取的惊人暴利，全系于他们对南海贸易的垄断。布朗特及其同伙——怕是连他们自己都不太相信自己一半的鬼话——编造了无数天花乱坠的故事，吹嘘将与所有西班牙太平洋殖民地自由通商，用纺织杂货从秘鲁和墨西哥换回成船的黄金白银等等；仿佛所有这些美事每年都能产出相当于公司股本两三倍的利润。授权该项安排的法案获得通过时，南海公司股价已涨至票面价值的百分之四百。罗伯特·沃波尔先生（即后来的沃波尔爵士）与少数几人在议会里竭力反对该法案，却终归徒劳。在投机家布朗特及其同伙美妙故事的催动下，南海股票在四月短暂歇息后再度飙升，那只泡沫越吹越大、五彩斑斓，几乎遮蔽了可怜蠢笨的约翰牛（John Bull）的整个视野——彻底把他那颗牛脑袋搅得晕头转向，一时之间陷入彻头彻尾的癫狂。4 月 12 日，董事们开放认购 5,000,000 英镑新股，每 100 英镑面值却开价 300 英镑，也就是说一开盘便是百分之三百。短短几天内认购额便翻了一倍；换言之，在明明只有五百万英镑额度可卖的情况下，约翰牛竟为一千万股本认缴了三千万美元。再过几天，这批认购者转手便以买入价的两倍卖出。4 月 21 日，公司表决决定仲夏派发百分之十的股息。一两天后，另一批五百万新股开放认购，起售价直接定在百分之四百；短短几小时内便被一抢而空，还超额认购了一半。5 月底，南海股价涨到了五百比一。到了 28 日，涨到五百五十。再过四天，不知怎的竟猛窜到八百九十。整段日子里，投机客布朗特一直在拼命吹、使劲吹他那只大泡沫。一整个夏天，他和同伙吹了又吹；一整个夏天，泡沫越胀越大，凌空飘浮，流光溢彩；无论贵贱男女、王公贵族还是淑女贵妇、牧师、公主、公爵夫人、商人、赌徒、小贩、裁缝乃至男仆，人人都在买进卖出。8 月初，南海股价站上了百分之一千！而它实际只值大约百分之二十五。当时的“华尔街”——交易所巷（Exchange Alley）——挤得水泄不通。这群贪婪的傻瓜喧嚣嘈杂、失控狂热到极点，以至于同一只股票在同一时刻，巷子这头的卖价有时竟比巷子那头高出百分之十。

===Original===

The growth of this monstrous, noxious bubble hatched out a multitude of young cockatrices. Not only was the stock of the India Company, the Bank of England, and other sound concerns, much increased in price by sympathy with this fury of speculation, but a great number of utterly ridiculous schemes and barefaced swindles were advertised and successfully imposed on the public. Any piece of paper purporting to be stock could be sold for money. Not the least thought of investigating the solvency of advertisers seems to have occurred to anybody. Nor was any rank free from the poison. Almost a hundred projects were before the public at once, some of them incredibly brazen humbugs. There were schemes for a wheel for perpetual motion﻿—capital, $5,000,000; for trading in hair (for wigs), in those days “a big thing;” for furnishing funerals to any part of Britain; for “improving the art of making soap;” for importing walnut-trees from Virginia﻿—capital, $10,000,000; for insuring against losses by servants﻿—capital, $15,000,000; for making quicksilver malleable; “Puckle’s Machine Company,” for discharging cannonballs and bullets, both round and square, and so on. One colossal genius in humbugging actually advertised in these words: “A company for carrying on an undertaking of great advantage, but nobody to know what it is.” The capital he called for was $2,500,000, in shares of $500 each; deposit on subscribing, $10 per share. Each subscriber was promised $500 per share per annum, and full particulars were to be given in a month, when the rest of the subscription was to be paid. This great financier, having put forth his prospectus, opened his office in Cornhill next morning at nine o’clock. Crowds pressed upon him. At three p.m., John Bull had paid this immense humbug $10,000, being deposits on a thousand shares subscribed for. That night, the financier﻿—a shrewd man!﻿—modestly retired to an unknown place upon the Continent, and was never heard of again. Another humbug almost as preposterous, was that of the “Globe Permits.” These were square pieces of playing-cards with a seal on them, having the picture of the Globe Tavern, and with the words, “Sailcloth Permits.” What they “permitted” was a subscription at some future period to a sailcloth-factory, projected by a certain capitalist. These “permits” sold at one time for $300 each.
But the more sensible members of Government soon exerted their influence against these lesser and more palpable humbugs. Some accounts say that the South Sea Company itself grew jealous, for it was reckoned that these “sideshows” called for a total amount of $1,500,000,000, and itself took legal means against them. At any rate, an “order in council” was published, peremptorily dismissing and dissolving them all.

===Chinese===

这只庞大毒瘤般的泡沫在膨胀过程中，又孵化出一大群小毒蛇。不仅东印度公司、英格兰银行等稳健商号的股价受此投机狂潮联动而水涨船高，更有数不清荒唐透顶的计划与明目张胆的骗局大登广告，并成功强加于公众头上。随便哪张自称是股票的纸片都能换来现钞。似乎压根儿没有一个人动过查一查登广告者偿付能力的念头。社会上也没有哪一个阶层能免受此毒害。一时间，同时摆在公众面前的集资项目多达近百个，其中有些简直是厚颜无耻到令人难以置信的骗局（humbugs）。譬如有筹建永动轮的计划——股本 5,000,000 美元；有经营假发用人发的买卖——在当年可是“一桩大生意”；有向不列颠任何角落承包葬礼的；有“改良制皂工艺”的；有从弗吉尼亚进口核桃树的——股本 10,000,000 美元；有承保仆人造成损失的保险——股本 15,000,000 美元；有让水银变得可锻造的；还有“帕克尔机枪公司”（Puckle’s Machine Company），号称既能发射圆形又能发射方形炮弹与子弹，如此等等。其中一位欺罔界的旷世奇才，竟公然登出如下广告：“本公司旨在经营一项利益极其丰厚之事业，然任何人不得知晓其为何事。”他号称募资 2,500,000 美元，分为每股 500 美元；认购定金每股 10 美元。每位认购者获承诺每年每股分红 500 美元，并称一个月后缴付余款时自会公布全部详情。这位大金融家发出招股书后，次日清晨九点在科恩希尔（Cornhill）开了张。人群蜂拥而至。下午三点，约翰牛已向这位超级骗子缴足了 10,000 美元，即一千股的认购定金。当天夜里，这位精明的金融家便谦逊地悄然退隐欧洲大陆某处未知之地，从此人间蒸发、音讯全无。另一桩同样荒谬绝伦的骗局叫做“地球许可证”（Globe Permits）。那不过是印着火漆戳记的方形扑克牌，上面绘着地球酒馆的图案，并印着“帆布许可证”字样。它所“许可”的，不过是将来某时有权认购某位资本家筹建的一家帆布厂股票。这些“许可证”一度竟卖到每张 300 美元。

不过，政府中较为明智的成员很快便出手干预这些规模较小、破绽也更明显的骗局。有些记载说，南海公司自己也起了妒心——据估算，这些“杂耍副业”号称募资总额高达 1,500,000,000 美元——于是亲自采取法律手段打压它们。无论如何，枢密院随即颁布了一道“枢密院令”，断然勒令并解散了所有这些项目。

===Original===

During August, it leaked out that Sir John Blunt and some other “insiders” had sold out their South Sea stock. There was also some charges of unfairness in managing subscriptions. After so long and so intense an excitement, the time for reaction and collapse was come. The price of stock began to fall in spite of all that the directors could do. September 2, it was down to 700.
A general meeting of the company was held to try to whitewash matters, but in vain. The stock fell, fell, fell. The great humbug had received its deathblow. Thousands of families saw beggary staring them in the face, grasping them with its iron hand. The consternation was inexpressible. Out of it a great popular rage began to flame up, just as fires often break out among the prostrate houses of a city ruined by an earthquake. Efforts were meanwhile vainly made to stay the ruin by help from the Bank of England. Bankers and goldsmiths (then often doing a banking business) absconded daily. Business corporations failed. Credit was almost paralyzed. In the end of September, the stock fell to 175, 150, 135.
Meanwhile violent riots were feared. South Sea directors could not be seen in the streets without being insulted. The King, then in Hanover, was imperatively sent for home, and had to come. So extensive was the misfortune and the wrath of the people, so numerous the public meetings and petitions from all over the kingdom, that Parliament found it necessary to grant the public demand, and to initiate a formal inquiry into the whole enterprise. This was done; and the foolish, swindled, disappointed, angry nation, through this proceeding, vented all the wrath it could upon the persons and estates of the managers and officers of the South Sea Company. They were forbidden to leave the kingdom, their property was sequestrated, they were placed in custody and examined. Those of them in Parliament were insulted there to their faces, several of them expelled, the most violent charges made against them all. A secret investigating committee was set to rip up the whole affair. Knight, the treasurer, who possessed all the dangerous secrets of the concern, ran away to Calais and the Continent, and so escaped.

===Chinese===

八月间，风声走漏，传出约翰·布朗特爵士与若干“内部人士”已抛空手头南海股票。此外还爆出若干操纵认购、有失公允的指控。经历了如此漫长而又狂热的亢奋之后，反噬与崩盘的时刻终于降临。任凭董事们使出浑身解数，股价仍开始节节下挫。9 月 2 日，已跌至 700 。

公司召开股东大会企图粉饰太平，却无济于事。股价跌了又跌，一泻千里。这场惊天骗局已挨了致命一击。成千上万个家庭眼睁睁看着赤贫逼到眼前，用铁爪死死攥住自己。恐慌之深难以言表。随之而来的是一场席卷全国的滔天民怨，恰如地震摧毁的城市废墟中往往腾起冲天大火。其间，人们曾徒劳地向英格兰银行求援以挽狂澜。银行家与金匠（当时常兼营银行业务）每天都有人卷款潜逃。商号纷纷倒闭。信用体系几近瘫痪。9 月底，股价暴跌至 175 、 150 ，乃至 135 。

与此同时，当局唯恐爆发暴力骚乱。南海公司的董事们只要在街上露面，便免不了遭人唾骂。当时正在汉诺威的国王被火速召回国内，不得不立刻赶回。这场灾祸牵连之广、民愤之烈、全国各地集会与请愿之频密，迫使议会不得不顺应民意，对整桩事业启动正式调查。调查随即展开；这个愚蠢、受骗、失望而又愤怒的国度，借由这场审查把满腔怒火尽数倾泻到南海公司掌柜与高管的人身与家产上。他们被禁止离境，财产遭查封，人身被羁押审讯。其中身为议员者在议会大厅里遭当面羞辱，好几人被驱逐出院，针对所有人的最严厉指控接踵而至。一个秘密调查委员会受命将整桩黑幕彻底翻个底朝天。掌握着公司全部危险机密的司库奈特（Knight）逃往加来并溜进欧洲大陆，就此逍遥法外。

===Original===

The books were found to have been either destroyed, secreted, or mutilated and garbled. Stock bribes of $250,000, $150,000, $50,000 had been paid to the Earl of Sunderland, the Duchess of Kendal (the King’s favorite,) Mr. Craggs (one of the Secretaries of State,) and others. Mr. Aislabie, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, had accumulated $4,250,000 and more out of the business. Many other noblemen, gentlemen, and reputable merchants were disgracefully involved.
The trials that were had resulted in the imprisonment, expulsion or degradation of Aislabie, Craggs, Sir George Caswell (a banker and member of the House,) and others. Blunt, a Mr. Stanhope, and a number more of the chief criminals were stripped of their wealth, amounting to from $135,000 to $1,200,000 each, and the proceeds used for the partial relief of the ruined, except amounts left to the culprits to begin the world anew. Blunt, the chief of all the swindlers, was stripped of about $925,000, and allowed only $5,000. By this means and by the use of such actual property as the Company did possess, about one-third of the money lost by its means was ultimately paid to the losers. It was a long time, however, before the tone of public credit was thoroughly restored.
The history of the South Sea bubble should always stand as a beacon to warn us that reckless speculation is the bane of commerce, and that the only sure method of gaining a fortune, and certainly of enjoying it, is to diligently prosecute some legitimate calling, which, like the quality of mercy, is “twice blessed.” Every man’s occupation should be beneficial to his fellow-man as well as profitable to himself. All else is vanity and folly.

===Chinese===

账簿被查出不是遭销毁、藏匿，便是被篡改撕毁得残缺不全。查明曾向桑德兰伯爵（Earl of Sunderland）、肯德尔公爵夫人（Duchess of Kendal，国王的宠妃）、克拉格斯先生（Mr. Craggs，国务大臣之一）等人行贿过 250,000 美元、 150,000 美元、 50,000 美元不等的股票贿赂。财政大臣艾斯拉比先生（Mr. Aislabie）仅靠这桩买卖便聚敛了 4,250,000 美元以上的巨款。此外还有大批贵族、绅士与体面商人极不光彩地卷入其中。

随后的审判导致艾斯拉比、克拉格斯、乔治·卡斯韦尔爵士（Sir George Caswell，银行家兼下院议员）等人或锒铛入狱、或被除名、或遭革职降等。布朗特、一位斯坦厄普先生（Mr. Stanhope）以及另外多名主犯被抄没家产——每人从 135,000 美元到 1,200,000 美元不等——所得款项除留给罪犯一点儿重新做人的本钱外，其余均用于部分救济倾家荡产的受害者。所有骗子中的头号元凶布朗特被没收了约 925,000 美元，仅获准保留 5,000 美元。借此手段，外加动用公司确实持有的真实资产，最终大约有三分之一的损失得以赔付给亏损者。然而，公共信用的元气却经历了漫长岁月方才彻底复原。

南海泡沫的历史理应永远矗立如一座灯塔，警示我们：鲁莽投机乃是商业之毒瘤；而致富并安稳享用的唯一可靠正道，唯有勤勉经营某项正当职业——它正如同慈悲的美德一般，“施受皆蒙福泽”。每个人的营生都应当既利人又利己。其余一切，皆属虚妄与愚行。

### XXVII / 第二十七章

===Original===

Business humbugs﻿—​John Law﻿—​The Mississippi scheme﻿—​Johnny Crapaud as greedy as Johnny Bull.
In the “good old times,” people were just as eager after money as they are now; and a great deal more vulgar, unscrupulous, and foolish in their endeavors to get it. During about two hundred years after the discovery of America, that continent was a constant source of great and little money humbugs. The Spaniards and Portuguese and French and English all insisted upon thinking that America was chiefly made of gold; perhaps believing, as the man said about Colorado, that the hardship of the place was, that you have to dig through three or four feet of solid silver before the gold could be reached. This curious delusion is shown by the fact that the early charters of lands in America so uniformly reserved to the King his proportion of all gold and silver that should be found. And if gold were not to be had, these lazy Europeans were equally crazy about the rich merchandise which they made sure of finding in the vast and solitary American mountains and forests.
In a previous letter, I have shown how one of those delusions, about the unbounded wealth to be obtained from the countries on the South Sea, caused the English South Sea bubble.
A similar belief, at the same time, in the neighboring country of France, formed the airy basis of a similar business humbug, even more gigantic, noxious, and destructive. This was John Law’s Mississippi scheme, of which I shall give an account in this chapter. It was, I think, the greatest business humbug of history.
Law was a Scotchman, shrewd and able, a really good financier for those days, but vicious, a gambler, unprincipled, and liable to wild schemes. He had possessed a good deal of property, had traveled and gambled all over Europe, was witty, entertaining, and capital company, and had become a favorite with the Duke of Orleans and other French nobles. When the Duke became Regent of France at the death of Louis XIV, in 1715, that country was horribly in debt, and its people in much misery, owing to the costly wars and flaying taxations of the late King. When, therefore, Law came to Paris with a promising scheme of finance in his hand, the Regent was particularly glad to see him, both as financier and as friend.

===Chinese===

商业骗局——约翰·劳——密西西比计划——“青蛙约翰”与“约翰牛”一般贪婪。

在“美好的旧时光”里，世人对金钱的贪求与今日并无二致；而他们在捞钱门道上的粗鄙、肆无忌惮与愚蠢，较之今日甚至有过之而无不及。美洲发现后的大约两百年间，那片大陆一直是形形色色大大小小金钱骗局（humbugs）取之不尽的源头。西班牙人、葡萄牙人、法国人与英国人个个死心塌地地以为美洲主要是由黄金铸成的；兴许就像某人谈起科罗拉多时说的那样，那地方唯一的苦处，在于你得先凿穿三四英尺厚的纯银层，才能挖到底下的金子。早期美洲土地特许状几乎千篇一律地替国王保留了所发现一切金银矿藏的分成，由此便可见这种奇特迷思之一斑。即便一时挖不着金子，这帮懒惰的欧洲人也同样疯狂地幻想能在辽阔幽深的美洲崇山峻岭与森林里找到俯拾皆是的珍奇货殖。

在前文一章里，我已经讲过关于从南海诸国攫取无尽财富的幻想如何催生了英国的南海泡沫。

同一时期，在邻国法国，一种如出一辙的幻想也成了另一场同样荒唐却更为庞大、毒害更深、破坏力更惨烈的商业骗局的空中楼阁。这便是约翰·劳（John Law）的密西西比计划（Mississippi scheme），我将在本章详述其始末。依我看来，它堪称史无前例的头号商业大骗局。

劳是个苏格兰人，精明干练，就那个年代而言确是一位颇有手腕的金融家；但他品行恶劣，是个毫无底线的赌徒，热衷于异想天开的疯狂计划。他曾拥有颇为丰厚的家产，游历并赌遍全欧洲，风趣健谈、极善交际，深得奥尔良公爵及其他法国贵族的宠信。1715 年路易十四驾崩，奥尔良公爵成为法国摄政王；彼时法国因先王连年发动耗资巨大的战争并施行剥皮拆骨般的苛捐杂税，早已债台高筑、民不聊生。因此，当劳手捧一套前途无量的财政方案来到巴黎时，摄政王无论是作为理财行家还是老友，都对他格外欣喜相迎。

===Original===

The Regent quickly fell in with Law’s plans; and in the spring of 1716, the first step﻿—not, however, so intended at the time﻿—toward the Mississippi Scheme was taken. This was, the establishment by royal authority of the banking firm of Law & Co., consisting of Law and his brother. This bank, by a judicious organization and issue of paper money, quickly began to help the distressed finances of the kingdom, and to invigorate trade and commerce. This success, which seems to have been an entirely sound and legitimate business success, made one sadly mistaken but very deep impression upon the ignorant and shallow mind of the Regent of France, which was the foundation of all the subsequent trouble. The Regent became firmly convinced, that if a certain quantity of bank bills could do so much good, a hundred thousand times as many bills would surely do a hundred thousand times as much. That is, he thought printing and issuing the bills was creating money. He paid no regard to the need of providing specie for them on demand, but thought he had an unlimited money factory in the city of Paris.
So far, so good. Next, Law planned, and, with the ever ready consent of the Regent, effected, an enlargement of the business of his bank, based on that delusion I spoke of about America. This enlargement was the formation of the Mississippi Company, and this was the contrivance which swelled into so tremendous a humbug. The company was closely connected with the banks, and received (to begin with) the monopoly of all trade to the Mississippi River, and all the country west of it. It was expected to obtain vast quantities of gold and silver from that region, and thus to make immense dividends on its stock. At home, it was to have the sole charge of collecting all the taxes and coining all the money. Stock was issued to the amount of one hundred thousand shares, at $200 (five hundred livres) each. And Law’s help to the Government funds was continued by permitting this stock to be paid for in those funds, at their par value, though worth in market only about a third of it. Subscriptions came in rapidly﻿—for the French community was far more ignorant about commercial affairs, finances, and the real resources of distant regions, than we can easily conceive of nowadays; and not only the Regent, but every man, woman, and child in France, except a very few tough and hardheaded old skeptics, believed every word Law said, and would have believed him if he had told stories a hundred times as incredible.
Well, pretty soon the Regent gave the associates﻿—the bank and the company﻿—two other monopolies: that of tobacco, always monstrously profitable, and that of refining gold and silver. Pretty soon, again, he created the bank a state institution, by the magnificent name of The Royal Bank of France. Having done this, the Regent could control the bank in spite of Law (or order either); for, in those days, the kings of France were almost perfectly despotic, and the Regent was acting king. I have mentioned the Regent’s terrible delusion about paper-money. No sooner had he the bank in his power, than he added to the reasonable and useful total of $12,000,000 of notes already out, a monstrous issue of $200,000,000 worth in one vast batch, with the firm conviction that he was thus adding so much to the par currency of France.
The Parliament of France, a body mostly of lawyers, originating in the Middle Ages, a steady, conservative, wise, and brave assembly, was always hostile to Law and his schemes. When this great expansion of paper-currency began, the Parliament made a resolute fight against it, petitioning, ordaining, threatening to hang Law, and frightening him well, too; for the thorough enmity of an assembly of old lawyers may well frighten anybody. At last, the Regent, by the use of the despotic power of which the Kings of France had so much, reduced these old fellows to silence by sticking a few of them in jail.

===Chinese===

摄政王很快便对劳的方案言听计从；1716 年春，迈向密西西比计划的第一步跨出了——尽管当时并未存心如此。这便是经王室特许成立了劳氏银行（Law & Co.），由劳及其兄弟合伙经营。这家银行凭借审慎的架构与纸币发行，迅速舒缓了王国窘迫的财政，为工商业注入了活力。这一似乎完全稳健合法的商业成功，却在法国摄政王浅薄无知的头脑里留下了一个极其错误而又根深蒂固的印象，也成了日后一切祸患的根源。摄政王笃信不疑：既然一定数量的银行钞票能带来如此巨大的好处，那么印出十万倍的钞票定然能带来十万倍的好处。也就是说，他以为印刷并发行钞票就等于创造金钱。他丝毫不顾及必须随时备足金银硬币以供兑付的需要，反而以为自己在巴黎城里坐拥一座取之不尽的印钞造币厂。

至此倒还算顺风顺水。紧接着，劳谋划并在摄政王一贯的痛快首肯下，着手扩大银行业务，其根基正是我方才提过的关于美洲的迷思。这番扩张便是组建“密西西比公司”，而这正是日后膨胀成弥天大骗局的机关。该公司与银行紧密捆绑，起初获授密西西比河及其以西所有地域全部贸易的垄断权。人们指望它能从那片蛮荒之地源源不断地攫取巨量黄金白银，从而为股票派发丰厚股息。在国内，它还独占征收全部国税与铸造全部货币的特权。公司发行了十万股股票，每股 200 美元（合五百里弗尔）。为继续替政府公债纾困，公司允许认购者用政府公债按票面价值抵付股款，尽管这些公债在市场上的实际售价仅值面值的三分之一左右。认购款如潮水般涌来——因为当时法国社会对商业实务、金融运作以及遥远地区真实物产的无知程度，远非今日我们所能想象；不仅摄政王本人，全法国上至达官显贵下至妇孺稚童，除了极少数顽固铁头老怀疑派之外，对劳的每一句话都深信不疑；哪怕他编出比这离奇一百倍的鬼话，众人也照信不误。

没过多久，摄政王又赐予这对合伙机构——银行与公司——另外两项垄断权：向来暴利惊人的烟草专卖权，以及金银精炼垄断权。紧接着没几天，他又将银行升格为国家机构，冠以“法兰西皇家银行”这一金光闪闪的尊号。既已如此，摄政王便可绕开劳（甚至绕开一切规章）直接操控银行；因为在那个年代，法国国王几乎拥有绝对专制权力，而摄政王正是代理国王。我先前已提过摄政王对纸币的可怕妄想。他刚一将银行攥进掌心，便在原本已流通的 12,000,000 美元合理有用钞票总额之外，一口气增发了惊人的 200,000,000 美元巨额纸币，且坚信自己此举实打实地替法国增添了等额的足值通货。

法兰西高等法院（Parlement）主要由法学家组成，发端于中世纪，向来是一座稳健、保守、睿智而又勇敢的机构，自始至终敌视劳及其计划。当这场纸币大膨胀拉开序幕时，高等法院奋起反抗，接连上书请愿、颁布禁令，甚至扬言要绞死劳——着实把劳吓得够呛；毕竟一帮老律师的彻底敌意任谁听了都得腿软。最终，摄政王动用法国国王最擅长的专制铁腕，把其中几个领头的关进大牢，这才让这群老家伙彻底闭了嘴。

===Original===

The cross-grained Parliament thus disposed of, everything was quickly made to “look lovely.” In the beginning of 1719, more grants were made to Law’s associated concerns. The Mississippi Company was granted the monopoly of all trade to the East Indies, China, the South Seas, and all the territories of the French India Company, and of the Senegal Company. It took a new and imposing name: “The Company of the Indies.” They had already, by the way, also obtained the monopoly of the Canada beaver-trade. Of this colossal corporation, monopolizing the whole foreign commerce of France with two-thirds or more of the world, its whole home finances, and other important interests besides, fifty thousand new shares were issued, as before, at $100 each. These might be bought as before, with Government securities at par. Law was so bold as to promise annual dividends of $20 per share, which, as the Government funds stood, was one hundred and twenty percent per annum! Everybody believed him. More than three hundred thousand applications were made for the new shares. Law was besieged in his house by more than twice as many people as General Grant had to help him take Richmond. The Great Humbug was at last in full buzz. The street where the wonderful Scotchman lived was busy, filled, crowded, jammed, choked. Dangerous accidents happened in it every day, from the excessive pressure. From the princes of the blood down to cobblers and lackeys, all men and all women crowded and crowded to subscribe their money, and to pay their money, and to know how many shares they had gotten. Law moved to a roomier street, and the crazy mob crowded harder than ever; so that the Chancellor, who held his court of law hard by, could not hear his lawyers.
A tremendous uproar surely, that could drown the voices of those gentlemen! And so he moved again, to the great Hotel de Soissons, a vast palace, with a garden of some acres. Fantastic circumstances variegated the wild rush of speculation. The haughtiest of the nobility rented mean rooms near Law’s abode, to be able to get at him. Rents in his neighborhood rose to twelve and sixteen times their usual amount. A cobbler, whose lines had fallen in those pleasant places, made $40 a day by letting his stall and furnishing writing materials to speculators. Thieves and disreputable characters of all sorts flocked to this concourse. There were riots and quarrels all the time. They often had to send a troop of cavalry to clear the street at night. Gamblers posted themselves with their implements among the speculators, who gambled harder than the gamblers, and took an occasional turn at roulette by way of slackening the excitement; as people go to sleep, or go into the country. A hunchback fellow made a good deal of money by letting people write on his back. When Law had moved into the Hotel de Soissons, the former owner, the Prince de Carignan, reserved the gardens, procured an edict confining all stock-dealings to that place; put up five hundred tents there, leased them at five hundred livres a month each, and thus made money at the rate of $50,000 a month. There were just two of the aristocracy who were sensible and resolute enough not to speculate in the stock﻿—the Duke de St. Simon and the old Marshal Villars.

===Chinese===

既已打发掉这个桀骜不驯的高等法院，一切很快便显得“一片大好”。1719 年初，更多特许权源源不断赐予劳的关联机构。密西西比公司获授东印度、中国、南海以及法属印度公司与塞内加尔公司全部属地的贸易垄断权。它换上了一个气势磅礴的新名号：“印度公司”（The Company of the Indies）。顺带一提，他们此前早已拿下了加拿大海狸皮贸易的垄断权。这家垄断了法国与全世界三分之二以上地区全部对外贸易、独占全国财政金融乃至其他诸多命脉利益的庞然大物，照旧发行了五万股新股，每股 100 美元。这些新股同样可以用政府债券按面值购买。劳胆大包天地许诺每股每年派息 20 美元——按当时政府公债的市价折算，年化收益率高达百分之一百二十！人人都信以为真。新股认购申请蜂拥而至，总数超过三十万份。劳的宅邸被围得水泄不通，围堵人数竟比格兰特将军攻打里士满时手下的兵力还要多出一倍有余。这场惊天大骗局终于彻底沸反盈天。那位神奇苏格兰人居住的街道整日熙熙攘攘、挤满、塞爆、堵死、水泄不通。因人群过度拥挤，街上每天都在发生危险的踩踏事故。上至皇室宗亲，下至补鞋匠与奴仆，所有男女老少挤了又挤，争着认购、争着交钱、争着打听自己分到了多少股。劳搬到一条更宽敞的街道，结果癫狂的人群挤得比先前还要凶猛；就连在近旁开庭审案的大法官，也听不清手下律师的辩词。

能压过那帮大律师嗓门的喧嚣，必定震耳欲聋！于是他只得再次搬家，迁入宏伟的苏瓦松府邸（Hotel de Soissons）——一座占地数英亩、附带广袤花园的巨大宫殿。形形色色的荒诞奇景把这场疯狂的投机潮点缀得五光十色。最傲慢的贵族在劳寓所附近租下寒酸陋室，只为能近水楼台攀上他。他家周边的房租暴涨到寻常价位的十二到十六倍。一个祖坟冒青烟、铺子恰巧设在这片福地的补鞋匠，靠出租自家小摊位并向投机客供应笔墨纸砚，每天净赚 40 美元。各路盗贼与不法之徒纷纷涌向这处人潮。骚乱与斗殴终日不断，当局夜间常常不得不派出一队骑兵前去清街。职业赌徒带着赌具混迹于投机客中间——殊不知这帮投机客赌得比赌徒还疯，偶尔玩两把轮盘赌反倒是为了“松弛一下神经”，好比寻常人去睡一觉或去乡下散散心。有个驼背汉甚至靠让人趴在他背上写字签单赚了大钱。劳搬进苏瓦松府邸后，原主人卡里尼昂亲王（Prince de Carignan）特意保留了花园产权，求得一道御旨规定所有股票交易一律只能在此处进行；他在园内支起五百顶帐篷，每顶每月租金五百里弗尔，单靠收租每月便稳进 50,000 美元。全法国贵族当中，神智足够清醒、意志足够坚定、自始至终未曾染指这只股票的，仅仅只有圣西门公爵（Duke de St. Simon）与老维拉尔元帅（Marshal Villars）两人。

===Original===

Law became infinitely the most important person in the kingdom. Great and small, male and female, high and low, haunted his offices and antechambers, hunted him down, plagued his very life out, to get a moment’s speech with him, and get him to enter their names as buyers of stock. The highest nobles would wait half a day for the chance. His servants received great sums to announce some visitor’s name. Ladies of the highest rank gave him anything he would ask of them for leave to buy stock. One of them made her coachmen upset her out of her carriage as Law came by, to get a word with him. He helped her up; she got the word, and bought some stock. Another lady ran into the house where he was at dinner, and raised a cry of fire. The rest ran out, but she ran further in to reach Law, who saw what she was at, and like a pecuniary Joseph, ran away as fast as he could.
As the frenzy rose toward its height, and the Regent took advantage of it to issue stock enough to pay the whole national debt, namely, three hundred thousand new shares, at $1,000 each, or a thousand percent in the par value. They were instantly taken. Three times as many would have been instantly taken. So violent were the changes of the market, that shares rose or fell twenty percent within a few hours. A servant was sent to sell two hundred and fifty shares of stock; found on reaching the gardens of the Hotel de Soissons, that since he left his master’s house the price had risen from $1,600 (par value $100 remember) to $2,000. The servant sold, gave his master the proceeds at $1,600 a share, put the remaining $100,000 in his own pocket, and left France that evening. Law’s coachman became so rich that he left service, and set up his own coach; and when his master asked him to find a successor, he brought two candidates, and told Law to choose, and he would take the other himself. There were many absurd cases of vulgarians made rich. There were also many robberies and murders. That committed by the Count de Horn, one of the higher nobility and two accomplices, is a famous case. The Count, a dissipated rascal, poniarded a broker in a tavern for the money the broker carried with him. But he was taken, and, in spite of the utmost and most determined exertions of the nobility, the Regent had him broken on the wheel in public, like any other murderer.
The stock of the Company of the Indies, though it dashed up and down ten and twenty percent from day to day, was from the first immensely inflated. In August 1719, it sold at 610 percent; in a few weeks more it arose to 1,200 percent. All winter it still went up until, in April 1720, it stood at 2,050 percent. That is, one one-hundred dollar share would sell for two thousand and fifty dollars.

===Chinese===

劳一跃成为整个法兰西王国举足轻重的第一号人物。无论尊卑男女、高官显贵还是市井小民，整日徘徊在他的办公室与前厅之外，对他围追堵截、缠得他几乎喘不过气，只求能与他说上一句话，求他把自己名字写进股票买家名册。最显赫的大贵族甘愿苦等半天只为碰碰运气。他家的仆役单靠替访客通报一声姓名便收受重金。最高贵的贵妇人为求获准买股，凡他开口无不百依百顺。有位贵妇见劳路过，竟命令车夫故意把自己的马车翻倒在路旁，只为趁机与他搭上一句话。劳上前扶起她；她如愿说上了话，也买到了股票。另一位贵妇闯进他正在赴宴的宅邸，放声高喊“着火啦！”其余宾客夺门而出，她却逆流往里冲，直扑劳而来；劳一眼看穿她的用意，活像一位坚守钱袋的约瑟，拔腿以最快速度溜之大吉。

随着狂热攀至顶峰，摄政王趁势增发了足以清偿全部国债的股票——即三十万股新股，每股售价 1,000 美元，相当于票面价值的百分之一千。新股瞬间被抢购一空；哪怕再多发行三倍，照样会被瞬间抢光。市场行情剧烈震荡，短短几小时内股价就能暴涨或暴跌百分之二十。一名仆人奉主人之命去卖掉两百五十股股票；赶到苏瓦松府邸花园时，发现自他离开主人家门口的这工夫，股价已从 1,600 美元（记住，票面价值才 100 美元）窜升到了 2,000 美元。这仆人卖掉股票后，按每股 1,600 美元把款子交还主人，将多出来的 100,000 美元揣进自己腰包，当晚便逃离了法国。劳的车夫发了大财，干脆辞职不干，自己买了豪华马车；当老东家请他帮忙物色接班人时，他领来两名候选人，让劳先挑，剩下那个他自己雇去驾车。一时间，市井粗鄙之徒一夜暴富的荒唐事层出不穷。与此同时，抢劫与谋杀案也频频发生。高等贵族之一霍恩伯爵（Count de Horn）伙同两名同党犯下的血案尤为著名：这位挥霍无度的无赖伯爵在一家酒馆里用匕首刺死一名股票经纪人，只为抢走对方随身携带的巨款。但他终究落网；尽管全体贵族竭尽全力多方营救，摄政王仍下令将他与寻常杀人犯一视同仁，当众施以车裂之刑。

印度公司的股价虽日复一日地上下剧烈震荡一二十个百分点，但自始至终都处于极度虚胖之中。1719 年 8 月，股价已达票面价值的 610 %；几周后便飙升至 1,200 %。整个冬天一路高歌猛进，直到 1720 年 4 月，竟站上了 2,050 %的高位。也就是说，一张面值一百美元的股票能卖出两千零五十美元。

===Original===

At this extreme point of inflation, the bubble stood a little, shining splendidly as bubbles do when they are nearest bursting, and then it received two or three quiet pricks. The Prince de Conti, enraged because Law would not send him some shares on his own terms, sent three wagon-loads of bills to Law’s bank, demanding specie. Law paid it, and complained to the Regent, who made him put two-thirds of it back again. A shrewd stock-gambler drew specie by small sums until he had about $200,000 in coin, and lest he should be forced to return it, he packed it in a cart, covered it with manure, put on a peasant’s disguise, and carted his fortune over the frontiers into Belgium. Some others quietly realized their means in like manner by driblets and funded them abroad.
By such means coin gradually grew very scarce, and signs of a panic appeared. The Regent tried to adjust matters by a decree that coin should be five percent less than paper; as much as to say, It is hereby enacted that there is a great deal more coin than there is! This did not serve, and the Regent decreed again, that coin should be worth ten percent less than paper. Then he decreed that the bank must not pay more than $22 at once in specie; and, finally, by a bold stretch of his authority, he issued an edict that no person should have over $100 in coin, on pain of fine and confiscation. These odious laws made a great deal of trouble, spying, and distress, and rapidly aggravated the difficulty they were meant to cure. The price of shares in the great company began to fall steadily and rapidly. Law and the Regent began to be universally hated, cursed, and threatened. Various foolish and vain attempts were made to stay the coming ruin, by renewing the stories about Louisiana sending out a lot of conscripted laborers, ordering that all payments must be made in paper, and printing a new batch of notes, to the amount of another $300,000,000. Law’s two corporations were also doctored in several ways. The distress and fright grew worse. An edict was issued that Law’s notes and shares should depreciate gradually by law for a year, and then be worth but half their face. This made such a tumult and outcry that the Regent had to retract it in seven days. On this seventh day, Law’s bank stopped paying specie. Law was turned out of his public employments, but still well treated by the Regent in private. He was, however, mobbed and stoned in his coach in the street, had to have a company of Swiss Guards in his house, and at last had to flee to the Regent’s own palace.
I have not space to describe in detail the ruin, misery, tumults, loss and confusion which attended the speedy descent of Law’s paper and shares to entire worthlessness. Thousands of families were made paupers, and trade and commerce destroyed by the painful process. Law himself escaped out of France poor; and, after another obscure and disreputable career of gambling, died in poverty at Venice, in 1729.
Thus this enormous business-humbug first raised a whole nation into a fool’s paradise of imaginary wealth, and then exploded, leaving its projector and many thousands of victims ruined, the country disturbed and distressed, long-enduring consequences, in vicious and lawless and unsteady habits, contracted while the delusion lasted, and no single benefit except one more most dearly-bought lesson of the wicked folly of mere speculation without a real business basis and a real business method. Let not this lesson be lost on the rampant and half-crazed speculators of the present day. Those who buy gold or flour, leather, butter, dry goods, groceries, hardware, or anything else on speculation, when prices are inflated far beyond the ordinary standard, are taking upon themselves great risks, for the bubble must eventually be pricked; and whoever is the “holder” when that time comes, must necessarily be the loser.

===Chinese===

在膨胀到这一极端顶点时，泡沫略微停顿了片刻，如同即将炸裂前的一切肥皂泡般闪烁着绚丽夺目的光芒，随即挨了两三记悄无声息的针刺。孔蒂亲王（Prince de Conti）因劳不肯按他开的条件拨给他新股而怀恨在心，派人拉了整整三大马车纸币前往劳的银行要求兑付金银硬币。劳如数兑付后，向摄政王告了状，摄政王下令亲王退回其中三分之二。一位精明的股票赌徒则化整为零、零星提现，直到攒足了大约 200,000 美元硬币；生怕被迫吐出来，他把钱装进一辆大车，上面盖满粪肥，自己乔装成农夫，赶着车把这笔巨款偷运越过边境送往比利时。另有几个人也如法炮制，悄没声儿地一点一滴套现并转移到海外。

经此折腾，市面硬币日渐匮乏，恐慌的苗头初现。摄政王企图靠一纸政令强行扭转局面，规定硬币价值比纸币低百分之五——这简直等于下令宣布：“兹立法规定，世上的硬币比实际存在的要多得多！”这一招并不奏效，摄政王于是再次下令，规定硬币比纸币低百分之十。接着他又下令银行单次兑付硬币不得超过 22 美元；最后干脆滥用权威，颁布敕令严禁任何人持有超过 100 美元硬币，违者罚款并充公。这些令人憎恶的恶法引发了无休止的纷扰、告密与恐慌，非但没能治病，反而火速恶化了原本想挽救的危机。那家大公司的股价开始持续暴跌。劳与摄政王顿成众矢之的，遭举国痛恨、咒骂与威胁。当局又接连使出种种愚蠢徒劳的招数企图挽狂澜于既倒：重炒路易斯安那送来大批强征劳工的冷饭、强令一切款项必须用纸币结算，并开动印钞机又印出一批高达 300,000,000 美元的新钞。劳名下的两家机构也被东拼西凑地折腾了好几回。民间的困苦与恐慌愈演愈烈。随后当局颁布敕令，规定劳发行的纸币与股票在一年内依法逐月贬值，期满后仅值票面的一半。此令一出顿时激起滔天民变与怒吼，逼得摄政王七天后便不得不收回成命。就在这第七天，劳的银行彻底停止兑付硬币。劳被革除一切公职，尽管摄政王私下仍对他礼遇有加。然而他坐着马车上街时屡遭暴民围攻砸石块，不得不在宅邸驻扎一连瑞士近卫军，最终甚至被迫逃进摄政王宫避难。

我已无篇幅细数劳的纸币与股票飞速跌成一文不值的废纸时，所伴随的破产、惨剧、暴乱、损失与混乱。成千上万个家庭倾家荡产，工商业在这场痛苦的崩盘中惨遭摧残。劳本人一贫如洗地逃离法国；在度过又一段穷困潦倒、声名狼藉的赌徒生涯后，于 1729 年在威尼斯贫病交加而死。

就这样，这场旷古未有的商业大骗局先是把一整个民族捧进虚幻财富的傻瓜乐园，随后轰然炸裂，只留下始作俑者与成千上万名受害者家破人亡、国家动荡不安、民生凋敝；在迷梦持续期间养成的骄奢淫逸、目无法纪、浮躁轻浮的恶习更是遗毒深远；到头来一无所获，唯余又一记代价惨痛的教训——警示世人，毫无实体实业根基与正当经营章法的纯粹投机，是何等罪孽深重的愚行。愿今日那些嚣张狂热、几近癫狂的投机客切莫辜负这一前车之鉴。凡是在价格远远虚高出寻常水准之时，借投机买进黄金、面粉、皮革、黄油、布匹、杂货、五金或其他任何货物的人，无异于将自己置于万丈深渊的险境之上；因为泡沫终有被刺破的一天，而届时谁成了最后的“接盘侠”，谁就注定血本无归。

## Part / 部

===Original===

V Medicine and Quacks

===Chinese===

第五部 医药与江湖郎中

### XXVIII / 第二十八章

===Original===

Doctors And imagination﻿—​Firing a joke out of a cannon﻿—​The Paris eye water﻿—​Majendie on medical knowledge﻿—​Old sands of life.
Medical humbugs constitute a very critical subject indeed, because I shall be almost certain to offend some of three parties concerned, namely; physicians, quacks, and patients. But it will never do to neglect so important a division of my whole theme as this.
To begin with, it is necessary to suggest, in the most delicate manner in the world, that there is a small infusion of humbug among the very best of the regular practitioners. These gentlemen, for whose learning, kindheartedness, self-devotion, and skill I entertain a profound respect, make use of what I may call the gaseous element of their practice, not for the lucre of gain, but in order to enlist the imaginations of their patients in aid of nature and great remedies.
The stories are infinite in number, which illustrate the force of imagination, ranging through all the grades of mental action, from the lofty visions of good men who dream of seeing heaven opened to them, and all its ineffable glories and delights, down to the low comedy conceit of the fellow who put a smoked herring into the tail of his coat and imagined himself a mermaid.
Probably, however, imagination displays its real power more wonderfully in the operations of the mind on the body that holds it, than anywhere else. It is true that there are some people even so utterly without imagination that they cannot take a joke; such as that grave man of Scotland who was at last plainly told by a funny friend quite out of patience, “Why, you wouldn’t take a joke if it were fired at you out of a cannon!”
“Sir,” replied the Scot, with sound reasoning and grave thought, “Sir, you are absurd. You cannot fire a joke out of a cannon!”

===Chinese===

医生与想象力——用大炮轰出一句笑话——巴黎洗眼水——马让迪论医学知识——“垂暮残沙老医师”。

医药界的欺罔（humbugs）实在是个极其棘手的话题，因为我几乎注定要开罪牵涉其中的三方人马之一——要么是正牌医生，要么是江湖郎中，要么是病人。然而，如此重要的一个分支，在我整部论题中却万万不可略过。

首先，我必须用世上最委婉妥帖的措辞指出：即便在最优秀的正规执业医师身上，也掺杂着那么一丁点儿“欺罔”的成分。我对这些先生的学识、仁心、奉献精神与医术怀有深深的敬意；他们之所以在行医时动用我姑且称之为“虚无缥缈之气”的手段，并非为了贪图钱财，而是为了调动病人的想象力，好协同自然之力与良药一道起效。

世上彰显想象力威力的故事多如牛毛，囊括了人类心智活动的各个层级——上至善人梦见天堂大门向自己敞开、目睹难以言喻的荣光与极乐的高尚异象，下至某个把一条熏鲱鱼塞进燕尾服后摆、便自以为化作美人鱼的滑稽汉子的低俗臆想。

然而，想象力展现其真正魔力最令人惊叹的舞台，莫过于心智对承载它的肉身所施加的作用。诚然，世上也有些人想象力匮乏到连个笑话都听不懂；譬如那位一本正经的苏格兰人，他那位爱开玩笑的朋友忍无可忍之下直截了当地对他说道：“我说，就算把笑话装进大炮里朝你轰过来，你也接不住！”

“先生，”那苏格兰人经过一番缜密推理与深沉思考，正色答道，“先生，您这话太荒谬了。您根本不可能把一个笑话装进大炮里轰出来！”

===Original===

But to return: It is certainly the case that frequently “the doctor” takes great care not to let the patient know what is the matter, and even not to let him know what he is swallowing. This is because a good many people, if at a critical point of disease, may be made to turn toward health if made to believe that they are doing so, but would be frightened, in the literal sense of the words, to death, if told what a dangerous state they are in.
One sort of regular practice humbug is rendered necessary by the demands of the patients. This is giving good big doses of something with a horrid smell and taste. There are plenty of people who don’t believe the doctor does anything to earn his money, if he does not pour down some dirty brown or black stuff very nasty in flavor. Some, still more exacting, wish for that sort of testimony which depends on internal convulsions, and will not be satisfied unless they suffer torments and expel stuff enough to quiet the inside of Mount Vesuvius or Popocatepetl.
“He’s a good doctor,” was the verdict of one of this class of leather-boweled fellows﻿—“he’ll work your innards for you!”
It is a milder form of this same method to give what the learned faculty term a placebo. This is a thing in the outward form of medicine, but quite harmless in itself. Such is a bread-pill, for instance; or a draught of colored water, with a little disagreeable taste in it. These will often keep the patient’s imagination headed in the right direction, while good old Dame Nature is quietly mending up the damages in “the soul’s dark cottage.”
One might almost fancy that, in proportion as the physician is more skillful, by so much he gives less medicine, and relies more on imagination, nature, and, above all, regimen and nursing. Here is a story in point. There was an old gentleman in Paris, who sold a famous eye-water, and made much gain thereby. He died, however, one fine day, and unfortunately forgot to leave the recipe on record. “His disconsolate widow continued the business at the old stand,” however﻿—to quote another characteristic French anecdote﻿—and being a woman of ready and decisive mind, she very quietly filled the vials with water from the river Seine, and lived respectably on the proceeds, finding, to her great relief, that the eye-water was just as good as ever. At last however, she found herself about to die, and under the stings of an accusing conscience she confessed her trick to her physician, an eminent member of the profession. “Be entirely easy, Madam,” said the wise man; “don’t be troubled at all. You are the most innocent physician in the world; you have done nobody any harm.”

===Chinese===

言归正传：现实中确实屡见不鲜，“大夫”常常煞费苦心地不让病人知道自己究竟得了什么病，甚至不让他知道自己吞下去的是什么药。这是因为许多人在疾病生死攸关的关头，只要让他相信自己正在好转，病情便真能转向康复；可倘若如实相告他已身处何等凶险之境，他便会——毫不夸张地按字面意思——活活吓死。

正规行医中还有一类“欺罔”，纯属应病人要求而不得已为之。那便是开出大剂量的、气味与滋味都极其恶心难闻的药水。世上有的是这种人：大夫若不给他灌下一大碗色泽污浊棕黑、味道令人作呕的苦药汁，他便不信大夫干了什么对得起诊金的事。更有甚者，要求更加苛刻，非得见识腹内翻江倒海的实证不可——若不饱受一番折腾、泻出足够让维苏威火山（Mount Vesuvius）或波波卡特佩特火山（Popocatepetl）腹内平息下来的污秽之物，便绝不肯罢休。

“他是个好大夫，”这类铁石肠胃汉子中的一位曾如此评价道，“他准能把你肚子里翻个底朝天！”

同一路数中较为温和的一种做法，便是开具医学界学究们所谓的“安慰剂”（placebo）。此物外表看似药物，实则本身毫无害处。譬如一粒面包药丸，或者一小口带点儿难喝怪味的有色糖水。这些东西往往能稳住病人的想象力，使其朝向正确的方向前行，与此同时，仁慈的老大自然母亲便悄无声息地修补起“灵魂的幽暗小屋”里的破损。

人们几乎不难想象，医生的医术越高明，他开的药反而越少，越发仰赖病人的想象力、自然自愈力，以及最重要的——饮食起居调养与悉心护理。下面这则故事恰可为证：从前巴黎有位老绅士，靠出售一种名声显赫的洗眼水赚得盆满钵满。然而某个晴朗的日子，他撒手人寰，偏偏忘了把配方记录在案。借用另一则颇具法国风味的轶事之言，“他悲痛欲绝的遗孀仍在老铺面继续经营这门营生”；这位夫人头脑机敏、行事果断，不动声色地直接拿塞纳河（river Seine）的河水灌满一只只小药瓶，靠着卖水过上了体面日子；更令她大松一口气的是，这洗眼水的疗效竟与从前一般无二。直到最后她自己也大限将至，在良心谴责的刺痛下，她向自己的主治医生——一位德高望重的医界名流——坦白了自己的骗局。“夫人，请放宽心，”这位智者宽慰道，“切莫为此烦恼。您是世上最清白无辜的医生；您从未伤害过任何人。”

===Original===

It is an old and illiberal joke to compare medicine to war, on the ground that the votaries of both seek to destroy life. It is, however, not far from the truth to say that they are alike in this; that they are both preeminently liable to mistakes, and that in both he is most successful who makes the fewest.
How can it be otherwise, until we know more than we do at present, of the great mysteries of life and death? It seems risky enough to permit the wisest and most experienced physician to touch those springs of life which God only understands. And it is enough to make the most stupid stare, to see how people will let the most disgusting quack jangle their very heartstrings with his poisonous messes, about as soon as if he were the best doctor in the world. A true physician, indeed, does not hasten to drug. The great French surgeon, Majendie, is even said to have commenced his official course of lectures on one occasion by coolly saying to his students: “Gentlemen, the curing of disease is a subject that physicians know nothing about.” This was doubtless an extreme way of putting the case. Yet it was in a certain sense exactly true. There is one of the geysers in Iceland, into which visitors throw pebbles or turfs, with the invariable result of causing the disgusted geyser in a few minutes to vomit the dose out again, along with a great quantity of hot water, steam, and stuff. Now the doctor does know that some of his doses are pretty sure to work, as the traveler knows that his dose will work on the geyser. It is only the exact how and why that is not understood.
But however mysterious is nature, however ignorant the doctor, however imperfect the present state of physical science, the patronage and the success of quacks and quackeries are infinitely more wonderful than those of honest and laborious men of science and their careful experiments.
I have come about to the end of my tether for this time; and quackery is something too monstrous in dimensions as well as character to be dealt with in a paragraph. But I may with propriety put one quack at the tail of this letter; it is but just that he should let decent people go before him. I mean “Old Sands of Life.” Everybody has seen his advertisement, beginning “A retired Physician whose sands of life have nearly run out,” etc. And everybody﻿—almost﻿—knows how kind the fellow is in sending gratis his recipe. All that is necessary is (as you find out when you get the recipe) to buy at a high price from him one ingredient which (he says) you can get nowhere else. This swindling scamp is in fact a smart brisk fellow of about thirty-five years of age, notwithstanding the length of time during which﻿—to use a funny phrase which somebody got up for him﻿—he has been “afflicted with a loose tailboard to his mortal sand-cart.” Some benevolent friend was so much distressed about the feebleness of “Old Sands of Life” as to send him one day a large parcel by express, marked “C.O.D.,” and costing quite a figure. “Old Sands” paid, and opening the parcel, found half a bushel of excellent sand.

===Chinese===

把医学比作战争，理由是两者的信徒都致力于毁灭生命——这不过是个老掉牙又刻薄的笑话。然而若说两者在下面这一点上颇为相似，倒离真相不远：二者都极易出错，且在两行之中，犯错最少者便是最成功之人。

在对生与死的伟大奥秘认知仍止步于当下水准之前，又怎能不如此呢？哪怕请最睿智、经验最丰富的医生去触碰唯有上帝才洞悉的生命机关，似乎都已足够冒险。而眼睁睁看着世人竟把最恶心透顶的江湖郎中请进门来，任他用有毒的糊涂药水拨弄自己的性命心弦，急切程度丝毫不亚于延请世上最好的大夫，这简直足以让最迟钝的人也目瞪口呆。真正的大夫从不急着下猛药。据说伟大的法国外科医生马让迪（Majendie）有一次在正式开讲系列课程时，一开口便冷静地对学生说道：“诸位先生，如何治愈疾病，乃是一门医生们一无所知的学问。”这番话无疑说得有些极端，却在某种意义上千真万确。冰岛有一处间歇泉，游客常往泉眼里扔进鹅卵石或草皮，结果屡试不爽：不出几分钟，厌恶至极的间歇泉便会连同大量热水、蒸汽和泥沙一并把这剂“药”吐出来。大夫确实知道自己的某些药剂十有八九会起反应，正如游客知道自己扔进泉眼的东西会让间歇泉喷发一般；人们唯独不明白的，正是其中确切的机理与缘由。

然而，无论大自然多么幽深莫测，无论医生多么无知，无论当今自然科学多么不完备，江湖郎中与江湖骗术所获得的追捧与成功，比起那些诚实勤勉的科学工作者及其审慎的实验来，实在要令人惊诧万倍。

这一篇的篇幅已然见底；而江湖骗术无论在规模还是性质上都太过庞杂，绝非区区一段文字所能打发。不过，在本章末尾顺手揪出一名江湖郎中倒也恰如其分——毕竟让他排在正派人后头才算公道。我说的是那位“垂暮残沙老医师”（Old Sands of Life）。人人都见过他的广告，开头总是“一位生命沙漏已近漏尽的退休医师……”云云。而且几乎人人都知道这仁兄有多“慷慨”，免费索取便寄给你祖传秘方。唯一的前提是（等你收到秘方才会发现），你必须花高价从他那儿买一味药引——据他说别处绝无出售。这个行骗的无赖其实是个三十五岁左右、精明干练的壮年小伙，尽管套用某人替他编的俏皮话，他这许多年来一直“饱受凡人运沙车后挡板松脱漏沙之苦”。某位心肠仁慈的朋友实在替“垂暮残沙老医师”的衰弱身子骨忧心不已，有一天便通过快递给他寄去一大包东西，标明“货到付款”（C.O.D.），运费可观。“老残沙”付了款，拆开包裹一看——里头装了整整半蒲式耳上等好沙子。

### XXIX / 第二十九章

===Original===

The consumptive remedy﻿—​E. Andrews, M.D.﻿—Born without birthrights﻿—​Hashish candy﻿—​Roback the Great﻿—​A conjurer opposed to lying.
There is a fellow in Williamsburg who calls himself a clergyman, and sells a “consumptive remedy,” by which I suppose he means a remedy for consumption. It is a mere slop corked in a vial; but there are a good many people who are silly enough to buy it of him. A certain gentleman, during last November, earnestly sought an interview with this reverend brother in the interests of humanity, but he was as inaccessible as a chipmunk in a stone fence. The gentleman wrote a polite note to the knave asking about prices, and received a printed circular in return, stating in an affecting manner the good man’s grief at having to raise his price in consequence of the cost of gold “with which I am obliged to buy my medicines” saith he, “in Paris.” This was both sad and unsatisfactory; and the gentleman went over to Williamsburg to seek an interview and find out all about the prices. He reached the abode of the man of piety, but, strange to relate, he wasn’t at home.
Gentleman waited.
Reverend brother kept on not being at home. When gentleman had waited to his entire satisfaction he came back.
It is understood it is practically out of the question to see the reverend brother. Perhaps he is so modest and shy that he will not encounter the clamorous gratitude which would obstruct his progress through the streets, from the millions saved by his consumptive remedy. It is a pity that the reverend man cannot enjoy the still more complete seclusion by which the state of New York testifies its appreciation of unobtrusive and retiring virtues like his, in the salubrious and quiet town of Sing Sing.

===Chinese===

肺痨灵药——医学博士 E. 安德鲁斯——生而无天赋权利——大麻糖——伟大的罗巴克——一位反对撒谎的术士。

威廉斯堡（Williamsburg）有个家伙自称是牧师，兜售一种“肺痨灵药”（consumptive remedy，我猜他指的是治肺痨的药）。那不过是拿软木塞封在小药瓶里的一摊浑水；可世上偏偏有不少蠢人甘愿掏钱向他买。去年十一月，有位绅士本着人道关切，热切求见这位可敬的牧师弟兄，可他却像石墙缝里的花栗鼠一样神龙见首不见尾。那位绅士给这无赖写了封客客气气的便条打听价钱，结果收到一张铅印传单，凄凄切切地诉说着这位善人因黄金涨价而不得不提价的苦衷——“毕竟，”他写道，“我不得不用黄金在巴黎采购药材。”这番答复既令人伤感又叫人不满意；于是那位绅士亲自过江去威廉斯堡登门求见，想把价钱问个明白。他抵达这位虔诚人士的寓所，可说来也怪——他不在家。

绅士等了一会儿。

可敬的弟兄继续不在家。等绅士等得彻底死了心，便打道回府了。

据大家伙儿了解，要想亲眼见着这位可敬的弟兄简直是天方夜谭。兴许他生性太过谦逊羞怯，生怕在街上走动时被他那肺痨灵药救活的千百万人团团围住、高呼感恩，堵得寸步难行吧。真可惜，这位可敬的先生竟未能去往风景宜人、宁静清幽的辛辛镇（Sing Sing），享受纽约州专门用来表彰他这等不事张扬、深居简出之美德的更加彻底的隐居生活（译注：辛辛州立监狱所在地）。

===Original===

A quack in an inland city, who calls himself E. Andrews, M.D., prints a “semi-occasional” document in the form of a periodical, of which a copy is lying before me. It is an awful hodgepodge of perfect nonsense and vulgar rascality. He calls it “The Good Samaritan and Domestic Physician,” and this number is called “volume twenty.” Only think what a great man we have among us﻿—unless the Doctor himself is mistaken. He says: “I will here state that I have been favored by nature and Providence in gaining access to stores of information that has *fell* to the lot of but very few persons heretofore, during the past history of mankind.” Evidently these “stores” were so vast that the great doctor’s brain was stuffed too full to have room left for English Grammar. Shortly, the Doctor thus bursts forth again with some views having their own merits, but not such as concern the healing art very directly: “The automaton powers of machinery”﻿—there’s a new style of machinery, you observe﻿—“must be made to **work for**, *instead* of *as now*, against mankind; the Land of *all nations* must be made **free** to Actual Settlers in **limited** quantities. No one must be born without *his birthright* being born with him.” The italics, etc., are the Doctor’s. What an awful thought is this of being born without any birthright, or, as the Doctor leaves us to suppose possible, having one’s birthright born first, and dodging about the world like a stray canary-bird, while the unhappy and belated owner tries in vain to put salt on its tail and catch it!
Well, this wiseacre, after his portentous introduction, fills the rest of his sixteen loosely printed double-columned octavo pages with a farrago of the most indescribable character, made up of brags, lies, promises, forged recommendations and letters, boasts of systematic charity, funny scraps of stuff in the form of little disquisitions, advertisements of remedies, hair-oils, cosmetics, liquors, groceries, thistle-killers, anti-bug mixtures, recipes for soap, ink, honey, and the Old Harry only knows what. The fellow gives a list of seventy-one specific diseases for which his Hashish Candy is a sure cure, and he adds that it is also a sure cure for all diseases of the liver, brain, throat, stomach, ear, and other internal disorders; also for “all long standing diseases”﻿—whatever that means!﻿—and for insanity! In this monstrous list are jumbled together the most incongruous troubles. “Bleeding at the nose, and abortions;” “worms, fits, poisons and cramps.” And the impudent liar quotes General Grant, General Mitchell, the Rebel General Lee, General McClellan, and Doctor Mott of this city, all shouting in chorus the praises of the Hashish Candy! Next comes the “Secret of Beauty,” a “preparation of Turkish Roses;” then alot of forged references, and an assertion that the Doctor gives to the poor five thousand pounds of bread every winter; then some fearful denunciations of the regular doctors.
But﻿—as the auctioneers say﻿—“I can’t dwell.” I will only add that the real villainy of this fellow only appears here and there, where he advertises the means of ruining innocence, or of indulging with impunity in the foulest vices. He will sell for $3.30, the “Mystic Weird Ring.” In a chapter of infamous blatherumskite about this ring he says: “The wearer can drive from, or draw to him, anyone, and for any purpose whatever.” I need not explain what this scoundrel means. He also will sell the professed means of robbery and swindling; saying that he is prepared to show how to remove papers, wills, titles, notes, etc., from one place to another “by invisible means.” It is a wonder that the Bank of Commerce can keep any securities in its vaults﻿—of course!

===Chinese===

某座内陆城市里有个江湖郎中，自封为“医学博士 E. 安德鲁斯”（E. Andrews, M.D.），自己印行一份“不定期”期刊式小报，我手头正放着一期。里头简直是一锅胡言乱语与粗俗无赖的大杂烩。他给这刊物取名《好撒玛利亚人与家庭医师》，这一期还标着“第二十卷”。想想看咱们中间竟出了这么一位大人物——除非博士本人搞错了。他写道：“鄙人谨此声明，蒙造化与天意垂青，得以接触到在人类过往历史上迄今仅有极少数人曾有幸获得的知识宝库。”显然这些“宝库”太过浩瀚，把大博士的脑瓜塞得满满当当，以至于连英语语法都腾不出地方安放了。没过几行，博士又抛出一通自带亮点的宏论——尽管与医术实在没多大直接干系：“机械的自动伟力”——瞧见没，一种新型机械！——“必须被用来**造福**而非*像眼下这样*祸害人类；*万邦*之土地必须以**有限**数量**免费**授予实际定居垦荒者。任何人降生时，绝不能不带着*他的天赋权利*一同降生。”斜体与加粗均为博士原著所有。一想到人降生时居然可能没带天赋权利，或者照博士留给我们的想象余地——一个人的天赋权利先他一步降生，像一只迷途的金丝雀在世间四处乱窜，而倒霉又迟到的主人却徒劳地想往它尾巴上撒盐好抓住它——这念头可真够骇人的！

好吧，这位聪明人发表完气势磅礴的导言后，把其余十六页稀疏排印的双栏八开本塞满了难以名状的杂七杂八货色：吹牛、撒谎、空头支票、伪造的推荐信与证明函、吹嘘自己如何系统性地行善、冒充小论文的滑稽碎段子，以及各种灵药、发油、化妆品、烈酒、杂货、除蓟剂、杀虫药水、肥皂配方、墨水、蜂蜜，天晓得还有些什么鬼玩意儿。这小子开出了一张七十一种特定疾病的清单，宣称他的“大麻糖”（Hashish Candy）药到病除；末了还补充说，它对肝脏、大脑、咽喉、胃部、耳朵及一切内脏疾病统统包治；还治“一切陈年痼疾”——天知道那是什么意思！——外加精神错乱！在这张骇人听闻的清单里，风马牛不相及的毛病被胡乱搅成一锅：“流鼻血与流产”；“寄生虫、癫痫、中毒与抽筋”。而且这厚颜无耻的撒谎精还胆敢引用格兰特将军、米切尔将军、叛军李将军、麦克莱伦将军以及本市莫特医生的名言，仿佛他们全在齐声高唱大麻糖的赞歌！紧接着是“驻颜秘诀”，一种“土耳其玫瑰制剂”；随后是一大堆伪造的证明人，以及声称博士每年冬天向穷人施舍五千磅面包；再往后则是对正规医生的几通恶毒诅咒。

不过——套用拍卖师的口头禅——“我不能在此久留”。我只需补充一点：这厮真正的恶毒之处，仅仅在字里行间若隐若现——他在那儿兜售摧残纯洁少女、或者放纵最龌龊恶习而逍遥法外的门道。他以 3.30 美元出售一枚“神秘诡异指环”。在一章关于此环的下流胡扯中，他写道：“佩戴者可随意驱逐或吸引任何人，达成任何目的。”这无赖究竟安的什么心，无需我多作解释。他还兜售号称可用于盗窃与诈骗的法门，声称随时准备传授如何“以无形手段”将契约、遗嘱、地契、票据等从一处挪到另一处。照这么说，商业银行居然还能把证券安稳锁在金库里，简直是个奇迹了！

===Original===

But enough of this degraded panderer to crime and folly. He is beneath notice, so far as he himself concerned; I devote the space to him, because it is well worth while to understand how base an imposture can draw a steady revenue from a nation boasting so much culture and intelligence as ours. It is also worth considering whether the authorities must not be remiss, who permit such odious deceptions to be constantly perpetrated upon the public.
I ought here to give a paragraph to the great C. W. Roback, one of whose Astrological Almanacs is before me. This erudite production is embellished in front with a picture of the doctor and his six brothers﻿—for he is the seventh son of a seventh son. The six elder brethren﻿—nice enough boys﻿—stand submissively around their gigantic and bearded junior, reaching only to his waist, and gazing up at him with reverence, as the sheaves of Joseph’s brethren worshipped his sheaf in his dream. At the end is a picture of Magnus Roback, the grandfather of C. W., a bullheaded, ugly old Dutchman, with a globe and compasses. This picture, by the way, is in fact a cheap likeness of the old discoverers or geographers. Within the book we find Gustavus Roback, the father of C. W., for whom is used a cut of Jupiter﻿—or some other heathen god﻿—half-naked, astraddle of an eagle, with a hook in one hand and a quadrant in the other; which is very much like the picture by one of the “Old Masters” of Abraham about to offer up Isaac, and taking a long aim at the poor boy with a flintlock horse-pistol. Doctor Roback is good enough to tell us where his brothers are: “One, a high officer in the Empire of China, another a Catholic Bishop in the city of Rome,” and so on. There is also a cut of his sister, whom he cured of consumption. She is represented “talking to her bird, after the fashion of her country, when a maiden is unexpectedly rescued from the jaws of death!”
Roback cures all sorts of diseases, discovers stolen property, insures children a marriage, and so on, all by means of “conjurations.” He also casts nativities and foretells future events; and he shows in full how Bernadotte, Louis Philippe, and Napoleon Bonaparte either did well or would have done well by following his advice. The chief peculiarity of this impostor is, that he really avoids direct pandering to vice and crime, and even makes it a specialty to cure drunkenness and﻿—of all things in the world﻿—lying! On this point Roback gives in full the certificate of Mrs. Abigail Morgan, whose daughter Amanda “was sorely given to fibbing, in so much that she would rather lie than speak the truth.” And the delighted mother certifies that our friend and wizard “so changed the nature of the girl that, to the best of our knowledge and belief, she has never spoken anything but the truth since.”
There is a conjurer “as is a conjurer.”
What an uproar the incantation of the great Roback would make, if set fairly to work among the politicians, for instance! But after all, on second thoughts, what a horrible mass of abominations would they lay bare in telling the truth about each other all round! No, no﻿—it won’t do to have the truth coming out, in politics at any rate! Away with Roback! I will not give him another word﻿—not a single chance﻿—not even to explain his great power over what he calls “Fits! Fits! Fits! Fits! Fits!”

===Chinese===

好了，关于这个堕落的罪恶与愚昧掮客，说得够多了。就他本人而言，实在不值一提；我之所以拨出篇幅写他，是因为我们很值得弄明白：在像我们这样一个自诩高度文明聪慧的国度里，究竟多么卑劣的骗局仍能财源滚滚。同样值得深思的是，任由这等令人发指的欺诈长年累月毒害公众，有关当局难道没有失职之嫌吗？

在此我还应当匀出一段篇幅，献给伟大的 C. W. 罗巴克（C. W. Roback），我手头正摆着他的一本《占星年鉴》。这部鸿篇巨制的扉页印着一幅博士与他六位兄长的合影——因为他是第七个儿子的第七个儿子。那六位兄长——个个都是眉清目秀的好小伙——恭顺地侍立在他们那位身材魁梧、蓄着大胡子的幼弟身旁，个头才刚刚够到他的腰际，仰起脸崇敬地瞻望着他，活像约瑟的梦中哥哥们的麦捆向他的麦捆下拜一般。书末印着 C. W. 的祖父马格努斯·罗巴克（Magnus Roback）的画像，那是个牛头牛脑、相貌丑陋的荷兰老头儿，手旁摆着地球仪和圆规。顺便说一句，这幅画其实是从旧时航海家或地理学家的廉价肖像里随便套用的。书里头还印着 C. W. 的父亲古斯塔夫·罗巴克（Gustavus Roback），用的竟是一幅朱庇特——或者别的什么异教神祇——的木刻版画：半裸着身子骑在一头老鹰背上，一手拎着钩子，一手拿着象限仪；这滑稽劲儿，跟某位“古典大师”画的亚伯拉罕正准备献祭以撒、手里却端着一把燧发马枪朝那可怜孩子长距离瞄准的画作有得一拼。罗巴克博士还颇为好心地告诉我们他几位兄长的下落：“一位在中华帝国身居高官，另一位在罗马城担任天主教主教，”如此等等。书里甚至还有一幅他妹妹的木刻画，据说他治好了她的肺痨。画上描绘着她“依照本国风俗，在少女意外从死神魔爪下获救之后，正对着她的小鸟倾诉衷肠！”

罗巴克能治百病、寻回失物、保佑儿女觅得良缘等等，全凭一手“施法念咒”。他还精通占星排盘、预言吉凶；并详尽论证了贝尔纳多特、路易-菲利普以及拿破仑·波拿巴当年是如何因听从了他的忠告而飞黄腾达——或者本可飞黄腾达。这位骗子最奇特之处在于，他居然真的避免直接迎合恶习与犯罪，甚至把戒酒与——天下之大无奇不有——戒除撒谎当成自己的拿手绝活！就这一点，罗巴克全文刊登了阿比盖尔·摩根夫人（Mrs. Abigail Morgan）的感谢信，信中说她的女儿阿曼达（Amanda）“素来酷爱扯谎，宁可撒谎也不愿说句真话”。而这位喜出望外的母亲作证道，我们这位老友兼巫师“彻底改变了女孩的天性，据我们所知所信，自那以后她再也没说过半句假话”。

这才叫“真材实料”的巫师呢。

试想一下，要是把伟大的罗巴克这套咒语正儿八经地施展在政客中间，该会掀起多大的轩然大波啊！可转念一想，要是政客们个个彼此坦诚相见、大实话满天飞，那得抖落出多少骇人听闻的丑事啊！不成不成——至少在政坛上，真相可万万不能露头！滚吧，罗巴克！我再也不给他多写一个字——连半个机会也不给——甚至不许他开口解释自己对所谓“抽风！抽风！抽风！抽风！抽风！”究竟有何神通！

### XXX / 第三十章

===Original===

Monsignore Cristoforo Rischio; or, Il Creso, the nostrum-vender of Florence﻿—​A model for our quack doctors.
Every visitor to Florence during the last twenty years must have noticed on the grand piazza before the Ducal Palace, the strange genius known as Monsignore Créso, or, in plain English, Mr. Croesus. He is so called because of his reputed great wealth; but his real name is Christoforo Rischio, which I may again translate, as Christopher Risk. Mrs. Browning refers to him in one of her poems﻿—the “Casa Guidi Windows,” I think﻿—and he has also been the staple of a tale by one of the Trollope brothers.
Twice every week, he comes into the city in a strange vehicle, drawn by two fine Lombardy ponies, and unharnesses them in the very center of the square. His assistant, a capital vocalist, begins to sing immediately, and a crowd soon collects around the wagon. Then Monsignore takes from the box beneath his seat a splendidly jointed human skeleton, which he suspends from a tall rod and hook, and also a number of human skulls. The latter are carefully arranged on an adjustable shelf, and Créso takes his place behind them, while in his rear a perfect chemist’s shop of flasks, bottles, and pillboxes is disclosed. Very soon his singer ceases, and in the purest Tuscan dialect﻿—the very utterance of which is music﻿—the Florentine quack-doctor proceeds to address the assemblage. Not being conversant with the Italian, I am only able to give the substance of his harangue, and pronounce indifferently upon the merit of his elocution. I am assured, however, that not only the common people, who are his chief patrons, but numbers of the most intelligent citizens, are always entertained by what he has to say; and certainly his gestures and style of expressions seem to betray great excellence of oratory. Having turned the skeleton round and round on its pivot, and minutely explained the various anatomical parts, in order to show his proficiency in the basis of medical science, he next lifts the skulls, one by one, and descants upon their relative perfection, throwing in a shrewd anecdote now and then, as to the life of the original owner of each cranium.
One skull, for example, he asserts to have belonged to a lunatic, who wandered for half a lifetime in the Val d’Ema, subsisting precariously upon entirely vegetable food﻿—roots, herbs, and the like; another is the superior part of a convict, hung in Arezzo for numerous offenses; a third is that of a very old man who lived a celibate from his youth up, and by his abstinence and goodness exercised an almost priestly influence upon the *borghesa*. When, by this miscellaneous lecture, he has both amused and edified his hearers, he ingeniously turns the discourse upon his own life, and finally introduces the subject of the marvellous cures he has effected. The story of his medical preparations alone, their components and method of distillation, is a fine piece of popularized art, and he gives a practical exemplification of his skill and their virtues by calling from the crowd successively, a number of invalid people, whom he examines and prescribes for on the spot. Whether these subjects are provided by himself or not, I am unable to decide; but it is very possible that by long experience, Christoforo﻿—who has no regular diploma﻿—has mastered the simpler elements of Materia Medica, and does in reality effect cures. I class him among what are popularly known as humbugs, however, for he is a pretender to more wisdom than he possesses. It was to me a strange and suggestive scene﻿—the bald, beak-nosed, coal-eyed charlatan, standing in the marketplace, so celebrated in history, peering through his gold spectacles at the upturned faces below him, while the bony skeleton at his side swayed in the wind, and the grinning skulls below, made grotesque faces, as if laughing at the gullibility of the people. Behind him loomed up the massive Palazzo Vecchio, with its high tower, sharply cut, and set with deep machicolations; to the left, the splendid Loggia of Orgagna, filled with rare marbles, and the long picture-gallery of the Uffizi, heaped with the rarest art-treasures of the world; to his right, the Giant Fountain of Ammanato, throwing jets of pure water﻿—one drop of which outvalues all the nostrums in the world; and in front, the Post Office, built centuries before, by Pisan captives. If any of these things moved the imperturbable Créso, he showed no feeling of the sort; but for three long hours, two days in the week, held his hideous clinic in the open daylight.

===Chinese===

克里斯托福罗·里斯基奥老爷，又名“克雷索”，佛罗伦萨的灵药小贩——堪称我国江湖郎中的楷模。

近二十年来凡是造访过佛罗伦萨的游客，想必都曾在公爵宫前的大广场上见过那位人称“克雷索老爷”（Monsignore Créso，用通俗英语讲就是“克罗伊斯先生”Mr. Croesus）的奇才。他之所以得此名号，是因为据说他家财万贯；不过他的真名实姓是克里斯托福罗·里斯基奥（Cristoforo Rischio），我也可以再意译为“克里斯托弗·冒险”（Christopher Risk）。布朗宁夫人在她的一首诗——我想是《圭迪宅邸之窗》（*Casa Guidi Windows*）——里曾提到过他，而特罗洛普兄弟中的一位也曾以他为原型写过一则故事。

每周两次，他会驾着一辆奇形怪状的马车进城，由两匹骏美的伦巴第小马拉着，直驱广场正中央卸下马具。他的助手是个极出色的歌手，立刻引吭高歌，转眼便在马车周围聚拢起一大群看客。随后，老爷从座位底下的箱子里取出一副关节衔接得极其精巧的人体骨架，挂在一根长杆的钩子上，又取出好几颗人头骨。头骨整齐地码放在一块可调节的架子上，克雷索站在头骨后方，而他身后则露出一整套药房行头，摆满了烧瓶、药瓶与药丸盒。很快，歌手停下歌唱，这位佛罗伦萨江湖郎中便用最纯正的托斯卡纳方言——光是听这口音调就宛若音乐——向围观人群发表演说。由于我不通意大利语，只能转述他演讲的大意，对他的口才优劣未敢妄评。不过有人向我保证，不仅作为他主要主顾的平民百姓，就连大批最有学识的市民也总是被他的谈吐逗得津津有味；单看他的手势与神态语气，确实显露出极高超的演说功力。他先把骨架在枢轴上转来转去，详尽讲解各处解剖部位，以显示自己在医学基础上的深厚造诣；接着逐一捧起头骨，品评各自头型的优劣，不时穿插一两则机智轶事，讲述每颗头颅原主人的生平。

譬如其中一颗头骨，他断言属于一名疯子，半辈子在埃马河谷（Val d’Ema）游荡，全靠草根、野草之类纯素食勉强果腹；另一颗则是一名重刑犯的脑袋，因罪行累累在阿雷佐（Arezzo）被处以绞刑；第三颗属于一位打年轻起便严守独身的老人，生前凭着节欲与善行，在市民阶层（*borghesa*）中享有近乎神父般的威望。一番杂学演讲把听众逗乐又“教化”一番后，他便巧妙地把话题转到自己生平，最终引出他所创下的种种神奇治愈案例。单是讲述他自家药剂的成分与蒸馏秘法，便是一篇精彩绝伦的通俗艺术；随后他从人群里接连叫出几名病患，当场把脉问诊、开方抓药，以实操印证自己的医术与药效。这些病人究竟是不是他自己事先安排的托儿，我无从断定；不过久病成医，克里斯托福罗虽无正规行医执照，倒也极有可能凭着多年阅历摸熟了《药物学》（Materia Medica）里最浅显的门道，真能治好几个病。尽管如此，我仍把他归入世人常说的“欺罔之徒”（humbugs），因为他装作拥有远超自己实际学识的智慧。在我看来，那真是一幅既诡异又耐人寻味的画面：这个秃顶、鹰钩鼻、炭黑眼珠的江湖术士站在历史上赫赫有名的集市广场上，透过金丝眼镜俯视着台下仰起的一张张面孔；身旁那副骨瘦嶙峋的骨架在风中摇曳，底下那一排咧嘴露齿的头骨做出怪诞的鬼脸，仿佛在嘲笑世人的轻信。在他身后，巍峨的旧宫（Palazzo Vecchio）高塔耸立，轮廓凌厉，顶着深深的堞口；左侧是奥尔卡尼亚凉廊（Loggia of Orgagna），陈列着稀世大理石雕像，紧挨着世界顶级艺术珍宝云集的乌菲齐长廊美术馆；右侧是阿马纳托巨人喷泉（Giant Fountain of Ammanato），喷涌着澄澈清泉——其中一滴便胜过世间一切灵丹妙药；正前方则是数世纪前由比萨俘虏修建的邮政总局。凡此种种名胜古迹，若有任何一处曾触动过泰然自若的克雷索，他也从未流露分毫；每周两天、每次整整三个钟头，他就这么在光天化日之下开办他那阴森可怖的露天诊所。

===Original===
