Seeing the man so often, and interested always in his manner﻿—as much so, indeed, as the peasants or contadini, who bought his vials and pillboxes without stint﻿—I became interested to know the main features of his life; and, by the aid of a friend, got some clues which I think reliable enough to publish. I do so the more willingly, because his career is illustrative, after an odd fashion, of contemporary Italian life.
He was the son of a small farmer, not far from Sienna, and grew up in daily contact with vinedressers and olive-gatherers, living upon the hard Tuscan fare of macaroni and maroon-nuts, with a cutlet of lean mutton once a day, and a pint of sour Tuscan wine. Being tolerably well educated for a peasant-boy, he imbibed a desire for the profession of an actor, and studied Alfieri closely.
Some little notoriety that he gained by recitations led him, in an evil hour, to venture an appearance *en grand role*, in Florence, at a third-rate theatre. His father had meanwhile deceased and left him the property; but to make the debut referred to, he sold almost his entire inheritance. As may be supposed, his failure was signal. However easy he had found it to amuse the rough, untutored peasantry of his neighborhood, the test of a large and polished city was beyond his merit.
So, poor and abashed, he sank to the lower walks of dramatic art, singing in choruses at the opera, playing minor parts in showpieces, and all the while feeling the sting of disappointed ambition and half-deserved penury.
One day found him, at the beginning of winter, without work, and without a soldo in his pocket. Passing a druggist’s shop, he saw a placard asking for men to sell a certain new preparation. The druggist advanced him a small sum for travelling expenses, and he took to peripatetic lectures at once, going into the country and haranguing at all the villages.
Here he found his dramatic education available. Though not good enough for an actor, he was sufficiently clever for a nomadic eulogizer of a patent-medicine. His vocal abilities were also of service to him in gathering the people together. The great secret of success in anything is to get a hearing. Half the object is gained when the audience is assembled.

===Chinese===

由于经常见到这人，且一直对他的做派饶有兴味——其程度绝不亚于那些毫不吝啬地买下他一瓶瓶药水和一盒盒药丸的乡下农夫——我便动了念头想打听他生平的主要经历；借助一位朋友的帮忙，我弄到了一些自认足够可靠、可以公之于众的线索。我之所以格外乐意写出来，是因为他的人生经历以一种奇特的方式折射出了当代意大利的社会风貌。

他是锡耶纳（Sienna）附近一个小农的儿子，从小天天与葡萄园丁和采橄榄工打交道，吃着托斯卡纳人粗粝的家常饭——通心粉与栗子，外加每天一片瘦羊肉排和一品脱酸涩的托斯卡纳葡萄酒。作为一个农家子弟，他受过相当不错的教育，于是心生当演员的念头，潜心研读阿尔菲耶里（Alfieri）的剧作。

朗诵表演替他赚来的一点点小名气，在某个倒霉的时辰怂恿他大胆闯进佛罗伦萨一家三流剧院，演起了*主角大戏*。在此期间他父亲过世，留给他一笔产业；可为了筹备那场首次登台，他几乎卖光了全部遗产。不出所料，他败得一塌糊涂。在乡邻粗犷无知的农夫面前逗乐固然轻而易举，可大都会挑剔优雅的观众这一关，却远远超出了他的本事。

于是，穷困潦倒又羞愧难当的他，只能沦落到戏剧界的底层谋生，在歌剧院唱合唱，在杂耍剧里跑龙套，整日忍受壮志未酬与半是咎由自取的贫寒煎熬。

入冬伊始的某一天，他丢了工作，兜里连一个索尔多（soldo）铜板也不剩。路过一家药店时，他瞥见门口贴着一张招人兜售某种新制剂的告示。药店老板预支给他一笔微薄盘缠，他立刻干起了巡回演说卖药的行当，下乡走遍各个村庄高谈阔论。

这下，他早年受过的戏剧训练派上了大用场。当正经演员虽不够格，可做个走街串巷吹捧秘方成药的游方演说家却绰绰有余。他的好嗓子在聚拢人气方面也帮了大忙。世上干任何事成功的秘诀，首先在于让人肯听你说话。只要把观众聚齐，事情便已成功了一半。

===Original===

Well! poor, vagabond, peddling Christopher Risk, selling so much for another party, conceived the idea of becoming his own capitalist. He resolved to prepare a medicine of his own; and, profiting by the assistance of a young medical student, obtained bona fide prescriptions for the commonest maladies. These he had made up in gross, originated labels for them, and concealing the real essences thereof by certain harmless adulterations, began to advertise himself as the discoverer of a panacea.
To gain no ill-will among the priests, whose influence is paramount with the peasantry, he dexterously threw in a reverent word for them in his nomadic harangues, and now and then made a sounding present to the Church.
He profited also by the superstitions abroad, and to the skill of Hippocrates added the roguery of Simon Magus. By report, he was both a magician and physician, and a knack that he had of slight-of-hand was not the least influential of his virtues.
His bodily prowess was as great as his suppleness. One day, at Fiesole, a foreign doctor presumed to challenge Monsignore to a debate, and the offer was accepted. While the two stood together in Cristoforo’s wagon, and the intruder was haranguing the people, the quack, without a movement of his face or a twitch of his body, jerked his foot against his rival’s leg and threw him to the ground. He had the effrontery to proclaim the feat as magnetic entirely, accomplished without bodily means, and by virtue of his black-art acquirements.
An awe fell upon the listeners, and they refused to hear the checkmated disputant further.
As soon as Cristoforo began to thrive, he indulged his dramatic taste by purchasing a superb wagon, team, and equipments, and hired a servant. Such a turnout had never been seen in Tuscany since the Medician days. It gained for him the name of Créso straightway, and, enabling him to travel more rapidly, enlarged his business sphere, and so vastly increased his profits.

===Chinese===

好嘛！这个穷困潦倒、四处流浪叫卖的克里斯托弗·冒险替别人卖了这么些货之后，动起了自己当老板的念头。他决心自制一种药剂；托一位年轻医学生的福，他弄到了几张针对最常见病症的正经药方。他把这些药方大批量配制出来，自己设计了商标，用几种无害的辅料遮掩住原本的药味，随后便开始吹嘘自己是包治百病之灵丹的发现者。

为了不得罪神父——神职人员在农民心目中拥有至高无上的影响力——他在四处游方演讲时巧妙地穿插几句敬重神父的话，还不时向教会献上一笔响当当的捐款。

他还充分利用民间盛行的迷信心理，把希波克拉底的医术与术士西门（Simon Magus）的狡诈融为一炉。一时间外头盛传他既是魔术师又是大夫，而他那手戏法绝活更是他诸多“本领”中最具号召力的一招。

他的身手矫健与他的嘴皮子一样了得。有一天在菲耶索莱（Fiesole），一名外国医生竟敢向老爷发起辩论挑战，老爷欣然应战。两人并肩站在克里斯托福罗的马车上，就在那不速之客正对着百姓高谈阔论之际，这个江湖郎中面不改色、身子纹丝不动，暗地里飞起一脚勾住对手小腿，把对方仰面朝天掀翻在地。他竟还厚颜无耻地宣称这一壮举纯粹是凭“磁力”隔空发功，未动一分肉体之力，全仗他深湛的黑魔法造诣。

围观听众顿时敬畏交加，再也不肯听那个一败涂地的挑战者多说半句。

克里斯托福罗刚一发迹，便大肆满足自己的戏剧癖好：购置了一辆极其气派的马车、骏马与全套奢华装备，还雇了一名仆人。自美第奇家族全盛时代以来，托斯卡纳还从未见过这等排场。这立刻替他赢得了“克雷索”（富豪）的绰号，更让他得以加快巡游步伐、拓展生意地盘，利润随之成倍暴涨。

===Original===

He arranged regular days and hours for each place in Tuscany, and soon became as widely known as the Grand Duke himself. When it was known that he had bought an old castle at Pontassieve on the banks of the Arno, his reputation still further increased. He was now so prosperous that he set the faculty at defiance. He proclaimed that they were jealous of his profounder learning, and threatened to expose the banefulness of their systems.
At the same time, his talk to the common people began to savor of patronage, and this also enhanced his reputation. It is much better, as a rule, to call attention up to you rather than charity down to you. The shrewd impostor became also more absolute now. It was known that the Grand Duke had once asked him to dine, and that Monsignore had the hardihood to refuse. Indeed, he sympathized too greatly with the aroused Italian spirit of unity and progress to compromise himself with the house of Austria. When at last the revolution came, Cristoforo was one of its best champions in Tuscany. His cantante sang only the march of Garibaldi and the victories of Savoy. His own speeches teemed with the gospel of Italy regenerated; and for a whole month he wasted no time in the sale of his bottighias and pillolas, but threw all his vehement, persuasive, and dramatic eloquence into the popular cause.
The end we know. Tuscany is a dukedom no longer, but a component part of a great peninsular kingdom with “Florence the Beautiful” for its capital.
And still before the ducal palace, where the deputies of Italy are to assemble, poor, vain Cristoforo Rischio makes his harangue every Tuesday and Saturday. He is now﻿—or was four years ago﻿—upward of sixty years of age, but spirited and athletic as ever, and so rich that it would be superfluous for him to continue his peripatetic career.
His life is to me noteworthy, as showing what may be gained by concentrating even humble energies upon a paltry thing. Had Créso persevered as well upon the stage, I do not doubt that he would have made a splendid actor. If he did so well with a mere nostrum, why should he not have gained riches and a less grotesque fame by the sale of a better article? He understood human nature, its credulities and incredulities, its superstitions, tastes, changefulness, and love of display and excitement. He has done no harm, and given as much amusement as he has been paid for. Indeed, I consider him more an ornamental and useful character than otherwise. He has brightened many a traveler’s recollections, relieved the tedium of many a weary hour in a foreign city, and, with all his deception, has never severed himself from the popular faith, nor sold out the popular cause. I dare say his death, when it occurs, will cause more sensation and evoke more tears, than that of any better physician in Tuscany.

===Chinese===

他为托斯卡纳的每一处市镇都排定了固定的出诊日期与钟点，很快便变得像大公爵本人一样家喻户晓。当人们得知他在阿诺河（Arno）畔的蓬塔谢韦（Pontassieve）买下了一座古堡时，他的声望更是水涨船高。如今的他飞黄腾达，已然公然蔑视正规医界。他宣称同行们是嫉妒他更为渊博的学问，并扬言要彻底揭露他们那套医术体系的毒害。

与此同时，他对平民百姓说话的口吻也带上了几分居高临下的恩赐意味，而这同样抬高了他的身价。照例，让众人仰望你的风光，总比让人俯视可怜你强得多。这位精明的骗子如今越发不可一世。坊间盛传大公爵曾邀他赴宴，而老爷竟有胆量一口回绝。确实，他对意大利觉醒的统一与进步精神怀有太深切的同情，断不肯与奥地利王室同流合污。当革命终于爆发时，克里斯托福罗成了托斯卡纳最卖力的斗士之一。他的歌手只唱加里波第（Garibaldi）进行曲与萨伏伊王朝（Savoy）的胜利赞歌。他自己的演讲也满口都是意大利重生的福音；整整一个月里，他顾不上卖半瓶药水、半盒药丸，把全部炽热、煽动而又富于戏剧张力的雄辩口才尽数投入了民众事业。

结局众人皆知。托斯卡纳不再是个公国，而成了伟大半岛王国的一部分，以“美丽之城佛罗伦萨”为首都。

直到今天，在意大利众议院议员即将集会的公爵宫前，可怜又虚荣的克里斯托福罗·里斯基奥依然每周二、周六准时登台演说。他如今——或者说四年前——已年逾六旬，却依旧精神矍铄、体格健硕，且早已富甲一方，大可不必再过这种江湖卖艺的生涯了。

在我眼里，他的生平颇值一提，因为它证明了哪怕把微薄的精力集中在一件微不足道的小事上，也能闯出多大的名堂。倘若克雷索当年在舞台上也能如此百折不挠，我毫不怀疑他本会成为一名杰出的演员。既然单凭区区江湖假药便能混得如此风生水起，卖一样更正经的商品，岂不照样能赢得财富与没那么怪诞的名声？他深谙人性——懂得人性的轻信与多疑、迷信、趣味、善变，以及对排场与刺激的嗜好。他并未害过人，而且给看客带来的欢愉绝对对得起他收下的钱。说实话，我倒觉得他与其说是祸害，不如说是既装点市井又颇有益处的人物。他点亮了无数旅人的回忆，消解了异乡客多少漫长乏味的时光；尽管满口欺瞒，却从未背离民众的信仰，也从未出卖人民的事业。我敢说，他日他百年归西之际，在托斯卡纳引起的轰动与赚取的眼泪，定然比当地任何一位正经良医辞世时还要多得多。

## Part / 部

===Original===

VI Hoaxes

===Chinese===

第六部 恶作剧与骗局

### XXXI / 第三十一章

===Original===

The Twenty-Seventh Street ghost﻿—​Spirits on ohe rampage.
In classing the ghost excitement that agitated our good people to such an extent some two years ago among the “humbugs” of the age, I must, at the outset, remind my readers that there was no little accumulation of what is termed “respectable” testimony, as to the reality of his ghostship in Twenty-seventh Street.
One fine Sunday morning, in the early part of 1863, my friends of the *Sunday Mercury* astonished their many thousands of patrons with an account that had been brought to them of a fearful spectre that had made its appearance in one of the best houses in Twenty-seventh Street. The narrative was detailed with circumstantial accuracy, and yet with an apparent discreet reserve, that gave the finishing touch of delightful mystery to the story.
The circumstances, as set forth in the opening letter (for many others followed) were briefly these:﻿—A highly respectable family residing on Twenty-seventh Street, one of our handsome uptown thoroughfares, became aware, toward the close of the year 1862, that something extraordinary was taking place in their house, then one of the best in the neighborhood. Sundry mutterings and whisperings began to be heard among the servants employed about the domicile, and, after a little while it became almost impossible to induce them to remain there for love or money. The visitors of the family soon began to notice that their calls, which formerly were so welcome, particularly among the young people of the establishment, seemed to give embarrassment, and that the smiles that greeted them, as early as seven in the evening gradually gave place to uneasy gestures, and, finally to positive hints at the lateness of the hour, or the fatigue of their host by nine o’clock.
The head of the family was a plain, matter-of-fact old gentleman, by no means likely to give way to any superstitious terrors﻿—one of your hardheaded business men who pooh-poohed demons, hobgoblins, and all other kinds of spirits, except the purest Santa Cruz and genuine old Otard; and he fell into a great rage, when upon his repeated gruff demands for an explanation, he was delicately informed that his parlor was “haunted.” He vowed that somebody wanted to drive him from the house; that there was a conspiracy afoot among the women to get him still higher up town, and into a bigger brownstone front, and refused to believe one word of the ghost-story. At length, one day, while sitting in his “growlery,” as the ladies called it, in the lower story, his attention was aroused by a clatter on the stairs, and looking out into the entry he saw a party of carpenters and painters who had been employed upon the parlor-floor, beating a precipitate retreat toward the front door.
“Stop!﻿—stop! you infernal fools! What’s all this hullabaloo about?” shouted the old gentleman.
No reply﻿—no halt upon the part of the mechanics, but away they went down the steps and along the street, as though Satan himself, or Moseby the guerrilla, was at their heels. They were pursued and ordered back, but absolutely refused to come, swearing that they had seen the Evil One, in propria persona; and threats, persuasions, and bribes alike proved vain to induce them to return. This made the matter look serious, and a family-council was held forthwith. It wouldn’t do to let matters go on in this way, and something must be thought of as a remedy. It was in this half-solemn and half-tragic conclave that the paterfamilias was at last put in possession of the mysterious occurrences that had been disturbing the peace of his domestic hearth.

===Chinese===

第二十七街的幽灵——幽灵大闹一场。

在把两年前曾令我们善良市民惶惶不安的那场闹鬼风波归入当代“欺罔”（humbugs）之列前，我首先必须提醒读者：关于第二十七街那位鬼魂阁下的真实存在，当时确实积攒了相当不少所谓的“体面人”证词。

1863 年初某个晴朗的星期天早晨，我在《周日水星报》（*Sunday Mercury*）的几位朋友抛出一则令成千上万订户瞠目结舌的新闻，详述有人向他们报料的一桩骇人幽灵现身事件，地点就在第二十七街一幢最体面的住宅里。这篇报道细节详实精确，偏又带着一副审慎克制的口吻，给整桩故事添上了最后一道令人欲罢不能的神秘色彩。

正如首封来信（随后还有大批来信跟进）所陈述的，事情经过简略如下：1862 年年底前后，住在城北一条漂亮繁华大街——第二十七街——的一户极体面人家，发觉自家那幢当时在邻里间数一数二的宅邸里正发生着非同寻常的怪事。府上雇用的佣人们开始窃窃私语、嘀嘀咕咕，没过多久，无论好言相劝还是加倍给钱，几乎再也留不住任何下人。登门造访的亲友也很快察觉，他们从前颇受欢迎——尤其受家里年轻人欢迎——的拜访，如今似乎令主人颇为尴尬；早在傍晚七点钟迎客时的笑脸，渐渐变成了坐立难安的小动作，直到九点钟便干脆明示天色已晚、主人疲倦。

这家的一家之主是位朴实务实、讲求实际的老绅士，绝不是会向任何迷信恐惧低头的人——他是那种头脑清醒的硬汉商人，对妖魔鬼怪以及一切“灵”（spirits）向来嗤之以鼻，唯独最纯正的圣克鲁斯朗姆酒（Santa Cruz）和货真价实的老奥塔德白兰地（old Otard，英文与“烈酒”双关）除外；当他一再粗声粗气地追问缘由，终于有人委婉告知他家客厅“闹鬼”时，他顿时勃然大怒。他赌咒发誓说准是有人想把他赶出这幢房子；准是女眷们串通一气，想逼他搬到更靠城北、门面更大的褐石豪宅去，对闹鬼之说一个字也不肯信。终于有一天，他正坐在楼下女眷们戏称为“发牢骚间”的书房里，忽然被楼梯上的一阵噼啪乱响惊动；探头往门厅一望，只见一帮原先在客厅地板干活的木匠与油漆工正仓皇失措地朝大门夺路而逃。

“站住！站住！你们这帮该死的蠢货！这么大呼小叫地闹什么？”老绅士怒吼道。

这帮工匠头也不回、步也不停，飞奔下台阶沿着大街狂飙而去，活像撒旦亲自出马、或是游击队长莫斯比（Moseby）正紧追在他们屁股后头。老绅士派人追上去喝令他们回来，可他们死活不肯回头，赌咒发誓说自己亲眼见到了“恶魔本尊”；威逼、利诱、劝说统统无济于事。这下事情显得严重了，家里立刻召开家庭会议。放任事态这样发展下去可不成，必须想个法子应付。正是在这次半是肃穆、半是悲壮的闭门密议上，这位一家之主终于被告知了连日来搅得他家宅不宁的种种神秘怪事。

===Original===

A ghost had been repeatedly seen in his best drawing-room!﻿—a genuine, undeniable, unmitigated ghost!
The spectre was described by the female members of the family as making his appearance at all hours, chiefly, however in the evening, of course. Now the good old orthodox idea of a ghost is, of a very long, cadaverous, ghastly personage, of either sex, appearing in white draperies, with uplifted finger, and attended or preceded by sepulchral sounds﻿—whist! hush! and sometimes the rattling of casements and the jingling of chains. A bluish glare and a strong smell of brimstone seldom failed to enhance the horror of the scene. This ghost, however, came it seems, in more ordinary guise, but none the less terrible for his natural style of approach and costume. He was usually seen in the front parlor, which was on the second story and faced the street. There he would be found seated in a chair near the fire place, his attire the garb of a carman or “carter” and hence the name “Carter’s Ghost” afterward frequently applied to him. There he would sit entirely unmoved by the approach of living denizens of the house, who, at first, would suppose that he was some drunken or insane intruder, and only discover their mistake as they drew near, and saw the firelight shining through him, and notice the glare of his frightful eyes, which threatened all comers in a most unearthly way. Such was the purport of the first sketch that appeared in the *Sunday Mercury*, stated so distinctly and impressively that the effect could not fail to be tremendous among our sensational public. To help the matter, another brief notice, to the same effect, appeared in the Sunday issue of a leading journal on the same morning. The news dealers and street-carriers caught up the novelty instanter, and before noon not a copy of the *Sunday Mercury* could be bought in any direction. The country issue of the *Sunday Mercury* had still a larger sale.
On Sunday morning, every sheet in town made some allusion to the Ghost, and many even went so far as to give the very (supposed) number of the house favored with his visitations. The result of this enterprising guess was ludicrous enough, bordering a little, too, upon the serious. Indignant householders rushed down to the *Sunday Mercury* office with the most amusing wrath, threatening and denouncing the astonished publishers with all sorts of legal action for their presumed trespass, when in reality, their paper had designated no place or person at all. But the grandest demonstration of popular excitement was revealed in Twenty-seventh Street itself. Before noon a considerable portion of the thoroughfare below Sixth Avenue was blocked up with a dense mass of people of all ages, sizes, sexes, and nationalities, who had come “to see the Ghost.” A liquor store or two, near by, drove a splendid “spiritual” business; and by evening “the fun” grew so “fast and furious” that a whole squad of police had to be employed to keep the sidewalks and even the carriageway clear. The “Ghost” was shouted for to make a speech, like any other new celebrity, and old ladies and gentlemen peering out of upper-story windows were saluted with playful tokens of regard, such as turnips, eggs of ancient date, and other things too numerous to mention, from the crowd. Nor was the throng composed entirely of Gothamites. The surrounding country sent in its contingent. They came on foot, on horseback, in wagons, and arrayed in all the costumes known about these parts, since the days of Rip Van Winkle. Cruikshanks would have made a fortune from his easy sketches of only a few figures in the scene. And thus the concourse continued for days together, arriving at early morn and staying there in the street until “dewy eve.”

===Chinese===

他家最气派的会客厅里屡次三番出现了幽灵！——一个货真价实、无可否认、不折不扣的幽灵！

据家中女眷描述，这幽灵在任何钟点都可能现身，当然主要还是在夜晚。照传统正统的鬼魂印象，幽灵多半是个身材修长、面如死灰、形容枯槁的人物，不分男女，披着白色裹尸布，竖起一根手指，伴随或先导着阴森森的坟墓声响——嘘！噤声！有时还夹杂着窗扇嘎吱作响与铁链叮当拖曳；幽蓝的鬼火与刺鼻的硫磺臭味更是极少缺席，以烘托恐怖气氛。然而，这位幽灵现身时打扮却寻常得多，可他那自然无奇的出场方式与衣着反倒丝毫不减其骇人程度。人们通常在二楼临街的前客厅里瞧见他：他就那么大喇喇地坐在壁炉旁的一张椅子上，身上穿着马车夫或“拉货车夫”（carter）的粗布衣服——因此后来世人便常常管他叫“车夫幽灵”（Carter’s Ghost）。哪怕家里活生生的住户走近，他也纹丝不动地坐在那儿；起初众人还以为是哪个醉汉或疯子闯了进来，直到走近些，猛然看见炉火的光芒竟穿透了他的身躯，又瞧见他那双骇人眼珠射出阴森诡异的凶光威慑着所有来人，方才惊觉大错特错。《周日水星报》头一篇报道的大意便是如此，叙述得清晰逼真、令人毛骨悚然，在我们这群嗜好猎奇的公众当中自然掀起了惊涛骇浪。更巧的是，同一天早晨另一家主流大报的周日版也登了一则内容相仿的简讯。报贩与街头送报童立刻敏锐地嗅到了商机，不到正午，《周日水星报》便在全城各处抢购一空。而《周日水星报》的乡下版销量更是翻倍暴涨。

那个星期天早上，全城每一张报纸都提到了这只幽灵，好几家甚至胆大包天地登出了据信闹鬼那幢宅子的确切门牌号。这番大胆猜测的结果滑稽至极，甚至有点儿闹大了。怒不可遏的户主们气急败坏地冲进《周日水星报》报社，以最令人发笑的盛怒威胁并痛斥吓蒙了的出版商，扬言要就这种无端侵犯提出各种法律诉讼——殊不知《水星报》压根儿从未点名过任何地址或人物。不过，民众狂热最壮观的场面还是发生在第二十七街本街。不到正午，第六大道以下的一大段街面便被各色人等挤得水泄不通——男女老少、高矮胖瘦、各国侨民齐聚一堂，全都是“来看幽灵的”。附近一两家酒馆趁机做起了一笔红火无比的“灵魂（烈酒）”生意；到了傍晚，“乐子”越闹越欢实失控，当局不得不出动一整队警察来维持人行道乃至车行道的畅通。人群像起哄刚出炉的名人一样高喊着要“幽灵”出来发表演讲，而探出楼上窗户张望的老太太老绅士们，则纷纷收获了人群抛来的俏皮致敬礼——诸如芜菁、陈年臭鸡蛋以及多到数不清的杂物。这股人潮也并非全由纽约市民构成，周边乡下也派来了大批人马：步行的、骑马的、坐马车的，身上穿着自瑞普·凡·温克尔（Rip Van Winkle）时代以来这一带见过的所有奇装异服。漫画家克鲁克香克（Cruikshanks）只要随手速写这场景里的几个人物，就足以发一笔大财。就这样，人群一连聚集了好几天，清晨赶来，一直守在街上直到“露水降临的黄昏”。

===Original===

As a matter of course, there were various explanations of the story propounded by various people﻿—all wondrously wise in their own conceit. Some would have it that “the Ghost” was got up by some of the neighbors, who wished, in this manner, to drive away disreputable occupants; others insisted that it was the revenge of an ousted tenant, etc., etc. Everybody offered his own theory, and, as is usual, in such cases, nobody was exactly right.
Meanwhile, the *Sunday Mercury* continued its publications of the further progress of the “mystery,” from week to week, for a space of nearly two months, until the whole country seemed to have gone ghost-mad. Apparitions and goblins dire were seen in Washington, Rochester, Albany, Montreal, and other cities.
The spiritualists took it up and began to discuss “the Carter Ghost” with the utmost zeal. One startling individual﻿—a physician and a philosopher﻿—emerged from his professional shell into full-fledged glory, as the greatest canard of all, and published revelations of his own intermediate intercourse with the terrific “Carter.” In every nook and corner of the land, tremendous posters, in white and yellow, broke out upon the walls and windows of news-depots, with capitals a foot long, and exclamation-points like drumsticks, announcing fresh installments of the “Ghost” story, and it was a regular fight between go-ahead vendors who should get the next batch of horrors in advance of his rivals.
Nor was the effect abroad the least feature of this stupendous “sell.” The English, French, and German press translated some of the articles in epitome, and wrote grave commentaries thereon. The stage soon caught the blaze; and Professor Pepper, at the Royal Polytechnic Institute, in London, invented a most ingenious device for producing ghosts which should walk about upon the stage in such a perfectly-astounding manner as to throw poor Hamlet’s father and the evil genius of Brutus quite into the “shade.” “Pepper’s Ghost” soon crossed the Atlantic, and all our theatres were speedily alive with nocturnal apparitions. The only real ghosts, however﻿—four in number﻿—came out at the Museum, in an appropriate drama, which had an immense run﻿—“all for twenty-five cents,” or only six and a quarter cents per ghost!

===Chinese===

顺理成章地，各路人等纷纷抛出了五花八门的解释——个个自以为高深莫测。有人言之凿凿地说，“幽灵”是某些邻居炮制出来的，意在借此赶走声名狼藉的住户；另一些人则坚持说，这是一个被赶走的房客实施的报复，如此等等。人人都端出自己的一套理论，而正如这类事向来如此，没一个人真正说中。

与此同时，《周日水星报》每周一期刊登这桩“悬案”的新进展，接连连载了将近两个月，直到全国上下仿佛都陷入了“幽灵疯”。华盛顿、罗切斯特、奥尔巴尼、蒙特利尔及其他城市纷纷传出有人目击鬼魂与凶恶妖魔的消息。

招魂论者也凑上来，满怀热忱地大肆讨论“车夫幽灵”。其中一位惊世骇俗的人物——集医生与哲学家头衔于一身——干脆破壳而出，化作整场闹剧里最荒诞的一只大野鸭，公开发表自己与恐怖“车夫”直接通灵交往的启示录。全国各地的每个角落、报刊亭的墙壁与橱窗上，都贴满了硕大的白底黄字海报，上面印着一英尺高的粗大字母和鼓槌般粗壮的感叹号，预告“幽灵”故事的下一期连载；进取心十足的报贩们简直打成一团，争抢着比对手更早拿到下一批惊悚猛料。

这场惊天大“忽悠”在海外引发的轰动同样不容小觑。英国、法国与德国报界节译了其中几篇文章，并一本正经地撰写了严肃评论。戏剧舞台很快也燃起了这把火；伦敦皇家理工学院的佩珀教授（Professor Pepper）发明了一套极其精巧的装置，能在舞台上变出自由行走的鬼魂，逼真震撼到把可怜的哈姆雷特他爹和布鲁图斯的恶灵都比得黯然失色。“佩珀鬼魂”（Pepper’s Ghost）很快跨越大西洋，我们这儿所有剧院顿时鬼影幢幢。不过，真正货真价实的鬼魂——总共四只——还是在我的博物馆里登台的，演了一出应景好戏，连演不衰、场场爆满——“统统只要二十五美分”，折合每只鬼才六又四分之一美分！

===Original===

But I must not forget to say that, really, the details given in the *Sunday Mercury* were well calculated to lead captive a large class of minds prone to luxuriate in the marvelous when well mixed with plausible reasoning. The most circumstantial accounts were given of sundry “gifted young ladies,” “grave and learned professors,” “reliable gentlemen”﻿—where are those not found?﻿—“lonely watchers,” and others, who had sought interviews with the “ghost,” to their own great enlightenment, indeed, but, likewise, complete discomfiture. Pistols were fired at him, pianos played and songs sung for him, and, finally, his daguerreotype taken on prepared metallic plates set upright in the haunted room. One shrewd artist brought out an “exact photographic likeness” of the distinguished stranger on cartes de visite, and made immense sales. The apparitions, too, multiplied. An old man, a woman, and a child made their appearance in the house of wonders, and, at last, a gory head with distended eyeballs, swimming in a sea of blood, upon a platter﻿—like that of Holofernes﻿—capped the climax.
Certain wiseacres here began to see political allusions in the Ghost, and many actually took the whole affair to be a cunningly devised political satire upon this or that party, according as their sympathies swayed them.
It would have been a remarkable portion of “this strange, eventful history,” of course, if “Barnum” could have escaped the accusation of being its progenitor.
I was continually beset, and frequently, when more than usually busy, thoroughly annoyed by the innuendoes of my visitors, that I was the father of “the Ghost.”
“Come, now, Mr. Barnum﻿—this is going a little too far!” some good old dame or grandfather would say to me. “You oughtn’t to scare people in this way. These ghosts are ugly customers!”
“My dear Sir,” or “Madam,” I would say, as the case might be, “I do assure you I know nothing whatever about the Ghost﻿—and as for ‘spirits,’ you know I never touch them, and have been preaching against them nearly all my life.”
“Well! well! you will have the last turn,” they’d retort, as they edged away; “but you needn’t tell us. We guess we’ve found the ghost.”
Now, all I can add about this strange hallucination is, that those who came to me to see the original “Carter,” really saw the “Elephant.”
The wonderful apparition disappeared, at length, as suddenly as he had come. The “Bull’s-Eye Brigade,” as the squad of police put on duty to watch the neighborhood, for various reasons, was termed, hung to their work, and flashed the light of their lanterns into the faces of lonely couples, for some time afterward; but quiet, at length, settled down over all: and it has been it seems, reserved for my pen to record briefly the history of “The Twenty-seventh Street Ghost.”

===Chinese===

不过我必须公道地说一句，《周日水星报》编造的那些细节确实妙手偶得，最能俘获一大批原本就爱在看似合情合理的推理中沉醉于奇闻异事的头脑。报道里绘声绘色地记述了形形色色的“天赋异禀的年轻女士”、“严肃博学的教授”、“可靠绅士”（世上哪儿找不着这种人呢？）、“孤身守夜人”以及其他人物，他们纷纷前去求见“幽灵”，结果固然大开眼界，却也无不狼狈收场。有人朝他开手枪，有人替他弹钢琴、唱小曲，最后甚至把涂好药膜的金属版立在闹鬼房间里，替他拍下了银版照。一位精明的画师趁机推出这位显赫神秘来客的“分毫不差肖像名片照”（cartes de visite），销量惊人。显灵的鬼魂也越变越多：那幢奇宅里接连冒出一位老人、一名女子和一个孩子，最后竟端出了一颗眼珠暴突、漂浮在盘中血海里的血淋淋人头——宛如霍洛费尔尼斯（Holofernes）的首级——把整出戏推向了最高潮。

某些自作聪明的人此刻开始从幽灵身上看出政治隐喻，不少人甚至依各自党派立场，当真把整件事当作针对这个党或那个党的精妙政治讽刺。

当然，如果在这段“离奇曲折的历史”中，“巴纳姆”能侥幸逃脱幕后黑手的指控，那才叫见鬼了。

我整天被团团围住，平日忙得不可开交时更是烦不胜烦——来客们个个含沙射影，认定我就是“那只幽灵”的生身老爹。

“得了吧，巴纳姆先生——这回可有点儿过火了！”某位好心的老太太或老爷爷总爱这么数落我，“您不该这么吓唬老百姓。这帮鬼魂可不好惹！”

“我亲爱的先生，”或者“夫人”，我总是视来者而定答道，“我向您担保，关于那只幽灵我实在是一无所知——至于说到‘烈酒（spirits，与‘鬼魂’双关）’，您知道我向来滴酒不沾，而且大半辈子都在公开宣讲反对它呢。”

“好吧！好吧！您这张嘴总归占理，”他们一面往外挪步一面回敬道，“可您也不用瞒着我们。我们猜自己早已找着真正的幽灵啦。”

关于这场离奇的集体幻觉，我眼下唯一能补充的是：那些跑来我这儿想看正牌“车夫”的人，倒是实打实地“开了眼界（saw the Elephant，美国俚语，意为见识了大场面或上了当）”。

终于，那位神奇的幽灵如同他骤然降临一般，悄无声息地消失了。出于种种缘由被派驻该街区巡逻的警队——人称“牛眼灯警队”（Bull’s-Eye Brigade）——此后又坚守岗位了好一阵子，时不时拿手提灯往幽会情侣脸上照一照；但最终，一切终于重归宁静：而简略记下“第二十七街幽灵”这段历史的重任，似乎便留给了我的笔端。

### XXXII / 第三十二章

===Original===

The moon-hoax.
The most stupendous scientific imposition upon the public that the generation with which we are numbered has known, was the so-called “Moon-Hoax,” published in the columns of the *New York Sun*, in the months of August and September, 1835. The sensation created by this immense imposture, not only throughout the United States, but in every part of the civilized world, and the consummate ability with which it was written, will render it interesting so long as our language shall endure; and, indeed, astronomical science has actually been indebted to it for many most valuable hints﻿—a circumstance that gives the production a still higher claim to immortality.
At the period when the wonderful “yarn” to which I allude first appeared, the science of astronomy was engaging particular attention, and all works on the subject were eagerly bought up and studied by immense masses of people. The real discoveries of the younger Herschel, whose fame seemed destined to eclipse that of the elder sage of the same name, and the eloquent startling works of Dr. Dick, which the Harpers were republishing, in popular form, from the English edition, did much to increase and keep up this peculiar mania of the time, until the whole community at last were literally occupied with but little else than “stargazing.” Dick’s works on “The Sidereal Heavens,” “Celestial Scenery,” “The improvement of Society,” etc., were read with the utmost avidity by rich and poor, old and young, in season and out of season. They were quoted in the parlor, at the table, on the promenade, at church, and even in the bedroom, until it absolutely seemed as though the whole community had “Dick” upon the brain. To the highly educated and imaginative portion of our good Gothamite population, the Doctor’s glowing periods, full of the grandest speculations as to the starry worlds around us, their wondrous magnificence and ever-varying aspects of beauty and happiness were inexpressibly fascinating. The author’s well-reasoned conjectures as to the majesty and beauty of their landscapes, the fertility and diversity of their soil, and the exalted intelligence and comeliness of their inhabitants, found hosts of believers; and nothing else formed the staple of conversation, until the beaux and belles, and dealers in small talk generally, began to grumble, and openly express their wishes that the Dickens had Doctor Dick and all his works.
It was at the very height of the furor above mentioned, that one morning the readers of the *Sun*﻿—at that time only twenty-five hundred in number﻿—were thrilled with the announcement in its columns of certain “Great Astronomical Discoveries Lately Made by Sir John Herschel, LL.D., F.R.S. etc., at the Cape of Good Hope,” purporting to be a republication from a Supplement to the *Edinburgh Journal of Science*. The heading of the article was striking enough, yet was far from conveying any adequate idea of its contents. When the latter became known, the excitement went beyond all bounds, and grew until the *Sun* office was positively besieged with crowds of people of the very first class, vehemently applying for copies of the issue containing the wonderful details.
As the pamphlet form in which the narrative was subsequently published is now out of print, and a copy can hardly be had in the country, I will recall a few passages from a rare edition, for the gratification of my friends who have never seen the original. Indeed, the whole story is altogether too good to be lost; and it is a great pity that we can not have a handsome reprint of it given to the world from time to time. It is constantly in demand; and, during the year 1859, a single copy of sixty pages, sold at the auction of Mr. Haswell’s library, brought the sum of $3.75. In that same year, a correspondent, in Wisconsin, writing to the *Sunday Times* of this city, inquired where the book could be procured, and was answered that he could find it at the old bookstore, No. 85 Center Street, if anywhere. Thus, after a search of many weeks, the Western bibliopole succeeded in obtaining a well-thumbed specimen of the precious work. Acting upon this chance suggestion, Mr. William Gowans, of this city, during the same year, brought out a very neat edition, in paper covers, illustrated with a view of the moon, as seen through Lord Rosse’s grand telescope, in 1856. But this, too, has all been sold; and the most indefatigable book-collector might find it difficult to purchase a single copy at the present time. I, therefore, render the inquiring reader no slight service in culling for him some of the flowers from this curious astronomical garden.

===Chinese===

月亮骗局。

我们这一代人所见证过的、针对公众的最庞大科学骗局，莫过于 1835 年 8 月和 9 月间连载于《纽约太阳报》（*New York Sun*）专栏的所谓“月亮骗局”（Moon-Hoax）。这场惊天伪作不仅在合众国全境、更在整个文明世界掀起了轩然大波；其文笔之炉火纯青，注定使之只要英语尚存于世便永远饶有趣味；事实上，天文学界甚至确曾从中获益良多，汲取了不少极有价值的启示——这一事实更赋予该作品不朽的地位。

当我所提及的这则奇谈初现报端之际，天文学正受到世人异乎寻常的关注，一切有关该学科的著作皆被大批民众争相抢购、潜心研读。小赫歇尔（younger Herschel）的真实科学发现——他的声名似乎注定要盖过同姓的前辈泰斗——加上哈珀兄弟出版社（Harpers）正依据英国版以通俗平价形式重印的迪克博士（Dr. Dick）那几部文采斐然、惊世骇俗的著作，大大助推并维系了当时这股独特的狂热，直到最终整个社会几乎除了“仰望星空”之外别无他事。无论贫富老幼、不分昼夜时令，人人都如饥似渴地捧读迪克所著的《恒星天宇》、《天界奇观》、《社会之改良》等书。客厅里、餐桌上、林荫道上、教堂中乃至卧室里，处处有人引经据典，仿佛整座城市的脑瓜里都装满了“迪克”。对于我们善良纽约市民中受过良好教育且富于想象力的阶层而言，博士那华丽饱满的排比句——满篇尽是对环绕我们的星辰世界、其奇妙壮丽以及变幻无穷的美与幸福的宏大畅想——具有难以言喻的魔力。作者关于外星地貌之雄浑秀美、土壤之肥沃多样、居民之智慧高洁与容貌俊美所作的条理分明的推论，赢得了成千上万的信徒；人们茶余饭后几乎无一例外只谈这个话题，直到城里的纨绔少爷、名媛淑女以及惯常闲扯的市井之徒纷纷抱怨起来，公然盼着见鬼去吧——把迪克博士连同他的全套大作统统打包送给阎王。

恰在上述狂热攀至顶点之际，一天早晨，《太阳报》的读者——当时订户还只有两千五百人——被报端一则惊人消息震得心潮澎湃，标题赫然是“约翰·赫歇尔爵士（Sir John Herschel, LL.D., F.R.S. 等）近来于好望角取得之伟大天文发现”，号称转载自《爱丁堡科学杂志》（*Edinburgh Journal of Science*）增刊。文章标题固然已够惊人，却远不足以概括内文之震撼。待内文传开，全城狂热顿时失控，愈演愈烈，直到《太阳报》报社被头等体面阶层的人潮围得水泄不通，众人争先恐后抢购载有这些神奇细节的那期报纸。

由于该报道随后印行的小册子早已绝版，眼下国内几乎一册难求，我姑且从一部珍稀版本中摘录几段，以飨从未见过原著的朋友们。说真的，整篇故事实在精彩得不容湮没；世人未能时不时重印一部精装本以飨大众，实乃一大憾事。它向来备受追捧； 1859 年，哈斯韦尔先生（Mr. Haswell）藏书拍卖会上，区区一本六十页的单行本竟拍出了 3.75 美元的高价。同年，威斯康星州的一位读者致信本市《星期日泰晤士报》（*Sunday Times*），打听何处能买到此书，得到的答复是：若说世上还有地方找得到，那便是中央街 85 号的旧书店。于是，历经数周苦寻，那位西部藏书家果真淘到了一本翻得卷了边的珍本。受此机缘启发，本市的威廉·高恩斯先生（Mr. William Gowans）于同年推出了一版极其精致的平装本，书内还配有 1856 年透过罗斯勋爵（Lord Rosse）巨型望远镜所见月球景致的插图。然而这一版也早已售罄；哪怕最孜孜不倦的藏书家，如今恐怕也难以买到一册。因此，我从这座奇趣盎然的天文花园里替求知若渴的读者采撷几朵奇葩，倒也不失为一桩美事。

===Original===

The opening of the narrative was in the highest *Review* style; and the majestic, yet subdued, dignity of its periods, at once claimed respectful attention; while its perfect candor, and its wealth of accurate scientific detail exacted the homage of belief from all but cross-grained and inexorable skeptics.
It commences thus:

> “In this unusual addition to our Journal, we have the happiness to make known to the British public, and thence to the whole civilized world, recent discoveries in Astronomy, which will build an imperishable monument to the age in which we live, and confer upon the present generation of the human race a proud distinction through all future time. It has been poetically said, that the stars of heaven are the hereditary regalia of man, as the intellectual sovereign of the animal creation. He may now fold the Zodiac around him with a loftier consciousness of his mental superiority,” etc., etc.

The writer then eloquently descanted upon the sublime achievement by which man pierced the bounds that hemmed him in, and with sensations of awe approached the revelations of his own genius in the far-off heavens, and with intense dramatic effect described the younger Herschel surpassing all that his father had ever attained; and by some stupendous apparatus about to unveil the remotest mysteries of the sidereal space, pausing for many hours ere the excess of his emotions would allow him to lift the veil from his own overwhelming success.
I must quote a line or two of this passage, for it capped the climax of public curiosity:

> “Well might he pause! He was about to become the sole depository of wondrous secrets which had been hid from the eyes of all men that had lived since the birth of time. He was about to crown himself with a diadem of knowledge which would give him a conscious preeminence above every individual of his species who then lived or who had lived in the generations that are passed away. He paused ere he broke the seal of the casket that contained it.”

Was not this introduction enough to stimulate the wonder bump of all the stargazers, until

> “Each particular hair did stand on end,
> 
> Like quills upon the fretful porcupine?”

At all events, such was the effect, and it was impossible at first to supply the frantic demand, even of the city, not to mention the country readers.

===Chinese===

这篇奇文的开篇颇具顶级《评论》杂志的大家风范；行文雄浑庄严而又不失克制典雅，甫一落笔便令读者肃然起敬；而其坦诚无隐的口吻与丰沛精确的科学细节，更令除却少数顽固执拗、铁石心肠的怀疑派之外的所有人五体投地、深信不疑。

文章开篇如是写道：

> “在为本刊增补的这期特辑中，我们有幸向英国公众、并进而向整个文明世界公布天文学领域近来取得之重大发现；此等发现必将为我们所生活的时代树立一座不朽丰碑，并赋予当代人类一份垂诸后世的无上荣光。前人曾诗意地赞叹，苍穹繁星乃是人类作为万物灵长、智识主宰的世袭王权徽章。而今，人类更得以怀着愈发崇高的智识优越感，将黄道十二宫披作自己的袍服……”云云。

随后，作者以生花妙笔铺陈人类如何冲破自身局限的藩篱，怀着敬畏之心在遥远天际迎向自身天才所揭示的奥秘；继而以极富戏剧张力的笔触，描绘小赫歇尔如何超越其父生前一切成就，正欲借助某种惊天动地的仪器揭开恒星深空最幽微的奥秘，却因心潮澎湃难平，竟在掀开自己那惊人成就的面纱前伫立良久、久久不敢动手。

这段文字我非引用一两句不可，因为它把公众的好奇心吊到了最高点：

> “他确实该停下来深思！自时间诞生以来，世间一切生灵的双眼从未窥见过的奇妙天机，此刻即将由他一人独掌。他即将为自己戴上一顶知识的皇冠，使他清醒地感受到自己已凌驾于当代乃至过往历代一切同类之上。在揭开盛装这无上秘宝的宝匣封印之前，他停下了脚步。”

试问这样的开场白，岂不足以撩拨起所有观星迷头颅上的好奇瘤，直到叫他们：

> “每一根头发都竖立起来，
> 
> 宛如受惊豪猪身上的棘刺”？

无论如何，效果确实如此；起初光是应付本城读者疯狂的购报需求便已捉襟见肘，更不用提外地乡间的订户了。

===Original===

I may very briefly sum up the outline of the discoveries alleged to have been made, in a few paragraphs, so as not to protract the suspense of my readers too long.
It was claimed that the *Edinburgh Journal* was indebted for its information to Doctor Andrew Grant﻿—a savant of celebrity, who had, for very many years, been the scientific companion, first of the elder and subsequently of the younger Herschel, and had gone with the latter in September, 1834, to the Cape of Good Hope, whither he had been sent by the British Government, acting in conjunction with the Governments of France and Austria, to observe the transit of Mercury over the disc of the sun﻿—an astronomical point of great importance to the lunar observations of longitude, and consequently to the navigation of the world. This transit was not calculated to occur before the 7th of November, 1835 (the year in which the hoax was printed;) but Sir John Herschel set out nearly a year in advance, for the purpose of thoroughly testing a new and stupendous telescope devised by himself under this peculiar inspiration, and infinitely surpassing anything of the kind ever before attempted by mortal man. It has been discovered by previous astronomers and among others, by Herschel’s illustrious father, that the sidereal object becomes dim in proportion as it is magnified, and that, beyond a certain limit, the magnifying power is consequently rendered almost useless. Thus, an impassable barrier seemed to lie in the way of future close observation, unless some means could be devised to illuminate the object to the eye. By intense research and the application of all recent improvements in optics, Sir John had succeeded in securing a beautiful and perfectly lighted image of the moon with a magnifying power that increased its apparent size in the heavens six thousand times. Dividing the distance of the moon from the Earth, viz.: 240,000 miles, by six thousand, we have forty miles as the distance at which she would then seem to be seen; and as the elder Herschel, with a magnifying power, only one thousand, had calculated that he could distinguish an object on the moon’s surface not more than 122 yards in diameter, it was clear that his son, with six times the power, could see an object there only twenty-two yards in diameter. But, for any further advance in power and light, the way seemed insuperably closed until a profound conversation with the great savant and optician, Sir David Brewster, led Herschel to suggest to the latter the idea of the readoption of the old fashioned telescopes, without tubes, which threw their images upon reflectors in a dark apartment, and then the illumination of these images by the intense hydro-oxygen light used in the ordinary illuminated microscope. At this suggestion, Brewster is represented by the veracious chronicler as leaping with enthusiasm from his chair, exclaiming in rapture to Herschel:
“Thou art the man!”
The suggestion, thus happily approved, was immediately acted upon, and a subscription, headed by that liberal patron of science, the Duke of Sussex, with £10,000, was backed by the reigning King of England with his royal word for any sum that might be needed to make up £70,000, the amount required. No time was lost; and, after one or two failures, in January 1833, the house of Hartley & Grant, at Dumbarton, succeeded in casting the huge object-glass of the new apparatus, measuring twenty-four feet (or six times that of the elder Herschel’s glass) in diameter; weighing 14,826 pounds, or nearly seven tons, after being polished, and possessing a magnifying power of 42,000 times!﻿—a perfectly pure, spotless, achromatic lens, without a material bubble or flaw!

===Chinese===

为了不让读者诸君久悬心头，我用几段简短文字来概括一下这番号称取得的发现大纲。

文章宣称，《爱丁堡科学杂志》的消息得自著名学者安德鲁·格兰特博士（Doctor Andrew Grant）——此人多年来先是担任老赫歇尔、继而担任小赫歇尔的科学助手，并于 1834 年 9 月随小赫歇尔一同前往好望角；彼时英国政府联手法国与奥地利政府，派遣他前去观测水星凌日的天象——这一天文观测对于月球经度测算、进而对全球航海事业均具有极其重大的意义。经推算，这次水星凌日要到 1835 年（即骗局刊出当年）11 月 7 日才会发生；但约翰·赫歇尔爵士却提前将近一年动身，只为彻底检验一台由他自己在独特灵感启迪下亲自设计、远远超越凡人此前任何尝试的超级巨型望远镜。前代天文学家——其中包括赫歇尔声名显赫的父亲——早已发现，天体被放大的倍数越高，影像便越显昏暗，因此超过某一极限后，放大倍率几乎毫无用处。如此一来，除非能想出某种法子替人眼照亮被观测的星体，否则日后近距离精细观测的道路似乎便被彻底堵死。经过潜心钻研并运用光学领域的一切最新成果，约翰爵士已成功获取了一幅清晰明亮、美轮美奂的月球影像，其放大倍率将月球在天穹中的视尺寸放大了六千倍。用月地距离 240,000 英里除以六千，便得出四十英里——这相当于把月球拉近到区区四十英里外加以审视；而当年老赫歇尔仅凭一千倍放大倍率，便推算出自己能分辨月球表面直径不超过 122 码的物体，由此显而易见，他儿子凭借六倍于此的倍率，便能看清月球上直径仅二十二码的物体。然而，要想在倍率与亮度上更进一步，前路似乎已被死死封住——直到在与伟大科学家兼光学大师大卫·布鲁斯特爵士（Sir David Brewster）的一席深谈后，赫歇尔向对方提出了一个构想：重新启用无镜筒的老式望远镜，将影像投射到暗室中的反光镜上，再借用寻常照明显微镜所用的强烈氢氧焰光去照亮这些影像。据这位“忠实”的编年史作者描述，听到这一提议，布鲁斯特激昂地从椅子上跳起身来，狂喜地对着赫歇尔高呼：

“你就是那个人！”

这一既获欣然赞同的构想立刻付诸实施；以慷慨赞助科学著称的萨塞克斯公爵（Duke of Sussex）带头捐出 10,000 英镑，现任英王陛下更以御口金言担保，无论凑足所需 70,000 英镑总额尚差多少，王室一力补齐。事不宜迟；经历一两次失败后， 1833 年 1 月，邓巴顿的哈特利与格兰特铸造厂（Hartley & Grant）终于成功浇铸出这台新仪器的巨型物镜，直径达二十四英尺（相当于老赫歇尔镜片的六倍），抛光后重达 14,826 磅（将近七吨），拥有高达 42,000 倍的放大威力！——而且还是一块纯净无瑕、毫无色差、找不到半个气泡或瑕疵的完美透镜！

===Original===

Of course, after so elaborate a description of so astounding a result as this, the *Edinburg Scientific Journal* (i.e., the writer in the *New York Sun*) could not avoid being equally precise in reference to subsequent details, and he proceeded to explain that Sir JohnHerschel and his amazing apparatus having been selected by the Board of Longitude to observe the transit of Mercury, the Cape of Good Hope was chosen because, upon the former expedition to Peru, acting in conjunction with one to Lapland, which was sent out for the same purpose in the eighteenth century, it had been noticed that the attraction of the mountainous regions deflected the plumb-line of the large instruments seven or eight seconds from the perpendicular, and, consequently, greatly impaired the enterprise. At the Cape, on the contrary, there was a magnificent tableland of vast expanse, where this difficulty could not occur. Accordingly, on the 4th of September, 1834, with a design to become perfectly familiar with the working of his new gigantic apparatus, and with the Southern Constellations, before the period of his observations of Mercury, Sir John Herschel sailed from London, accompanied by Doctor Grant (the supposed informant,) Lieutenant Drummond, of the Royal Engineers, F.R.A.S., and a large party of the best English workmen. On their arrival at the Cape, the apparatus was conveyed, in four days’ time, to the great elevated plain, thirty-five miles to the N.E. of Cape Town, on trains drawn by two relief-teams of oxen, eighteen to a team, the ascent aided by gangs of Dutch boors. For the details of the huge fabric in which the lens and its reflectors were set up, I must refer the curious reader to the pamphlet itself﻿—not that the presence of the “Dutch boors” alarms me at all, since we have plenty of boors at home, and one gets used to them in the course of time, but because the elaborate scientific description of the structure would make most readers see “stars” in broad daylight before they get through.
I shall only go on to say that, by the 10th of January, everything was complete, even to the two pillars “one hundred and fifty feet high!” that sustained the lens. Operations then commenced forthwith, and so, too, did the “special wonder” of the readers. It is a matter of congratulation to mankind that the writer of the hoax, with an apology (Heaven save the mark!) spared us Herschel’s notes of “the Moon’s tropical, sidereal, and synodic revolutions,” and the “phenomena of the syzygies,” and proceeded at once to the pith of the subject. Here came in his grand stroke, informing the world of complete success in obtaining a distinct view of objects in the moon “fully equal to that which the unaided eye commands of terrestrial objects at the distance of a hundred yards, affirmatively settling the question whether the satellite be inhabited, and by what order of beings,” “firmly establishing a new theory of cometary phenomena,” etc., etc. This announcement alone was enough to take one’s breath away, but when the green marble shores of the *Mare Nubium*; the mountains shaped like pyramids, and of the purest and most dazzling crystalized, wine-colored amethyst, dotting green valleys skirted by “round-breasted hills;” summits of the purest vermilion fringed with arching cascades and buttresses of white marble glistening in the sun﻿—when these began to be revealed, the delight of our Lunatics knew no bounds﻿—and the whole town went moon-mad! But even these immense pictures were surpassed by the “lunatic” animals discovered. First came the “herds of brown quadrupeds” very like a﻿—no! not a whale, but a bison, and “with a tail resembling that of the *bos grunniens*”﻿—the reader probably understands what kind of a “bos” that is, if he’s apprenticed to a theatre in midsummer with musicians on a strike; then a creature, which the hoax-man naively declared “would be classed on Earth as a monster”﻿—I rather think it would!﻿—“of a bluish lead color, about the size of a goat, with a head and a beard like him, and a single horn, slightly inclined forward from, the perpendicular”﻿—it is clear that if this goat was cut down to a single horn, other people were not! I could not but fully appreciate the exquisite distinction accorded by the writer to the female of this lunar animal﻿—for she, while deprived of horn and beard, he explicitly tells us, “had a much larger tail!” When the astronomers put their fingers on the beard of this “beautiful” little creature (on the reflector, mind you!) it would skip away in high dudgeon, which, considering that 240,000 miles intervened, was something to show its delicacy of feeling.
Next in the procession of discovery, among other animals of less note, was presented “a quadruped with an amazingly long neck, head like a sheep, bearing two long spiral horns, white as polished ivory, and standing in perpendiculars parallel to each other. Its body was like that of a deer, but its forelegs were most disproportionately long, and its tail, which was very bushy and of a snowy whiteness, curled high over its rump and hung two or three feet by its side. Its colors were bright bay and white, brindled in patches, but of no regular form.” This is probably the animal known to us on Earth, and particularly along the Mississippi River, as the “guyascutus,” to which I may particularly refer in a future article.

===Chinese===

理所当然，对如此惊世骇俗的成果作了这番详尽铺垫之后，《爱丁堡科学杂志》（即《纽约太阳报》的撰稿人）在后续细节上也绝不敢有丝毫含糊；他接着解释道：经度委员会选定约翰·赫歇尔爵士及其惊人仪器前去观测水星凌日后，之所以挑中好望角，是因为早在十八世纪为同一目的派往秘鲁与拉普拉斯的联合考察中，人们便发现崇山峻岭的引力使大型仪器的铅垂线偏离垂直线七到八秒，从而大大妨碍了观测事业。好望角则恰恰相反，那里有一片辽阔壮观的高原平地，绝无此等困扰。于是， 1834 年 9 月 4 日，为了在水星观测正式开始前彻底熟悉这台新造巨型仪器的操作并观测南天星座，约翰·赫歇尔爵士自伦敦启航，随行者包括格兰特博士（即所谓报料人）、英国皇家工兵部队皇家天文学会会员德拉蒙德中尉（Lieutenant Drummond），以及一大批英国最顶尖的工匠。抵达好望角后，整套仪器耗时四天运抵开普敦东北方向三十五英里处的大高原，由两班接力牛队（每队十八头牛）拉车运送，上坡时还借重了成群荷兰布尔农夫（Dutch boors）的推拉之力。至于安放透镜与反光镜的那座庞大建筑究竟如何构造，好奇的读者还是自己去翻小册子吧——倒不是“荷兰粗汉（boors）”这词儿吓着我了，毕竟咱们自己家里也有的是粗汉，久而久之也就见怪不怪；实在是因为那套繁复的建筑科学说明，怕是没等读完就能让大多数读者在大白天眼冒“金星”。

我只需接着往下讲：到 1 月 10 日，一切准备就绪，连那两根支撑透镜、高达“一百五十英尺！”的石柱也竖立完毕。观测立刻展开，读者的“特别惊奇”也随之而来。人类真该庆幸，骗局的作者假惺惺地赔了个不是（天晓得他怎么好意思！），免去了我们苦读赫歇尔关于“月球回归月、恒星月与朔望月周期”以及“朔望现象”的笔记，直奔主题核心。这儿便是他的神来之笔——向全世界宣告观测取得了彻底成功，看清月球表面物体的清晰度“完全等同于肉眼在百码距离上观察地球物体的水准，从而肯定地解决了这颗卫星上是否有生物栖居、以及属于何等生物门类的问题”，“牢固确立了彗星现象的新理论”等等。单是这则宣告便足以令人屏息，而当云海（*Mare Nubium*）的绿色大理石海岸、宛如金字塔般耸立且由最纯净耀眼的酒红色紫水晶结晶而成的山脉点缀着“圆润乳房般山丘”环绕的翠绿山谷、最纯正朱红色的山巅垂挂着飞瀑流泉与阳光下熠熠生辉的白色大理石崖壁逐一显露时，我们这些“月球迷（Lunatics，与‘疯子’双关）”欣喜若狂——整座城市都为月亮发了疯！然而，更令人叹为观止的还在后头——月球上发现的“疯癫”动物！打头阵的是“成群的棕色四足兽”，活像——不，不是鲸鱼，而是野牛，“长着一条酷似牦牛（*bos grunniens*）的尾巴”——读者要是赶上盛夏乐师罢工时在剧院当过学徒，大概明白那是种什么“老牛（bos，谐音 boss 老板）”；紧接着又出现一种奇兽，骗子作者天真地宣称它“在地球上定会被归为怪物”——我倒也这么觉得！——“呈铅蓝色，体型约莫山羊大小，长着山羊般的脑袋与胡须，头顶独生一只微向前倾的角”——显而易见，这山羊固然被削减成只剩一只角，旁人可远不止长了一只角（按：角 horn 典出戴绿帽）！作者赋予这种月球动物雌性个体的精妙特征，我更是佩服得五体投地——他明明白白告诉我们，雌兽虽没有角和胡子，却“长着一条大得多的尾巴！”当农……不，天文学家把手指按在这头“俊美”小生灵的胡须上时（当然是按在反光镜投影上！），它竟会气鼓鼓地一蹦跳开——考虑到中间隔着 240,000 英里，这小家伙的敏感脾气可真够娇贵的。

发现队伍里紧随其后的，除其他不太起眼的动物外，还隆重推出了“一种脖子奇长无比的四足兽，长着绵羊般的脑袋，顶着两只如抛光象牙般洁白的螺旋长角，彼此平行直立。它的躯干似鹿，前腿却不成比例地奇长，蓬松雪白的尾巴高高卷过臀部，在身侧垂下两三英尺。毛色为亮栗色与白色相间，呈不规则斑纹。”这大概就是我们在地球上——尤其是密西西比河沿岸——人称“盖亚斯库图斯怪兽”（guyascutus）的家伙，日后我或许会另撰专文细说。

===Original===

But all these beings faded into insignificance compared with the first sight of the genuine Lunatics, or men in the moon, “four feet high, covered, except in the face, with short, glossy, copper-colored hair,” and “with wings composed of a thin membrane, without hair, lying snugly upon their backs from the top of their shoulders to the calves of their legs,” “with faces of a yellowish flesh-color﻿—a slight improvement on the large orangutan.” Complimentary for the Lunatics! But, says the chronicler, Lieutenant Drummond declared that “but for their long wings, they would look as well on a parade-ground as some of the cockney militia!” A little rough, my friend the reader will exclaim, for the aforesaid militia.
Of course, it is impossible, in a sketch like the present, to do more than give a glimpse of this rare combination of astronomical realities and the vagaries of mere fancy, and I must omit the Golden-fringed Mountains, the Vale of the Triads, with their splendid triangular temples, etc., but I positively cannot pass by the glowing mention of the inhabitants of this wonderful valley﻿—a superior race of Lunatics, as beautiful and as happy as angels, “spread like eagles” on the grass, eating yellow gourds and red cucumbers, and played with by snow-white stags, with jet-black horns! The description here is positively delightful, and I even now remember my poignant sigh of regret when, at the conclusion, I read that these innocent and happy beings, although evidently “creatures of order and subordination,” and “very polite,” were seen indulging in amusements which would not be deemed “within the bounds of strict propriety” on this degenerate ball. The story wound up rather abruptly by referring the reader to an extended work on the subject by Herschel, which has not yet appeared.
One can laugh very heartily, now, at all this; but nearly everybody, the gravest and the wisest, too, was completely taken in at the time: and the *Sun*, then established at the corner of Spruce Street, where the *Tribune* office now stands, reaped an increase of more than fifty thousand to its circulation﻿—in fact, there gained the foundation of its subsequent prolonged success. Its proprietors sold no less than $25,000 worth of the “Moon Hoax” over the counter, even exhausting an edition of sixty thousand in pamphlet form. And who was the author? A literary gentleman, who has devoted very many years of his life to mathematical and astronomical studies, and was at the time connected as an editor with the *Sun*﻿—one whose name has since been widely known in literature and politics﻿—Richard Adams Locke, Esq., then in his youth, and now in the decline of years. Mr. Locke, who still survives, is a native of the British Isles, and, at the time of his first connection with the New York press, was the only shorthand reporter in this city, where he laid the basis of a competency he now enjoys. Mr. Locke declares that his original object in writing the Moon story was to satirize some of the extravagances of Doctor Dick, and to make some astronomical suggestions which he felt diffident about offering seriously.

===Chinese===

然而，与初次目睹货真价实的“月球人”（Lunatics）相比，所有这些生灵都顿时黯然失色：“身高四英尺，除面部外浑身覆满短而油亮的铜色毛发”，“生有一对无毛薄膜翅膀，从肩头一直服帖地收拢在背后直至小腿肚”，“面呈淡黄肉色——较之大型红毛猩猩略胜一筹。”对月球人而言这夸奖可真够体面的！不过，编年史作者写道，德拉蒙德中尉却宣称：“若非长着那对长翅膀，他们在阅兵场上的仪态怕是跟伦敦东区某些民兵一样气派！”亲爱的读者朋友定会惊呼，这话对上述民兵可有点儿太刻薄了。

诚然，在如今这样一篇简略素描里，绝无可能面面俱到地呈现这桩天文事实与纯粹奇想的绝妙混合体；我只得略过镶金边山脉、拥有壮丽三角形神庙的三合谷（Vale of the Triads）等等，但我万万不能放过对那座神奇山谷居民的热情描写——那是一支更高等的月球人种族，像天使般俊美幸福，像“展翅雄鹰”般舒展躺在草地上，啃着黄葫芦与红黄瓜，身旁还有长着乌黑长角的雪白雄鹿嬉戏相伴！这段描写实在美妙绝伦，我至今仍记得读到结尾时那声痛心的惋惜长叹：这群天真快乐的生灵虽然显然“严守秩序与上下尊卑”且“极其彬彬有礼”，却被人窥见正在纵情享乐某些在我们这颗堕落星球上会被视作“有失端庄体统”的嬉戏。整篇故事收尾得颇为突兀，只推说详情请见赫歇尔即将出版的一部详尽专著——而那部专著至今尚未问世。

事到如今，人人都能对这一切开怀大笑；可在当时，几乎所有人——包括最严肃、最睿智的学者——全都彻彻底底地上了当：当时报社设在斯普鲁斯街街角（即如今《论坛报》社址所在处）的《太阳报》，借此一举暴涨了五万多份发行量——事实上，正是从那时起奠定了它此后长盛不衰的基业。报社老板光是柜台零售《月亮骗局》便卖出了不下 25,000 美元，甚至把六万册小册子版抢购一空。那么作者究竟是谁呢？是一位把人生多年光阴献给数学与天文学研究的文人绅士，当时正担任《太阳报》编辑——此后他在文学界与政界都大名鼎鼎——理查德·亚当斯·洛克先生（Richard Adams Locke, Esq.），彼时风华正茂，如今已步入暮年。依然健在的洛克先生生于不列颠群岛，初入纽约报界时曾是全城唯一一名速记记者，正是在这儿打下了他如今安享丰足晚年的家底。洛克先生坦言，他最初撰写月球故事的初衷，不过是为了讽刺迪克博士的某些荒诞臆测，并顺带提出几条他不好意思一本正经发表的天文学设想。

===Original===

Whatever may have been his object, his hit was unrivaled; and for months the press of Christendom, but far more in Europe than here, teemed with it, until Sir John Herschel was actually compelled to come out with a denial over his own signature. In the meantime, it was printed and published in many languages, with superb illustrations. Mr. Endicott, the celebrated lithographer, some years ago had in his possession a splendid series of engravings, of extra folio size, got up in Italy, in the highest style of art, and illustrating the “Moon Hoax.”
Here, in New York, the public were, for a long time, divided on the subject, the vast majority believing, and a few grumpy customers rejecting the story. One day, Mr. Locke was introduced by a mutual friend at the door of the *Sun* office to a very grave old orthodox Quaker, who, in the calmest manner, went on to tell him all about the embarkation of Herschel’s apparatus at London, where he had seen it with his own eyes. Of course, Locke’s optics expanded somewhat while he listened to this remarkable statement, but he wisely kept his own counsel.
The discussions of the press were very rich; the *Sun*, of course, defending the affair as genuine, and others doubting it. The *Mercantile Advertiser*, the *Albany Daily Advertiser*, the *New York Commercial Advertiser*, the *New York Times*, the *New Yorker*, the *New York Spirit of ’76*, the *Sunday News*, the *United States Gazette*, the *Philadelphia Inquirer*, and hosts of other papers came out with the most solemn acceptance and admiration of these “wonderful discoveries,” and were eclipsed in their approval only by the scientific journals abroad. The *Evening Post*, however, was decidedly skeptical, and took up the matter in this irreverent way:

> “It is quite proper that the *Sun* should be the means of shedding so much light on the Moon. That there should be winged people in the moon does not strike us as more wonderful than the existence of such a race of beings on the Earth; and that there does still exist such a race, rests on the evidence of that most veracious of voyagers and circumstantial of chroniclers, Peter Wilkins, whose celebrated work not only gives an account of the general appearance and habits of a most interesting tribe of flying Indians; but, also, of all those more delicate and engaging traits which the author was enabled to discover by reason of the conjugal relations he entered into with one of the females of the winged tribe.”

The moon-hoax had its day, and some of its glory still survives. Mr. Locke, its author, is now quietly residing in the beautiful little home of a friend on the Clove Road, Staten Island, and no doubt, as he gazes up at the evening luminary, often fancies that he sees a broad grin on the countenance of its only well-authenticated tenant, “the hoary solitary whom the criminal code of the nursery has banished thither for collecting fuel on the Sabbath-day.”

===Chinese===

无论他当初抱着何种目的，这一炮都打得空前绝后；一连数月，整个基督教世界的报刊——欧洲更甚于本土——无不连篇累牍地转载热议，直到逼得约翰·赫歇尔爵士本人不得不亲自署名发表辟谣声明。在此期间，该文被译成多种文字出版，并配有精美的插图。著名石版画家恩迪科特先生（Mr. Endicott）几年前手头还藏有一套特大对开本的精美铜版画，乃意大利名家以最高艺术水准精心制作，专程为《月亮骗局》配图。

在纽约本地，公众对此事的态度也曾长期两极分化：绝大多数人深信不疑，只有少数几个脾气古怪的主顾拒绝采信。有一天，在《太阳报》社门口，经一位共同朋友引见，洛克先生结识了一位神情极其庄重的正统老贵格会教徒；那位老先生用最平静笃定的口吻，向他滔滔不绝地讲述自己当年在伦敦亲眼目睹赫歇尔那台巨型仪器装船起运的全过程。听着这番惊人证言，洛克的双眼自然微微睁大了些，却明智地守口如瓶、未露半点口风。

报界展开的论战更是精彩纷呈：《太阳报》自然力挺此事货真价实，别家则将信将疑。《商业广告报》、《奥尔巴尼每日广告报》、《纽约商业广告报》、《纽约时报》、《纽约客》、 《纽约 76 精神报》、《周日新闻》、《合众国公报》、《费城问询报》以及成百上千家其他报纸纷纷发文，以最庄重肃穆的态度全盘接受并赞叹这些“伟大发现”，其热忱程度仅被海外的科学期刊略压一筹。唯独《晚邮报》（*Evening Post*）态度坚决怀疑，以如下大不敬的口吻调侃此事：

> “由《太阳报》（Sun）出面替‘月亮’洒下如此万丈光芒，倒也再合适不过。月球上居然住着长翅膀的人，在我们看来并不比地球上存在这么一种生灵更令人惊奇；而地球上确实至今存在这等种族，有那位最信实可靠的航海家兼最详尽严谨的编年史作者彼得·威尔金斯（Peter Wilkins）为证——他那部名著不仅记述了一个极其有趣的飞翔印第安部落的外貌特征与生活习性，更得益于作者与该飞翔部落一名女子结成的夫妻之缘，得以细细披露其中种种更为微妙动人的体态特征。”

月亮骗局曾风靡一时，它的余韵荣光至今犹存。它的作者洛克先生如今正安安静静地居住在斯塔滕岛克洛夫路（Clove Road, Staten Island）一位朋友雅致的小宅里；毫无疑问，每当他仰望夜空中那轮明月时，脑海里定然常常浮现出月亮上唯一有据可查的住户脸上那抹咧嘴大笑——那位“因在安息日拾柴而被童谣刑律流放到广寒宫的白发独居老人”。

### XXXIII / 第三十三章

===Original===

The miscegenation hoax﻿—​A great literary sell﻿—​Political humbugging﻿—​Tricks of the wire-pullers﻿—​Machinery employed to render the pamphlet notorious﻿—​Who were sold and how it was done.
Some persons say that “all is fair in politics.” Without agreeing with this doctrine, I nevertheless feel that the history of Ancient and Modern Humbugs would not be complete without a record of the last and one of the most successful of known literary hoaxes. This is the pamphlet entitled “Miscegenation,” which advocates the blending of the white and black races upon this continent, as a result not only inevitable from the freeing of the negro, but desirable as a means of creating a more perfect race of men than any now existing. This pamphlet is a clever political quiz; and was written by three young gentlemen of the *World* newspaper, namely. D. G. Croly, George Wakeman, and E. C. Howell.
The design of “Miscegenation” was exceedingly ambitious, and the machinery employed was probably among the most ingenious and audacious ever put into operation to procure the endorsement of absurd theories, and give the subject the widest notoriety. The object was to so make use of the prevailing ideas of the extremists of the Anti-Slavery party, as to induce them to accept doctrines which would be obnoxious to the great mass of the community, and which would, of course, be used in the political canvass which was to ensue. It was equally important that the “Democrats” should be made to believe that the pamphlet in question emanated from a “Republican” source. The idea was suggested by a discourse delivered by Mr. Theodore Tilton, at the Cooper Institute, before the American Anti-Slavery Society, in May 1863, on the negro, in which that distinguished orator argued, that in some future time the blood of the negro would form one of the mingled bloods of the great regenerated American nation. The scheme once conceived, it began immediately to be put into execution. The first stumbling-block was the name “amalgamation,” by which this fraternizing of the races had been always known. It was evident that a book advocating amalgamation would fall stillborn, and hence some new and novel word had to be discovered, with the same meaning, but not so objectionable. Such a word was coined by the combination of the Latin *miscere*, to mix, and *genus*, race: from these, miscegenation﻿—a mingling of the races. The word is as euphonious as “amalgamation,” and much more correct in meaning. It has passed into the language, and no future dictionary will be complete without it. Next, it was necessary to give the book an erudite appearance, and arguments from ethnology must form no unimportant part of this matter. Neither of the authors being versed in this science, they were compelled to depend entirely on encyclopedias and books of reference. This obstacle to a New York editor or reporter was not so great as it might seem. The public are often favored in our journals with dissertations upon various abstruse matters by men who are entirely ignorant of what they are writing about. It was said of Cuvier that he could restore the skeleton of an extinct animal if he were only given one of its teeth, and so a competent editor or reporter of a city journal can get up an article of any length on any given subject, if he is only furnished one word or name to start with. There was but one writer on ethnology distinctly known to the authors, which was Prichard; but that being secured, all the rest came easily enough. The authors went to the Astor Library and secured a volume of Prichard’s works, the perusal of which of course gave them the names of many other authorities, which were also consulted; and thus a very respectable array of scientific arguments in favor of Miscegenation were soon compiled. The sentimental and argumentative portions were quickly suggested from the knowledge of the authors of current politics, of the vagaries of some of the more visionary reformers, and from their own native wit.

===Chinese===

《种族混血论》骗局——一场伟大的文学恶作剧——政坛欺罔术——幕后操盘手的伎俩——为令小册子臭名远扬而动用的宣传机器——谁上了当以及他们是如何上当的。

有人说“政坛之上不择手段”。我虽不赞同这种信条，但仍觉得倘若遗漏了最近这场、也是已知最成功的文学恶作剧之一，这部《古今世界之欺罔》史便算不上完整。我说的是那本名为《种族混血论》（*Miscegenation*）的小册子——它鼓吹美洲大陆上白人与黑人两大种族应当融合，声称这不仅是解放黑奴后不可避免的结果，更是造就出比现存任何人类都更为完美的种族的理想途径。这本小册子其实是一出机智狡黠的政治恶搞，出自《世界报》（*World*）三位年轻绅士之手：D. G. 克罗利（D. G. Croly）、乔治·韦克曼（George Wakeman）以及 E. C. 豪厄尔（E. C. Howell）。

《种族混血论》的企图心极其宏大，而它所动用的整套宣传机器，恐怕是有史以来为骗取荒谬理论背书、将某一议题炒作得举世皆知而运转过的最精巧、最大胆的装置之一。其真实用意，是利用废奴派极端分子当中盛行的观念，诱使他们全盘接受一套令社会绝大多数民众深恶痛绝的学说，好在紧接着即将展开的总统竞选拉票战中当作攻击把柄。同样重要的是，必须让“民主党人”深信不疑，这本小册子正是出自“共和党人”之手。这一念头源于 1863 年 5 月西奥多·蒂尔顿先生（Mr. Theodore Tilton）在库珀学院（Cooper Institute）向美国反奴隶制协会发表的一篇关于黑人的演讲；在那篇演说中，这位杰出的演说家论证道，将来某天黑人的血液必将汇入伟大重生的美利坚民族的混合血脉之中。主意既定，他们立刻付诸实施。头一块绊脚石在于“混杂”（amalgamation）这个旧词——向来人们都用这个词指代种族融合。显而易见，一本公开鼓吹“混杂”的书一问世便会胎死腹中，因此必须发明一个意思相同却不那么刺耳的新奇词汇。于是他们将拉丁词 *miscere*（混合）与 *genus*（种族）拼合在一起，造出了 *miscegenation*（种族混血）一词。这个词读起来与“amalgamation”一样悦耳，词义却精准得多。如今它已正式进入英语词库，日后任何一本词典若不收入它都算不上完备。第二步，必须给这本书披上一层博学严谨的外衣，因此人种学论据在书中须占据举足轻重的地位。三位作者偏偏谁也不通这门学问，只得完全仰赖百科全书与参考书。对纽约报社的编辑或记者而言，这道难关远不像外人以为的那么棘手。我们各大报刊向来经常刊登由对所写主题一窍不通之人撰写的各色高深论文，以飨公众。前人曾赞叹居维叶（Cuvier）单凭一颗牙齿便能复原一整副灭绝动物的骨架；同样道理，大报社里一位称职的编辑或记者，只要手头有一个切入词汇或人名，便能洋洋洒洒写出任意篇幅的任意专题文章。作者们唯一明确知晓的人种学家只有普里查德（Prichard）；可只要抓住这根线头，其余一切便顺藤摸瓜水到渠成。作者们跑去阿斯特图书馆（Astor Library）借出一卷普里查德的著作，翻阅之下自然顺带找出了大批其他权威学者的名字，一一查阅引用；于是，一整排像模像样、力挺“种族混血”的科学论据很快便拼凑成型。至于感性抒情与雄辩说理的部分，则全凭作者们对当下时政的熟稔、对某些空想改革家奇谈怪论的了解，以及他们自身的机智才情信手拈来。

===Original===

The book was at first written in a most cursory manner the chapters got up without any order or reference to each other, and afterward arranged. As the impression sought to be conveyed was a serious one, it would clearly not do to commence with the extravagant and absurd theories to which it was intended that the reader should gradually be led. The scientific portion of the work was therefore given first, and was made as grave and terse and unobjectionable as possible; and merely urged, by arguments drawn from science and history, that the blending of the different races of men resulted in a better progeny. As the work progressed, they continued to “pile on the agony,” until, at the close, the very fact that the statue of the Goddess of Liberty on the Capitol, is of a bronze tint, is looked upon as an omen of the color of the future American!

> “When the traveler approaches the City of Magnificent Distances,” it says, “the seat of what is destined to be the greatest and most beneficent power on Earth, the first object that will strike his eye will be the figure of Liberty surmounting the Capitol; not white, symbolizing but one race, nor black, typifying another, but a statue representing the composite race, whose sway will extend from the Atlantic to the Pacific Ocean, from the Equator to the North Pole﻿—the Miscegens of the Future.”

The Book once written, plans were laid to obtain the endorsement of the people who were to be humbugged. It was not only necessary to humbug the members of the Reform and Progressive party, but to present﻿—as I have before said﻿—such serious arguments that Democrats should be led to believe it as a bona fide revelation of the “infernal” designs of their antagonists. In both respects there was complete success. Although, of course, the mass of the Republican leaders entirely ignored the book, yet a considerable number of Anti-Slavery men, with more transcendental ideas, were decidedly “sold.” The machinery employed was exceedingly ingenious. Before the book was published, proof-copies were furnished to every prominent abolitionist in the country, and also to prominent spiritual mediums, to ladies known to wear Bloomers, and to all that portion of our population who are supposed to be a little “soft” on the subject of reform. A circular was also enclosed, requesting them, before the publication of the book, to give the author the benefit of their opinions as to the value of the arguments presented, and the desirability of the immediate publication of the work; to be enclosed to the American News Company, 121 Nassau Street, New York﻿—the agents for the publishers. The bait took. Letters came pouring in from all sides, and among the names of prominent persons who gave their endorsements were Albert Brisbane, Parker Pillsbury, Lucretia Mott, Sarah M. Grimke, Angelina G. Weld, Dr. J. McCune Smith, Wm. Wells Brown. Mr. Pillsbury was quite excited over the book, saying; “Your work has cheered and gladdened a winter-morning, which I began in cloud and sorrow. You are on the right track. Pursue it, and the good God speed you.” Mr. Theodore Tilton, upon receiving the pamphlet, wrote a note promising to read it, and to write the author a long and candid letter as soon as he had time; and saying, that the subject was one to which he had given much thought. The promised letter, I believe, however, was never received; probably because, on a careful perusal of the book, Mr. Tilton “smelt a rat.” He might also have been influenced by an ironical paragraph relating to himself, and arguing that, as he was a “pure specimen of the blonde,” and “when a young man was noted for his angelic type of feature,” his sympathy for the colored race was accounted for by the natural love of opposites. Says the author with much gravity:

> “The sympathy Mr. Greeley, Mr. Phillips and Mr. Tilton feel for the negro is the love which the blonde bears for the black; it is the love of race, a sympathy stronger to them than the love they bear to woman. It is founded upon natural law. We love our opposites. It is the nature of things that we should do so, and where Nature has free course, men like those we have indicated, whether Anti-Slavery or Pro-Slavery, Conservative or Radical, Democrat or Republican, will marry and be given in marriage to the most perfect specimens of the colored race.”

===Chinese===

这本书起初写得极其草率，各章节毫无次序、互不照应地拼凑而成，事后才加以编排。既然想要营造出严肃正经的印象，自然不能一上来就抛出那些旨在一步步诱导读者入瓮的荒诞狂论。因此，全书的科学论证部分被摆在最前面，写得尽可能庄重、简练而又无懈可击；仅仅借用科学与历史论据力陈：不同人种的融合能够孕育出更优秀的后代。随着篇幅推进，他们步步“加码煽情”，直到全书结尾，甚至连国会大厦穹顶上那座自由女神像呈古铜色这一事实，都被当成了未来美国人肤色的祥瑞预兆！

> “当旅人走近这座‘辽阔宏伟之城’，”书中写道，“走近那注定成为地球上最伟大、最仁慈之强权的所在，首先映入他眼帘的，便是高踞国会大厦之巅的自由神像；她既非象征单一白种人的纯白，亦非代表另一肤色的纯黑，而是一尊象征着复合种族的雕像——该种族的统治将从大西洋延伸至太平洋，从赤道绵亘至北极——那便是‘未来的混血儿’。”

书稿既成，他们随即部署计策，骗取那些待宰羔羊的公开背书。他们不仅要糊弄改革派与进步党成员，还要——正如我先前所说——端出足以让民主党人信以为真的严肃论证，以为这真是政敌“恶毒阴谋”的货真价实大暴露。在这两方面，他们都取得了圆满成功。尽管绝大多数共和党领袖自然对这本小册子置之不理，但相当一批怀抱超验空想的反奴隶制人士却实实在在地“上了当”。他们动用的宣传机器精妙绝伦。在书正式出版前，校样便被分送给全国每一位知名废奴主义者，同时送给知名灵媒、素以穿灯笼裤（Bloomers）闻名的女权女士，以及所有在改革议题上被认为脑筋略微“软和”的民众。随书附有一份征求意见函，恳请诸位在出版前不吝赐教，评判书中论点之价值及立即付梓之必要，回信请寄交纽约拿骚街 121 号美国新闻公司（American News Company）——出版商代理处。鱼饵奏效了。四面八方的回信如雪片般飞来，欣然签字背书的知名人士名单中包括阿尔伯特·布里斯班（Albert Brisbane）、帕克·皮尔斯伯里（Parker Pillsbury）、卢克丽霞·莫特（Lucretia Mott）、萨拉·M. 格里姆克（Sarah M. Grimke）、安吉丽娜·G. 韦尔德（Angelina G. Weld）、J. 麦丘恩·史密斯博士（Dr. J. McCune Smith）以及威廉·韦尔斯·布朗（Wm. Wells Brown）。皮尔斯伯里先生读后兴奋不已，回信称：“大作令我原本在阴霾与忧伤中开启的冬日清晨焕发欢欣与鼓舞。您走在了正确的轨道上。请坚持下去，愿仁慈的上帝保佑您一路顺风。”西奥多·蒂尔顿先生收到小册子后也写了张便条，承诺抽空拜读并尽快写一封坦诚长信，还说这正是他素来深思熟虑的课题。不过据我所知，那封许诺的长信始终未曾寄到；多半是因为蒂尔顿先生细读全书后“嗅出了猫腻”。兴许他也受到了书中一段专门讽刺他本人的文字的影响——那段话煞有介事地论证道，鉴于他是“纯正白皙金发碧眼的典范”，且“年轻时即以天使般容貌著称”，他对有色人种的同情恰恰源于“异性相吸、相反相成”的自然法则。作者无比庄重地写道：

> “格里利先生、菲利普斯先生与蒂尔顿先生对黑人所怀的同情，乃是金发白人对黑人的爱慕；这是种族之爱，一种比他们对女性的爱还要强烈的共情。它植根于自然法则。我们热爱与自己截然相反之人。世间万物本性如此；凡在自然法则自由运行之处，像我们点出的这几位先生，无论反奴还是拥奴、保守还是激进、民主党还是共和党，终将迎娶并嫁与有色人种中最完美的典范。”

===Original===

So far, things worked favorably; and, having thus bagged a goodly number of prominent reformers, the next effort was to get the ear of the public. Here, new machinery was brought into play. A statement was published in the *Philadelphia Inquirer* (a paper which, ever since the war commenced, has been notorious for its “sensation” news,) that a charming and accomplished young mulatto girl was about to publish a book on the subject of the blending of the races, in which she took the affirmative view. Of course, so piquant a paragraph was immediately copied by almost every paper in the country. Various other stories, equally ingenious and equally groundless, were set afloat, and public expectation was riveted on the forthcoming work.
Some time in February last, the book was published. Copies, of course, were sent to all the leading journals. The “Anglo-African,” the organ of the colored population of New York, warmly, and at great length, endorsed the doctrine. The *Anti-Slavery Standard*, edited by Mr. Oliver Johnson, gave over a column of serious argument and endorsement to the work. Mr. Tilton, of the *Independent*, was not to be caught napping. In that journal, under date of February 25, 1864, he devoted a two-column leader to the subject of Miscegenation and the little pamphlet in question. Mr. Tilton was the first to announce a belief that the book was a hoax. I quote from his article:

> “Remaining a while on our table unread, our attention was specially called to it by noticing how savagely certain newspapers were abusing it.”

---

> “The authorship of the pamphlet is a well-kept secret; at least it is unknown to us. Nor, after a somewhat careful reading, are we convinced that the writer is in earnest. Our first impression was, and remains, that the work was meant as a piece of pleasantry﻿—a burlesque upon what are popularly called the extreme and fanatical notions of certain radical men named therein. Certainly, the essay is not such a one as any of these gentlemen would have written on the subject, though some of their speeches are conspicuously quoted and commended in it.”

---

> “If written in earnest, the work is not thorough enough to be satisfactory; if in jest, we prefer Sydney Smith﻿—or McClellan’s Report. Still, to be frank, we agree with a large portion of these pages, but disagree heartily with another portion.”

---

> “The idea of scientifically undertaking to intermingle existing populations according to a predetermined plan for reconstructing the human race﻿—for flattening out its present varieties into one final unvarious dead-level of humanity﻿—is so absurd, that we are more than ever convinced such a statement was not written in earnest!”

---

Mr. Tilton, however, hints that the colored race is finally in some degree to form a component part of the future American; and that, in time, “the negro of the South, growing paler with every generation, will at last completely hide his face under the snow.”
One of the editorial writers for the *Tribune* was so impressed with the book that he wrote an article on the subject, arguing about it with apparent seriousness, and in a manner with some readers supposed to be rather favorable than otherwise to the doctrine. Mr. Greeley and the publishers, it is understood, were displeased at the publication of the article. The next morning nearly all the city journals had editorial articles upon the subject.

===Chinese===

至此一切顺风顺水；既已把好一批知名改革家收入囊中，下一步便是抢占公众的耳朵。这时，一套新机器开动了。《费城问询报》（*Philadelphia Inquirer*——这家报纸自战争爆发以来向以制造“耸动新闻”闻名）刊出一则消息，称一位迷人而又才华横溢的年轻混血女郎即将出版一部探讨种族融合的著作，并在书中持赞成态度。不出所料，如此辛辣撩人的一段小消息立刻被全国几乎每一家报纸转载。其他种种同样精妙、同样子虚乌有的传闻也纷纷放出，公众的胃口被吊得高高的，翘首以盼这本即将问世的奇书。

去年二月间，该书正式出版。赠阅本自然寄给了所有主流报刊。纽约有色人种的喉舌《英裔非洲人报》（*Anglo-African*）以大篇幅热情洋溢地力挺这一学说。由奥利弗·约翰逊先生（Mr. Oliver Johnson）主编的《反奴隶制标准报》（*Anti-Slavery Standard*）辟出一整栏多篇幅，发表严肃论证予以背书。不过，《独立报》（*Independent*）的蒂尔顿先生可没那么容易打盹上钩。在该报 1864 年 2 月 25 日的一期上，他用整整两栏社论探讨种族混血议题及这本小册子。蒂尔顿先生是第一个公开表示相信此书纯属恶作剧的人。我姑且摘录他文章中的几段：

> “它曾在我们案头搁置许久未曾翻阅，直到我们留意到某些报纸正以极其凶狠的口吻痛骂它，这才引起我们的特别关注。”

---

> “这本小册子的作者是谁，保守得密不透风；至少我们一无所知。而且，经过一番颇为仔细的通读，我们并不认为作者是认真的。我们的第一印象——至今依然如此——是此作意在充当一则滑稽戏谑，旨在讽刺世俗所谓书中点名的某些激进人士的极端狂热观念。诚然，这篇文章绝非那几位先生本人就该主题会写出的文字，尽管书中显眼地引用并赞扬了他们的几篇演说。”

---

> “若是认真撰写，此书的论证远不够透彻令人满意；若是戏谑之作，我们宁可读西德尼·史密斯（Sydney Smith）——或者麦克莱伦的战报。不过平心而论，我们对其中很大一部分篇幅表示赞同，对另一部分则由衷反对。”

---

> “企图按预定计划科学地混杂现有人口以重构人类种族——将人类现有的丰富多样性碾平为一个最终千篇一律的死水微澜——这一想法荒唐至极，令我们比以往任何时候都更加确信，此等言论绝非认真写就！”

---

不过，蒂尔顿先生仍暗示有色人种最终将在某种程度上成为未来美国人的组成部分；久而久之，“南方的黑人随着世代更替肤色日渐浅淡，终将把自己的面孔彻底隐没在白雪之下。”

《论坛报》（*Tribune*）的一位社论主笔对此书印象深刻，竟撰文一本正经地探讨起来，行文口吻在某些读者看来甚至颇为赞同该学说。据了解，格里利先生与报社老板对该文的刊出十分不满。次日清晨，全城几乎所有报纸都就此发表了社论文章。

===Original===

The next point was, to get the miscegenation controversy into Congress. The book, with its endorsements, was brought to the notice of Mr. Cox, of Ohio (commonly called “Sunset Cox;”) and he made an earnest speech on the subject. Mr. Washburne replied wittily, reading and commenting on extracts from a work by Cox, in which the latter deplored the existence of the prejudice against the Africans. A few days after, Mr. Kelly, of Pennsylvania, replied very elaborately to Mr. Cox, bringing all his learning and historical research to bear on the topic. It was the subject of a deal of talk in Washington afterward. Mr. Cox was charged by some of the more shrewd members of Congress with writing it. It was said that Mr. Sumner, on reading it, immediately pronounced it a hoax.
Through the influence of the authors, a person visited James Gordon Bennett, of the *Herald*, and spoke to him about *Miscegenation*. Mr. Bennett thought the idea too monstrous and absurd to waste an article upon.
“But,” said the gentleman, “the Democratic papers are all noticing it.”
“The Democratic editors are asses,” said Bennett.
“Senator Cox has just made a speech in Congress on it.”
“Cox is an ass,” responded Bennett.
“Greeley had an article about it the other day.”
“Well, Greeley’s a donkey.”
“The *Independent* yesterday had a leader of a column and a half about it.”
“Well, Beecher is no better,” said Bennett. “They’re all asses. But what did he say about it?”
“Oh, he rather endorsed it.”
“Well, I’ll read the article,” said Bennett. “And perhaps I’ll have an article written ridiculing Beecher.”
“It will make a very good handle against the radicals,” said the other.
“Oh, I don’t know,” said Bennett. “Let them marry together, if they want to, with all my heart.”

===Chinese===

下一步，便是把“种族混血”之争捅进美国国会。这本小册子连同那批名流背书信被呈到俄亥俄州议员考克斯先生（Mr. Cox，人称“日落”考克斯）案头；他随即就此发表了一通慷慨激昂的演说。沃什伯恩先生（Mr. Washburne）机智反击，当场朗读并点评了考克斯自己早年著作中的段落——考克斯在书里曾痛心疾首地哀叹世人对非洲裔存在偏见。几天后，宾夕法尼亚州的凯利先生（Mr. Kelly）又洋洋洒洒地长篇驳斥考克斯，把自己毕生学识与历史考据统统搬上了台面。此后好一阵子，这成了华盛顿政坛街头巷尾的热议焦点。几位更精明的国会议员甚至反过来指控考克斯才是那本小册子的幕后作者。据说萨姆纳先生（Mr. Sumner）读完之后，当场便断定这是一场恶作剧。

在三位作者的牵线搭桥下，有个人专门去拜访《先驱报》（*Herald*）的詹姆斯·戈登·贝内特（James Gordon Bennett），向他提起《种族混血论》。贝内特先生起初觉得这主意荒唐透顶，根本不值得浪费一篇社论的笔墨。

“可是，”那位来客说道，“民主党的报纸全都在关注这事儿呢。”

“民主党的编辑全是一帮蠢驴，”贝内特说。

“考克斯议员刚在国会上就此发表了演说。”

“考克斯也是头蠢驴，”贝内特回敬道。

“格里利前几天刚登了一篇文章。”

“哼，格里利就是头长耳朵毛驴。”

“《独立报》昨天也登了一篇一栏半长的社论。”

“嗯，比彻（Beecher）也好不到哪儿去，”贝内特说，“他们全都是蠢驴。不过他到底说了什么？”

“哦，他倒是颇为赞同。”

“那好，我倒要读读那篇文章，”贝内特说道，“没准儿我再让人写篇文章嘲讽一顿比彻。”

“这用来攻击激进派可是个绝佳把柄，”另一人顺势劝道。

“哦，这我可不知道，”贝内特说，“他们自己真想互相通婚，我打心眼儿里随他们去。”

===Original===

For some days, the *Herald* said nothing about it, but the occasion of the departure of a colored regiment from New York City having called forth a flattering address to them from the ladies of the “Loyal League,” the *Herald*, saw a chance to make a point against Mr. Charles King and others; and the next day it contained a terrific article, introducing miscegenation in the most violent and offensive manner, and saying that the ladies of the “Loyal League” had offered to marry the colored soldiers on their return! After that, the *Herald* kept up a regular fusillade against the supposed miscegenic proclivities of the Republicans. And thus, after all, Bennett swallowed the “critter” horns, hoofs, tail, and all.
The authors even had the impudence to attempt to entrap Mr. Lincoln into an endorsement of the work, and asked permission to dedicate a new work, on a kindred subject, “Melaleukation,” to him. Honest Old Abe however, who can see a joke, was not to be taken in so easily.
About the time the book was first published, Miss Anne E. Dickinson happened to lecture in New York. The authors here exhibited a great degree of acuteness and tact, as well as sublime impudence, in seizing the opportunity to have some small hand bills, with the endorsement of the book, printed and distributed by boys among the audience. Before Miss Dickinson appeared, therefore, the audience were gravely reading the miscegenation handbill; and the reporters, noticing it, coupled the facts in their reports. From this, it went forth, and was widely circulated, that Miss Dickinson was the author!
Dr. Mackay, the correspondent of the *London Times*, in New York, was very decidedly sold, and hurled all manner of big words against the doctrine in his letters to “The Thunderer;” and thus “the leading paper of Europe” was, for the hundredth time during the American Rebellion, decidedly taken in and done for.
The *Saturday Review*﻿—perhaps the cleverest and certainly the sauciest of the English hebdomadals﻿—also berated the book and its authors in the most pompous language at its command. Indeed, the *Westminster Review* seriously refers to the arguments of the book in connection with Dr. Broca’s pamphlet on Human Hybridity, a most profound work. “Miscegenation” was republished in England by Trübner & Co.; and very extensive translations from it are still passing the rounds of the French and German papers.
Thus passes into history one of the most impudent as well as ingenious literary hoaxes of the present day. There is probably not a newspaper in the country but has printed much about it; and enough of extracts might be collected from various journals upon the subject to fill my whale-tank.
It is needless to say that the book passed through several editions. Of course, the mass of the intelligent American people rejected the doctrines of the work, and looked upon it either as a political dodge, or as the ravings of some crazy man; but the authors have the satisfaction of knowing that it achieved a notoriety which has hardly been equalled by any mere pamphlet ever published in this country.

===Chinese===

此后好几天，《先驱报》对此一直只字未提；然而，恰逢一支有色人种军团从纽约市开拔前线，“忠诚联盟”（Loyal League）的女士们向将士们献上了一篇热情洋溢的致词，《先驱报》顿时嗅到了攻击查尔斯·金先生（Mr. Charles King）等人的良机；次日，该报便登出一篇火药味十足的檄文，以最激烈、最刺耳的笔调把“种族混血”扯了进来，声称“忠诚联盟”的女士们竟然主动提出等有色人种士兵凯旋归来便嫁给他们！自那以后，《先驱报》便对共和党人所谓“热衷种族混血的倾向”展开了连珠炮般的猛烈轰击。兜了一圈下来，贝内特终究还是把这头“怪兽”连角带蹄加尾巴一口吞进了肚里。

三位作者甚至厚颜无耻到企图诱骗林肯先生替此书背书，厚着脸皮请求准许将另一部同类题材的新作《黑白混血论》（*Melaleukation*）献给他。然而“诚实的老亚伯”（Honest Old Abe）向来听得懂玩笑话，绝没那么容易上当受骗。

大致就在该书初版前后，安妮·E. 迪金森小姐（Miss Anne E. Dickinson）恰巧来纽约发表演讲。三位作者在此展现出了极高明的敏锐机变与胆大妄为：他们抓住这一契机，印制了一批附有该书背书词的小传单，雇了几个男孩在听众席间分发。于是，在迪金森小姐登台之前，台下听众都在一本正经地捧读“种族混血”传单；记者们见状，便在报道中把两件事捆绑在了一起。消息就此不胫而走、传遍四方，竟说迪金森小姐正是那本奇书的作者！

《伦敦泰晤士报》（*London Times*）驻纽约记者麦凯博士（Dr. Mackay）也实打实地上了当，在寄往“雷神”（The Thunderer，《泰晤士报》绰号）的通讯里堆砌了一大堆华丽大词痛斥这一学说；于是，“欧洲第一大报”在美国内战期间第一百次干脆利落地中了圈套、栽了跟头。

《星期六评论》（*Saturday Review*）——或许是英国周刊中最机智、也无疑是最傲慢刻薄的一家——同样动用其所能驾驭的最浮夸傲慢的辞藻，把此书及其作者痛骂了一通。更有甚者，《威斯敏斯特评论》（*Westminster Review*）竟一本正经地将此书的论点与布罗卡博士（Dr. Broca）那部极富深度的专著《人类杂交论》（*Human Hybridity*）并列讨论。《种族混血论》随后由特吕布纳公司（Trübner & Co.）在英格兰重印发行；其大段译文至今仍在法国与德国报刊间流传不息。

就这样，当代最厚颜无耻也最精妙绝伦的文学恶作剧之一，正式载入了史册。全国恐怕找不出哪家报纸未曾大篇幅报道过此事；若把各家报刊就此发表的摘录收集起来，怕是足够填满我那座白鲸大水箱。

不用说，这本书连着印了好几版。自然，绝大多数聪慧的美国民众摒弃了书中的学说，要么视之为政客伎俩，要么当作某个疯子的呓语；但几位作者也足可自得地知道，他们这本区区小册子所掀起的轰动名声，在本国出版史上几乎无出其右。

## Part / 部

===Original===

VII Ghosts and Witchcrafts

===Chinese===

第七部 幽灵与巫术

### XXXIV / 第三十四章

===Original===

Haunted houses﻿—​A night spent alone with a ghost﻿—​Kirby, the actor﻿—​Colt’s pistols versus hobgoblins﻿—​The mystery explained.
A great many persons believe more or less in haunted houses. In almost every community there is some building that has had a mysterious history. This is true in all countries, and among all races and nations. Indeed it is to this very fact that the ingenious author of the “Twenty-seventh-street Ghost” may attribute his success in creating such an excitement. In fact, I will say, “under the rose,” he predicted his hopes of success entirely upon this weakness in human nature. Even in “this day and age of the world” there are hundreds of deserted buildings which are looked upon with awe, or terror, or superstitious interest. They have frightened their former inhabitants away, and left the buildings in the almost undisputed possession of real moles, bats, and owls, and imaginary goblins and sprites.
In the course of my travels in both hemispheres I have been amazed at the great number of such cases that have come under my personal observation.
