The “agent,” or “secretary,” or “prime-minister,” or whatever he might be called, was very mysterious as to the objects, purposes, history, and antecedents of her Highness, and the quidnuncs were in despair until, one morning, the *Bristol Mirror*, then a leading paper, came out with a flaring announcement, expressing the pleasure it felt in acquainting the public with the fact, that a very eminent and interesting foreign personage had arrived from her home in the remotest East to proffer His Majesty, George III, the unobstructed commerce and friendship of her realm, which was as remarkable for its untold wealth as for its marvelous beauty. The lady was described as a befitting representative of the loveliness and opulence of this new Golconda and Ophir in one, since her matchless wealth and munificence were approached only by her ravishing personal charms. The other papers took up the topic, and were even more extravagant. *Felix Farley’s Journal* gave a long narrative of her wanderings and extraordinary adventures in the uttermost East, as gleaned, of course, from her garrulous agent. The island of her chief residence was described as being of vast extent and fertility, immensely rich and populous, and possessing many rare and beautiful arts unknown to the nations of Europe. The princess had become desperately enamored of a certain young Englishman of high rank, who had been shipwrecked on her coast, but had afterward escaped, and as she learned, safely reached a port in China, and thence departed for Europe. The Princess had hereupon set out upon her journeyings over the world in search of him. In order to facilitate her enterprise, and softened by the deep affection she felt for the son of Albion, she had determined to break through the usages of her country, and form an alliance with that of her beloved.

===Chinese===

卡里布公主，又名“群岛女王”。

1812 年，布里斯托尔（Bristol）是大不列颠第二大商业都市，尤其拥有庞大的东印度贸易。当地居民中不乏被公认精明过人、且家财万贯的富商；此外还有好一批贵族世家，受到追随“高贵门第”的卑躬屈膝式谄媚敬仰。总的来说，布里斯托尔也算得上是一座颇为时髦、富裕、有教养而又聪慧的城市——就其本身条件而言。

1812 — 13 年冬日里一个晴朗的夜晚，布里斯托尔首屈一指的客栈“白狮旅馆”（White Lion hotel）因一则消息而陷入一阵美妙的躁动：据报，一位绝代佳人且富可敌国的贵妇——卡里布公主（Princess Cariboo）——刚刚乘船自东方某港口抵达。她的代理人——一个皮肤黝黑、干瘪瘦小、说着蹩脚英语的亚洲人——通报了这一消息，并预订了旅馆里最豪华的一整套房间。不用说，上下人等立刻忙得脚不沾地地筹备起来；这位既美貌又出身高贵的神秘异乡客引发了一波又一波越来越强烈的兴奋浪潮，直到这位佳人终于屈尊驾临。她于十点钟左右浩浩荡荡地抵达，随行两三辆马车塞满了仆役与行李——仆役个个肤色晦暗奇特、服饰怪诞，行李的形状与质地更是奇异到难以想象。无论东道主还是住客，众人热切的期待不仅未曾落空，甚至被这位神秘来客的绝世美貌、她与随从那一身华贵非凡的东方盛装，以及那堆庞大无比的行李彻底超越——行李沉重气派这一点，在英国旅馆里与在世上任何别处一样极具分量。况且这位异乡贵客打赏仆役出手极阔绰，赏钱向来一律付金币。

众人很快便发现，这位贵妇人连一个英语单词也不会说，就连她的代理人——那个皮肤黝黑、野性十足、古灵精怪的小个子——英语也说得磕磕巴巴，提出的所有要求全是用极其“支离破碎的洋泾浜英语（broken China）”表达的。店老板心想这正是开出天价账单的绝佳良机，于是排场十足地布置了房间并备下盛筵，蜡烛高烧、侍者如云、铃声不断、礼数周全。然而这位贵妇宛如真正的公主，虽然颇为满意且和蔼可亲，却把这一切视作理所当然，并执意只用她自己的厨子——那是个扁脸、塌鼻梁、穿着黄马裤、长着杏仁眼的东方人，光溜溜剃净的脑壳后脑勺垂着一根长辫子。这位先生拎着奇形怪状的小铜锅在厨房院子里跑来跑去，按英国仆役的看法，在里头用草药、大米、咖喱粉等等烹制出各种“魔鬼般的怪菜”供女主人享用。接下来的三四天里，白狮旅馆因“公主”及其大批随从的奇特举止而近乎陷入狂热。公主天天穿着布里斯托尔最八卦的市民也从未见过的奇装异服争奇斗艳，随从们则沉迷于翻跟头杂耍和震耳欲聋的吟唱，几乎把街坊邻里吵聋。他们尤其喜欢唱一种古怪的鼻音民谣，每晚午夜开唱，一直嚎到天快亮，把邻居们折磨得几近发狂。那声音听起来像一群暴怒野猫组成的委员会在开音乐会，末尾总拖着长长的呜咽调子，先是低声抽泣，随即猛地一扯嗓子，爆发出响亮而单调的嚎叫。不过话说回来，这些睡在女主人套房隔壁垫子上、吃着自家御厨菜肴的随从们总体上倒极有礼貌、安分守己，而且望向公主的眼神流露出无比的敬畏。那位“代理人”——或称“秘书”、“首相”，随你怎么叫——对殿下的来意、目的、身世与来历始终讳莫如深，急得全城八卦迷抓耳挠腮；直到一天早晨，当时当地的主流报纸《布里斯托尔镜报》（*Bristol Mirror*）刊出一则醒目通告，欣然向公众宣布：一位极为显赫且引人入胜的外国贵人已从她位于最遥远东方的国土启程抵达，特来向乔治三世陛下献上她那以无尽财富与绝世美景著称之王国的自由通商与友谊。报道将这位女士描绘成这座集“戈尔康达”与“俄斐”于一体的新奇宝地的绝佳化身，因为世间唯有她倾国倾城的美貌方能与她无与伦比的财富与慷慨相匹敌。其他报纸纷纷跟进，吹嘘得更加天花乱坠。《费利克斯·法利日志》（*Felix Farley’s Journal*）刊登了长篇连载，详述她在极东之地的漫游与传奇历险——自然全是从她那健谈的代理人嘴里套出来的。她主居的岛屿被形容为幅员辽阔、土地肥沃、富庶繁华、人口稠密，且掌握着许多欧洲各国闻所未闻的珍奇绝技。据说公主疯狂爱上了一位年轻的高贵英国绅士——此人在她的海岸遭遇海难，后来逃生，据她打听已平安抵达中国某港口并启程返回欧洲。公主于是毅然踏上环球寻夫之旅。为了促成这桩姻缘，兼之被对这位“阿尔比恩（Albion）之子”的深情所软化，她决定打破本国陈规，与心上人的祖国结成同盟。

===Original===

Such were the statements everywhere put in circulation; and when the Longbows of the place got full hold of it, Gulliver, Peter Wilkins, and Sinbad the Sailor were completely eclipsed. Diamonds as big as hen’s eggs, and pearls the size of hazelnuts, were said to be the commonest buttons and ornaments the Princess wore, and her silks and shawls were set beyond all price.
The announcement of this romantic and mysterious history, this boundless wealth, this interesting mission from majesty to majesty in person and the reality which everyone could see of so much grace and beauty, supplied all that was wanting to set the upper-tendom of the place in a blaze. It was hardly etiquette for a royal visitor to receive much company before having been presented at Court; but as this princely lady came from a point so far outside of the pale of Christendom, and all its formalities, it was deemed not out of place, to show her befitting attentions; and the ice once broken, there was no arresting the flood. The aristocracy of Bristol vied with each other in seeing who should be first and most extravagant in their demonstrations. The street in front of the “White Lion” was day after day blocked up, with elegant equipages, and her reception-rooms thronged with “fair women and brave men.” Milliners and mantuamakers pressed upon the lovely and mysterious Princess Cariboo the most exquisite hats, dresses, and laces, just to acquaint her with the fashionable style and solicit her distinguished patronage; dry-goodsmen sent her rare patterns of their costliest and richest stuffs, perfumers their most exquisite toilet-cases, filled with odors sweet; jewellers, their most superb sets of gems; and florists and visitors nearly suffocated her with the scarcest and most delicate exotics. Pictures, sketches, and engravings, oil-paintings, and portraits on ivory of her rapturous admirers, poured in from all sides, and her own fine form and features were reproduced by a score of artists. Daily she was fêted, and nightly serenaded, until the Princess Cariboo became the furore of the United Kingdom. Magnificent entertainments were given her in private mansions; and at length, to cap the climax, Mr. Worrall, the Recorder of Bristol, managed, by his influence, to bring about for her a grand municipal reception in the town-hall, and people from far and near thronged to it in thousands.
In the meantime the papers were gravely trying to make out whether the Cariboo country meant some remote portion of Japan, or the Island of Borneo, or some comparatively unfamiliar archipelago in the remotest East, and the *Mirror* was publishing type expressly cut for the purpose of representing the characters of the language in which the Princess spoke and wrote. They were certainly very uncouth, and pretended sages, who knew very well that there was no one to contradict them, declared that they were “ancient Coptic!”

===Chinese===

诸如此类的说辞在街头巷尾四处流传；等到当地那帮吹牛大王接手发挥一番后，格列佛、彼得·威尔金斯乃至水手辛巴达统统被比得黯然失色。人人都传说鸡蛋般大小的钻石与榛子般大小的珍珠不过是公主身上最寻常的纽扣与佩饰，至于她的丝绸与披肩更是无价之宝。

这番浪漫神秘的身世、取之不尽的财富、国王亲赴国王御前的动人使命，再加上众人亲眼所见的优雅仪态与绝世容颜，万事俱备，顿时把当地上流社会点燃得烈火烹油。按宫廷礼仪，王室贵宾在尚未觐见英王之前原不该大肆会客；但鉴于这位尊贵公主来自基督教世界及其繁文缛节之外的极远之邦，向她献上应有的殷勤倒也不算失礼；而坚冰一旦打破，洪水便再也无可阻挡。布里斯托尔的贵族名流争先恐后，竞相攀比谁的排场最先、谁的献礼最阔绰。“白狮旅馆”门前的街道一连数日被华丽马车堵得水泄不通，她的会客厅里挤满了“窈窕淑女与英武男儿”。女帽商与女装裁缝硬塞给美丽神秘的卡里布公主最精致的礼帽、衣裙与蕾丝，只求让她熟悉时下流行款式并垂青惠顾；布匹商送来最昂贵华美面料的珍稀样板；香水商献上装满芬芳香露的最考究梳妆匣；珠宝商奉上最璀璨夺目的成套宝石；花商与访客们用最稀罕娇贵的异域奇花几乎把她淹没。素描、版画、油画以及倾慕得神魂颠倒的追求者们的象牙微雕肖像从四面八方涌来，她本人那曼妙的身姿与容颜也被二十来位画家争相临摹。白天宴饮不断，夜间小夜曲不绝，直到“卡里布公主”成了轰动整个联合王国的狂潮。私人豪宅里为她举办了一场又一场盛大宴会；最后，更是将气氛推至顶点——布里斯托尔市首席法官沃勒尔先生（Mr. Worrall）动用自身影响力，在市政厅为她操办了一场盛大的市政欢迎会，远近成千上万民众蜂拥而至。

与此同时，各大报纸正一本正经地考证“卡里布国”究竟是指日本某处偏远角落、婆罗洲岛，还是极东某片鲜为人知的群岛；《镜报》甚至专门刻制了一套铅字，用来排印公主口说手写的文字。那些鬼画符确实古怪粗陋，可自封博学的砖家明知无人能够反驳，便信誓旦旦地宣称那是“古科普特语（ancient Coptic）！”

===Original===

Upon reading the sequel of the story, one is irresistibly reminded of the ancient Roman inscription discovered by one of Dickens’ characters, which some irreverent rogue subsequently declared to be nothing more nor less than “Bil Stumps His Mark.”
All this went on for about a fortnight, until the whole town and a good deal of the surrounding country had made complete fools of themselves, and only the “naughty little boys” in the streets held out against the prevailing mania, probably because they were not admitted to the sport. Their salutations took the form of an inharmonious thoroughfare-ballad, the chorus of which terminated with:

> “Boo! hoo! hoo! 
> 
> And who’s the Princess Cariboo?”

yelled out at the top of their voices.
At length one day, the luggage of her Highness was embarked upon a small vessel to be taken round by water to London, while she announced, through her “agent,” her intention to reach the capital by post-coaching.
Of course, the most superb traveling-carriages and teams were placed at her disposal; but, courteously declining all these offers, she set out in the nighttime with a hired establishment, attended by her retinue.
Days and weeks rolled on, and yet no announcement came of the arrival of her Highness at London or at any of the intervening cities after the first two or three towns eastward of Bristol. Inquiry began to be made, and, after long and patient but unavailing search, it became apparent to divers and sundry dignitaries in the old town that somebody had been very particularly “sold.”

===Chinese===

读到这桩故事的下文时，任谁都难免想起狄更斯笔下某个人物发现的那块“古罗马铭文”——后来被一个大不敬的混账拆穿，那上面刻的压根儿不是别的，正是“比尔·斯坦普斯留记”（Bil Stumps His Mark）。

整出闹剧持续了大约两星期，直到全城以及周边大片乡下人都把自己耍成了彻头彻尾的傻瓜，唯独街上的“顽皮小男孩”们顶住了这股狂潮——多半是因为人家不带他们玩儿。他们致敬的方式是唱起一首跑调的街头童谣，副歌结尾处扯开嗓门大吼：

> “呜！呼！呼！
> 
> 卡里布公主到底是谁呀？”

终于有一天，殿下的行李被装上一艘小船走水路运往伦敦，而她自己则通过“代理人”宣布打算乘驿车前往首都。

理所当然，城里最气派的旅行马车与骏马纷纷送上门供她差遣；但她彬彬有礼地谢绝了所有美意，在一个夜晚雇了一支车队，带着随从悄然启程。

几天过去，几周过去，然而除了布里斯托尔以东头两三座小城外，伦敦乃至沿途任何城市却始终不见殿下驾临的半点消息。人们开始四处打听，经过漫长耐心却毫无结果的搜寻之后，这座古城里的各路达官显贵终于恍然大悟：某些人这次着实被人狠狠“涮了一把”。

===Original===

The landlord at the “White Lion” who had accepted the agent’s order for £1,000 on a Calcutta firm in London; poor Mr. Worrall, who had been Master of Ceremonies at the town hall affair, and had spent large sums of money; and the tradespeople and others who sent their finest goods, all felt that they had “heard something drop.” The Princess Cariboo had disappeared as mysteriously as she came.
For years, the people of Bristol were unmercifully ridiculed throughout the entire Kingdom on account of this affair, and burlesque songs and plays immortalized its incidents for successive seasons.
One of these insisted that the Princess was no other than an actress of more notoriety than note, humbly born in the immediate vicinity of the old city, where she practiced this gigantic hoax, and that she had been assisted in it by a set of dissolute young noblemen and actors, who furnished the money she had spent, got up the oriental dresses, published the fibs, and fomented the excitement. At all events, the net profit to her and her confederates in the affair must have been some £10,000.
Within a few months, and since the first publication of the above paragraphs, the English newspapers have recorded the death of the “Princess Cariboo,” who it appears afterward married in her own rank in life and spent a considerable number of years of usefulness in the leech trade﻿—an occupation not without a metaphorical likeness to her early and more ambitious exploit.

===Chinese===

白狮旅馆的老板曾收下代理人开在伦敦一家加尔各答商号名下的 1,000 英镑汇票；可怜的沃勒尔先生曾任市政厅欢迎会的司仪并自掏腰包破费巨款；还有那些送出自家最上等货物的商贩与各界人士——此刻人人心中都觉得仿佛“听见什么东西砸在地上了”。卡里布公主就如同她神秘降临一般，神秘得无影无踪。

此后好些年，布里斯托尔人因这桩丑事遭到全英国上下毫不留情的嘲笑，一连好几个演出季都有讽刺歌曲与滑稽戏把这出闹剧搬上舞台，使之流芳百世。

其中一出戏一口咬定，这位公主并非别人，正是生于这座古城近郊贫寒人家的某位恶名远大于名气的女演员，就在家门口演了这出弥天大骗局；助她成事的则是一群放荡不羁的年轻贵族与演员，他们掏出她挥霍的银子、置办东方服饰、在报上编造谎言并煽风点火。无论如何，她与同伙在这桩买卖里净赚的利润必定高达约 10,000 英镑。

就在近几个月内——自上述段落首次刊发以来——英国报纸报道了“卡里布公主”去世的消息；据称她后来嫁了个门当户对的寻常丈夫，在蚂蟥（水蛭，leech，兼喻吸血寄生者）买卖行当里安分守己、颇有建树地度过了好些年光阴——这营生与她早年那场更具野心的壮举倒也不乏几分隐喻上的神似。

### XL / 第四十章

===Original===

Count Cagliostro, alias Joseph Balsamo, known also as “Cursed Joe.”
One of the most striking, amusing, and instructive pages in the history of humbug is the life of Count Alessandro di Cagliostro, whose real name was Joseph or Giuseppe Balsamo. He was born at Palermo, in 1743, and very early began to manifest his brilliant talents for roguery.
He ran away from his first boarding-school, at the age of eleven or twelve, getting up a masquerade of goblins, by the aid of some scampish schoolfellows, which frightened the monkish watchmen of the gates away from their posts, nearly dead with terror. He had gained little at this school, except the pleasant surname of Beppo Maldetto (or cursed Joe.) At the age of thirteen he was a second time expelled from the convent of Cartegirone, belonging to the order of Benfratelli, the good fathers having in vain endeavored to train him up in the way he should go.
While in this convent, the boy was in charge of the apothecary, and probably picked up more or less of the smattering of chemistry and physics which he afterwards used. His final offense was a ridiculous and characteristic one. He was a greedy and thievish fellow, and was by way of penalty set to read aloud about the ancient martyrs, those dry though pious old gentlemen, while the monks ate dinner. Thus put to what he liked least, and deprived of what he liked best, he impudently extemporized, instead of the stories of holy agonies, all the indecorous scandal he could think of about the more notorious disreputable women of Palermo, putting their names instead of those of the martyrs.
After this, Master Joe proceeded to distinguish himself by forging opera-tickets, and even documents of various kinds, indiscriminate pilfering and swindling, interpreting visions, conjuring, and finally, it is declared, a touch of genuine assassination.

===Chinese===

卡廖斯特罗伯爵，又名约瑟夫·巴尔萨莫，外号“被诅咒的乔”。

在欺罔（humbug）史上，最引人注目、风趣横生而又发人深省的篇章之一，莫过于亚历山德罗·迪·卡廖斯特罗伯爵（Count Alessandro di Cagliostro）的生平——他的真名是约瑟夫或朱塞佩·巴尔萨莫（Giuseppe Balsamo）。他于 1743 年生于巴勒莫，年纪轻轻便早早显露出干无赖勾当的过人才华。

十一二岁时，他伙同几个调皮捣蛋的同窗策划了一场“群魔化妆游行”，把守门的修士门卫吓得魂飞魄散、弃岗而逃，自己趁机从头一所寄宿学校逃之夭夭。他在那所学校里几乎没学到什么，唯独挣得了个雅号“Beppo Maldetto”（即“被诅咒的乔”）。十三岁时，他又第二次被逐出善兄弟修会（Benfratelli）所属的卡尔塔吉罗内修道院（Cartegirone），好心的神父们曾试图把他引向正途，终究徒劳无功。

在这座修道院期间，这小子曾负责照管药房，多半正是在那儿学到了一鳞半爪日后派上大用场的化学与医理皮毛。他最终被逐的罪过既滑稽又极符合他的本性：他本是个贪吃又手脚不干净的家伙，受罚在修士们用正餐时站在一旁高声朗读古代殉道者的事迹——那些虽虔诚却枯燥乏味的老先生。被逼着干最讨厌的活儿、又被剥夺了最爱的吃喝，他竟厚颜无耻地临场瞎编，把神圣的殉道苦难换成了自己所能想出的关于巴勒莫最臭名昭著的淫荡女人的种种下流丑闻，硬把她们的名字安在殉道者头上。

打那以后，小乔少爷便开始大显身手：伪造歌剧院门票乃至各式各样的文书、见啥偷啥、四处坑蒙拐骗、解梦算卦、装神弄鬼，最后据称甚至还沾过一两回货真价实的暗杀。

===Original===

Pretty soon he made a foolish, greedy goldsmith, one Marano, believe that there was a treasure hidden in the sand on the seashore near Palermo, and induced the silly man to go one night to dig it up. Having reached the spot, the dupe was made to strip himself to his shirt and drawers, a magic circle was drawn round him with all sorts of raw-head and bloody-bones ceremonies, and Beppo, exhorting him not to leave the ring, lest the spirits should kill him, stepped out of sight to make the incantations to raise them. Almost instantly, six devils, horned, hoofed, tailed, and clawed, breathing fire and smoke, leaped from among the rocks and beat the wretched goldsmith senseless, and almost to death. They were of course Cursed Joe and some confederates; and taking Marano’s money and valuables, they left him. He got home in wretched plight, but had sense enough left to suspect Master Joe, whom he shortly promised, after the Sicilian manner, to assassinate. So Joe ran away from Palermo, and went to Messina. Here he said he fell in with a venerable humbug, named Athlotas, an “Armenian Sage,” who united his talents with Beppo’s own, in making a peculiar preparation of flax and hemp and passing it off upon the people of Alexandria, in Egypt, as a new kind of silk. This feat made not only a sensation but plenty of money; and the two swindlers now traversed Greece, Turkey, and Arabia, in various directions, stirring up the Oriental “old fogies” in amazing style. Harems and palaces, according to Cagliostro’s own apocryphal story, were thrown open to them everywhere, and while the Sharif of Mecuca took Balsao under his high protection, one of the Grand Muftis actually gave him splendid apartments in his own abode. It is only necessary to reflect upon the unbounded reverence felt by all good Mussulmen for these exalted dignitaries, to comprehend the height of distinction thus attained by the Palermo thimble-rigger. But, among the many obscure records that exist in the Italian, French, and German languages, touching this arch impostor, there is a hint of a night adventure in the harem of a high and mighty personage, at Mecca, whereby the latter was put out of doors, with his robes torn and his beard singed, by his own domestics, and left to wander in the streets, while Beppo, in disguise, received the salaams and sequins of the establishment, including the attentions of the fair ones therein caged, for an entire night. His escape to the seacoast after this adventure was almost miraculous; but escape he did, and shortly afterward turned up in Rome, with the title (conferred by himself) of Count Cagliostro, the reputation of enormous wealth, and genuine and enthusiastic letters of recommendation from Pinto, Grand Master of the Knights of Malta. Pinto was an alchemist, and had been fooled to the top of his bent by the cunning Joseph.
These letters introduced our humbug into the first families of Rome; who, like some other first families, were first also as fools. He also married a very beautiful, very shrewd, and very wicked Roman donzella, Lorenza Feliciani by name; and the worthy couple, combining their various talents, and regarding the world as their oyster, at once proceeded to open it in the most scientific style. I cannot follow this wonderful human chameleon in all his transformations under his various names of Fischio, Melissa, Fenice, Anna, Pellegrini, Harat, and Belmonte, nor state the studies and processes by which he picked up sufficient knowledge of physic, chemistry, the hidden properties of numbers, astronomy, astrology, mesmerism, clairvoyance, and the genuine old-fashioned “black art;” but suffice it to say, that he travelled through every part of Europe, and set it in a blaze with excitement.

===Chinese===

没过多久，他哄得一个愚蠢贪婪的金匠——名叫马拉诺（Marano）——深信巴勒莫近旁海滩沙地里埋着一笔宝藏，并诱使那傻瓜某夜前去掘宝。抵达地点后，这冤大头被要求脱得只剩贴身衬衣与衬裤，周围画上魔法阵，做足了骷髅血骨式的吓人仪式；贝波（即小乔）叮嘱他千万别踏出圆圈，免得被鬼魂取了性命，随后便闪身躲到暗处假装念咒招魂。转眼之间，六个长角、生蹄、拖尾巴、伸利爪、口喷烟火的魔鬼从礁石丛中猛扑而出，把可怜的金匠揍得昏死过去、险些一命呜呼。那些魔鬼自然正是“被诅咒的乔”及其几名同伙；他们搜刮走马拉诺的银钱与贵重物品，扬长而去。金匠狼狈不堪地爬回家，脑子倒还算清醒，立刻怀疑上了小乔少爷，照西西里人的规矩发誓不久便要结果他的性命。于是乔连夜逃离巴勒莫，跑到了墨西拿（Messina）。据他自己说，在那儿他遇上了一位德高望重的老骗子——名叫阿特洛塔斯（Athlotas）的“亚美尼亚贤哲”；两人联手施展才华，用亚麻与大麻捣鼓出一种特殊制剂，在埃及亚历山大港冒充新型丝绸大肆兜售。这一手不仅引起轰动，还捞进了大把钞票；随后这俩骗子便分头游历希腊、土耳其与阿拉伯各地，把东方那些“老顽固”搅得天翻地覆。按照卡廖斯特罗自己那套真假难辨的吹嘘，各地的后宫与宫殿无不向他们敞开大门；麦加谢里夫将巴尔萨莫置于自己崇高庇护之下，一位大穆夫提甚至在自家府邸里拨给他华丽的套房。只要想想所有虔诚穆斯林对这些尊贵显要怀有何等无上的敬仰，便不难理解这位来自巴勒莫的街头老千究竟爬到了何等显赫的地位。不过，在意大利文、法文与德文留存的诸多关于这位头号大骗子的隐晦记载中，隐约提到了他在麦加某位权势熏天的大人物后宫里的一场夜间奇遇：那位大人物竟被自己的仆人扯破长袍、烧焦胡须赶出家门流落街头，而乔装改扮的贝波却在府中享用了整整一夜的跪拜礼、金币赏赐，外加笼中佳丽们的殷勤侍奉。那场风流险事之后他逃到海边的经过简直堪称奇迹；但他终究逃了出来，没过多久便现身罗马，顶着（自己封给自己的）卡廖斯特罗伯爵头衔、腰缠万贯的声名，手里还攥着马耳他骑士团大团长平托（Pinto）亲笔写的货真价实、热情洋溢的推荐信——原来平托是个炼金术士，早被狡猾的约瑟夫糊弄得五体投地。

这些推荐信把我们的骗子引荐进了罗马最高贵的名门望族——这些豪门跟世上某些其他名门一样，论蠢也是头等一。他还迎娶了一位绝代美貌、极其精明却又极其邪恶的罗马千金，芳名洛伦扎·费利恰尼（Lorenza Feliciani）；这对绝配夫妻强强联手，把整个世界视作一只生蚝，立刻用最科学的方法撬开大快朵颐。我无暇追踪这只人类变色龙在菲斯基奥、梅利莎、费尼切、安娜、佩莱格里尼、哈拉特、贝尔蒙特等种种化名下的历次蜕变，也无暇细述他靠何种钻研与门道攒下了足够用的医术、化学、数字秘学、天文学、占星术、麦斯麦催眠术、千里眼以及正宗老派“黑魔法”知识；总而言之，他游遍欧洲每一个角落，所到之处无不掀起狂热风潮。

===Original===

There were always enough of silly coxcombs, young and old, of high degree, to be allured by the siren smiles of his “Countess;” and dupes of both sexes everywhere, to swallow his yarns and gape at his juggleries. In the course of his rambles, he paid a visit to his great brother humbug, the Count of St. Germain, in Westphalia, or Schleswig, and it was not long afterward that he began to publish to the world his grand discoveries in Alchemy, of the Philosopher’s Stone, and the Elixir of Life, or Waters of Perpetual Youth. These and many similar wonders were declared to be the result of his investigations under the Arch of Old Egyptian Masonry, which degree he claimed to have revived. This notion of Egyptian Masonry, Cagliostro is said to have found in some manuscripts left by one George Cofton, which fell into our quack’s hands. This degree was to give perfection to human beings, by means of moral and physical regeneration. Of these two the former was to be secured by means of a Pentagon, which removes original sin and renews pristine innocence. The physical kind of regeneration was to be brought about by using the “prime matter” or philosopher’s stone, and the “Acacia,” which two ingredients will give immortal youth. In this new structure, he assumed the title of the “Grand Cophta” and actually claimed the worship of his followers; declaring that the institution had been established by Enoch and Elias, and that he had been summoned by “spiritual” agencies to restore it to its pristine glory. In fact, this pretension, which influenced thousands upon thousands of believers, was one of the most daring impostures that ever saw the light; and it is astounding to think that, so late as 1780, it should, for a long time, have been entirely successful. The preparatory course of exercises for admission to the mystic brotherhood has been described as a series of “purgation, starvation, and desperation,” lasting for forty days! and ending in “physical regeneration” and an immortality on Earth. The celebrated Lavater, a mild and genial, but feeble man, became one of Cagliostro’s disciples, and was bamboozled to his heart’s content﻿—in fact, made to believe that the Count could put the devil into him, or take him out, as the case might be.
The wondrous “Water of Beauty,” that made old wrinkled faces look young, smooth, and blooming again, was the special merchandise of the Countess, and was, of course, in great request among the faded beaux and dowagers of the day, who were easily persuaded of their own restored loveliness. The transmutation of baser metals into gold usually terminated in the transmigration of all the gold his victims had into the Count’s own purse.

===Chinese===

世上永远少不了大批老少皆有、出身显贵的蠢纨绔，甘愿拜倒在“伯爵夫人”海妖般的迷人微笑裙下；也到处有男男女女的冤大头，生吞他的荒诞故事，对着他的戏法目瞪口呆。漫游途中，他还专程去威斯特伐利亚或石勒苏益格拜访过他的同行大骗子圣日耳曼伯爵（Count of St. Germain）；没过多久，他便开始向全世界公布他在炼金术上的伟大发现——贤者之石与长生不老药，又称“青春永驻之水”。凡此种种奇迹，他均宣称是自己在“古埃及共济会拱门”之下潜心钻研的成果，并号称由他亲手复兴了这一秘传阶位。据说“埃及共济会”这一构想，是卡廖斯特罗从落入他手里的某个名叫乔治·科夫顿（George Cofton）遗留的手稿里翻出来的。这一阶位号称能通过道德与肉体的双重重生，使人类臻于至善至美。二者之中，前者须借由“五芒星”达成，以洗去原罪、重焕太初纯真；后者则须服用“太初原质”（即贤者之石）与“金合欢”（Acacia），这两味药引合用便可赐予不朽青春。在这套新架构中，他自封“大科普塔”（Grand Cophta），竟要求信徒像神明般膜拜自己；他宣称该会由圣经先祖以诺与以利亚创立，而他本人则是受“灵界”召唤前来重振其昔日荣光的。说实话，这一蛊惑了成千上万信徒的妄言，堪称有史以来最胆大妄为的骗局之一；一想到晚至 1780 年，它居然还能长期大获成功，实在令人骇然。加入这一神秘兄弟会的预备修行课程，据称是一连串“清肠、饥饿与绝望”的苦修，足足持续四十天！最终便可达成“肉体重生”与尘世长生。就连大名鼎鼎的拉瓦特尔（Lavater）——一位温和敦厚却意志薄弱的学者——也成了卡廖斯特罗的门徒，被糊弄得心满意足，甚至深信伯爵随时能把魔鬼塞进他体内，或者再抽出来。

至于能让满脸皱纹的老脸重返青春、光洁红润的神奇“驻颜神水”，则是伯爵夫人的独家专卖，自然在当时容颜凋零的公子哥与贵妇遗孀中间抢手无比，她们轻而易举便被哄得相信自己重焕绝世芳华。而所谓点石成金术，最终结局通常都是把受害者兜里所有的真金白银统统“点”进伯爵自己的荷包里。

===Original===

In 1776, the Count and Countess came to London. Here, funnily enough, they fell into the hands of a gambler, a shyster, and a female scamp, who together tormented them almost to death, because the Count would not pick them out lucky numbers to gamble by. They persecuted him fairly into jail, and plagued and outswindled him so awfully, that, after a time, the poor Count sneaked back to the Continent with only fifty pounds left out of three thousand which he had brought with him.
One incident of Cagliostro’s English experience was the affair of the “Arsenical Pigs”﻿—a notice of which may be found in the “Public Advertiser,” of London of September 3, 1786. A Frenchman named Morande, was at that time editing there a paper in his own language, entitled *Le Courrier de l’Europe*, and lost no opportunity to denounce the Count as a humbug. Cagliostro, at length, irritated by these repeated attacks, published in the *Advertiser* an open challenge, offering to forfeit five thousand guineas if Morande should not be found dead in his bed on the morning after partaking of the flesh of a pig, to be selected by himself from among a drove fattened by the Count﻿—the cooking, etc., all to be done at Morande’s own house, and under his own eye. The time was fixed for this singular repast, but when it came round, the French Editor “backed down” completely, to the great delight of his opponent and his credulous followers.
Cagliostro and his spouse now resumed their travels upon the Continent, and, by their usual arts and trades, in a great measure renewed their fallen fortunes. Among other new dodges, he now assumed so supernatural a piety that (he said) he could distinguish an unbeliever by the smell! which, of course, was just the opposite of the “odor of sanctity.” The Count’s claim to have lived for hundreds of years was, by some, thoroughly believed. He ascribed his immortality to his own Elixir, and his comparatively youthful appearance to his “Water of Beauty,” his Countess readily assisting him by speaking of her son, a Colonel in the Dutch service, fifty years old, while she appeared scarcely more than twenty.
At length, in Rome, he and the Countess fell into the clutches of the Holy Office; and both having been tried for their manifold offenses against the Church, were found guilty, and, in spite of their contrition and eager confessions, immured for life; the Count within the walls of the Castle of Sante Leone, in the Duchy of Urbino, where, after eight years’ imprisonment, he died in 1795, and the Countess in a suburban convent, where she died some time after.
The portraits of Cagliostro, of which a number are extant, are pictures of a strong-built, bull-necked, fat, gross man, with a snub nose, a vulgar face, a look of sensuality and low hypocritical cunning.
The celebrated story of “The Diamond Necklace,” in which Cagliostro, Marie Antoinette, the Cardinal de Rohan, and others were mixed in such a hodgepodge of rascality and folly, must form a narrative by itself.

===Chinese===

1776 年，伯爵与伯爵夫人来到伦敦。说来好笑，在这儿他们竟落入了一名赌徒、一名讼棍和一名女无赖的魔掌；这三人因伯爵不肯替他们挑出稳赢的彩票幸运数字，联手把夫妻俩折磨得死去活来。他们硬是把伯爵逼进了大牢，百般敲诈勒索、黑吃黑，搞得可怜的伯爵过了一阵子只得灰溜溜地逃回欧洲大陆——随身带来的三千英镑，最后只剩下了区区五十英镑。

卡廖斯特罗在英格兰经历中的一桩插曲，便是“砒霜肥猪”事件——此事的启事至今仍可在 1786 年 9 月 3 日的伦敦《公众广告报》（*Public Advertiser*）上查到。当时有位名叫莫朗德（Morande）的法国人在伦敦主编一份法文报纸《欧洲信使报》（*Le Courrier de l’Europe*），逢机会便痛斥伯爵是个大骗子。卡廖斯特罗被这番连番攻击激怒，终于在《广告报》上刊登了一封公开战书，扬言：请莫朗德亲手从伯爵养肥的一群猪里挑出一头——烹调等一切工序全在莫朗德自己家里、在他自己眼皮底下完成——倘若莫朗德吃下猪肉的次日清晨没有暴毙在床上，伯爵甘愿输掉五千几尼！这场奇特盛宴的日期都已定好，可临到头，那位法国主编却彻底“打了退堂鼓”，令他的对手及其轻信的信徒们欢呼雀跃。

卡廖斯特罗与妻子随即重返欧洲大陆漫游，重操旧业施展惯用伎俩，在很大程度上重振了衰落的家业。在其他新花招之中，他此刻又装出一副超凡脱俗的虔诚模样，声称自己单凭气味便能嗅出不信神的家伙——这自然恰恰与“圣洁之芳”反其道而行之。伯爵自称已活了数百年之久，居然真有不少人深信不疑。他将自己的长生不老归功于自制灵药，将相对年轻的容貌归功于“驻颜神水”；而他的伯爵夫人也配合得天衣无缝，逢人便谈起自己那位在荷兰军队服役、年方五十的上校儿子，而她自己看上去却简直只有二十岁出头。

最终在罗马，他与伯爵夫人落入了宗教裁判所（Holy Office）的掌心；两人因犯下累累冒犯教会之罪受审定罪，尽管苦苦忏悔、争相招供，仍被判处终身监禁：伯爵被囚禁在乌尔比诺公国（Duchy of Urbino）圣莱奥城堡（Castle of Sante Leone）的高墙之内，坐了八年牢后于 1795 年死在狱中；伯爵夫人则被关进城郊一座修道院，不久后亦死于院内。

卡廖斯特罗流传至今的画像不少，画中皆是一个体格粗壮、脖颈如牛、肥硕臃肿的汉子，生着一只塌鼻梁、一张粗俗的脸，眼神里透着贪婪好色与卑劣虚伪的狡黠。

至于那桩把卡廖斯特罗、玛丽·安托瓦内特王后、罗昂红衣主教（Cardinal de Rohan）等人搅进同一锅无赖与愚蠢大杂烩的著名“钻石项链事件”，则必须另辟专章单独叙说了。

### XLI / 第四十一章

===Original===

The diamond necklace.
In my sketch of Joseph Balsamo, alias the Count Alessandro de Cagliostro, I referred to the affair of the diamond necklace, known in French history as the *Collier de la Reine*, or Queen’s necklace, from the manner in which the name and reputation of Marie Antoinette, the consort of Louis XVI, became entangled in it. I shall now give a brief account of this celebrated imposition﻿—perhaps the boldest and shrewdest ever known, and almost wholly the work of a woman.
On the Quai de la Ferraille, not far from the Pont Neuf, stood the establishment, part shop, part manufactory, of Messrs. Boehmer & Bassange, the most celebrated jewelers of their day. After triumphs which had given them worldwide fame during the reign of Louis XV, and made them fabulously rich, they determined, with the advent of Louis XVI, to eclipse all their former efforts and crown the professional glory of their lives. Their correspondents in every chief jewel market of the world were summoned to aid their enterprise, and in the course of some two or three years they succeeded in collecting the finest and most remarkable diamonds that could be procured in the whole world of commerce.
The next idea was to combine all these superb fragments in one grand ornament to grace the form of beauty. A necklace was the article fixed upon, and the best experience and most delicate taste that Europe could boast were expended on the design. Each and every diamond was specially set and faced in such manner as to reveal its excellence to the utmost advantage, and all were arranged together in the style best calculated to harmonize their united effect. Form, shape, and the minutest shades of color were studied, and the result, after many attempts and many failures, and the anxious labor of many months, was the most exquisite triumph that the genius of the lapidary and the goldsmith could conceive.
The whole necklace consisted of three triple rows of diamonds, or nine rows in all, containing eight hundred faultless gems. The triple rows fell away from each in the most graceful and flexible curves over each side of the breast and each shoulder of the wearer, the curves starting from the throat, whence a magnificent pendant, depending from a single knot of diamonds, each as large as a hazelnut, hung down halfway upon the bosom in the design of a cross and crown, surrounded by the lilies of the royal house﻿—the lilies themselves dangling on stems which were strung with smaller jewels. Rich clusters and festoons spread from the loop over each shoulder, and the central loop on the back of the neck was joined in a pattern of emblematic magnificence corresponding with that in front.
It was in 1782 that this grand work was finally completed, and the happy owners gloated with delight over a monument of skill as matchless in its way as the Pyramids themselves. But, alas! the necklace might as well have been constructed of the common boulders piled in those same pyramids as of the finest jewels of the mine, for all the good it seemed destined to bring the poor jewelers, beyond the rapture of beholding it and calling it theirs.
The necklace was worth 1,500,000 francs, equivalent to more than $300,000 in gold, as money then went, or nearly $500,000 in gold, nowadays. Rather too large a sum to keep locked up in a casket, the reader will confess! And then it seems that Messrs. Boehmer & Bassange had not entirely paid for it yet. They had ten creditors on the diamonds in different countries, and an immense capital still locked up in their other jewelry.
Of course, then, after their first delight had subsided, they were most anxious to sell an article that had to be constantly and painfully watched, and that might so easily disappear. How many a nimble-fingered and stouthearted rogue would not, in those days, have imperiled a dozen lives to clutch that blazing handful of dross, convertible into an Elysium of pomp and pleasure! It would hardly have been a safe noonday plaything in moral Gotham, let alone the dissolute Paris of eighty years ago!

===Chinese===

钻石项链事件。

在前文勾勒约瑟夫·巴尔萨莫（即亚历山德罗·迪·卡廖斯特罗伯爵）的生平素描时，我曾提及那桩在法国历史上称为“王后项链”（*Collier de la Reine*）的事件——只因路易十六的王后玛丽·安托瓦内特的芳名与声誉被深深卷入其中。眼下，我便来简述这桩举世闻名的骗局——它或许是有史以来最大胆、最狡黠的诈骗案，而且几乎完全出自一名女子之手。

在距离新桥（Pont Neuf）不远的废铁码头（Quai de la Ferraille），坐落着当时最负盛名的御用珠宝商伯默与巴桑日先生（Messrs. Boehmer & Bassange）的商号，一半是铺面，一半是工坊。他们在路易十五统治时期已屡创杰作，享誉全球且积累了富可敌国的家财；路易十六登基伊始，他们便立志要超越过往一切成就，为毕生职业生涯加冕至高荣光。他们向全世界每一个主要珠宝市场的代理商发出征召，耗时两三年，终于搜罗齐了整个商业世界所能寻得的最上等、最非凡的钻石。

紧接着的构想，便是将所有这些稀世珍宝熔于一炉，打造成一件装点绝世芳华的旷世首饰。他们选定了项链这一形制，并将全欧洲所能夸耀的顶尖工艺经验与最细腻的审美品味倾注于设计之中。每一颗钻石都经过独门镶嵌与切面打磨，以最大限度绽放其璀璨光华，全部宝石又以最和谐统一的整体格局排布衔接。形制、轮廓乃至最细微的色泽深浅都经过反复推敲；经历无数次尝试与失败、耗费数月呕心沥血之后，终于诞生了宝石匠与金匠天才所能构想出的最精致杰作。

整条项链由三组三排钻石构成——共计九排，镶嵌着八百颗完美无瑕的钻石。三排钻链自佩戴者颈喉处起垂，以最优雅灵动的弧线垂落于胸前两侧与双肩之上；颈喉正中一枚由硕大如榛子的单颗钻石结成的扣环下，悬垂着一尊华美绝伦的吊坠，垂落至胸口正中，造型为十字架与王冠，周围簇拥着王室百合花徽——百合花本身垂悬在串满小颗宝石的细枝上。丰饶的宝石簇与花彩自双肩扣环向四周铺展，颈后正中的主扣环亦以与胸前相呼应的象征性华丽纹样衔接。

1782 年，这件伟大作品终于完工，欣喜若狂的东主们对着这座技艺丰碑痴迷赞叹，宛如面对金字塔本身般无与伦比。然而，唉！对于这两位可怜的珠宝商而言，这条项链除了供他们饱览并自诩拥有之外，简直跟金字塔里堆砌的寻常巨石没什么两样，半点儿福气也没能给他们带来。

这条项链价值 1,500,000 法郎，按当时的币值折合逾 300,000 美元黄金，按如今币值则将近 500,000 美元黄金。读者诸君定会承认，这笔巨款实在太庞大，断不宜长久锁在一只首饰盒里！况且伯默与巴桑日先生似乎连货款都尚未付清。他们在各国还有十位钻石债主，另有庞大资金压在其他珠宝存货里。

因此，最初的狂喜消退之后，他们自然心急如焚地想脱手这件必须日夜提心吊胆看守、又极易失窃的烫手山芋。在那个年代，不知有多少手脚麻利、胆大包天的盗贼甘愿拿十二条命去搏一搏，只为抓走那一把熠熠生辉、转手便能换来极乐享乐与荣华富贵的石头！别说是八十年前风气靡乱的巴黎，哪怕放在如今道德严谨的纽约，这玩意儿大白天摆出来也绝非安全玩物！

===Original===

The first thought, of course, that kindled in the breasts of Boehmer and Bassange was, that the only proper resting-place for their matchless bauble was the snowy neck of the Queen Marie Antoinette, then the admired and beloved of all! Her peerless beauty alone could live in the glow of such supernal splendor, and the French throne was the only one in Christendom that could sustain such glittering weight. Moreover, the Queen had already once been a good customer to the court jewelers, for in 1774 she bought four diamonds of them for $75,000.
Louis XV would not have hesitated to fling it on the shoulders of the Du Barry, and Louis XVI, in spite of his odd notions upon economy and just administration, easily listened to the delicate insinuations of his court-jewelers; and, one fine morning, laid the necklace in its casket on the table of his Queen. Her Majesty, for a moment, yielded to the promptings of feminine weakness, and danced and laughed with the glee of an overjoyed child in the new sunshine of those burning, sparkling, dazzling gems. Once and once only she placed it on her neck and breast, and probably the world has never before or since seen such a countenance in such a setting. It was almost the head of an angel shining in the glory of the spheres. But a better thought prevailed, and quickly removing it, she, with a wave of her beautiful hand, declined the gift and besought the King to apply the sum to any other purpose that would be useful or honorable to France, whose finances were sadly straitened. “We want ships of war more than we do necklaces,” said she. The King was really delighted at this act of the Queen’s, and the incident soon becoming widely known, gave the latter immense popularity for at least twenty-four hours after it occurred. In fact, the amount was really applied to the construction of a grand line-of-battle ship called the Suffren, after the great Admiral of that name.
Boehmer, who seems to have been the business manager of the jeweler firm, found his necklace as troublesome as the cobbler did the elephant he won in a raffle, and tried so perseveringly to induce the Queen to buy it, that he became a real torment. She seems to have thought him a little cracked on the subject; and one day, when he obtained a private audience, he besought her either to buy the necklace or to let him go and drown himself in the Seine. Out of all patience, the Queen intimated that he would have been wiser to secure a customer to begin with; that she would not buy; that if he chose to throw himself into the Seine it would be entirely on his own responsibility; and that as for the necklace, he had better pick it to pieces and sell it. The poor German (for Boehmer was a native of Saxony) departed in deep distress, but accepted neither his own suggestion nor the Queen’s.
For some months after this, the court jewelers busied themselves in peddling their necklace about among the courts of Europe. But none of these concerns found it convenient just then to pay out three hundred and sixty thousand dollars for a concatenation of eight hundred diamonds; and still the sparkling elephant remained on the jewelers’ hands.

===Chinese===

伯默与巴桑日心头燃起的头一个念头，自然是：这件绝世珍宝唯一相配的归宿，唯有当时万民景仰爱戴的玛丽·安托瓦内特王后那雪白粉颈！唯有她倾国倾城的美貌方能压得住如此超凡夺目的华彩，也唯有法兰西王座方能在整个基督教世界中担得起这般璀璨的分量。况且王后早年也曾是这两位御用珠宝商的大主顾——早在 1774 年，她便曾花 75,000 美元向他们买过四颗钻石。

若是路易十五，定会毫不犹豫地将它披在宠妃杜巴丽夫人肩上；而路易十六尽管在节俭与廉政方面有些古板念头，却也很快听进了御用珠宝商的委婉暗示；于是在一个晴朗的早晨，他把装在首饰盒里的项链摆在了王后桌上。王后陛下一时间顺从了女性天性的小小软弱，在那些炽烈闪烁、耀眼夺目的宝石晨光中，像个乐坏了的孩子般雀跃欢笑起来。她仅此一次、也是唯一一次将它戴在颈胸之间——世间此前此后恐怕再未见过如此绝世容颜与如此华贵配饰的相映生辉。那简直宛若一位天使的头颅在诸天星辰的荣光中熠熠闪耀。然而，更为高尚的念头占了上风，她迅速摘下项链，纤手轻挥婉拒了这份厚礼，并恳请国王把这笔钱用在任何对法兰西有益或增光的正经事上——毕竟当时法国财政早已捉襟见肘。“我们需要战舰，远胜过需要项链，”她说道。国王对王后的此番举动欣喜不已；此事很快传遍四方，至少在事发后的二十四小时内替王后赢得了极高的民望。事实上，这笔款项后来果真被用来建造了一艘宏伟的战列舰，以伟大海军上将之名命名为“叙弗朗号”（Suffren）。

似乎担任商号总经理的伯默发觉自己手头这条项链简直像补鞋匠抽奖赢来的一头大象一样麻烦，于是死缠烂打地反复游说王后买下它，烦得她不胜其扰。王后似乎觉得他在这件事上有点儿魔怔了；有一天他好不容易求得一次私下觐见，竟苦苦哀求王后要么买下项链，要么准许他跳进塞纳河淹死算了。忍无可忍之下，王后淡淡指出：他当初本该先找好买主再动手；她绝不会买；他要是执意跳塞纳河，后果完全自负；至于那条项链，他最好还是拆散了零卖。可怜的德国人（伯默生于萨克森）悲痛欲绝地退下，既没听自己的话去跳河，也没听王后的话去拆项链。

此后几个月里，两位御用珠宝商忙着跑遍欧洲各国宫廷四处兜售项链。然而当时哪家王室手头都不宽裕，谁也不愿一口气掏出三十六万美元买一串八百颗钻石的连环；这头闪闪发光的“大象”依然压在珠宝商手里。

===Original===

Time passed on. Madame Campan, one of the Queen’s confidential ladies, happened to meet Boehmer one day, and the necklace was alluded to.
“What is the state of affairs about the necklace,” asked the lady.
“Highly satisfactory,” replied Boehmer, whose serenity of countenance Madame Campan had already remarked. “I have sold it to the Sultan at Constantinople, for his favorite Sultana.”
This the lady thought rather curious, but she was glad the thing was disposed of, and said no more.
Time passed on again. In the beginning of August 1785, Boehmer took the trouble to call on Madame Campan at her country-house, somewhat to her surprise.
“Has the Queen given you no message for me?” he inquired.
“No!” said the lady; “What message should she give?”
“An answer to my note,” said the jeweler.
Madame remembered a note which the Queen had received from Boehmer a little while before, along with some ornaments sent by his hands to her as a present from the King. It congratulated her on having the finest diamonds in Europe, and hoped she would remember him. The Queen could make nothing of it, and destroyed it. Madame Campan therefore replied,
“There is no answer, the Queen burned the note. She does not even understand what you meant by writing that note.”
This statement very quickly elicited from the now startled German a story which astounded the lady. He said the Queen owed him the first installment of the money for the diamond necklace; that she had bought it after all; that the story about the Sultana was a lie told by her directions to hide the fact; since the Queen meant to pay by installments, and did not wish the purchase known. And Boehmer said, she had employed the Cardinal de Rohan to buy the necklace for her, and it had been delivered to him for her, and by him to her.

===Chinese===

光阴荏苒。有一天，王后的心腹女官康庞夫人（Madame Campan）巧遇伯默，闲聊间提起了那条项链。

“项链的事儿如今怎么样了？”夫人问道。

“极其圆满，”伯默答道——康庞夫人早已留意到他脸上那副泰然自若的神情，“我已经把它卖给君士坦丁堡的苏丹，献给他最宠爱的妃子了。”

夫人心里虽觉得有些蹊跷，但终究替他高兴总算脱了手，便没再多问。

时光又流逝过去。 1785 年 8 月初，伯默竟专程登门拜访康庞夫人的乡间别墅，令她颇感意外。

“王后可曾托您带什么口信给我？”他问道。

“没有啊！”夫人答道，“她能带什么口信？”

“回复我那张便条的口信呀，”珠宝商说。

夫人这才想起不久前王后确实收到过伯默的一张便条，当时是随同一批国王赠送的首饰由他经手送来的。便条里恭贺王后拥有了全欧洲最璀璨的钻石，并盼她勿忘自己的效劳。王后当时看得一头雾水，随手便烧掉了。于是康庞夫人答道：

“没有回音，王后把便条烧了。她甚至压根儿不明白您写那张便条是什么意思。”

这番话立刻从惊骇失色的德国人嘴里掏出了一段令夫人目瞪口呆的内情。他说王后欠着他钻石项链的头一期分期付款；她终究还是买下了那条项链；先前卖给苏丹宠妃的说法纯属奉她之命编造的幌子，只因王后打算分期付款且不愿买项链之事外泄。伯默还言之凿凿地说，王后委托了罗昂红衣主教（Cardinal de Rohan）替她出面购买，项链早已交到主教手里转呈王后，而主教也已亲手交给了她。

===Original===

Now the Queen, as Madame Campan knew very well, had always strongly disliked this Cardinal; he had even been kept from attending at Court in consequence, and she had not so much as spoken to him for years. And so Madame Campan told Boehmer, and further she told him he had been imposed upon.
“No,” said the man of sparklers decisively, “It is you who are deceived. She is decidedly friendly to the cardinal. I have myself the documents with her own signature authorizing the transaction, for I have had to let the bankers see them in order to get a little time on my own payments.”
Here was a monstrous mystification for the lady of honor, who told Boehmer to instantly go and see his official superior, the chief of the king’s household. She herself being very soon afterwards summoned to the Queen’s presence, the affair came up, and she told the Queen all she knew about it. Marie Antoinette was profoundly distressed by the evident existence of a great scandal and swindle, with which she was plainly to be mixed up through the forged signatures to the documents which Boehmer had been relying on.
Now for the Cardinal.
Louis de Rohan, a scion of the great house of Rohan, one of the proudest of France, was descended of the blood royal of Brittany; was a handsome, proud, dissolute, foolish, credulous, unprincipled noble, now almost fifty years old, a thorough rake, of large revenues, but deeply in debt. He was Peer of France, Archbishop of Strasburg, Grand Almoner of France, Commander of the Order of the Holy Ghost, Commendator of the benefice of St. Wast d’Arras, said to be the mostwealthy in Europe, and a Cardinal. He had been ambassador at Vienna a little after Marie Antoinette was married to the Dauphin, and while there had taken advantage of his official station to do a tremendous quantity of smuggling. He had also further and most deeply offended the Empress Maria Theresa, by outrageous debaucheries, by gross irreligion, and above all by a rather flat but in effect stingingly satirical description of her conduct about the partition of Poland. This she never forgave him, neither did her daughter Marie Antoinette; and accordingly, when he presented himself at Paris soon after she became Queen, he received a curt repulse, and an intimation that he had better go to﻿—Strasburg.
Now in those days a sentence of exclusion from Court was to a French noble but just this side of a banishment to Tophet; and de Rohan was just silly enough to feel this infliction most intensely. He went however, and from that time onward, for year after year, lived the life of a persevering Adam thrust out of his paradise, hanging about the gate and trying all possible ways to sneak in again. Once, for instance, he had induced the porter at the palace of the Trianon to let him get inside the grounds during an illumination, and was recognized by the glow of his cardinal’s red stockings from under his cloak. But he was only laughed at for his pains; the porter was turned off, and the poor silly miserable cardinal remained “out in the cold,” breaking his heart over his exclusion from the most tedious mess of conventionalities that ever was contrived﻿—except those of the court of Spain.

===Chinese===

然而康庞夫人心里再清楚不过：王后向来极其厌恶这位红衣主教；正因如此，他甚至被禁止入宫觐见，王后已有好几年未曾与他说过半句话。康庞夫人如实相告，并直截了当地告诉伯默他必定是受了骗。

“不，”这位珠宝商斩钉截铁地说道，“受蒙蔽的是您。王后对红衣主教明明极其友善。我手里就攥着她亲笔签名的授权交易文书，为了恳求银行家宽限我的付款期限，我甚至不得不拿给他们看过。”

这位荣誉女官顿时坠入云里雾里，只得嘱咐伯默立刻去见他的顶头上司——王室总管。不久之后她本人奉召入宫觐见王后，提起此事，她便将所知一切原原本本禀报了王后。玛丽·安托瓦内特深感痛心与忧虑：显然一桩巨大的丑闻与诈骗案已经爆发，而凭着伯默所依赖的那些伪造签名文书，她自己注定要被牵连其中。

现在，让我们说说那位红衣主教。

路易·德·罗昂（Louis de Rohan）乃是法国最显赫骄傲的罗昂家族后裔，流着布列塔尼王室血脉；他相貌英俊、傲慢放荡、愚蠢轻信而又毫无操守，彼时年近五十，是个彻头彻尾的花花公子，虽坐拥巨额年俸却债台高筑。他身兼法兰西贵族院成员、斯特拉斯堡大主教、法兰西首席施赈官、圣灵骑士团司令官、欧洲公认最肥沃的阿拉斯圣瓦斯特圣俸领主，以及红衣主教等显赫头衔。玛丽·安托瓦内特嫁给法国王太子后不久，他曾出任驻维也纳大使，期间仗着外交官身份大肆走私。此外，他还因荒淫无度、公然亵渎宗教，尤其是用一通看似平淡实则辛辣刻薄的言辞讽刺奥地利女皇玛丽亚·特蕾莎在瓜分波兰一事上的行径，深深得罪了女皇。女皇至死未曾原谅他，她的女儿玛丽·安托瓦内特同样记恨在心；因此，当她荣登王后宝座不久、罗昂兴冲冲跑回巴黎觐见时，立刻吃了一记冷冰冰的闭门羹，并被暗示最好滚回——斯特拉斯堡去。

在那个年代，被逐出宫廷对一位法国贵族而言，简直仅次于流放地狱；而罗昂恰好愚蠢到对这种惩罚痛彻心扉。他虽只得乖乖离去，但从那时起年复一年，活像一个被赶出伊甸园却死不甘心的亚当，整日徘徊在乐园门外，挖空心思企图重新溜进去。譬如有一回，趁着特里亚农宫（Trianon）举办灯会，他买通门房偷偷混进园子，结果斗篷底下露出红衣主教特有的大红长筒袜，在灯光下熠熠生辉，当场被人认出。一番苦心只换来满朝耻笑；门房被革职，可怜愚蠢又凄惨的红衣主教依旧被“晾在冷风里”，只因被排除在世上除了西班牙宫廷之外最枯燥乏味的繁文缛节之外而肝肠寸断。

===Original===

About 1783, this great fool fell in with an equally great knave, who must be spoken of here, where he begins to converge along with the rest, towards the explosion of the necklace swindle. This was Cagliostro, who at that time came to Strasburg and created a tremendous excitement with his fascinating Countess, his Egyptian masonry, his Spagiric Food (a kind of Brandreth’s pill of the period,) which he fed out to poor sick people, his elixir of life, and other humbugs.
The Cardinal sent an intimation that he would like to see the quack. The quack, whose impudence was far greater than the Cardinal’s pride, sent back this sublime reply: “If he is sick let him come to me, and I will cure him. If he is well, he does not need to see me, nor I him.”
This piece of impudence made the fool of a cardinal more eager than ever. After some more affected shyness, Cagliostro allowed himself to be seen. He was just the man to captivate the Cardinal, and they were quickly intimate personal friends, practising transmutation, alchemy, masonry, and still more particularly conducting a great many experiments on the Cardinal’s remarkably fine stock of Tokay wine. Whatever poor de Rohan had to do, he consulted Cagliostro about it, and when the latter went to Switzerland, his dupe maintained a constant communication with him in cipher.
Lastly is to be mentioned Jeanne de St. Remi, Countess de Lamotte de Valois de France, the chief scoundrel, if the term may be used of a woman﻿—of the necklace affair. She seems to have been really a descendant of the royal house of Valois, to which Francis I belonged; through an illegitimate son of Henry II created Count de St. Remi. The family had run down and become poor and rascally, one of Jeanne’s immediate ancestors having practiced counterfeiting for a living. She herself had been protected by a certain kind hearted Countess de Boulainvilliers; was receiving a small pension from the Court of about $325 a year; had married a certain tall soldier named Lamotte; had come to Paris, and was living in poverty in a garret, hovering about as it were for a chance to better her circumstances. She was a quick-witted, bright-eyed, brazen-faced hussy, not beautiful, but with lively pretty ways, and indeed somewhat fascinating.
Her protectress, the countess de Boulainvilliers, was now dead; while she was alive Jeanne had once visited her at de Rohan’s palace of Saverne, and had thus scraped a slight acquaintance with the gay Cardinal, which she resumed during her abode at Paris.

===Chinese===

大约在 1783 年，这位大蠢货撞上了一位旗鼓相当的大无赖——此处必须提他一笔，因为正是从这时起，他与其余各路人马开始汇合，一步步走向项链骗局的大爆发。此人便是卡廖斯特罗；当时他来到斯特拉斯堡，凭着迷人的伯爵夫人、埃及共济会、分发给穷苦病人的“炼金滋补丸”（相当于那个时代的布兰德雷斯药丸）、长生不老药以及其他欺罔把戏，在当地掀起了巨大轰动。

红衣主教派人传话，表示愿意见一见这位江湖郎中。可这郎中的脸皮厚得远远压过红衣主教的傲气，竟回了这么一句绝妙的答复：“他若病了，便叫他自己来找我，我自会治好他。他若没病，那他用不着见我，我也用不着见他。”

这番狂妄回话反倒让那傻瓜主教更加求贤若渴。又装模作样地推辞了几回后，卡廖斯特罗总算肯赏脸相见。他恰好是最能拿捏红衣主教脾胃的那号人物，两人很快便成了莫逆之交，整天一块儿捣鼓点石成金、炼金术与共济会，尤其是针对红衣主教窖藏的那批极品托卡伊葡萄酒开展了大量“实验”。无论可怜的罗昂要办什么事，必先向卡廖斯特罗请教；后来后者去了瑞士，他这冤大头还一直用密码与他保持密切通信。

最后要登场的是让娜·德·圣雷米，即法兰西瓦卢瓦的拉莫特伯爵夫人（Jeanne de St. Remi, Countess de Lamotte de Valois）——整桩项链案的头号恶棍（如果这个词能用来形容女人的话）。她似乎确实是弗朗索瓦一世所属的瓦卢瓦王室后裔，出自亨利二世受封为圣雷米伯爵的一名私生子一脉。只是家道中落，变得既穷困又无赖，让娜的一位直系先祖甚至曾靠伪造钱币维生。她本人曾蒙一位好心的布兰维利耶伯爵夫人（Countess de Boulainvilliers）庇护，从宫廷领着每年约 325 美元的微薄年金，嫁了个名叫拉莫特的高个子军人，随后迁居巴黎，穷困潦倒地蜗居在一间阁楼里，像只盘旋的秃鹫般伺机改善处境。她是个机灵狡黠、双目炯炯、厚颜无耻的小泼妇，相貌虽不算美艳，举止却活泼俏皮，倒也颇有几分迷人风韵。

她的庇护人布兰维利耶伯爵夫人此时已故；早年夫人在世时，让娜曾随她拜访过罗昂在萨韦尔讷（Saverne）的府邸，由此与这位风流红衣主教攀上了一点儿交情，定居巴黎后便重新续上了这份关系。

===Original===

Everybody at Paris knew about the Diamond Necklace, and about de Rohan’s desire to get into court favor. This sharp-witted female swindler now came in among the elements I have thus far been describing, to frame necklace, jeweller, cardinal, queen, and swindler, all together into her plot, just as the keystone drops into an arch and locks it up tight.
No mortal knows where ideas come from. Suddenly a conception is in the mind, whence, or how, we do not know, any more than we know Life. The devil himself might have furnished that which now popped into the cunning, wicked mind of this adventuress. This is what she saw all at once:
Boehmer is crazy to sell his necklace. De Rohan is crazy after the Queen’s favor. I am crazy after money. Now if I can make De Rohan think that the Queen wants the necklace, and will become his friend in return for his helping her to it; if I can make him think I am her agent to him, then I can steal the diamonds in their transit.
A wonderfully cunning and hardy scheme! And most wonderful was the cool, keen promptitude with which it was executed.
The countess began to hint to the cardinal that she was fast getting into the Queen’s good graces, by virtue of being a capital gossip and storyteller; and that she had frequent private audiences. Soon she added intimations that the Queen was far from being really so displeased with the cardinal, as he supposed. At this the old fool bit instantly, and showed the keenest emotions of hope and delight. On a further suggestion, he presently drew up a letter or memoir humbly and plaintively stating his case, which the countess undertook to put into the Queen’s hands. It was the first of over *two hundred* notes from him, notes of abasement, beseeching argument, expostulation, and so on, all entrusted to Jeanne. She burnt them, I suppose.

===Chinese===

当时巴黎人尽皆知那条钻石项链，也人尽皆知罗昂渴望重获宫廷圣眷。此刻，这位冰雪聪明的女骗子切入了我前文所铺陈的各方势力之间，将项链、珠宝商、红衣主教、王后与骗子本人严丝合缝地拼进同一张罗网，恰如拱心石精准落入拱顶，将整座拱门锁得死死的。

凡人无人知晓念头究竟从何而来。灵光骤然闪现脑海，不知所起、不知所由，正如同我们不知生命之源。此刻蹦进这位女冒险家狡诈邪恶脑瓜里的主意，兴许正是魔鬼亲自递过去的。她一眼看穿了全局：

伯默卖项链卖得发狂。罗昂讨王后欢心讨得发狂。我想要钱想得发狂。眼下我只要能让罗昂相信王后想要这条项链，且只要他替她弄到手，王后便愿与他重修旧好；只要我能让他相信我就是王后派给他的密使，我便能在半道上把那批钻石吞个精光！

好一桩奇巧绝伦而又胆大包天的阴谋！更令人叹服的，则是她付诸实施时那份冷静、敏锐与雷厉风行。

伯爵夫人开始向红衣主教透出口风，说自己凭着讲故事说趣闻的一手绝活，正迅速赢得王后欢心，频频获准私下觐见。没过多久，她又顺势暗示：王后对红衣主教其实远不像他以为的那么恼怒。这老傻瓜立刻咬了钩，激动得喜出望外、满怀希冀。经她进一步指点，他很快写下一封卑躬屈膝、凄切申诉的信函或陈情书，托伯爵夫人亲手呈给王后。这只是他写下的*两百多*封信函中的头一封——满纸尽是自贬身份、苦苦哀求、申辩陈情，统统交到了让娜手里。我猜她转手就烧了个精光。

===Original===

In order to make her dupe sure that she told the truth about her access to the Queen, Jeanne more than once made him go and watch her enter a side gate into the grounds of the Trianon palace, to which she had somehow obtained a key; and after waiting he saw her come out again, sometimes under the escort of a man, who was, she said one Desclos, a confidential valet of the Queen. This was Villette de Rétaux, a “pal” of Jeanne’s and of her husband Lamotte, who had, by the way, become a low-class gambler and swindler by occupation.
Next Jeanne talked about the Queen’s charities; and on one occasion, told how much the amiable Marie Antoinette longed to expend certain sums for benevolent purposes if she only had them﻿—but she was out of funds, and the King was so close about money!
The poor cardinal bit again﻿—“If the Queen would only allow him the honor to furnish the little amount!”
The countess evidently hadn’t thought of that. She reflected﻿—hesitated. The cardinal urged. She consented﻿—it was not much﻿—and was so kind as to carry the cash herself. At their next meeting she reported that the Queen was delighted, telling a very nice story about it. The cardinal would only be too happy to do so again. And sure enough he did, and quite a number of times too; contributing in all to the funds of the countess in this manner, about $25,000.
Well: after a time the cardinal is at Strasburg, when he receives a note from the countess that brings him back again as quick as post-horses can carry him. It says that there is something very important, very secret, very delicate, that the queen wants his help about. He is overflowing with zeal. What is it? Only let him know﻿—his life, his purse, his soul, are at the service of his liege lady.
His purse is all that is needed. With infinite shyness and circumspection, the countess gradually, half unwillingly, lets him find out that it is the diamond necklace that the Queen wants. By diabolical ingenuities of talk she leads de Rohan to the full conviction that if he secures the Queen that necklace, he will thenceforward bask in all the sunshine of court favor that she can show or control.
And at proper times sundry notes from the Queen are bestowed upon the enraptured noodle. These are written in imitation of the Queen’s handwriting, by that Villette de Rétaux who personated the Queen’s valet, and who was an expert at counterfeiting.

===Chinese===

为了让这冤大头确信自己确实能自由出入王后寝宫，让娜不止一次叫他躲在暗处，亲眼看着她从一扇偏门走进特里亚农宫的花园——不知她从哪儿弄来了那扇门的钥匙；等上一阵子，他便看见她又走出来，有时身旁还跟着一个男人护送，据她说是王后的心腹贴身男仆德克洛（Desclos）。其实那男人正是维莱特·德·雷托（Villette de Rétaux），让娜与她丈夫拉莫特的狐朋狗友（顺带一提，拉莫特此刻早已沦为一名靠赌博与诈骗糊口的下流混混）。

紧接着，让娜聊起了王后的慈善善举；有一回她感叹说，慈悲心肠的玛丽·安托瓦内特多么渴望拿出一笔钱去行善济贫，偏偏自己手头拮据，而国王管钱又管得死紧！

可怜的红衣主教再次上钩——“不知王后可否赏脸，让他有幸代垫这点儿微薄款项！”

伯爵夫人显然“压根儿没想到这一层”。她沉吟片刻——犹豫不决。主教苦苦恳求。她这才勉强答应——毕竟数额不大——还好心地亲自替他把现钞送去。下次见面时，她汇报说王后高兴极了，还顺带编了一段动听的故事。红衣主教受宠若惊，表示随时效劳、乐此不疲。果不其然，他一而再、再而三地掏腰包；前后借此方式为伯爵夫人的私人金库“捐献”了大约 25,000 美元。

好了：过了一阵子，红衣主教正在斯特拉斯堡，忽然收到伯爵夫人一封急信，催得他动用驿马以最快速度星夜兼程赶回巴黎。信中称有件极其紧要、极其机密、极其微妙的大事，王后急需他相助。他顿时满腔热忱沸腾起来。究竟何事？只管吩咐——他的性命、他的钱袋、他的灵魂，尽归他的君主夫人差遣！

其实人家需要的仅仅是他的钱袋而已。伯爵夫人装出无比羞怯而又慎重的模样，半推半就地一点点让他自己“猜出”，原来王后心心念念的正是那条钻石项链。凭着魔鬼般的如簧巧舌，她把罗昂哄得深信不疑：只要他替王后弄到那条项链，从此往后他便能尽情沐浴在王后所能施予或掌控的全部宫廷恩宠阳光之中。

在恰当时机，几封“王后亲笔便条”也被赐予这位神魂颠倒的蠢材。这些字条全由那位假扮王后男仆的维莱特·德·雷托模仿王后笔迹伪造而成——此人正是伪造字迹的一把好手。

===Original===

A last and sublime summit of impudent pretension is reached by a secret interview which the Queen, says the countess, desires to grant to her beloved servant the cardinal. This suggestion was rendered practicable by one of those mere coincidences which are found though rarely in history, and which are too improbable to put into a novel﻿—the casual discovery of a young woman of loose character who looked much like the Queen. Whether her name was d’Essigny or Gay d’Oliva, is uncertain; she is usually called by the latter. She was hired and taught; and with immense precautions, this ostrich of a cardinal was one night introduced into the gardens of the Trianon, and shown a little nook among the thickets where a stately female in the similitude of the Queen received him with soft spoken words of kindly greeting, allowed him to kneel and kiss a fair and shapely hand, and showed no particular timidity of any kind. Yet the interview had scarcely more than begun before steps were heard. “Someone is coming,” exclaimed the lady, “it is Monsieur and Madame d’Artois﻿—Wemust part. There”﻿—she gave him a red rose﻿—“You know what that means! Farewell!” And away they went﻿—Mademoiselle d’Oliva to report to her employers, and the cardinal, in a seventh heaven of ineffable tomfoolery, to his hotel.
But the interview, and the lovely little notes that came sometimes, “fixed” the necklace business! And if further encouragement had been needed, Cagliostro gave it. For the cardinal now consulted him about the future of the affair, having indeed kept him fully informed about it for a long time, as he did of all matters of interest. So the quack set up his tabernacles of mummery in a parlor of the cardinal’s hotel, and conducted an Egyptian Invocation there all night long in solitude and pomp; and in the morning he decreed (in substance) “go ahead.” And the cardinal did so. Boehmer and Bassange were only too happy to bargain with the great and wealthy church and state dignitary. A memorandum of terms and time of payment was drawn up, and was submitted to the Queen. That is, swindling Jeanne carried it off, and brought it back, with an entry made by Villette de Rétaux in the margin, thus: “*Bon, bon﻿—Approuvé, Marie Antoinette de France.*” That is, “Good, good﻿—I approve. Marie Antoinette de France.” The payment was to be by installments, at six months, and quarterly afterwards; the Queen to furnish the money to the cardinal, while he remained ostensibly holden to the jewellers, she thus keeping out of sight.
So the jewels were handed over to the cardinal de Rohan; he took them one evening in great state to the lodgings of the countess, where with all imaginable formality there came a knock at the door, and when it was open a tall valet entered who said solemnly “On the part of the Queen!” De Rohan *knew* it was the Queen’s confidential valet, for he saw with his own eyes that it was the same man who had escorted the countess from the side gate at the Trianon! And so it was; to wit, Villette de Rétaux, who, calmly receiving the fifteen hundred thousand franc treasure, marched but as solemnly as he had come in.

===Chinese===

整场厚颜无耻骗局的最后一重巅峰，则是伯爵夫人转达说王后渴望秘密接见她心爱的忠仆红衣主教。这一大胆设想之所以能付诸实施，全赖历史上虽罕见、写进小说却显得过于离奇的巧合——他们无意间撞见了一名行为放荡、容貌却酷似王后的年轻女子。她究竟叫德西尼还是盖伊·多利瓦（Gay d’Oliva）已不可考，通常被称为后者。她被重金雇来并加以调教；在一个夜晚，做足了重重掩护后，这头把脑袋埋进沙子里的鸵鸟主教被领进了特里亚农宫花园，引至树丛深处一角幽静所在；在那里，一位仪态端庄、酷似王后的女子用温柔亲切的语调迎候他，准许他跪下亲吻一只白皙修长的玉手，且丝毫不见任何局促羞怯。然而幽会才刚开始不久，远处便传来了脚步声。“有人来了，”那女子惊呼道，“是阿图瓦亲王和夫人——我们必须分手了。拿着”——她递给他一朵红玫瑰——“你懂得这是什么意思！再会！”随后两人各自散去——多利瓦小姐回去向雇主复命，而红衣主教则飘飘然坠入不可名状的蠢笨七重天，回到自己的府邸。

正是这次秘密幽会，加上时不时飘来的甜美小字条，彻底“敲定”了项链的买卖！就算还需要什么额外鼓舞，卡廖斯特罗也适时送上了。因为红衣主教此刻就此事的吉凶向他占卜求教——事实上长久以来他凡事必向这位江湖郎中通气。于是郎中就在主教府的一间客厅里摆开装神弄鬼的祭坛，彻夜举行了一场庄严孤寂的“埃及招魂仪式”；到了清晨，他降下神谕（大意如此）：“放心去干！”红衣主教便照办了。伯默与巴桑日巴不得能与这位集教会与国家重任于一身的豪门显贵做成买卖。双方拟妥了一纸付款条件与期限的备忘录，呈交王后御览。也就是说，女骗子让娜拿走了备忘录，带回来时页边已由维莱特·德·雷托添上一行批注：“*Bon, bon — Approuvé, Marie Antoinette de France.*”意即：“甚好，甚好——准此。法兰西的玛丽·安托瓦内特。”货款约定分期支付，六个月后首付，此后按季付款；由王后暗中把钱交给红衣主教，名义上则由主教向珠宝商担保，王后由此隐身幕后。

于是珠宝交到了罗昂红衣主教手中；某天傍晚，他排场十足地带着项链来到伯爵夫人的寓所；在极尽正式的礼数之中，门外响起敲门声，门一开，走进一名高大男仆，肃穆地宣称：“奉王后之命！”罗昂*一眼认出*这正是王后的心腹男仆，因为他亲眼见过此人正是护送伯爵夫人走出特里亚农宫偏门的那一个！确实没错，正是维莱特·德·雷托；他泰然自若地接过那件价值一百五十万法郎的稀世珍宝，随即如同他进门时一般庄严肃穆地大步离去。

===Original===

As that counterfeiting rascal goes out of the door, the diamond necklace itself disappears from our knowledge. The swindle was consummated, but there is no whisper of the disposition of the spoils. Villette, and Jeanne’s husband Lamotte, went to London and Amsterdam, and had some money there; but seemingly no more than the previous pillages upon the cardinal might have supplied; nor did the countess’ subsequent expenditures show that she had any of the proceeds.
But that is not the last of the rest of the parties to the affair, by any means. Between this scene and the time when the anxious Boehmer, having a little bill to meet, beset Madame Campan about his letter and the money the Queen was to pay him, there intervened six months. During that time countess Jeanne was smoothing as well as she could, with endless lies and contrivances, the troubles of the perplexed cardinal, who “couldn’t seem to see” that he was much better off in spite of his loyal performance of his part of the bargain.
But this application by Boehmer, and the enormous swindle which it was instantly evident had been perpetrated on somebody or other, of course waked up a commotion at once. The Baron de Breteuil, a deadly enemy of de Rohan, got hold of it all, and in his overpowering eagerness to ruin his foe, quickly rendered the matter so public that it was out of the question to hush it up. It seems probable that Jeanne de Lamotte expected that the business would be kept quiet for the sake of the Queen, and that thus any very severe or public punishments would be avoided and perhaps no inquiries made. It is clear that this would have been the best plan, but de Breteuil’s officiousness prevented it, and there was nothing for it but legal measures. De Rohan was arrested and put in the Bastile, having barely been able to send a message in German to his hotel to a trusty secretary, who instantly destroyed all the papers relating to the affair. Jeanne was also imprisoned, and Miss Gay d’Oliva and Villette de Rétaux, being caught at Brussels and Amsterdam, were in like manner secured. As for Cagliostro, he was also imprisoned, some accounts saying that he ostentatiously gave himself up for trial.

===Chinese===

随着那个伪造文书的无赖迈出房门，钻石项链本身便从我们的视野中彻底消失了。骗局已告大功告成，至于赃物究竟如何处置，却连半点风声也未曾走漏。维莱特与让娜的丈夫拉莫特先后去了伦敦与阿姆斯特丹，手头虽有些银钱，但似乎并未超出先前从红衣主教身上榨取的数目；伯爵夫人随后的日常开销也看不出她手里握有任何卖钻所得。

然而，涉案其余各方的故事可远远没完。从那一幕到焦头烂额的伯默因急着应付一张到期账单而缠住康庞夫人打听信函与王后欠款，其间整整隔了六个月。在这半年里，让娜伯爵夫人使尽浑身解数，靠着没完没了的谎言与花招安抚着满腹狐疑的红衣主教——主教虽忠心耿耿履行了自己的那一半交易，却“死活看不出”自己处境究竟好转了多少。

然而，伯默这一上门追讨，外加显而易见有人中了惊天大骗局的事实，自然立刻掀起了轩然大波。罗昂的死对头布勒特伊男爵（Baron de Breteuil）掌握了全部内情，他急欲置政敌于死地，迅速把事情闹得满城风雨，再也无法私下捂住。让娜·德·拉莫特原本多半指望宫廷为了维护王后清誉会把此事压下来，从而免于公开严惩、甚至免于深究。平心而论，这本该是最妥当的收场；偏偏布勒特伊多事搅局，事态已别无选择，唯余诉诸法律一途。罗昂被捕并投入巴士底狱，临行前勉强用德语给府上一名心腹秘书递了个口信，后者立刻烧毁了所有相关文件。让娜亦被收监，而盖伊·多利瓦小姐与维莱特·德·雷托分别在布鲁塞尔和阿姆斯特丹落网押回。至于卡廖斯特罗，也同样锒铛入狱——有记载说他是大摇大摆主动投案受审的。

===Original===

This was a public trial before the Parliament of Paris, with much form.
The result was that the cardinal, appearing to be only fool, not knave, was acquitted. Gay d’Oliva appeared to have known nothing except that she was to play a part, and she had been told that the Queen wanted her to do so, so she was let go. Villette was banished for life. Lamotte, the countess’ husband, had escaped to England, and was condemned to the galleys in his absence, which didn’t hurt him much. Cagliostro was acquitted. But Jeanne was sentenced to be whipped, branded on the shoulder with the letter *V* for *Voleuse* (thief), and banished.
This sentence was executed in full, but with great difficulty; for the woman turned perfectly furious on the public scaffold, flew at the hangman like a tiger, bit pieces out of his hands, shrieked, cursed, rolled on the floor, kicked, squirmed and jumped, until they held her by brute force, tore down her dress, and the red hot iron going aside as she struggled, plunged full into her snowy white breast, planting there indelibly the horrible black *V*, while she yelled like a fiend under the torment of the smoking brand. She fled away to England, lived there some time in dissolute courses, and is said to have died in consequence of falling out of a window when drunk, or as another account states, of being flung out by the companions of her orgy, whom she had stung to fury by her frightful scolding. Before her death she put forth one or two memoirs﻿—false, scandalous things.
The unfortunate Queen never entirely escaped some shadow of disrepute from the necklace business. For to the very last, both on the trial and afterwards, Jeanne de Lamotte impudently stuck to it that at least the Queen had known about the trick played on the Cardinal at the Trianon, and had in fact been hidden close by and saw and laughed heartily at the whole interview. So sore and morbid was the condition of the public mind in France in those days, when symptoms of the coming Revolution were breaking out one very side, that this odious story found many and willing believers.

===Chinese===

这场审讯在巴黎高等法院公开举行，排场盛大、仪轨繁复。

审判结果如下：红衣主教看来纯粹是个蠢货而非奸贼，获判无罪释放；盖伊·多利瓦似乎除了知道自己受雇演一场戏、且以为是王后本人授意之外一无所知，亦获释；维莱特被判终身流放；伯爵夫人的丈夫拉莫特早已逃往英格兰，被缺席判处苦役划桨刑，倒也没伤着他一根毫毛；卡廖斯特罗获判无罪。唯独让娜被判处公开鞭刑、肩头烙上代表“女贼”（*Voleuse*）的字母 *V* 烙印，并予流放。

这项判决被不折不扣地执行了，却费了极大的劲：这女人在公共行刑台上彻底发了疯，像猛虎般扑向刽子手，从他手上生生咬下几块肉，尖叫、咒骂、满地打滚、拳打脚踢、扭动蹦跳，直到众人靠蛮力死死按住她、扯下她的衣裙；可就在她拼命挣扎之际，烧红的烙铁滑偏了位置，直直戳进她雪白的胸脯，在那里留下了一枚永不磨灭的恐怖黑色 *V* 字印记，而她在冒烟烙铁的剧痛下发出恶魔般的凄厉嚎叫。随后她逃往英格兰，在那里放荡度日过了一阵子；据说她最终因醉酒失足跌出窗外摔死——另一种说法则称，是被她那张恶毒泼辣的嘴惹得忍无可忍的酒肉同伙从窗户扔出去摔死的。临死前，她还抛出一两本回忆录——满纸尽是虚假污蔑的诽谤之词。

不幸的王后始终未能彻底洗清钻石项链事件泼在她身上的污名。因为直到最后一刻，无论在法庭上还是获刑之后，让娜·德·拉莫特都厚颜无耻地死咬不放，坚称王后至少对特里亚农宫戏弄红衣主教的那场把戏知情，甚至当时就躲在近旁偷看，且对着整场幽会捧腹大笑。当时大革命的征兆已在法国四处冒头，民心痛楚而病态到了极点，这番恶毒谣言竟然引来了大批乐意轻信的听众。

### XLII / 第四十二章

===Original===

The Count de St. Germain, sage, prophet, and magician.
Superior to Cagliostro, even in accomplishments, and second to him in notoriety only, was that human nondescript, the so-called Count de St. Germain, whom Fredrick the Great called, “a man no one has ever been able to make out.”
The Marquis de Crequy declares that St. Germain was an Alsatian Jew, Simon Wolff by name, and born at Strasburg about the close of the seventeenth or the beginning of the eighteenth century; others insist that he was a Spanish Jesuit named Aymar; and others again intimate that his true title was the Marquis de Betmar, and that he was a native of Portugal. The most plausible theory, however, makes him the natural son of an Italian princess, and fixes his birth at San Germano, in Savoy, about the year 1710; his ostensible father being one Rotondo, a tax-collector of that district.
This supposition is borne out by the fact that he spoke all his many languages with an Italian accent. It was about the year 1750 that he first began to be heard of in Europe as the Count St. Germain, and put forth the astounding pretensions that soon gave him celebrity over the whole continent. The celebrated Marquis de Belleisle made his acquaintance about that time in Germany, and brought him to Paris, where he was introduced to Madame de Pompadour, whose favor he very quickly gained. The influence of that famous beauty was just then paramount with Louis XV, and the Count was soon one of the most eminent men at court. He was remarkably handsome﻿—as an old portrait at Friersdorf, in Saxony, in the rooms he once occupied, sufficiently indicated; and his musical accomplishments, added to the ineffable charm of his manners and conversation, and the miracles he performed, rendered him an irresistible attraction, especially to the ladies, who appear to have almost idolized him. Endowed with an enchanting voice, he could also play every instrument then in vogue, but especially excelled upon the violin, which he could handle in such a manner as to give it the effect of a small orchestra. Cotemporary writers declare that, in his more ordinary performances, a connoisseur could distinctly hear the separate tones of a full quartet when the count was extemporizing on his favorite Cremona. His little work, entitled *La Musique Raisonnée*, published in England, for private circulation only, bears testimony to his musical genius, and to the wondrous eccentricity, as well as beauty, of his conceptions. But it was in alectromancy, or divination by signs and circles; hydromancy, or divination by water; cleidomancy, or divination by the key, and dactylomancy, or divination by the fingers, that the count chiefly excelled, although he, at the same time, professed alchemy, astrology, and prophecy in the higher branches.

===Chinese===

圣日耳曼伯爵——贤哲、先知与魔法师。

在才艺造诣上甚至胜过卡廖斯特罗一筹、仅在恶名上屈居其后的，便是那个难以归类的奇人——所谓的圣日耳曼伯爵（Count de St. Germain），腓特烈大帝曾称他为“一个世上从未有人能够看透的人”。

克雷基侯爵（Marquis de Crequy）断言圣日耳曼是一名阿尔萨斯犹太人，名叫西蒙·沃尔夫（Simon Wolff），约生于十七世纪末或十八世纪初的斯特拉斯堡；另有人坚称他是名叫艾马尔（Aymar）的西班牙耶稣会士；还有人暗示他的真实头衔是贝特马尔侯爵（Marquis de Betmar），原籍葡萄牙。然而最合情合理的说法，认为他是一位意大利公主的私生子，约于 1710 年生于萨伏伊的圣杰尔马诺（San Germano），名义上的父亲则是当地一个名叫罗通多（Rotondo）的税吏。

他尽管通晓多国语言，开口却无一不带着意大利口音，这一事实恰恰印证了上述推测。大约在 1750 年前后，他开始以圣日耳曼伯爵之名在欧洲崭露头角，并抛出了一系列惊世骇俗的奇谈怪论，很快便令他红遍整个欧洲大陆。当时大名鼎鼎的贝勒伊尔侯爵（Marquis de Belleisle）在德国与他结识，随即将他带回巴黎，引荐给蓬帕杜夫人（Madame de Pompadour），他极快便博得了夫人的青睐。彼时那位绝代佳人在路易十五面前权倾朝野，伯爵很快便跻身宫廷最炙手可热的人物之列。他生得俊美非凡——萨克森弗里斯多夫（Friersdorf）他曾下榻的房间里挂着的一幅旧肖像画足以证明这一点；加之他卓越的音乐造诣、举止谈吐中难以言喻的迷人魅力，以及他所施展的种种神迹，使他成为无可抗拒的魅力化身，尤其令贵妇们如痴如醉、几近奉若神明。他天赋一副迷人的嗓音，当时流行的每一种乐器无不精通，尤擅小提琴，拉奏起来竟能营造出一支小型管弦乐队的宏阔音响。同时代作家记载，哪怕在最寻常的演奏中，行家都能清晰听出伯爵用他心爱的克雷莫纳名琴即兴演奏时奏出的完整四重奏声部。他在英格兰印行、仅限私人传阅的小册子《理性音乐》（*La Musique Raisonnée*），便见证了他的音乐天才，以及他构思中奇崛诡谲而又优美绝伦的特质。不过，伯爵最拿手的绝活还是借符箓与圆圈占卜、水占术、钥匙占卜术以及指占术，同时他亦自诩精通高阶炼金术、占星术与预言术。

===Original===

The fortunes of the Count St. Germain rose so rapidly in France, that in 1760 he was sent by Louis XV, to the Court of England, to assist in negotiations for a peace. M. de Choiseul, then Prime Minister of France, however, greatly feared and detested the Count; and secretly wrote to Pitt, begging the latter to have that personage arrested, as he was certainly a Russian spy. But St. Germain, through his attendant sprites, of course, received timely warning, and escaped to the Continent. In England, he was the inseparable friend of Prince Lobkowitz﻿—a circumstance that gave some color to his alleged connection with the Russians. His sojourn there was equally distinguished by his devotion to the ladies, and his unwavering success at the gaming-table, where he won fabulous sums, which were afterward dispensed with imperial munificence. It was there, too, that he put forward his claims to the highest rank in Masonry; and, of course, added, thereby, immensely to the éclat of his position. He spoke English, French, Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, German, Russian, Polish, the Scandinavian, and many of the Oriental tongues, with equal fluency; and pretended to have traveled over the whole Earth, and even to have visited the most distant starry orbs frequently, in the course of a lifetime which, with continual transmigrations, he declared to have lasted for thousands of years. His birth, he said, had been in Chaldea, in the dawn of time; and that he was the sole inheritor of the lost sciences and mysteries of his own and the Egyptian race. He spoke of his personal intimacy with all the twelve Apostles﻿—and even the august presence of the Savior; and one of his pretensions would have been most singularly amusing, had it not bordered upon profanity. This was no less an assertion than that he had upon several occasions remonstrated with the Apostle Peter upon the irritability of his temperament! In regard to later periods of history, he spoke with the careless ease of an everyday looker on; and told anecdotes that the researches of scholars afterwards fully verified. His predictions were, indeed, most startling; and the cotemporaneous evidence is very strong and explicit, that he did foretell the time, place, and manner of the death of Louis XV, several years before it occurred. His gift of memory was perfectly amazing. Having once read a journal of the day, he could repeat its contents accurately, from beginning to end; and to this endowment he united the faculty of writing with both hands, in characters like copperplate. Thus, he could indite a love-letter with his right while he composed a verse with his left hand, and, apparently, with the utmost facility﻿—a splendid acquisition for the Treasury Department or a literary newspaper! He would, however, have been ineligible for any faithful Post Office, since he read the contents of sealed letters at a glance; and, by his clairvoyant powers, detected crime, or, in fact, the movements of men and the phenomena of nature, at any distance. Like all the great Magi, and Brothers of the Rosy Cross, of whom he claimed to be a shining light, he most excelled in medicine; and along with remedies for “every ill that flesh is heir to,” boasted his *Aqua Benedetta* as the genuine elixir of life, capable of restoring youth to age, beauty and strength to decay, and brilliant intellect to the exhausted brain; and, if properly applied, protracting human existence through countless centuries. As a proof of its virtues, he pointed to his own youthful appearance, and the testimony of old men who had seen him sixty or seventy years earlier, and who declared that time had made no impression on him. Strangely enough, the Margrave of Anspach, of whom I shall presently speak, purchased what purported to be the recipe of the *Aqua Benedetta*, from John Dyke, the English Consul at Leghorn, towards the close of the last century; and copies of it are still preserved with religious care and the utmost secrecy by certain noble families in Berlin and Vienna, where the preparation has been used (as they believe) with perfect success against a host of diseases.

===Chinese===

圣日耳曼伯爵在法国青云直上，以至于 1760 年路易十五竟派他出使英国宫廷协助斡旋和谈。然而，时任法国首相的舒瓦瑟尔先生（M. de Choiseul）对伯爵既畏惧又痛恨；他秘密致信皮特（Pitt），恳请英方将此人逮捕，称他铁定是俄国间谍。但圣日耳曼当然借着他那群随身侍从精灵及时得了风声，逃回了欧洲大陆。在英格兰期间，他与洛布科维茨亲王（Prince Lobkowitz）形影不离——这一交情倒为他涉嫌勾结俄国人的指控添了几分佐证。他在英国逗留期间，同样以对贵妇们的殷勤周到以及牌桌上战无不胜的惊人手气闻名——他在赌桌上赢下巨额财富，随后便以帝王般的阔绰大肆挥霍施舍。也正是在英国，他自诩拥有共济会最高阶位，自然借此大大抬高了自身声望。他操英语、法语、西班牙语、葡萄牙语、意大利语、德语、俄语、波兰语、斯堪的纳维亚诸语乃至多种东方语言均流利如母语；并宣称自己足迹遍及全球，甚至在漫长到靠不断转世延续了数千年的一生中，还频频造访最遥远的星辰。他说自己降生于太初黎明时的迦勒底，是本族与埃及民族失传科学与秘法的唯一继承人。他谈起自己与十二门徒、乃至在救世主威严面前的私交如数家珍；其中有一番吹嘘若非近乎亵渎神明，倒真显得滑稽绝伦：他竟断言自己曾不止一次劝诫使徒彼得，要他改改那副暴躁易怒的脾气！谈及近代各段历史时，他语气轻松随意得宛如每日亲历的旁观者，随口讲出的轶事日后往往被学者考据证实分毫不差。他的预言确实惊世骇俗；同时代留下极强且明确的证言，证实他确曾在路易十五驾崩前好几年便精准预言了国王死期、地点与死状。他的记忆力更是惊人：报纸读过一遍便能从头至尾一字不差地背诵下来；除此之外他还具备双手同时书写的绝技，字迹工整如铜版印刷——于是他能右手写情书、左手赋诗，且显得游刃有余，这本领要是放在财政部或文学报馆里，简直是求之不得的奇才！不过任何讲求保密的邮局倒绝不敢录用他，因为他瞥一眼便能看穿密封信件的内容，且凭千里眼法力无论相隔多远都能侦破罪案、洞悉世人行踪与自然异象。一如所有伟大的东方贤士与玫瑰十字会弟兄（他自诩为其中一颗璀璨明珠），他最精通医道；除了宣称拥有包治“血肉之躯所罹一切苦痛”的灵药外，更吹嘘他的“圣水”（*Aqua Benedetta*）乃是货真价实的长生不老药，能使垂暮重返青春、衰朽重焕美貌与气力、枯竭头脑重现灵光，若使用得法，更能使人类寿命绵延无数个世纪。为证明其奇效，他指向自己年轻俊朗的容颜，并搬出六七十年前见过他的老人们的证词——那些老人均声称岁月在他脸上未曾留下丝毫痕迹。说来也怪，我稍后要提到的安斯巴赫藩侯（Margrave of Anspach）上世纪末竟从英国驻里窝那领事约翰·戴克（John Dyke）手中买下了号称“圣水”的配方；直到如今，柏林与维也纳的某些贵族世家依然虔诚珍藏并严密保守着它的抄本，据他们深信，这剂药水对治好一大批疾病屡试不爽。

===Original===

Still another peculiarity of the Count would be highly advantageous to any of us, particularly at this period of high prices and culinary scarcity. He never ate nor drank; or, at least, he was never seen to do so! It is said that boarding house regime in these days is rapidly accustoming a considerable class of our fellow-citizens to a similar condition, but I can scarcely believe it.
Again, the Count would fall into cataleptic swoons, which continued often for hours, and even days; and, during these periods, he declared that he visited, in spirit, the most remote regions of the Earth, and even the farthest stars, and would relate, with astonishing power, the scenes he there had witnessed!
He, of course, laid claim to the transmutation of baser metals into gold, and stated that, in 1755, while on a visit to India, to consult the erudition of the Hindu Brahmins, he solved, by their assistance, the problem of the artificial crystallization of pure carbon﻿—or, in other words, the production of diamonds! One thing is certain, viz.: that upon a visit to the French ambassador to the Hague, in 1780, he, in the presence of that functionary, induced him to believe and testify that he broke to pieces, with a hammer, a superb diamond, of his own manufacture, the exact counterpart of another, of similar origin, which he had just sold for 5,500 louis d’or.
His career and transformations on the Continent were multiform. In 1762, he was mixed up with the dynastic conspiracies and changes at St. Petersburg; and his importance there was indicated ten years later, by the reception given to him at Vienna by the Russian Count Orloff, who accosted him joyously as “caro padre” (dear father,) and gave him twenty thousand golden Venetian sequins.
From Petersburg he went to Berlin, where he at once attracted the attention of Frederick the Great, who questioned Voltaire about him; the latter replying, as it is said, that he was a man who knew all things, and would live to the end of the world﻿—a fair statement, in brief, of the position assumed by more than one of our ward politicians!

===Chinese===

伯爵还有另一项奇特本领，尤其在如今这物价飞涨、伙食匮乏的年头，对我们任何人都堪称求之不得：他从不吃也不喝——至少从没有人见过他吃喝！据说如今寄宿公寓的伙食制度正迅速把我们相当一批同胞调教得接近这种境界，不过我倒不太相信。

此外，伯爵还时不时会陷入僵直昏厥状态，常常一睡便是好几个钟头甚至好几天；据他宣称，在此期间他的灵魂出窍游历地球最偏远的角落乃至最遥远的星辰，醒来后便能以惊人的感染力细数自己亲眼目睹的奇景！

他自然也自诩精通点石成金术，并声称 1755 年他造访印度向印度教婆罗门求教渊博学问时，在他们协助下攻克了纯碳人工结晶的难题——换句话说，也就是人造钻石！有一件事确凿无疑： 1780 年他拜访法国驻海牙大使时，当着那位外交官的面，哄得对方深信并作证他亲手用锤子砸碎了一颗自己炼制的绝美钻石——而另一颗同样来历的姊妹钻，他刚刚以 5,500 路易金币的高价卖了出去。

他在欧洲大陆上的行踪与化身可谓千变万化。 1762 年，他卷入了圣彼得堡的宫廷阴谋与政变；十年后他在维也纳受到俄国奥尔洛夫伯爵（Count Orloff）的盛大接待，后者欣喜地尊称他为“亲爱的父亲”（caro padre），并奉上两万枚威尼斯金币，足见他当年在俄国举足轻重的地位。

离开圣彼得堡后，他前往柏林，立刻引起了腓特烈大帝的注意；国王向伏尔泰打听此人底细，据说伏尔泰答道：他是个无所不知、且能活到世界末日的人——这简短评价倒也颇为贴切地概括了我们这儿好几位街区政客自以为是的派头！

===Original===

In 1774, he took up his abode at Schwabach, in Germany, under the name of Count Tzarogy, which is a transposition of Ragotzy, a well-known noble name. The Margrave of Anspach met him at the house of his favorite Clairon, the actress, and became so fond of him, that he insisted upon his company to Italy. On his return, he went to Dresden, Leipzig, and Hamburg, and finally to Eckernfiorde, in Schleswig, where he took up his residence with the Landgrave Karl of Hesse; and at length, in 1783, tired, as he said, of life, and disdaining any longer immortality, he gave up the ghost.
It was during St. Germain’s residence in Schleswig that he was visited by the renowned Cagliostro, who openly acknowledged him as master, and learned many of his most precious secrets from him﻿—among others, the faculty of discriminating the character by the handwriting, and of fascinating birds, animals, and reptiles.
To trace the wanderings of St. Germain is a difficult task, as he had innumerable aliases, and often totally disappeared for months together. In Venice, he was known as the Count de Bellamare; at Pisa, as the Chevalier de Schoening; at Milan, as the Chevalier Welldone; at Genoa, as the Count Soltikow, etc.
In all these journeys, his own personal tastes were quiet and simple, and he manifested more attachment for a pocket-copy of Guarini’s *Pastor Fido*﻿—his only library﻿—than for any other object in his possession.
On the whole, the Count de St. Germain was a man of magnificent attainments, but the use he made of his talents proved him to be also a most magnificent humbug.

===Chinese===

1774 年，他化名“察罗吉伯爵”（Count Tzarogy）——这不过是显赫贵族姓氏拉科奇（Ragotzy）的字母倒置——定居德国施瓦巴赫。安斯巴赫藩侯在宠姬女演员克莱龙（Clairon）家中结识了他，对他喜爱备至，硬邀他一同游历意大利。归来后，他辗转德累斯顿、莱比锡与汉堡，最终来到石勒苏益格的埃克恩弗德，与黑森的卡尔伯爵（Landgrave Karl of Hesse）同住；终于在 1783 年，据他说因厌倦了尘世生活、不屑再享长生不老，便咽了气。

正是在圣日耳曼寓居石勒苏益格期间，大名鼎鼎的卡廖斯特罗前来拜谒，公开尊他为师，并从他那儿学走了许多最珍贵的秘诀——其中包括凭笔迹识人性格，以及摄服飞禽走兽与爬虫的本领。

追索圣日耳曼的漫游足迹绝非易事，因为他化名无数，且常常一连数月彻底人间蒸发。在威尼斯他化名贝拉马雷伯爵；在比萨叫舍宁骑士；在米兰叫韦尔登骑士；在热那亚则叫索尔蒂科夫伯爵，如此等等。

在所有这些漂泊旅程中，他私下的个人趣味却颇为清雅简朴，对他随身唯一的藏书——一本袖珍版瓜里尼（Guarini）的《忠实的牧羊人》（*Pastor Fido*）——表现出比对任何其他财物都要深厚的眷恋。

总而言之，圣日耳曼伯爵固然是一位才华横溢之士，但他对自己才智的运用，却证明了他同样是一位才华横溢的超级大骗子（humbug）。

### XLIII / 第四十三章

===Original===

Riza Bey, the Persian envoy to Louis XIV.
The most gorgeous, and with one sole exception the most glorious reign that France has known, so far as military success is concerned, was that of Louis XIV, the Grand Monarque. His was the age of lavish expenditure, of magnificent structures, grand festivals, superb dress and equipage, aristocratic arrogance, brilliant campaigns, and great victories. It was, moreover, particularly distinguished for the number and high character of the various special embassies sent to the court of France by foreign powers. Among these, Spain, the Netherlands, Great Britain, and Venice rivaled each other in extravagant display and pomp. The singular and really tangible imposture I am about to describe, practiced at such a period and on such a man as Louis of France, was indeed a bold and dashing affair.
*L’etat c’est moi*﻿—“I am the State,” was Louis’ celebrated and very significant motto; for in his own hands he had really concentrated all the powers of the realm, and woe to him who trifled with a majesty so real and so imperial!
However, notwithstanding all this imposing strength, this mighty domineering will, and this keen intelligence, a man was found bold enough to brave them all in the arena of pure humbug. It was toward the close of the year 1667, when Louis, in the plenitude of military success, returned from his campaign in Flanders, where his invincible troops had proven too much for the broad breeched but gallant Dutchmen. In the short space of three months he had added whole provinces, including some forty or fifty cities and towns, to his dominions; and his fame was ringing throughout Christendom. It had even penetrated to the farthest East; and the King of Siam sent a costly embassy from his remote kingdom, to offer his congratulations and fraternal greeting to the most eminent potentate of Europe.
Louis had already removed the pageantries of his royal household to his magnificent new palace of Versailles, on which the wealth of conquered kingdoms had been lavished, and there, in the Great Hall of Mirrors, received the homage of his own nobles and the ambassadors of foreign powers. The utmost splendor of which human life was susceptible seemed so common and familiar in those days, that the train was dazzling indeed that could excite any very particular attention. What would have seemed stupendous elsewhere was only in conformity with all the rest of the scene at Versailles. But, at length, there came something that made even the pampered courtiers of the new Babylon stare﻿—a Persian embassy. Yes, a genuine, actual, living envoy from that wonderful Empire in the East, which in her time had ruled the whole Oriental world, and still retained almost fabulous wealth and splendor.

===Chinese===

里扎贝伊，出使路易十四的波斯使节。

就武功而言，法兰西历史上最辉煌壮丽、仅有一例可与之比肩的统治时期，莫过于“太阳王”路易十四的时代。那是一个挥金如土、大兴土木、盛典连连、华服骏马、贵族傲慢、战功赫赫、捷报频传的时代。尤为显著的是，外国列强派往法国宫廷的各色特使数量之众、规格之高亦冠绝一时。其中西班牙、尼德兰、大不列颠与威尼斯争奇斗艳，排场铺张、奢华无比。而我即将讲述的这桩奇特且有凭有据的骗局，竟敢在这样一个时代、对着法兰西的路易十四这样的人物公然施展，实在堪称胆大包天、气魄惊人。

“朕即国家”（*L’état c’est moi*），这句路易十四的名言可谓意味深长；因为彼时全国大权确实尽归他一人独揽，谁敢戏弄如此威严赫赫的帝王，必遭大祸！

然而，尽管坐拥如此慑人的国力、如此专横的意志与如此敏锐的才智，世上偏偏还是冒出了一个胆大妄为之徒，敢在纯粹的欺罔（humbug）竞技场上向他叫板。事情发生在 1667 年底前后：当时路易十四正值军威鼎盛之际，刚刚结束佛兰德斯战役凯旋归来——他麾下所向披靡的军队已证明，那些穿着肥大马裤却勇猛顽强的荷兰人终究不是对手。短短三个月间，他便将整片行省连同四五十座城镇收入版图；他的威名响彻整个基督教世界，甚至传到了最遥远的东方，连暹罗国王也从万里之外的王国遣来耗资不菲的使团，向欧洲最显赫的君主致以祝贺与兄弟般的问候。
