路易十四早已将王室的盛大排场迁往他那座耗尽被征服王国财富而建成的宏伟新宫——凡尔赛宫，并在大镜厅（Great Hall of Mirrors）接受本国贵族与外国使节的朝觐。在那个年代，人世所能想象的极致奢华已然司空见惯，若非耀眼夺目到极致，休想引起多少特别关注；在别处足以令人目瞪口呆的盛景，到了凡尔赛不过是寻常底色。然而，终于来了一件连这座“新巴比伦”里养尊处优的朝臣们也瞪大眼睛的稀罕事——一支波斯使团。没错，一位货真价实、活生生的特使，来自东方那座昔日曾统治整个东方世界、至今仍保有近乎神话般财富与辉煌的神奇帝国。

===Original===

It was announced formally, one morning, to Louis, that His Most Serene Excellency, Riza Bey, with an interminable tail of titles, hangers-on and equipages, had reached the port of Marseilles, having journeyed by way of Trebizond and Constantinople, to lay before the great “King of the Franks” brotherly congratulations and gorgeous presents from his own illustrious master, the Shah of Persia. This was something entirely to the taste of the vain French ruler, whom unlimited good fortune had inflated beyond all reasonable proportions. He firmly believed that he was by far the greatest man who had ever lived; and had an embassy from the moon or the planet Jupiter been announced to him, would have deemed it not only natural enough, but absolutely due to his preeminence above all other human beings. Nevertheless, he was, secretly, immensely pleased with the Persian demonstration, and gave orders that no expense should be spared in giving the strangers a reception worthy of himself and France.
It would be needless for me to detail the events of the progress of Riza Bey from Marseilles to Paris, by way of Avignon and Lyons. It was certainly in keeping with the pretensions of the Ambassador. From town to town the progress was a continued ovation. Triumphal arches, bonfires, chimes of bells, and hurrahing crowds in their best bibs and tuckers, military parades and civic ceremonies, everywhere awaited the children of the farthest East, who were stared at, shouted at﻿—and by some wretched cynics sneered and laughed at﻿—to their hearts’ content. All modern glory very largely consists in being nearly stunned with every species of noise, choked with dust, and dragged about through the streets, until you are well nigh dead. Witness the Japanese Embassy and their visit to this country, where, in some cases, the poor creatures, after hours of unmitigated boring with all sorts of mummery, actually had their pigtails pulled by Young America in the rear, and﻿—as at the windows of Willard’s Hotel in Washington﻿—were stirred up with long canes, like the Polar Bear or the Learned Seal.
Still Riza Bey and his dozen or two of dusky companions did not, by any means, cut so splendid a figure as had been expected. They had with them some camels, antelopes, bulbuls, and monkeys﻿—like any travelling caravan, and were dressed in the most outrageous and outlandish attire. They jabbered, too, a gibberish utterly incomprehensible to the crowd, and did everything that had never been seen or done before. All this, however, delighted the populace. Had they been similarly transmogrified, or played such queer pranks themselves, it would only have been food for mockery; but the foreign air and fame of the thing made it all wonderful, and, as the chief rogue in the plot had foreseen, blinded the popular eye and made his “embassy” a complete success.
At length, after some four weeks of slow progress, the “Persians” arrived at Paris, where they were received, as had been expected, with tremendous éclat. They entered by Barriére du Trône, so styled because it was there that Louis Quatorze himself had been received upon a temporary throne, set up, with splendid decorations and triumphal arches, in the open air, when he returned from his Flanders campaign. Riza Bey was upon this occasion a little more splendid than he had been on his way from the seacoast, and really loomed up in startling style in his tall, black, rimless hat of wool, shaped precisely like an elongated flowerpot, and his silk robes dangling to his heels and covered with huge painted figures and bright metal decorations of every shape and size unknown, to European man-millinery. A circlet or collar, apparently of gold, set with precious stones (California diamonds!) surrounded his neck, and monstrous glittering rings covered all the fingers, and even the thumbs of both his hands. His train, consisting of sword, cup, and pipe bearers, doctors, chief cooks, and bottle-washers, cork extractors and chiropodists (literally so, for it seems that sharing the common lot of humanity, great men have corns even in Persia,) were similarly arrayed as to fashion, but less stupendously in jewelry.

===Chinese===

一天早晨，朝臣正式向路易十四禀报：至高无上、德高望重的里扎贝伊阁下（Riza Bey）带着一长串没完没了的头衔、随从与车马仪仗，已途经特拉布宗与君士坦丁堡抵达马赛港，特来向伟大的“法兰克之王”呈递其英明主上波斯沙阿的兄弟般祝贺与华美厚礼。这正中那位虚荣心极强的法国君主下怀——无休止的好运早已把他吹捧得膨胀到不可理喻的地步。他坚信自己是古往今来最伟大的人物；哪怕有人通报说月球或木星派来了使团，他也会觉得再自然不过，甚至理应如此以彰显他凌驾于全人类之上的至尊地位。话虽如此，他私下里对波斯人的这番仰慕之举还是欣喜若狂，立即下旨不惜一切代价，以配得上他本人与法兰西的规格隆重接待这批异域贵宾。

里扎贝伊一行从马赛出发、经由阿维尼翁与里昂前往巴黎的沿途盛况，我便无需细表了。那排场自然与这位大使的身价相称。每过一座城镇，无不是连绵不断的欢呼盛典。凯旋门、篝火、钟鸣，以及穿着最体面盛装欢呼雀跃的百姓、阅兵式与市政庆典，处处迎候着这些极东之地的子民；他们任由众人瞪眼打量、高声喝彩——当然也少不了被几个刻薄的犬儒私下嗤笑——玩得好不痛快。世上所有现代荣耀，很大程度上无非就是被各色喧嚣震得几近耳聋、被灰尘呛得喘不过气、被人拽着游街示众直到精疲力竭。君不见当年日本使团造访我国时的光景：有几回，那帮可怜虫在忍受了几个钟头各色装神弄鬼的枯燥折磨后，竟然在后头被美国顽童扯辫子，甚至在华盛顿威拉德旅馆的窗前，被人拿长手杖捅来捅去，活像动物园里的北极熊或会算术的海豹。

平心而论，里扎贝伊和他那十几二十个皮肤黝黑的随从，排场其实远不如人们原先想象的那般富丽堂皇。他们带着几头骆驼、羚羊、夜莺与猴子——活像一支寻常流动商队——身上穿着极其怪诞离奇的异域服饰。他们嘴里叽里呱啦说着人群完全听不懂的鸟语，一举一动皆是世人前所未闻、前所未见。然而，恰恰是这一切让老百姓乐坏了。要是本国人自己打扮成这副德行或耍这种怪把戏，准保沦为笑柄；可一旦披上异域风情与盛名的外衣，便统统显得神奇无比——正如这场阴谋的头号无赖早已料到的那样，这迷住了世俗的眼睛，让他的“使团”大获全胜。

终于，经过约莫四周的缓慢行进，“波斯人”抵达了巴黎，不出所料地受到了极其隆重的欢迎。他们从王座城门（Barrière du Trône）入城——该城门之所以得名，正是因为当年路易十四佛兰德斯凯旋归来时，曾在此处露天搭起一座饰有华丽装饰与凯旋门的临时宝座接受欢呼。此番进城，里扎贝伊打扮得比从海滨一路走来时更加华贵几分，着实令人眼前一亮：头戴一顶高耸的无檐黑羊毛毡帽，形状活像一只拉长了的花盆；丝绸长袍拖到脚后跟，上面绘满巨大图案，缀着各式各样欧洲男装裁缝闻所未闻的亮闪闪金属饰物。脖子上套着一圈看似纯金、镶满宝石（实乃“加州钻石”即水晶玻璃！）的项圈，十根手指——甚至连两只大拇指——都戴满了硕大耀眼的戒指。他身后的随从队伍由佩剑官、司爵官、烟斗侍从、御医、御厨、洗瓶杂役、拔木塞工乃至修脚师组成（千真万确，看来无论多伟大的人物都逃不脱凡人宿命，哪怕在波斯也会长鸡眼），衣着样式相仿，只是珠宝没那么吓人罢了。

===Original===

Well, after the throng had scampered, crowded, and shouted themselves hoarse, and had straggled to their homes, sufficiently tired and pocket-picked, the Ambassador and his suite were lodged in sumptuous apartments in the old royal residence of the Tuileries, under the care and charge of King Louis’ own assistant Majordomo and a guard of courtiers and regiments of Royal Swiss. Banqueting and music filled up the first evening; and upon the ensuing day His Majesty, who thus did his visitors especial honor, sent the Duc de Richelieu, the most polished courtier and diplomatist in France, to announce that he would graciously receive them on the third evening at Versailles.
Meanwhile the most extensive preparations were made for the grand audience thus accorded; and when the appointed occasion had arrived, the entire Gallery of Mirrors with all the adjacent spaces and corridors, were crowded with the beauty, the chivalry, the wit, taste, and intellect of France at that dazzling period. The gallery, which is three hundred and eighty feet in length by fifty in height, derives its name from the priceless mirrors which adorn its walls, reaching from floor to ceiling, opposite the long row of equally tall and richly mullioned windows that look into the great court and gardens. These windows, hung with the costliest silk curtains and adorned with superb historical statuary, give to the hall a light and aerial appearance indescribably enchanting; while the mirrors reflect in ten thousand variations the hall itself and its moving pageantry, rendering both apparently interminable. Huge marble vases filled with odorous exotics lined the stairways, and twelve thousand wax lights in gilded brackets, and chandeliers of the richest workmanship, shone upon three thousand titled heads.
Louis the Great himself never appeared to finer advantage. His truly royal countenance was lighted up with pride and satisfaction as the Envoy of the haughty Oriental king approached the splendid throne on which he sat, and as he descended a step to meet him and stood there in his magnificent robes of state, the Persian envoy bent the knee, and with uncovered head presented the credentials of his mission. Of the crowd that immediately surrounded the throne, it is something to say that the Grand Colbert, the famous Minister, and the Admiral Duquesne were by no means the most eminent, nor the lovely Duchess of Orleans and her companion, the bewitching Mademoiselle de Kerouaille, who afterward changed the policy of Charles II, of England, by no means the most beautiful personages in the galaxy.
A grand ball and supper concluded this night of splendor, and Riza Bey was fairly launched at the French court; every member of which, to please the King, tried to outvie his compeers in the assiduity of his attentions, and the value of the books, pictures, gems, equipages, arms, etc., which they heaped upon the illustrious Persian. The latter gentleman very quietly smoked his pipe and lounged on his divan before company, and diligently packed up the goods when he and his “jolly companions” were left alone. The presents of the Shah had not yet arrived, but were daily expected via Marseilles, and from time to time the olive-colored suite was diminished by the departure of one of the number with his chest on a special mission (so stated) to England, Austria, Portugal, Spain, and other European powers.

===Chinese===

好吧，待人群跑累了、挤够了、喊哑了嗓子，各自拖着疲惫身躯与被掏空的衣兜散回家后，大使及其随从被安置在古老王宫杜伊勒里宫（Tuileries）的豪华套房里，由路易国王自己的副总管、一班侍臣以及瑞士皇家卫队负责照料护卫。头一天晚上在盛宴与丝竹管弦中度过；次日，陛下为示对贵宾的殊荣，特派全法国最温文尔雅的朝臣兼外交家黎塞留公爵（Duc de Richelieu）前去传旨，宣布将于第三天傍晚在凡尔赛宫隆重接见使团。

其间，宫廷为这场盛大觐见进行了极其周密的筹备；等到约定良辰一到，整座镜厅连同毗邻的所有厅堂走廊，挤满了那个璀璨时代的法兰西佳丽、骑士、才子、名流与精英。镜厅长三百八十英尺、高五十英尺，因壁上镶嵌的无价巨镜而得名——巨镜从地板直抵穹顶，正对着一长排同样高大、窗棂华丽的落地长窗，窗外便是大庭院与御花园。长窗垂挂着最名贵的丝绸帷幔，点缀着精美的历史雕塑，令整座大厅显得明亮通透、仙气缥缈、美不胜收；而巨镜将大厅本身与流动的华美仪仗折射出万千重幻影，仿佛绵延无尽。楼梯两侧摆满插着芬芳异域奇花的硕大大理石花瓶，镀金壁灯架与工艺最奢华的枝形吊灯上燃着一万两千支蜡烛，照耀着三千颗顶着贵族头衔的脑袋。

路易大帝本人从未显得如此风采照人。当那位傲慢东方君主的特使走近他高踞的华丽宝座时，国王那张真正威严的帝王面庞因骄傲与满足而容光焕发；国王走下一级台阶迎上前去，身披宏伟国袍巍然立定，波斯使节随即屈膝行礼，脱帽呈递出使国书。单说紧簇在宝座周围的那群重臣名媛便足以窥见盛况：鼎鼎大名的财政大臣“伟大科尔贝尔”（Grand Colbert）与海军上将迪凯纳（Admiral Duquesne）绝非其中最显赫者，而娇艳的奥尔良公爵夫人与她那位迷死人不偿命的女伴凯鲁艾尔小姐（Mademoiselle de Kerouaille，日后她竟左右了英王查理二世的国策）也远非这片星河中最绝色的人物。

一场盛大舞会与夜宴为这个辉煌之夜画上句号，里扎贝伊自此在法国宫廷站稳了脚跟；朝中上下为了讨好国王，争相攀比谁对这位尊贵的波斯人献殷勤更勤快，谁堆到他面前的书籍、名画、宝石、马车、兵器等厚礼更贵重。这位波斯绅士在人前只是安安静静地抽着水烟袋、斜倚在长榻上养神，一旦外人散去、只剩下他和那帮“快活伙伴”时，便立刻手脚麻利地把礼物打包装箱。波斯沙阿的国礼此时尚未运到，但据称每天都指望着从马赛运来；隔三岔五，那支橄榄肤色的随从队伍便会少掉一两个人——说是扛着箱子奉派前往英格兰、奥地利、葡萄牙、西班牙及其他欧洲列强执行特殊使命去了。

===Original===

In the meantime, the Bey was fêted in all directions, with every species of entertainment, and it was whispered that the fair ones of that dissolute court were, from the first, eager in the bestowal of their smiles. The King favored his Persian pet with numerous personal interviews, at which, in broken French, the Envoy unfolded the most imposing schemes of Oriental conquest and commerce that his master was cordially willing to share with his great brother of France. At one of these chatty tête-á-têtes, the munificent Riza Bey, upon whom the King had already conferred his own portrait set in diamonds, and other gifts worth several millions of francs, placed in the Royal hand several superb fragments of opal and turquoise said to have been found in a district of country bordering on the Caspian sea, which teemed with limitless treasures of the same kind, and which the Shah of Persia proposed to divide with France for the honor of her alliance. The king was enchanted; for these mere specimens, as they were deemed, must, if genuine, be worth in themselves a mint of money; and a province full of such﻿—why, the thought was charming!
Thus the great Kingfish was fairly hooked, and Riza Bey could take his time. The golden tide that flowed in to him did not slacken, and his own expenses were all provided for at the Tuileries. The only thing remaining to be done was a grand foray on the tradesmen of Paris, and this was splendidly executed. The most exquisite wares of all descriptions were gathered in, without mention of payment; and one by one the Persian phalanx distributed itself through Europe until only two or three were left with the Ambassador.
At length, word was sent to Versailles that the gifts from the Shah had come, and a day was appointed for their presentation. The day arrived, and the Hall of Audience was again thrown open. All was jubilee; the King and the court waited, but no Persian﻿—no Riza Bey﻿—no presents from the Shah!
That morning three men, without either caftans or robes, but very much resembling the blacklegs of the day in their attire and deportment, had left the Tuileries at daylight with a bag and a bundle, and returned no more. They were Riza Bey and his last bodyguard; the bag and the bundle were the smallest in bulk but the most precious in value of a month’s successful plunder. The turquoises and opals left with the King turned out, upon close inspection, to be a new and very ingenious variety of colored glass, now common enough, and then worth, if anything, about thirty cents in cash.
Of course, a hue and cry was raised in all directions, but totally in vain. Riza Bey, the Persian Shah, and the gentlemen in flowerpots, had “gone glimmering through the dream of things that were.” *L’etat c’est moi* had been sold for thirty cents! It was afterward believed that a noted barber and suspected bandit at Leghorn, who had once really traveled in Persia, and there picked up the knowledge and the ready money that served his turn, was the perpetrator of this pretty joke and speculation, as he disappeared from his native city about the time of the embassy in France, and did not return.
All Europe laughed heartily at the Grand Monarque and his fair court-dames, and “An Embassy from Persia” was for many years thereafter an expression similar to “Walker!” in English, or “Buncombe!” in American conversation, when the party using it seeks to intimate that the color of his optics is not a distinct pea-green!

===Chinese===

与此同时，里扎贝伊在各方各面都受到百般款待，各色宴饮娱乐应有尽有；坊间甚至窃窃私语，那座风气靡乱的宫廷里的绝色佳丽们打头一天起便争着向他抛媚眼。国王更是频频赐予这位波斯宠臣单独密谈的机会；在密谈中，特使操着磕磕巴巴的法语，向他铺陈波斯沙阿极其乐意与他伟大的法兰西兄弟共享的东方征服与通商宏图。在其中一次促膝长谈时，慷慨大方的里扎贝伊——国王此前已赏赐给他镶钻御容肖像及其他价值数百万法郎的厚礼——亲手将几块极其华美的欧泊与绿松石碎块放进国王掌心，称其产自里海沿岸某地区，当地此类珍宝取之不尽，波斯沙阿提议以此与法兰西平分，只求结盟之荣。国王听罢心花怒放；毕竟在众人看来，单是这几块“样品”若属真品便已价值连城，更何况一整省都遍地是这种宝贝——哎呀，光想想就美妙极了！

就这样，这条“大鱼王”彻底咬了钩，里扎贝伊大可从容收线。涌向他的黄金潮水丝毫不见减退，而他自己在杜伊勒里宫的一切开销全由王室包办。眼下唯一剩下的收尾工作，便是对巴黎商家来一场大扫荡——这招也执行得漂亮绝伦。各式各样最精致昂贵的货物源源不断搬进使馆，压根儿不提付账二字；接着那支波斯方阵一个接一个分散溜往欧洲各地，直到大使身边只剩下两三个人。

终于，消息传到凡尔赛宫：波斯沙阿的国礼已经运抵，宫廷随即择定了呈献吉日。吉日降临，觐见大厅再度敞开。满朝欢腾雀跃；国王与群臣翘首以待，却不见半个波斯人——不见里扎贝伊——也不见沙阿的国礼！

原来就在当天清晨破晓时分，三个既没穿长衫也没披锦袍、衣着举止倒极像当时赌场老千的男子，拎着一只提包和一个包裹离开了杜伊勒里宫，从此一去不复返。他们正是里扎贝伊和他的最后两名贴身侍卫；那只提包与那个包裹，正是他们一个月来满载而归的战利品中体积最小、价值却最连城的部分。至于留给国王的那几块绿松石与欧泊，经仔细查验，竟是一种当时新巧绝伦的彩色玻璃仿制品——如今早已司空见惯，而在当时顶多也就值三十美分现钱。

不用说，四面八方立刻炸开了锅、全城通缉，却统统徒劳无功。里扎贝伊、波斯沙阿连同那群戴花盆帽的绅士，早已“在往事的幽梦里飘渺消逝”。“朕即国家”竟然被三十美分给卖了！事后人们普遍相信，这桩妙绝的恶作剧兼投机买卖，正是里窝那（Leghorn）一名曾真的游历过波斯、并在当地攒下所需见识与本钱的知名理发师兼疑犯强盗干的好事——毕竟他在波斯使团现身法国前后从家乡失踪，此后再未回去。

全欧洲对着“太阳王”与他那群美艳朝臣痛快地嘲笑了好一阵子；自那以后许多年间，“波斯来的使团”在日常谈吐中便成了一句流行暗语，相当于英语里的“胡扯！”（Walker!）或美语里的“鬼话！”（Buncombe!）——说话人以此暗示自己眼珠子可没长成嫩绿的豌豆色（即没那么好骗）！

## Part / 部

===Original===

IX Religious Humbugs

===Chinese===

第九部 宗教欺罔

### XLIV / 第四十四章

===Original===

Diamond cut diamond; or, yankee superstitions﻿—​Matthias the impostor﻿—​New York follies thirty years ago.
There is a story that on a great and solemn public occasion of the Romish Church, a Pope and a Cardinal were, with long faces, performing some of the gyrations of the occasion, when, instead of a pious ejaculation and reply, which were down in the program, one said to the other gravely, in Latin “*mundus vult decipi*;” and the other replied, with equal gravity and learning, “*decipiatur ergo*:” that is, “All the world chooses to be fooled.”﻿—“Let it be fooled then.”
This seems, perhaps, a reasonable way for priests to talk about ignorant Italians. It may seem inapplicable to cool, sharp, school-trained Protestant Yankees. It is not, however﻿—at least, not entirely. Intelligent Northerners have, sometimes, superstition enough in them to make a first-class Popish saint. If it had not been so, I should not have such an absurd religious humbug to tell of as Robert Matthews, notorious in our goodly city some thirty years ago as “Matthias, the Impostor.”
In the summer of 1832, there was often seen riding in Broadway, in a handsome barouche, or promenading on the Battery (usually attended by a sort of friend or servant,) a tall man, of some forty years of age, quite thin, with sunken, sharp gray eyes, with long, coarse, brown and gray hair, parted in the middle and curling on his shoulders, and a long and coarse but well-tended beard and mustache. These Esau-like adornments attracted much attention in those close-shaving days. He was commonly dressed in a fine green frock-coat, lined with white or pink satin, black or green pantaloons, with polished Wellington boots drawn on outside, fine cambric ruffles and frill, and a crimson silk sash worked with gold and with twelve tassels, for the twelve tribes of Israel. On his head was a steeple-crowned patent-leather shining black cap with a shade.
Thus bedizened, this fantastic-looking personage marched gravely up and down, or rode in pomp in the streets. Sometimes he lounged in a bookstore or other place of semipublic resort; and in such places he often preached or exhorted. His preachments were sufficiently horrible. He claimed to be God the Father; and his doctrine was, in substance, this:﻿—“The true kingdom of God on Earth began in Albany in June 1830, and will be completed in twenty-one years, or by 1851. During this time, wars are to stop, and I, Matthias, am to execute the divine judgments and destroy the wicked. The day of grace is to close on December 1, 1836; and all who do not begin to reform by that time, I shall kill.” The discourses by which this blasphemous humbug supported his pretensions were a hodgepodge of impiety and utter nonsense, with rants, curses and cries, and frightful threats against all objectors. Here is a passage from one;﻿—“All who eat swine’s flesh are of the devil; and just as certain as he eats it he will tell a lie in less than half an hour. If you eat a piece of pork, it will go crooked through you, and the Holy Ghost will not stay in you; but one or the other must leave the house pretty soon. The pork will be as crooked in you as rams’ horns.” Again, he made these pleasant points about the ladies: “They who teach women are of the wicked. All females who lecture their husbands their sentence is: ‘Depart, ye wicked, I know you not.’ Everything that has the smell of woman will be destroyed. Woman is the cap-sheaf of the abomination of desolation, full of all deviltry.” There, ladies! Is anything further necessary to convince you what a peculiarly wicked and horrible humbug this fellow was?

===Chinese===

强中自有强中手，或曰扬基人的迷信——骗子马蒂亚斯——三十年前的纽约蠢事。

有则故事说，在罗马天主教会一场盛大肃穆的公开仪式上，一位教皇与一位红衣主教正板着长脸履行仪式中的种种繁琐仪轨；不料，到了本该念诵虔诚祈祷词与应答词的环节，其中一人却用拉丁语一本正经地对另一人说道：“*mundus vult decipi*”；另一人亦以同样庄严博学的口吻答道：“*decipiatur ergo*”——翻译过来便是：“普天下人都甘愿受骗。”——“那就让他们受骗好了。”

教士们拿这话来议论愚昧的意大利人，或许还算合情合理；放在冷静精明、受过学校教育的新教徒扬基人身上，似乎就格格不入了。然而事实并非如此——至少不完全如此。聪明的北方佬肚子里有时照样藏着足够造就一位头等天主教圣徒的迷信。若非如此，我也不会有这般荒唐的宗教欺罔（humbug）可讲——说的正是大约三十年前在我们这座体面城市里臭名昭著的罗伯特·马修斯（Robert Matthews），人称“骗子马蒂亚斯”（Matthias, the Impostor）。

1832 年夏天，人们常常看见一个身影乘坐华丽的双座四轮敞篷马车驰骋百老汇大道，或在巴特里公园（Battery）散步（身旁通常跟着一名似友似仆的随从）：此人身材高挑，约莫四十岁年纪，颇为消瘦，一双深陷的灰色眼珠精明锐利；棕灰相间的粗长头发从中分开、披垂至肩头卷曲，蓄着一把同样粗长却修剪得整整齐齐的大胡子与八字胡。在那个流行剃光胡须的年代，这副宛如《圣经》中多毛以扫（Esau）般的仪容格外引人注目。他平时身穿一件精致的绿色双排扣长礼服，内衬白色或粉红缎面，下穿黑色或绿色长裤，外面套着锃亮的惠灵顿长筒靴，领口袖口饰有精细细麻布褶边，腰间系着一条绣金红丝绸腰带，垂着十二个流苏，象征以色列十二支派。头上则顶着一顶高尖顶、漆皮闪亮、带帽檐的黑帽子。

这般花里胡哨地打扮一番后，这位怪诞人物便庄重地在大街上踱来踱去，或坐着马车招摇过市。有时他也会在书店或其他半公共场所逗留，并在那儿高声布道或劝诫。他的布道词实在骇人听闻。他自封为圣父上帝，其教义大意如下：“地上真正的上帝之国已于 1830 年 6 月在奥尔巴尼开启，并将于二十一年内、即到 1851 年建成。在此期间，战争将会止息，而我，马蒂亚斯，将执行神圣审判并毁灭恶人。恩典之日将于 1836 年 12 月 1 日截止；凡届时尚未开始悔改者，我必杀之。”这个亵渎神明的骗子用来支撑其妄言的说教，全是亵渎与彻头彻尾的胡言乱语大杂烩，夹杂着狂吠、诅咒、嚎叫以及对一切反对者的恐怖威胁。以下摘录其中一段：“凡吃猪肉者皆属魔鬼；且他一旦吃下，不出半小时必定撒谎。你若吃下一块猪肉，它会在你肚里拐弯乱窜，圣灵便绝不肯留在你体内；二者必有一方很快搬出这屋子。那猪肉在你体内会像公羊角一样弯弯曲曲。”此外，关于女士们他还发表过如下高论：“教导女人之人皆属恶类。凡训斥丈夫之女子，其判词皆为：‘滚开，你们这帮恶人，我不认识你们。’凡带有女人气味之物必遭毁灭。女人乃荒凉可憎之物的顶尖麦捆，满腹尽是魔鬼伎俩。”听见没，诸位女士！还需要再多举什么例子，才能让你们确信这厮是个多么格外恶毒可怖的骗子吗？

===Original===

If we had followed this impostor home, we should have found him lodged, during most of his stay in New-York city, with one or the other of his three chief disciples. These were Pierson, who commonly attended him abroad, Folger, and﻿—for a time only﻿—Mills. All three of these men were wealthy merchants. In their handsome and luxuriously-furnished homes, this noxious humbug occupied the best rooms, and controlled the whole establishment, directing the marketing, meal times, and all other household-matters. Master, mistress (in Mr. Folger’s home,) and domestics were disciples, and obeyed the scamp with an implicitness and prostrate humility even more melancholy than absurd, both as to housekeeping and as to the ceremonies, washing of feet, etc., which he enjoined. When he was angry with his female disciples, he frequently whipped them; but, being a monstrous coward, he never tried it on a man. The least opposition or contradiction threw him into a great rage, and set him screaming, and cursing, and gesticulating like any street drab. When he wished more clothes, which was pretty often, one of his dupes furnished the money. When he wanted cash for any purpose indeed, they gave it him.
This half-crazy knave and abominable humbug was Robert Matthews, who called himself Matthias. He was of Scotch descent, and born about 1790, in Washington county, New York; and his blood was tainted with insanity, for a brother of his died a lunatic. He was a carpenter and joiner of uncommon skill, and up to nearly his fortieth year lived, on the whole, a useful and respectable life, being industrious, a professing Christian of good standing, and (having married in 1813) a steady family-man. In 1828 and 1829, while living at Albany, he gradually became excited about religious subjects; his first morbid symptoms appearing after hearing some sermons by Rev. E. N. Kirk, and Mr. Finney the revivalist. He soon began to exhort his fellow-journeymen instead of minding his work, so uproariously that his employer turned him away.
He discovered a text in the Bible that forbid Christians to shave. He let his hair and beard grow; began street-preaching in a noisy, brawling style; announced that he was going to set about converting the whole city of Albany﻿—which needed it badly enough, if we may believe the political gentlemen. Finding however, that the Lobby, or the Regency, or something or other about the peculiar wickedness of Albany, was altogether too much for him, he began, like Jonah at Nineveh, to announce the destruction of the obstinate town; and at midnight, one night in June, 1826, he waked up his household, and saying that Albany was to be destroyed next day, took his three little boys﻿—two, four, and six years old﻿—his wife and oldest child (a daughter refusing to go,) and “fled to the mountains.” He actually walked the poor little fellows forty miles in twenty-four hours, to his sister’s in Washington county. Here he was reckoned raving crazy; was forcibly turned out of church for one of his brawling interruptions of service, and sent back to Albany, where he resumed his street-preaching more noisily than ever. He now began to call himself Matthias, and claimed to be a Jew. Then he went on a long journey to the Western and Southern States, preaching his doctrines, getting into jail, and sometimes fairly cursing his way out; and, returning to New York city, preached up and down the streets in his crazy, bawling fashion, sometimes on foot and sometimes on an old bony horse.

===Chinese===

倘若我们一路尾随这个骗子回家，便会发觉他在纽约市逗留的大部分时间里，轮流寄宿在他三位头号门徒家中。这三人分别是平日总陪他出行的皮尔森（Pierson）、福尔杰（Folger），以及——仅限一阵子的——米尔斯（Mills）。这三位全都是家资丰厚的富商。在他们华丽奢靡的豪宅里，这个毒瘤般的骗子霸占着最好的房间，掌控着整座宅邸，发号施令管起买菜、开饭时间乃至一切家务琐事。男主人、女主人（在福尔杰先生家里）连同仆役全是他的信徒，对这无赖言听计从、五体投地，无论持家还是奉行他规定的洗脚等宗教仪式，那副卑微顺从的模样与其说荒唐，不如说令人悲哀。每当对女信徒动怒，他动辄挥鞭抽打；但因生性是个彻头彻尾的懦夫，他从未敢对男人动过一指头。哪怕一丁点儿反对或顶嘴，都会令他暴跳如雷，像街头泼妇般尖叫、咒骂、张牙舞爪。每当他想添置新衣——这事儿频繁得很——便有某个冤大头乖乖掏钱。事实上，无论他因何由头要现钱，他们都如数奉上。

这个半疯癫的无赖兼可憎骗子，正是自封“马蒂亚斯”的罗伯特·马修斯。他有苏格兰血统，约于 1790 年生于纽约州华盛顿县；家族血脉里带着疯癫基因，因为他的一个兄弟便是发疯而死的。他原本是个手艺出众的木匠兼细木工，直到将近四十岁之前，大体上过着勤勉正派、有益社会的生活：为人踏实，是名声良好的基督徒，且（自 1813 年成婚以来）顾家安分。 1828 年与 1829 年居住在奥尔巴尼期间，他对宗教话题日渐狂热；最初的病态征兆，出现在他听了几场 E. N. 柯克牧师（Rev. E. N. Kirk）与宗教复兴布道家芬尼先生（Mr. Finney）的讲道之后。很快，他便抛下木工活儿专心向工友们高声劝诫，闹得鸡犬不宁，雇主只得将他辞退。

他从《圣经》里翻出一段据称禁止基督徒剃须的经文，于是任由头发胡须疯长；开始在街头喧哗咆哮地布道，宣称自己即将着手感化整座奥尔巴尼城——若信得过政界老爷们的话，那座城确实急需感化。然而他很快发现，奥尔巴尼的议会游说团、摄政集团（指当地政党机器）或者其他什么特有的罪孽实在顽固不化，远非他所能应付；于是他学起尼尼微城的约拿，开始预言这座冥顽不灵的城池即将毁灭。 1826 年 6 月的某天半夜，他叫醒全家，声称奥尔巴尼次日便要天火焚城，随后带着三个幼子——年仅两岁、四岁和六岁——与妻子（唯有长女拒绝同行），“逃往山中”。他居然逼着可怜的幼童们在二十四小时内徒步走完四十英里，投奔华盛顿县的姐姐家。当地人认定他彻底疯了；他因大闹教堂打断礼拜被强行架出，随后被押送回奥尔巴尼，在那里变本加厉地继续街头咆哮布道。此时他开始自称马蒂亚斯，并宣称自己是犹太人。接着他踏上前往西部与南部各州的漫长游历，沿途宣扬他的邪说，时不时吃牢饭，有时靠一通恶毒咒骂居然真能骂出大牢；最后回到纽约市，继续沿着大街小巷疯癫咆哮地布道，时而步行，时而骑着一匹瘦骨嶙峋的老马。

===Original===

His New York city dupes, Elijah Pierson and Benjamin H. Folger and their families, together with a Mr. Mills and a few more, figured prominently in the chief chapter of Matthews’ career, during two years and a half, from May, 1832, to the fall of 1834.
Pierson and Folger were the leaders in the folly. These men, merchants of wealth and successful in business, were of that sensitive and impressible religious nature which is peculiarly credulous and liable to enthusiasms and delusions. They had been, with a number of other persons, eagerly engaged in some extravagant religious performances, including excessive fasts and asceticisms, and a plan, formed by one of their lady friends, to convert all New York by a system of female visitations and preachings﻿—a plan not so very foolish, I may just remark, if the she apostles are only pretty enough!
Pierson, the craziest of the crew, besides other wretched delusions, had already fancied himself Elijah the Tishbite; and when his wife fell ill and died a little while before this time, had first tried to cure her, and then to raise her from the dead, by anointing with oil and by the prayer of faith, as mentioned in the Epistle of Saint James.
Curiously enough, a sort of lair or nest, very soft and comfortable, was thus made ready for our religious humbug, just as he wanted it worst; for in these days he was but seedy. He heard something of Pierson, I don’t know how; and on the 5th of May, 1832, he called on him. Very quickly the poor fellow recognized the long-bearded prophetical humbug as all that he claimed to be﻿—a possessor and teacher of all truth, and as God himself.
Mills and Folger easily fell into the same pitiable foolery, on Pierson’s introduction. And the lucky humbug was very soon living in clover in Mills’ house, which he chose first; had admitted the happy fools, Pierson and Folger, as the first two members of his true church; Pierson, believing that from Elijah the Tishbite he had become John the Baptist, devoted himself as a kind of servant to his new Messiah; and the deluded men began to supply all the temporal wants of the impostor, believing their estates set apart as the beginning of the material Kingdom of God!
After three months, some of Mills’ friends, on charges of lunacy, caused Mills to be sent to Bloomingdale Asylum, and Matthias to be thrust into the insane poor’s ward at Bellevue, where his beard was forcibly cut off, to his extreme disgust. His brother, however, got him out by a habeas corpus, and he went to live with Folger. Mills now disappears from the story.

===Chinese===

自 1832 年 5 月至 1834 年秋这整整两年半间，马修斯生涯中最风光的篇章便围绕着他在纽约市的几名冤大头展开——伊莱贾·皮尔森（Elijah Pierson）、本杰明·H. 福尔杰（Benjamin H. Folger）及其家眷，外加一位米尔斯先生（Mr. Mills）和少数几人。

皮尔森与福尔杰是这场闹剧的领头羊。这两位事业有成、家财万贯的商人，恰恰生就一副敏感易感、极易轻信且最易陷入狂热与妄想的宗教性情。此前不久，他们便已与一批同伴热衷于种种极端的宗教苦行，包括过度禁食、苦修，以及由他们一位女性友人提出的“靠女信徒挨家挨户登门布道感化全纽约”的计划——顺带一提，只要那些女使徒生得够漂亮，这计划倒也算不上太蠢！

这一伙人中最疯癫的皮尔森，除了其他可怜妄想之外，早已自以为是古先知提斯比人以利亚（Elijah the Tishbite）；不久前他妻子患病去世时，他还依照《雅各书》所载，先是企图用抹油与信心祈祷替她治病，死后又企图使她死而复生。

说来也巧，恰在我们这位宗教骗子最落魄寒酸、急需安身立命之时，这么一个既柔软又舒适的安乐窝已替他铺设妥当。不知他从何处听说了皮尔森的名头，便在 1832 年 5 月 5 日登门造访。没费多少工夫，那可怜虫便认定眼前这位长须飘飘的先知骗子果真是他自诩的一切——真理的掌握者与导师，乃至上帝本尊。

经皮尔森引见，米尔斯与福尔杰轻而易举地也跌进了同一场可悲的愚行。交了好运的骗子很快便住进了米尔斯家里过起了神仙日子（这是他头一个挑中的落脚处）；他接纳了皮尔森与福尔杰这对欢天喜地的傻瓜作为他“真教会”的头两名成员；皮尔森深信自己已从提斯比人以利亚转世成了施洗约翰，甘心像仆役般侍奉他的新弥赛亚；而这群被蒙蔽的富商开始包办骗子在俗世的一切所需，笃信自家的产业已被划归上帝地上天国的起点！

三个月后，米尔斯的几位亲友以精神失常为由，将米尔斯送进了布卢明代尔疯人院（Bloomingdale Asylum），并把马蒂亚斯塞进了贝尔维尤医院（Bellevue）的疯癫贫民病房；在那里，他的大胡子被人强行剃光，气得他咬牙切齿。不过，他的兄弟凭一纸人身保护令将他保释出来，他随即搬去与福尔杰同住。至此，米尔斯退出了这段故事。

===Original===

Matthias remained in the full enjoyment of his luxurious establishment, until September, 1834, it is true, with a few uncomfortable interruptions. He was always both insolent and cowardly, and thus often irritated some strong-minded auditor, and got himself into some pickle where he had to sneak out, which he did with much ease. In his seedy days the landlord of a hotel in whose barroom he used to preach and curse, put him down when he grew too abusive, by coolly and sternly telling him to go to bed. Mr. Folger himself had one or two brief intervals of sense, in one of which, angered at some insolence of Matthias, he seized him by the throat, shook him well, and flung him down upon a sofa. The humbug knowing that his living was in danger, took this very mildly, and readily accepted the renewed assurances of belief which poor Folger soon gave him. In the village of Sing Sing where Folger had a country-seat which he called Mount Zion, Matthias was exceedingly obnoxious. His daughter had married a Mr. Laisdell; and the humbug, who claimed that all Christian marriages were void and wicked, by some means induced the young wife to come to Sing Sing, where he whipped her more than once quite cruelly. Her husband came and took her away after encountering all the difficulty which Matthias dared make; and, at a hearing in the matter before a magistrate, he was very near getting tarred and feathered, if not something worse, and the danger frightened him very much.
He barely escaped being shaved by violence, and being thrown overboard to test his asserted miraculous powers, at the hands of a stout and incredulous farmer on the steamboat between Sing Sing and New York. While imprisoned at Bellevue before his trial, he was tossed in a blanket by the prisoners, to make him give them some money. The unlucky prophet dealt out damnation to them in great quantities; but they told him it wouldn’t work, and the poor humbug finally, instead of casting them into hell, paid them a quarter of a dollar apiece to let him off. When he was about to leave Folger’s house, some roguish young men of Sing Sing forged a warrant, and with a counterfeit officer seized the humbug, and a second time shaved him by force. He was one day terribly “set back” as the phrase is, by a sharpish answer. He gravely asserted to a certain man that he had been on the Earth eighteen hundred years. His hearer, startled and irreverent, exclaimed:
“The devil you have! Do you tell me so?”
“I do,” said the prophet.
“Then,” rejoined the other, “all I have to say is, you are a remarkably good-looking fellow for one of your age.”
The confounded prophet grinned, scowled, and exclaimed indignantly:
“You are a devil, Sir!” and marched off.

===Chinese===

直到 1834 年 9 月，马蒂亚斯一直舒舒服服地享用着他那奢华排场，其间虽也夹杂着几桩不太愉快的波折。他向来既傲慢又怯懦，因此常常激怒头脑清醒的听众，把自己卷进麻烦里，末了只得灰溜溜地开溜——好在他开溜的本事倒极利索。早年落魄时，他常在一家旅馆酒吧里布道兼骂街，每当他骂得太过火，老板便冷冷地、严厉地喝令他滚去睡觉，立时就把他镇住了。福尔杰先生自己也曾有过一两次短暂的清醒时刻：其中一回因受不了马蒂亚斯的嚣张，一把揪住他的脖子狠命摇晃，将他甩翻在沙发上。这骗子深知饭碗岌岌可危，竟忍气吞声地受了下来，没过多久便欣然接受了可怜的福尔杰回心转意后再度奉上的虔诚效忠。在福尔杰拥有一处名为“锡安山”乡间别墅的辛辛村，马蒂亚斯更是人人喊打。他的女儿嫁给了一位莱斯德尔先生（Mr. Laisdell）；这骗子宣称一切基督教婚姻皆属无效且邪恶，不知用什么手段把年轻媳妇哄到了辛辛，在那里不止一次极其残忍地鞭打她。她丈夫赶来，顶住马蒂亚斯敢使出的一切阻挠把妻子接走；随后在治安法官面前听证此事时，马蒂亚斯险些被人涂柏油粘羽毛游街——甚至更糟——吓得他魂飞魄散。

在辛辛往返纽约的蒸汽船上，一名体格健壮、毫不买账的农夫还差点儿强行剃光他的胡子并将他扔下船去，以检验他自吹的神通。受审前关押在贝尔维尤期间，狱友们拿毯子把他抛到半空颠着玩，逼他掏钱孝敬。这位倒霉的先知连珠炮般向他们降下天谴诅咒；可囚犯们告诉他这招不好使，可怜的骗子最后非但没把他们打入地狱，反倒每人塞了两角五分钱才买得自己脱身。临近搬离福尔杰家时，辛辛镇几个调皮捣蛋的年轻人伪造了一张逮捕令，找来个假警官抓走这骗子，第二次强行剃光了他的胡子。还有一天，他被一句犀利的回敬噎得够呛：他一本正经地向某人宣称自己已在地球上活了一千八百年。听众吃了一惊，大不敬地叫道：

“见鬼，真有这事！你没唬我吧？”

“千真万确，”先知答道。

“那好，”另一人接话道，“我唯一能说的是，就您这把年纪而言，您保养得可真够帅气的。”

被噎得哑口无言的先知龇了龇牙、沉下脸，愤愤然吼道：

“先生，你就是个魔鬼！”说罢拂袖而去。

===Original===

In the beginning of August, 1834, the unhappy Pierson died in Folger’s house, under circumstances amounting to strong circumstantial evidence that Matthias, with the help of the colored cook, an enthusiastic disciple, had poisoned him with arsenic. The rascal pretended that his own curse had slain Pierson. There was a post mortem, an indictment, and a trial, but the evidence was not strong enough for conviction. Being acquitted, he was at once tried again for an assault and battery on his daughter by the aforesaid whippings; and on this charge he was found guilty and sent to the county jail for three months, in April, 1835. The trial for murder was just before﻿—the prophet having lain in prison since his apprehension for murder in the preceding autumn. Mr. Folger’s delusion had pretty much disappeared by the end of the summer of 1834. He had now become ruined, partly in consequence of foolish speculations jointly with Pierson, believed to be conducted under Divine guidance, and partly because his strange conduct destroyed his business reputation and standing. The death of Pierson, and some very queer matters about another apparent poisoning-trick, awakened the suspicions of the Folgers; and after a good deal of scolding and trouble with the impostor, who hung on to his comfortable home like a good fellow, Folger finally turned him out, and then had him taken up for swindling. He had been too foolish himself, however, to maintain this charge; but, shortly after, the others, for murder and assault, followed, with a little better success.
This imprisonment seems to have put a sudden and final period to the prophetical and religious operations of Master Matthias, and to the follies of his victims, too. I know of no subsequent developments of either kind. Matthias disappears from public life, and died, it is said, in Arkansas; but when, or after what further career, I don’t know. He was a shallow knave, and undoubtedly also partly crazy and partly the dupe of his own nonsense. If he had not so opportunely found victims of good standing, he would not have been remembered at all, except as George Munday, the “hatless prophet,” and “Angel Gabriel Orr,” are remembered﻿—as one more obscure, crazy street-preacher. And as soon as his accidental supports of other people’s money and enthusiasm failed him, he disappeared at once. Many of my readers will remember distinctly, as I do, the remarkable career of this man, and the humiliating position in which his victims were placed. In the face of such an exposition as this of the weakness and credulity of poor human nature in this enlightened country of common schools and colleges, in the boasted wide-awake nineteenth century, who shall deny that we can study with interest and profit the history of impositions which have been practiced upon mankind in every possible phase throughout every age of the world, including the age in which we live? There is literally no end to these humbugs; and the reader of these pages, weak as may be my attempts to do the subject justice, will learn that there is no country, no period, and no sphere in life which has not been impiously invaded by the genius of humbug, under more disguises and in more shapes than it has entered into the heart of man to conceive.

===Chinese===

1834 年 8 月初，不幸的皮尔森死在福尔杰家中，种种迹象构成强有力的间接证据，指向马蒂亚斯在一名狂热信徒黑人厨娘的协助下用砒霜毒死了他。这无赖却假惺惺地宣称是自己的诅咒杀死了皮尔森。随后进行了尸检、起诉与庭审，但证据终究不足以定罪。刚获无罪释放，他立刻又因前述鞭打亲生女儿一案被控殴打伤害罪受审；这一次他被判有罪，于 1835 年 4 月被送进县监狱服刑三个月。谋杀案审判恰在此前不久——这位先知自前一年秋天因谋杀罪名被捕起便一直关在狱中。到了 1834 年夏末，福尔杰先生的迷梦已然醒了大半。此刻的他已倾家荡产，一部分原因在于他与皮尔森一道受所谓“神明指引”搞了几桩愚蠢透顶的投机，另一部分则因他怪诞的举止彻底毁掉了自己的商业信誉与地位。皮尔森之死，外加另一桩看似下毒把戏的蹊跷事，终于警醒了福尔杰一家；与这个死皮赖脸赖在舒适豪宅不肯走的骗子好一番争吵纠缠后，福尔杰终于将他扫地出门，随后以诈骗罪报警抓他。不过他自己实在太蠢，没能撑住这项指控；好在紧接着谋杀与殴打两项控罪接踵而至，总算略有所获。

这次牢狱之灾似乎骤然彻底终结了马蒂亚斯老爷的先知宗教大业，也终结了他那些受害者的愚行。此后两方面均未再听说任何下文。马蒂亚斯就此淡出公众视野，据说死在阿肯色州；至于死于何年、临死前又干过什么勾当，我便不得而知。他不过是个浅薄的无赖，无疑也带着几分疯癫，半是骗人、半是自己信了自己的鬼话。若非他恰逢其会地撞上了几位有头有脸的冤大头，他原本绝不会被世人铭记，顶多像“无帽先知”乔治·芒迪与“天使加百列·奥尔”一样，沦为又一个籍籍无名的街头疯癫布道者罢了。一旦旁人的钱财与狂热这两根意外支柱崩塌，他便立刻销声匿迹。我的许多读者定然像我一样，清楚记得此人传奇的一生，以及他的受害者沦落的屈辱境地。面对这般铁证——在这所公立学校与高等学府林立的开明国度里，在自诩精明警醒的十九世纪，可怜的人性竟仍如此脆弱轻信——谁还能否认，研究古往今来（包括我们所生活的时代）人类在各个层面遭受的欺罔史，既饶有趣味又大有裨益呢？世上的骗局简直永无止境；哪怕拙笔力有不逮，读者翻阅这些篇章后也必将明白：世上没有任何一个国度、任何一个时代、任何一个生活领域，未曾被欺罔之灵肆无忌惮地侵入——其伪装之多、形态之繁，远非人心所能臆测。

### XLV / 第四十五章

===Original===

A religious humbug on John Bull﻿—​Joanna Southcott﻿—​The second Shiloh.
Joanna Southcott was born at St. Mary’s Ottery in Devonshire, about the year 1750. She was a plain, stout-limbed, hard-fisted farmer lass, whose toils in the field﻿—for her father was in but very moderate circumstances﻿—had tawned her complexion and hardened her muscles, at an early age. As she grew toward woman’s estate, necessity compelled her to leave her home and seek service in the city of Exeter, where for many years, she plodded on very quietly in her obscure path, first, as a domestic hireling, and subsequently as a washer woman.
I have an old and esteemed friend on Staten Island whose father, still living, recollects Joanna well, as she used to come regularly to his house of a Monday morning, to her task of cleansing the family linen. He was then but a little lad, yet he remembers her quite well, with her stout, robust frame, and buxom and rather attractive countenance, and her queer ways. Even then she was beginning to invite attention by her singular manners and discourse, which led many to believe her demented.
It was at Exeter that Joanna became religiously impressed, and joined the Wesleyan Methodists, as a strict and extreme believer in the doctrines of that sect. During her attendance upon the Wesleyan rites, she became intimate with one Sanderson, who, whether a designing rogue, or only a very fanatical believer, pretended that he had discovered in the good washerwoman a Bible prodigy; and it was not long before the poor creature began literally, to “see sights” and dream dreams of the most preternatural description, for which Sanderson always had ready some very telling interpretation. Her visions were of the most thoroughly “mixed” character withal, sometimes transporting her to the courts of heaven, and sometimes to a very opposite region, celebrated for its latent and active caloric. When she ranged into the lower world, she had a very unpleasant habit of seeing sundry scoffers and unbelievers (in herself) belonging to the congregation, in very close but disadvantageous intercourse with the Evil One, who was represented as having a particular eye to others around her, even while they laid claim to special piety. Of course, such revelations as these could not be tolerated in any well regulated community, and when some most astounding religious gymnastics performed by Joanna in the midst of prayers and sermons, occurred to heap up the measure of her offenses, it became full time to take the matter in hand, and the prophetess was expelled. Now, those whom she had not served up openly with brimstone, agreeing with her about those whom she had thus “cooked,” and delighted in their own exemption from that sort of dressing, seceded inconsiderable numbers, and became Joanna’s followers. This gave her a nucleus to work upon, and between 1790 and 1800, she managed to make herself known throughout Britain, proclaiming that she was to be the destined Mother of the Second Messiah, and although originally quite illiterate, picking up enough general information and Bible lore, to facilitate her publication of several very curious, though sometimes incoherent works. One of the earliest and most startling of these was her “Warning to the whole World, from the Sealed Prophecies of Joanna Southcott, and other communications given since the writings were opened on the 12th of January, 1803.” This foretold the close approach of the great red dragon of the Revelations, “with seven heads and ten horns, and seven crowns upon his heads,” and the birth of the “man-child who was to rule all nations with a rod of iron.”

===Chinese===

针对约翰牛的宗教骗局——乔安娜·索斯科特——第二位示罗。

乔安娜·索斯科特（Joanna Southcott）约于 1750 年生于德文郡奥特里圣玛丽（St. Mary’s Ottery）。她原本是个相貌平平、四肢粗壮、手掌粗糙的农家姑娘；因父亲家境颇为拮据，她年纪轻轻便下地劳作，晒黑了肤色、练硬了筋骨。长大成人后，生活所迫使她离家前往埃克塞特城谋生；在那里，她安安静静、默默无闻地熬了好些年，起初做女佣，后来当洗衣妇。

我在斯塔滕岛有位德高望重的老朋友，他的老父亲至今健在，对乔安娜记忆犹新——当年她每周一早晨都会准时到他家里清洗全家的亚麻衣物。那时他还是个小男孩，却清晰记得她那副结实魁梧的身板、丰满而颇有几分姿色的面庞，以及古怪的举止。甚至早在那时，她便已凭着奇特的言行举止惹人注目，叫不少人以为她神志失常。

正是在埃克塞特，乔安娜深受宗教感召，加入了卫斯理宗循道会，成为该派教义严苛而极端的信徒。参加卫斯理宗礼拜期间，她与一个名叫桑德森（Sanderson）的人过从甚密；此人不知是别有用心的无赖，还是极端狂热的信徒，竟假称自己在这位好心的洗衣妇身上发现了《圣经》奇才；没过多久，这个可怜女人便真的开始“看见异象”、做起极其超自然的奇梦，而桑德森总能信手拈来一套极有说服力的解梦之词。她的幻象可谓“五味杂陈”：时而把她送上天国宫廷，时而又把她抛进截然相反、以潜热与明火著称的地域（地狱）。每当她神游地府时，总有个极讨厌的习惯——看见会众里那些嘲笑她、不信她的人正与魔鬼打得火热却处境凄惨，且魔鬼对她身边其他人（哪怕正自诩格外虔诚之辈）也格外虎视眈眈。不用说，在任何治理有方的教会里，这等“启示”都绝难容忍；当乔安娜在祈祷与讲道正酣之际突然上演几出惊世骇俗的宗教体操，彻底溢出了容忍限度后，教会终于出手整顿，将这位女先知逐出教门。于是，那些尚未被她当众用硫磺火湖“烹煮”过的人，因赞同她对那些已被“料理”之人的揭批、又暗自庆幸自己幸免于难，便成群结队地脱离教会，追随乔安娜而去。这为她攒下了起家的班底；在 1790 年至 1800 年间，她设法让自己名扬全不列颠，宣称自己注定成为“第二位弥赛亚”的母亲；而且尽管原本目不识丁，她却攒够了常识与《圣经》典故，接连出版了好几部古怪甚至语无伦次的著作。其中最早、最骇人听闻的一部，便是《致全世界的警告：摘自乔安娜·索斯科特之封印预言及 1803 年 1 月 12 日启封以来所获之其他启示》。该书预言《启示录》中“七头十角、头上戴着七个冠冕”的大红龙已迫近眼前，并宣告“将用铁杖辖管万国的男婴”即将降生。

===Original===

In 1805, a shoemaker named Tozer built her a chapel in Exeter at his own expense, and it was, from the first, constantly filled on service-days with eager worshipers. Here she gave exhortations, and prophesied in a species of religious frenzy or convulsion, sometimes uttering very heavy prose, and sometimes the most fearful doggerel rhyme resembling﻿—well﻿—perhaps our album effusions here at home! Indeed, I can think of nothing else equally fearful. In these paroxysms, Joanna raved like an ancient Pythoness whirling on her tripod, and to just about the same purpose. Yet, it was astonishing to see how the thing went down. Crowds of intelligent people came from all parts of the United Kingdom to listen, be converted, and to receive the “seals” (as they were called) that secured their fortunate possessor unimpeded and immediate admission to heaven. Of course, tickets so precious could not be given away for nothing, and the seal trade in this new form proved very lucrative.
The most remarkable of all these conversions was that of the celebrated engraver, William Sharp, who, notwithstanding his eminent position as an artist, by no means bore out his name in other things. He had previously become thoroughly imbued with the notions of Swedenborg, Mesmer, and the famous Richard Brothers, and was quite ripe for anything fantastic. Such a convert was a perfect godsend to Joanna, and she was easily persuaded to accompany him to London, where her congregations rapidly increased to enormous proportions, even rivaling those now summoned by the “drum ecclesiastical” and orthodox of the Rev. Mr. Spurgeon.
The whole sect extended until, in 1813, it numbered no less than one hundred thousand members, signed and “sealed”﻿—Mr. Sharp occupying a most conspicuous position at the very footstool of the Prophetess. Late in 1813, appeared the *Book of Wonders*, “in five parts,” and it was a clincher. Poor Sharp came in largely for the expenses, but valiantly stood his ground against it all. At length, in 1814, the great Joanna dazzled the eyes of her adherents and the world at large with her *Prophecies Concerning the Prince of Peace*. This delectable manifesto flatly announced to mankind that the second Shiloh, so long expected, would be born of the Prophetess at midnight, on October 19, in that same year, i.e. 1814. The inspired writer was then *enceinte*, although a virgin, as she expressly and solemnly declared, and in the sixty-fourth year of her age. Among the other preternatural concomitants of this anticipated eventful birth, was the fact that the period of her pregnancy had lasted for several years.

===Chinese===

1805 年，一位名叫托泽（Tozer）的鞋匠自掏腰包在埃克塞特为她建了一座礼拜堂，自打落成起每逢礼拜日便挤满了热切的信众。她在此地发狂抽搐般地高声劝诫与预言，时而吐出沉闷晦涩的散文，时而吟出极其可怕的打油诗——活像……呃……咱们家里纪念册上的酸诗！说真的，我想不出世上还有什么东西比那更吓人。每逢癫狂发作，乔安娜便宛如坐在三脚神座上打转的古希腊皮媞亚女祭司般胡言乱语，效果也一般无二。然而，这玩意儿竟如此受人买账，实在令人瞠目。联合王国各地成群结队的聪明人蜂拥而至，聆听布道、皈依入教，并领取所谓“印封”（seals）——据称拥有此物便能畅通无阻、即刻直入天堂。如此金贵的门票自然不能白送，这种新花样的“印封买卖”很快就成了日进斗金的暴利行当。

所有皈依者中最轰动的一位，莫过于著名铜版画家威廉·夏普（William Sharp）——他作为艺术家固然地位崇高，在其他事上却实在愧对“夏普（敏锐）”这个姓氏。他此前早已深中斯韦登伯格、梅斯梅尔以及大名鼎鼎的理查德·布拉泽斯学说之毒，脑子早就熟透了，什么荒诞奇谈都吃得下。这等大名流送上门来简直是天赐良机，乔安娜毫不费力便被他说动一同前往伦敦；在那里她的会众规模迅速膨胀到惊人地步，甚至足以与如今司布真牧师（Rev. Mr. Spurgeon）敲响“正统教会战鼓”召来的庞大信众相匹敌。

整个教派不断扩张，直到 1813 年，已登记“盖印”的信徒竟不下十万人——夏普先生高居女先知脚凳旁最显赫的位置。 1813 年底，《奇迹之书》（*Book of Wonders*）“五卷本”问世，一锤定音。可怜的夏普为此掏出大笔印费，却依然英勇地死守阵地。终于，在 1814 年，伟大的乔安娜抛出《关于和平之君的预言》（*Prophecies Concerning the Prince of Peace*），令门徒与全世界目眩神迷。这份绝妙宣言直截了当地向全人类宣告：世人翘首以盼的“第二位示罗”（Shiloh，即弥赛亚），将于同年（即 1814 年） 10 月 19 日午夜由女先知本人亲生降世。这位受神启示的作者当时已“身怀六甲”，尽管她明确而郑重地声明自己仍是处女，且年方六十四岁。这场万众瞩目的分娩还伴随另一桩超自然奇象：她的孕期据说已经持续了好几年。

===Original===

Of course, this stupendous announcement threw the whole sect into ecstasies of religious exultation; while, on the other hand, it afforded a fruitful subject of ridicule for the utterly irreverent London pamphleteers. Poor Sharp, who had caused a magnificent cradle and baby-wardrobe to be got ready at his own expense, was most unmercifully scored. The infant was caricatured with a long gray beard and spectacles, with Sharp in a duster carefully rocking him to sleep, while Joanna the Prophetess treated the engraver to some “cuts” in her own style, with a bunch of twigs.
On the appointed night, the street in which Joanna lived was thronged with the faithful, who, undeterred by sarcasm, fully credited her prediction. They bivouacked on the sidewalks in motley crowds of men, women, and children; and as the hours wore on, and their interest increased, burst forth into spontaneous psalmody. The adjacent thoroughfares were as densely jammed with curious and incredulous spectators, and the mutton pie and ballad businesses flourished extensively. The interior of the house, with the exception of the sick chamber, was illuminated in all directions, and the dignitaries of the sect held the anterooms and corridors, “in full fig,” to receive the expected guest. But the evening passed, then midnight came, then morning, butalas! no Shiloh; and, little by little, the disappointed throngs dispersed! Poor Joanna, however, kept her bed, and finally, after many fresh paroxysms and prophecies, on the 27th of December, 1814, gave up the ghost﻿—the indefatigable Sharp still declaring that she had gone to heaven for a season, only to legitimatize the unborn infant, and would re-arise again from death, after four days, with the Shiloh in her arms. So firm was this faith in him and many other respectable persons, that the body of the Prophetess was retained in her house until the very last moment. When the dissection demanded by the majority of the sect could no longer be delayed, that operation was performed, and it was found that the subject had died of ovarian dropsy; but was﻿—as she had always maintained herself to be﻿—a virgin. Dr. Reece, who had been a devout believer, but was now undeceived, published a full account of this and all the other circumstances of her death, and another equally earnest disciple bore the expenses of her burial at St. John’s Wood, and placed over her a tombstone with appropriate inscriptions.
As late as 1863, there were many families of believers still existing near Chatham, in Kent; and even in this country can here and there be found admirers of the creed of Joanna Southcott, who are firmly convinced that she will reappear some fine morning, with Sanderson on one side of her and Sharp on the other.

===Chinese===

理所当然，这则惊天动地的宣告令整个教派陷入宗教狂喜的癫狂；而另一方面，它也成了毫无敬畏之心的伦敦小册子作家们取之不尽的嘲讽素材。可怜的夏普自掏腰包置办了一张华丽摇篮与全套婴儿衣橱，结果遭到了最无情的群嘲。讽刺漫画里把那个尚未出世的婴儿画成蓄着长长灰白胡须、架着眼镜的小老头，夏普穿着罩衫小心翼翼地摇着摇篮哄他入睡，而女先知乔安娜则抡起一捆树枝，按她自己的风格赏了这位铜版画家几记“刻痕”（cuts，双关“雕版”与“鞭打”）。

到了约定之夜，乔安娜居住的街道挤满了虔诚的信徒——他们丝毫不为冷嘲热讽所动，对她的预言深信不疑。男女老幼杂处一堂，干脆在人行道上露营守夜；随着时间一分一秒流逝、心头愈发激动，众人情不自禁地齐声唱起圣诗。邻近的街道也同样被好奇又半信半疑的看客堵得水泄不通，羊肉馅饼摊与街头民谣小贩生意兴隆至极。宅子内部除产房外灯火通明，教派众位显贵“盛装出席”，守候在前厅与走廊，恭迎那位期待已久的贵客驾临。然而，黄昏过去了，午夜降临了，黎明又破晓了，可叹呀！半个示罗的影子也没见着；失望的人群只得一点一点散去！可怜的乔安娜从此卧床不起，在经历了无数次新的抽搐与预言之后，终于在 1814 年 12 月 27 日咽气归西——而百折不挠的夏普依然坚称她只是暂时升天去替未出世的婴儿办理合法名分，四天之后便会抱着示罗死而复生。他与许多其他体面人士对此深信不疑，以至于女先知的遗体一直留在宅中直到腐烂前的最后一刻。等到教派绝大多数成员强烈要求的解剖实在无法再拖延下去时，解剖终于进行，结果查明死者死于卵巢水肿；不过她倒确实——正如她一贯自称的那样——仍是个处女。曾是虔诚门徒、此刻终于大梦初醒的里斯医生（Dr. Reece）发表了一份详尽报告，记述了此次解剖及她临终的种种细节；另一位同样热忱的信徒则出资将她安葬在圣约翰伍德（St. John’s Wood），并在墓前立了一块镌刻着恰当碑文的墓碑。

晚至 1863 年，肯特郡查塔姆附近仍有不少家庭信奉此教；哪怕在我们这个国度，也时不时能找着乔安娜·索斯科特教义的崇拜者，他们至今仍坚信：某个晴朗的早晨，她定会再度现身，左手边站着桑德森，右手边站着夏普。

### XLVI / 第四十六章

===Original===

The first humbug in the world﻿—​Advantages of studying the impositions of former ages﻿—​Heathen humbugs﻿—​The ancient mysteries﻿—​The cabiri﻿—​Eleusis﻿—​Isis.
The domain of humbug reaches back to the Garden of Eden, where the Father of lies practised it upon our poor, innocent first grandmother, Eve. This was the first and worst of all humbugs. But from that eventful day to the present moment, falsehood, hypocrisy, deception, imposition, cant, bigotry, false appearances and false pretences, superstitions, and all conceivable sorts of humbugs, have had a full swing, and he or she who watches these things most closely, and reflects most deeply upon these various peculiarities, bearings, and results, will be best qualified to detect and to avoid them. For this reason, I should look upon myself as somewhat of a public benefactor, in exposing the humbugs of the world, if I felt competent to do the subject full justice.
Next to the fearful humbug practiced upon our first parents, came heathen humbugs generally. All heathenism and idolatry are one grand complex humbug to begin with. All the heathen religions always were, and are still, audacious, colossal, yet shallow and foolish, humbugs. The heathen humbugs were played off by the priests, the shrewdest men then alive. It is a curious fact that the heathen humbugs were all solemn. This was because they were intended to maintain the existing religions, which, like all false religions, could not endure ridicule. They always appealed to the pious terrors of the public, as well as to its ignorance and appetite for marvels. They offered nothing pleasant, nothing to love, nothing to gladden the heart and lift it up in joyful gratitude, true adoration, and childlike confidence, prayer, and thanksgiving. On the contrary, awful noises, fearful sights, frightful threats, foaming at the mouth, dark sayings, secret processions, bloody sacrifices, grim priests, costly offerings, sleeps in darksome caverns to wait for a dream from the god﻿—these were the machineries of the ancient heathen. They were as crude and as ferocious as those of the King of Dahomey, or of the barbarous negroes of the Guinea coast. But they often show a cunning as keen and effective as that of any quack, or Philadelphia lawyer, or Davenport Brother, or Jackson Davis of today.

===Chinese===

世上第一桩骗局——研习前代欺诈之益处——异教骗局——古代秘仪——卡比里秘仪——厄琉西斯秘仪——伊西斯秘仪。

欺罔（humbug）的疆域一直可以追溯到伊甸园；在那里，谎言之父将它施展在我们可怜、天真的始祖祖母夏娃身上。那是世上第一桩、也是最恶劣的一场骗局。自那个多事之秋起直至此刻，虚伪、矫饰、欺瞒、诈伪、伪善套话、狂热偏执、虚张声势与假仁假义、迷信以及一切所能想象的欺罔，莫不大行其道；谁最密切地观察这些行径，并最深沉地反思其种种特征、影响与后果，谁便最有资格识破它们并避而远之。正因如此，倘若我自认才力足以胜任此题，我也颇愿将自己在揭露世界骗局一事上的努力视作造福公众的善举。

紧随施展在我们人类始祖身上的那场可怕骗局之后的，便是形形色色的异教骗局。说到底，一切异教崇拜与偶像崇拜本身就是一座庞大繁复的超级骗局。古往今来的一切异教宗教，过去是、如今依然是既狂妄庞大又浅薄愚蠢的欺罔。异教骗局的操盘手是祭司——当时活在世上最精明的一群人。说来奇特，异教骗局个个摆出一副庄严肃穆的嘴脸。原因在于它们旨在维护现存宗教，而一切虚假宗教都经不起半点嘲弄。它们向来诉诸民众虔诚的恐惧心理，以及民众的无知与猎奇嗜好。它们拿不出任何令人愉悦、值得热爱、能令心灵欢欣并生出喜乐感恩、真诚敬仰、赤子般信赖、祈祷与谢恩的东西。恰恰相反：阴森怪响、骇人异象、恐怖威胁、口吐白沫、晦涩神谕、秘密游行、血腥献祭、面目狰狞的祭司、昂贵的供品、睡在幽暗洞穴里苦等神明托梦——这些便是古代异教的整套机关。它们与达荷美国王或几内亚海岸野蛮黑人的把戏一般粗鄙残暴；然而其中展现出的狡黠却往往犀利有效，丝毫不逊于当今任何江湖郎中、费城大律师、达文波特兄弟或杰克逊·戴维斯。

===Original===

The most prominent of the heathen humbugs were the mysteries, the oracles, the sibyls (N.B., the word is often misspelled sybils,) and augury. Every respectable Pagan religion had some mysteries, just as every respectable Christian family has a bible﻿—and, as an ill-natured proverb has it, a skeleton. It was considered a poor religion﻿—a one horse religion, so to speak﻿—that had no mysteries.
The chief mysteries were those of the Cabiri, of Eleusis, and of Isis. These mysteries used exactly the same kind of machinery which proves so effective every day in modern mysteries, viz., shows, processions, voices, lights, dark rooms, frightful sights, solemn mummeries, striking costumes, big talks and preachments, threats, gabbles of nonsense, etc., etc.
The mysteries of the Cabiri are the most ancient of which anything is known. These Cabiri were a sort of “Original old Dr. Jacob Townsends” of divinities. They were considered senior and superior to Jupiter, Neptune, Plato, and the gods of Olympus. They were Pelasgic, that is, they belonged to that unknown ancient people from whom both the Greek and the Latin nations are thought to have come. The Cabiri afterward figured as the “elder gods” of Greece, the inventors of religion, and of the human race in fact, and were kept so very dark that it is not even known, with any certainty, who they were. The ancient heathen gods, like modern thieves, very usually objected to pass by their real names. The Cabiri were particularly at home in Lemnos, and afterward in Samothrace.
Their mysteries were of a somewhat unpleasant character, as far as we know them. The candidate had to pass a long time almost starved, and without any enjoyment whatever; was then let into a dark temple, crowned with olive, tied round with a purple girdle, and frightened almost to death with horrid noises, terrible sights of some kind, great flashes of light and deep darkness between, etc., etc. There was a ceremony of absolution from past sin, and a formal beginning of a new life. It is a curious fact, that this performance seems to have been a kind of pious marine insurance company; as the initiated, it was believed, could not be drowned. Perhaps they were put in a way to obtain a drier strangulation. The reason why these ceremonies were kept so successfully secret, is plain. Each man, as he was let in, and found what nonsense it was, was sure to hold his tongue and help the next man in, as in the modern case of the celebrated “Sons of Malta.” It is to be admitted, however, to the credit of the Cabiri, that a doctrine of reformation, or of living a better practical life, seems to have been part of their religion. This is an interesting recognition, by heathen consciences, of one of the greatest moral truths which Christianity has enforced. Something of the same kind can be traced in other heathen mysteries. But these heathen attempts at virtue invariably rotted out into aggravations of vice. No religion except Christianity ever contained the principle of improvement in it. Bugaboos and hobgoblins may serve for a time to frighten the ignorant into obedience; but if they get a chance to cheat the devil, they will be sure to do it. Nothing but the great doctrine of Christian love and brotherhood, and of a kind and paternal Divine government, has ever proved to be permanently reformatory, and tending to lift the heart above the vices and passions to which poor human nature is prone.

===Chinese===

异教骗局中最显赫的，莫过于秘仪（mysteries）、神谕（oracles）、西比尔女预言家（sibyls，注：此词常被误拼为 sybils）与占卜术（augury）。每一门像样的异教宗教都少不了一套秘仪，正如每一个体面的基督教家庭都备有一本《圣经》——以及照刻薄俗话说的，柜子里藏着一具骷髅。凡是没有秘仪的宗教，便被视作寒酸的宗教——用句俗话说，一匹马拉的小破宗教。

最主要的秘仪分别是卡比里秘仪（Cabiri）、厄琉西斯秘仪（Eleusis）与伊西斯秘仪（Isis）。这些秘仪所动用的整套机关，与当代神秘社团天天玩得风生水起的把戏如出一辙：奇观排场、队列游行、怪声、灯光、暗室、骇人景象、肃穆哑剧、奇装异服、大话连篇与说教、恐吓、不知所云的胡言乱语等等。

卡比里秘仪是人类所知最古老的秘仪。这群卡比里神祇堪称众神里的“原装正牌老雅各布·汤森德博士”。人们认为他们比朱庇特、尼普顿、普路托（原文作 Plato）以及奥林匹斯诸神辈分更高、神通更大。他们属于佩拉斯吉人（Pelasgic）——即那个据信同时孕育了希腊与拉丁民族的神秘古族。卡比里诸神后来在希腊化作“长老神”，乃至宗教与人类的始祖；而且他们被遮掩得极其严密，以至于连他们究竟是谁至今都无从确考。古代异教神明就跟现代小偷一样，向来忌讳报出真名实姓。卡比里诸神最初在利姆诺斯岛（Lemnos）香火最盛，后来又迁往萨莫色雷斯岛（Samothrace）。

据我们所知，他们的秘仪颇具几分令人不适的色彩。求道者必须先经历漫长的近乎饥饿的苦修，断绝一切世俗享乐；随后被领进一座漆黑的神庙，头戴橄榄枝冠，腰系紫色腰带，在骇人听闻的怪响、某种可怖幻象、一阵阵刺目强光与伸手不见五指的深沉黑暗交替折磨下吓得魂飞魄散，等等。随后还有一套赦免过往罪孽、正式开启新生的仪式。说来奇特，整场演出似乎还兼营着某种虔诚的“海上保险公司”业务——因为人们相信，受过戒的信徒绝不会溺水身亡。兴许他们只是被安排了某种更干爽的绞死法子吧。这些仪式之所以能如此成功地保守秘密，缘由再浅显不过：每一个人走进去后发觉里面全是胡闹，出来时定会管住嘴巴，顺手拉下一个人入坑——正如现代那著名的“马耳他之子”（Sons of Malta）社团一般。不过平心而论，也该替卡比里说句公道话：劝人改过迁善、过上更端正现实生活的教义，似乎确实是他们宗教的一部分。这倒是异教良知对基督教所大力弘扬的最伟大道德真理之一的一记有趣呼应。在其他异教秘仪中也能隐约寻见类似踪迹。然而，异教徒这些向善的尝试无一例外终究腐化堕落，反倒助长了恶习。除了基督教之外，世上从未有过任何宗教自身蕴含着自我革新的原则。妖魔鬼怪兴许能暂时把无知之辈吓得服服帖帖；可一旦逮着机会骗过魔鬼，他们准会毫不犹豫地动手。唯有基督教关于博爱与手足情谊、关于仁慈如父之神圣治理的伟大教义，方才经受住了时间考验，证明能持久改造人心，将灵魂提升到超越可怜人性所易堕入的恶习与情欲之上。

===Original===

The mysteries of Eleusis were celebrated every year at Eleusis, near Athens, in honor of Ceres, and were a regular “May Anniversary,” so to speak, for the pious heathens of the period. It took just nine days to complete them; long enough for a puppy to get its eyes open. The candidates were very handsomely put through. On the first day, they got together; on the second, they took a wash in the sea; on the third, they had some ceremonies about Proserpine; on the fourth, no mortal knows what they did; on the fifth, they marched round a temple, two and two, with torches, like a Wide-Awake procession; on the sixth, seventh, and eighth, there were more processions, and the initiation proper, said to have been something like that of Freemasonry; so that we may suppose the victims rode the goat and were broiled on the gridiron. On the ninth day, the ceremony, they say, consisted in overturning two vessels of wine. I fear by this means that they all got drunk; and the more so, because the coins of Eleusis have a hog on one side, as much as to say, We make hogs of ourselves.
There was a set of mysteries at Athens, called Thesmophoria, and one at Rome, called the mysteries of the *Bona Dea*, which were celebrated by married women only. Various notions prevailed as to what they did. But can there be any reasonable doubt about it? They were, I fear, systematic conspirators’ meetings, in which the more experienced matrons instructed the junior ones how to manage their husbands. If this was not their object, then it was to maintain the influence of the heathen clergy over the heathen ladies. Women have always been the constituents of priests where false religions prevailed, as they have, for better purposes, of the ministers of the Gospel among Christians.
The mysteries of the goddess Isis, which originated in Egypt, were, in general, like those of Ceres at Eleusis. The Persian mysteries of Mithra, which were very popular during part of the latter days of the Roman empire, were of the same sort. So were those of Bacchus, Juno, Jupiter, and various other heathen gods. All of them were celebrated with great solemnity and secrecy; all included much that was terrifying; and all of their secrets have been so faithfully kept that we have only guesses and general statements about the details of the performances. Their principal object seems to have been to secure the initiated against misfortunes, and to gain prosperity in the future. Some have imagined that very wonderful and glorious truths were revealed in the midst of these heathen humbugs. But I guess that the more we find out about them, the bigger humbugs they will appear, as happened to the travelers who held a post mortem on the great heathen god in the story. This was a certain very terrible and powerful divinity among some savage tribes, of whom dreadful stories were told﻿—very authentic, of course! Some unbelieving scamps of travelers, by unlawful ways, managed to get into the innermost sacred place of the temple one night. They found the god to be done up in a very large and suspicious looking bundle. Having sacrilegiously cut the string, they unrolled one envelop of mats and cloths after another, until they had taken off more than a hundred wrappers. The god grew smaller, and smaller, and smaller; and the wonder of the travelers what he could be, larger and larger. At last, the very innermost of all the coverings fell off, and the great heathen god was revealed in all his native majesty. It was a cracked soda-water bottle! This indicates﻿—what is beyond all question the fact﻿—that the heathen mysteries had their foundation in gas. Indeed, the whole composition of these impositions was, gammon, deception, hypocrisy﻿—Humbug! Truly, the science of Humbug is entitled to some consideration, simply for its antiquity, if for nothing else.

===Chinese===

厄琉西斯秘仪每年在雅典近郊的厄琉西斯举行，旨在祭祀农神刻瑞斯（Ceres），对当时的虔诚异教徒而言，简直相当于固定的“五月周年庆典”。整套仪式足足持续九天——长到足够一只小狗崽睁开眼睛了。求道者们一路被安排得体体面面：头一天，众人聚齐；第二天，下海洗个澡；第三天，举行几场围绕普洛塞庇娜（Proserpine）的仪式；第四天，天晓得他们干了些什么；第五天，两人一排举着火把绕神庙游行，活像“觉醒者”（Wide-Awake）的火炬游行队；第六、第七与第八天，继续游行外加正式入教仪式——据说颇似如今共济会的入会仪式，由此我们不妨猜想那些受戒者也骑了山羊、上了烤架；到了第九天，据说仪式内容是打翻两坛葡萄酒。我担心他们八成借此全都喝了个酩酊大醉；更何况厄琉西斯的硬币背面还铸着一头猪，仿佛在坦白：“我们把自己喝成了猪。”

雅典另有一套秘仪叫做“立法女神节”（Thesmophoria），罗马则有一套叫做“仁慈女神节”（*Bona Dea*），均严限已婚妇女参加。世人对她们在里面究竟干些什么众说纷纭。可这难道还有什么合理的疑问吗？依我看，那多半是有组织的阴谋聚会，由经验老到的主妇传授新媳妇如何拿捏自家丈夫。退一步说，即便目的不是这个，那也是为了维系异教祭司对异教贵妇的影响力。凡在虚假宗教盛行之处，妇女历来都是祭司最忠实的基本盘——正如在基督徒中间，她们出于更高尚的目的，也是福音牧师最得力的支持者。

发源于埃及的伊西斯女神秘仪，总体上与厄琉西斯祭祀刻瑞斯的秘仪大同小异。罗马帝国晚期一度极为盛行的波斯密特拉（Mithra）秘仪亦属此类；巴克斯、朱诺、朱庇特及其他诸般异教神祇的秘仪莫不如此。所有这些秘仪皆以极度的肃穆与隐秘举行，皆包含大量骇人听闻的环节，且其秘密保守得如此严实，以至于我们对具体仪式的细节至今只有猜测与笼统说法。其主要目的似乎都是为了保佑受戒者免遭灾祸、在来日飞黄腾达。有人曾幻想这些异教欺罔之中必定隐藏着极其奇妙辉煌的真理。但我敢猜，我们对它们了解得越深，它们便越发显出是一场更大的骗局——正如那则故事里对伟大的异教神明进行“验尸”的旅人所遇见的窘境。故事说的是某个野蛮部落供奉着一位极其可怖、法力无边的大神，关于他的骇人传说数不胜数——自然个个“千真万确”！某夜，几个不信邪的混账旅人使出非法手段潜入了神庙最深处的至圣所。他们发现那尊神明被裹在一个硕大无比、颇为可疑的包裹里。这伙人亵渎神明地割断绳索，一层又一层剥开草席与布匹，直到剥掉了一百多道包裹。那尊神明越变越小、越变越小、越变越小；而旅人们心中对他究竟是何方神圣的好奇却越来越大、越来越大。终于，最里头那层包布滑落下来，伟大的异教神明以全部本来威严赫然现身——原来是一只裂了缝的苏打水瓶子！这恰好印证了一个毫无疑问的事实：异教秘仪的根基全在“吹气（gas，双关苏打水气泡与吹牛空话）”。说到底，这整套骗局的配方无非就是胡扯、欺瞒、虚伪——一句话，欺罔（Humbug）！平心而论，即便不为别的，单凭这般悠久的历史，“欺罔学”也着实值得我们正眼相看。

### XLVII / 第四十七章

===Original===

Heathen humbugs No. 2﻿—​Heathen stated services﻿—​Oracles﻿—​Sibyls﻿—​Auguries.
Something must be said about the Oracles, the Sibyls, and the Auguries; which, besides the mysteries elsewhere spoken of, were the chief assistant humbugs or side shows used for keeping up the great humbug heathen religion.
One word about the regular worship of heathenism; what maybe called their stated services. They had no weekly day of worship, indeed no week, and no preaching such as ours is; that is, no regular instruction by the ministers of religion, intended for all the people. They had singing and praying after their fashion; the singing being a sort of chant of praise to whatever idol was under treatment at the time, and the praying being in part vain repetitions of the name of their god, and for the rest a request that the god would do or give whatever was asked of him as a fair business transaction, in return for the agreeable smell of the fine beef they had just roasted under his nose, or for whatever else they had given him; as, a sum of money, a pair of pantaloons (or whatever they wore instead,) a handsome golden cup. This made the temple a regular shop, where the priests traded off promised benefits for real beef; coining blessings into cash on the nail; a very thorough humbug. Such public religious ceremonies as the heathen had were mostly annual, sometimes monthly. There were also daily ones, which were, however, the daily business of the priests, and none of the business of the laymen. To return to the subject.
All the heathen oracles, old and new (for abundance of them are still agoing,) sibyls, auguries and all, show how universally and naturally, and humbly and helplessly too, poor human nature longs to see into the future, and longs for help and guidance from some power, higher than itself.
Thus considered, these shallow humbugs teach a useful lesson, for they constitute a strong proof of man’s inborn natural recognition of some God, of some obligation to a higher power, of some disembodied existence; and so they show a natural human want of exactly what the Christian revelation supplies, and constitute a powerful evidence for Christianity.

===Chinese===

异教骗局第 2 篇——异教常规礼拜——神谕——西比尔女预言家——占卜术。

关于神谕、西比尔与占卜术，实在不能不多说几句；除了前文提过的秘仪之外，这三样便是用来维系异教宗教这座大骗局的主要辅助骗术或“杂耍副业”。

先略提一句异教的常规崇拜——也就是所谓的固定礼拜。他们没有每周一次的礼拜日（事实上根本没有“星期”概念），也没有像我们这样的讲道——即宗教圣职人员面向全体民众所作的定期教诲。他们倒也有自己的吟唱与祈祷：吟唱无非是对当时正在侍奉的某尊偶像献上的赞美调子；祈祷则一半是徒劳地反复念叨神明的尊号，另一半则是像谈一笔公平买卖般恳求神明兑现所求——作为刚刚在神像鼻子底下烤出的香喷喷牛肉、或者献上的其他供品（诸如一笔钱、一条裤子或当时穿的类似衣物、一只精致金杯）的回报。如此一来，神庙便成了一家正经铺子，祭司们拿空头福分换实打实的牛肉，当场把祝福变现成现钞；真是彻头彻尾的欺罔（humbug）。异教徒的公共宗教庆典多半一年一度，有时一月一度。虽也有每日仪轨，那却只是祭司们的日常差事，与平信徒毫不相干。言归正传。

古往今来的一切异教神谕（眼下仍有大把正在营业）、西比尔女预言家、占卜术等等，无不显明可怜的人性是多么普遍、自然、卑微而又无助地渴望窥见未来，渴望得到某种高于自身的力量之扶助与指引。

从这个角度来看，这些浅薄的骗局倒也教给了我们有益的一课：它们有力地证明了人类生来便自然承认某种神明的存在、对更高主宰负有某种义务、以及存在某种超越肉身的灵性生命；由此恰恰显明了人类天性所渴求的正是基督教启示所赐予的一切，反倒成了基督教的强有力佐证。

===Original===

All the heathen religions, I believe, had oracles of some kind. But the Greek and Latin ones tell the whole story. Of these there were over a hundred; more than twenty of Apollo, who was the god of soothsaying, divination, prophecy, and of the supernatural side of heathen humbug generally; thirty or forty collectively of Jupiter, Ceres, Mercury, Pluto, Juno, Ino (a very good name for a goddess that gave oracles, though she didn’t know!), Faunus, Fortune, Mars, etc., and nearly as many of demigods, heroes, giants, etc., such as Amphiaraus, Amphilochus, Trophonius, Geryon, Ulysses, Calchas, Aesculapius, Hercules, Pasiphae, Phryxus, etc. The most celebrated and most patronized of them all was the great oracle of Apollo, at Delphi. The “little fee” appears to have been the only universal characteristic of the proceedings for obtaining an answer from the god. Whether you got your reply in words spoken by the rattling of an old pot, by observing an ox’s appetite, throwing dice, or sleeping for a dream, your own proceedings were essentially the same. “Terms invariably net cash in advance or its equivalent.” A fine ox or sheep sacrificed was cash; for after the god had had his smell (those ladies and gentlemen appear to have eaten as they say the Yankees talk﻿—through their noses,) all the rest was put carefully away by the reverend clergy for dinner, and saved so much on the butcher’s bill. If your credit was good, you might receive your oracle and afterward send in any little acknowledgment in the form of a golden goblet, or statue, or vase, or even of a remittance in specie. Such gifts accumulated in the oracle at Delphi and to an immense amount, and to the great emolument of Brennus, a matter of fact Gaulish commander, who, at his invasion of Greece, coolly carried off all the bullion, without any regard to the screeches of the Pythoness, and with no more scruples than any burglar.
The Delphian oracle worked through a woman, who, on certain days, went and sat on a three-legged stool over a hole in the ground in Apollo’s temple. This hole sent out gas; which, instead of being used like that afforded by holes in the ground at Fredonia, N.Y., to illuminate the village, was much more shrewdly employed by the clerical gentlemen to shine up the knowledge-boxes of their customers, and introduce the glitter of gold into their own pockets. I merely throw out the hint to any speculating Fredonian who owns a hole in the ground. Well, the Pythia, as this female was termed, warmed up her understanding over this hole, as you have seen ladies do over the register of a hot-air furnace, and becoming excited, she presently began to be drunk or crazy, and in her fit she gabbled forth some words or noises. These the priests took down, and then told the customer that the noises meant so-and-so! When business was brisk they worked two Pythias, turn and turn about (or, as they say at sea, watch and watch), and kept a third all cocked and primed in case of accident, besides; for this gas sometimes gave the priestess (literally) fits, which killed her in a few days.
Other oracles gave answers in many various ways. The priest quietly wrote down whatever answer he chose; or inspected the insides of a slaughtered beast, and said that the bowels meant this and that. At Telmessus the inquirer peeped into a well, where he must see a picture in the water which was his answer; at any rate, if this wouldn’t do he got none. This plan was evidently based on the idea that “truth is at the bottom of a well.” At Dodona, they hung brass pots on the trees and translated the banging these made when the wind blew them together. At Pherae, you whispered your question in the ear of the image of Mercury, and then shutting your ears until you got out of the marketplace, the first remark you heard from anybody was the answer, and you might make the best of it. At Pluto’s oracle at Charae, the priest took a dream, and in the morning told you what he chose. In the cave of Trophonius, after various terrifying performances, they pulled you through a hole the wrong way of the feathers, and then back again, and then stuck you upon a seat, and made you write down your own oracle, being what you had seen, which would, I imagine, usually be “the elephant.”
And so-forth, and so on. Humbug ad libitum!

===Chinese===

依我所知，一切异教宗教无不设有某种神谕。单看希腊与罗马的神谕便足以窥见全貌。其中仅希腊罗马一地便有一百多处神谕所：二十多处归阿波罗名下——他是预言、占卜、先知以及异教骗局中一切超自然门道的总管；朱庇特、刻瑞斯、墨丘利、普路托、朱诺、伊诺（Ino——对于一位啥也不知道却要降神谕的女神来说，这名字起得真妙，谐音“I know”我知道！）、法乌努斯、福尔图娜、玛尔斯等人名下合计三四十处；此外还有几乎同等数量的半神、英雄、巨人名下的神谕所，譬如安菲阿拉俄斯、安菲洛科斯、特洛福尼乌斯、革律翁、尤利西斯、卡尔卡斯、阿斯克勒庇俄斯、赫拉克勒斯、帕西法厄、弗里克索斯等等。其中最负盛名、香火最旺的，当属德尔斐（Delphi）的阿波罗大神谕所。向神明求签问卜的整套程序里，唯一放之四海而皆准的特征似乎便是“那点儿小费”。无论你求得的答复是靠老陶罐哐当乱响、观察公牛胃口、掷骰子还是睡一觉做梦，你自己该办的手续本质上都一模一样：“交易条件一律为现钞预付或等价实物”。献祭一头肥牛或肥羊便是现钞；因为等神明闻够了烤肉香味（那些男神女神似乎都像世人嘲笑扬基人说话那样——全靠鼻子“吃”东西），余下的整头牛羊便被可敬的祭司们小心翼翼收进厨房当晚饭，省下了好大一笔肉铺账单。倘若你信用够好，也可先领回神谕，事后再奉上黄金酒杯、雕像、花瓶甚至直接汇来一笔真金白银作为薄礼。此类贡品在德尔斐神谕所越积越多、堆积如山——直到务实的高卢统帅布伦努斯（Brennus）入侵希腊时，丝毫不理会女祭司的尖声诅咒，面不改色地把所有金银财宝扫荡一空，良心上丝毫不比寻常窃贼多半分愧疚。

德尔斐神谕是通过一名女子运作的：每逢特定日子，她便走进阿波罗神庙，坐在一张架于地洞之上的三脚凳上。地洞里冒出气体；祭司老爷们不像纽约州弗里多尼亚（Fredonia, N.Y.）人那样拿地洞里冒出的天然气来点亮村庄，而是更加精明地拿它来“点亮”主顾的脑瓜壳，把金光闪闪的金币引流进自己的腰包。我不过随口给弗里多尼亚拥有地洞的投机客们指条明路罢了。话说那位被称为“皮媞亚”（Pythia）的女子跨坐在洞口上方，像各位见过女士们蹲在暖气风口上烤身子那样烘热神志，渐入亢奋，很快便醉醺醺或疯癫起来，在抽搐发作中含糊吐出一串词句或怪声。祭司们随即记录下来，再告诉主顾那些怪声意味着如此这般！生意兴隆时，他们甚至轮班启用两名皮媞亚（按水手的话说，叫“轮值换班”），此外还备好第三名随时待命以防万一——因为那种毒气有时真的会让女祭司抽风发作，不出几天便一命呜呼。

其他神谕所则用五花八门的方式作答。有的祭司干脆不动声色地写下自己想好的任意答案；有的剖开献祭牲畜的肚腹，宣称内脏纹路预示着这般那般。在特尔梅索斯（Telmessus），求问者须探头往一口井里张望，必须从水面上瞧出自己的答案画面；总之，要是看不出名堂，那就白跑一趟。这套法子显然植根于“真理在井底”的古训。在多多纳（Dodona），他们把铜锅挂在树上，把风吹锅撞发出的叮当声翻译成神谕。在费拉埃（Pherae），你对着墨丘利神像的耳朵低声提问，然后捂紧耳朵走出集市广场，走到外头听见路人说的第一句话便是答案，随你自行领会。在哈拉埃（Charae）的普路托神谕所，由祭司代你睡一觉做梦，次日清晨爱怎么编就怎么编给你听。而在特洛福尼乌斯（Trophonius）洞穴里，经历一番惊悚恐怖的折腾后，他们倒拖着你钻过一个狭洞再拖回来，把你摁在椅子上，叫你自己写下刚才看见的神谕——我猜多半也就看见了个“大象”（上当了）吧。

如此这般，不一而足。真是随心所欲的欺罔！

===Original===

Like some of the more celebrated modern fortune-tellers, the managers of the oracles were frequently shrewd fellows, and could often pick up the materials of a very smart and judicious answer from the appearance of the customer and his question. Very often the answer was sheer nonsense. It was, in fact, believed by many that as a rule you couldn’t tell what the response meant until after it was fulfilled, when you were expected to see it. In many cases the answers were ingeniously arranged, so as to mean either a good or evil result, one of which was pretty likely.
Thus, one of the oracles answered a general who asked after the fate of his campaign as follows: (the ancients, remember, using no punctuation marks) “Thou shalt go thou shalt return never in war shalt thou perish.” The point becomes visible when you first make a pause before “never,” and then after it.
On a similar occasion, the Delphic oracle told Croesus that if he crossed the River Halys he would overthrow a great empire. This empire he chose to understand as that of Cyrus, whom he was going to fight. It came out the other way, and it was his own empire that was overthrown. The immense wisdom of the oracle, however, was tremendously respected in consequence!
Pyrrhus, of Epirus, on setting off against the Romans, received equal satisfaction, the Pythia telling him (in Latin) what amounted to this:
“I say that you Pyrrhus the Romans are able to conquer!”
Pyrrhus took it as he wished it, but found himself sadly thimble-rigged, the little joker being under the wrong cup. The Romans beat him, and most woefully too.
Trajan was advised to consult the oracle at Heliopolis, about his intended expedition against the Parthians. The custom was to send your query in a letter; so Trajan sent a blank note in an envelope. The god (very naturally) sent back a blank note in reply, which was thought wonderfully smart; and so the imperial dupe sent again, a square question:
“Shall I finish this war and get safe back to Rome?”
The Heliopolitan humbug replied by sending a piece of an old grapevine cut into pieces, which meant either: “You will cut them up,” or “They will cut you up;” and Trajan, like the little boy at the peepshow who asked: “which is Lord Wellington and which is the Emperor Napoleon?” had paid his penny and might take his choice.
Sometimes the oracles were quite jocular. A man asked one of them how to get rich? The oracle said: “Own all there is between Sicyon and Corinth.” Which places are some fifteen miles apart.
Another fellow asked how he should cure his gout? The oracle coolly said: “Drink nothing but cold water!”

===Chinese===

一如现代某些更负盛名的算命先生，神谕所的主持人往往都是精明透顶的家伙，常常能从主顾的衣着神态与问题本身里捕捉线索，拼凑出一番极其机智妥帖的答复。更多时候，答案纯属一派胡言。事实上，许多人坚信，照规矩你根本不可能在神谕应验之前看懂它的意思——唯有事后才算恍然大悟。在许多场合，神谕词句更是经过巧妙编排，正读反读既可解作大吉也可解作大凶，横竖总能蒙中一头。

譬如有一次，一位将军问及此番出征吉凶，神谕答复如下（记住，古人写字是不加标点符号的）：“你将前往你将归来绝不阵亡沙场。”只要你在“绝不”前面停顿，或者在“绝不”后面停顿，玄机便一目了然（译注：即“你将前往，你将归来，绝不阵亡沙场”或“你将前往，你将归来绝不，阵亡沙场”）。

在另一回相似的场合，德尔斐神谕告诉克罗伊斯（Croesus）：他若渡过哈里斯河（River Halys），必将倾覆一个大帝国。他一厢情愿地把这个帝国理解成他正准备攻打的居鲁士（Cyrus）帝国。结果却恰恰相反，倾覆的竟是他自己的帝国。然而，神谕的无上智慧反倒因此赢得万民敬仰！

伊庇鲁斯的皮洛士（Pyrrhus）起兵攻打罗马人之际，也讨得了同样令人满意的神谕，皮媞亚（用拉丁语）告诉他大意如下：

“我说你皮洛士罗马人能够征服！”

皮洛士按自己巴望的意思去领会，结果发觉自己被狠狠耍了一把——那颗小豆子压根儿藏在另一只碗底下。罗马人把他揍得落花流水、惨不忍睹。

图拉真（Trajan）曾听人劝告，就他计划远征帕提亚人一事向赫利奥波利斯（Heliopolis）神谕所求卜。当地规矩是把问题写在信里寄去；于是图拉真在信封里塞了一张白纸。神明（再自然不过地）也回了一张白纸，众人却赞叹聪明绝顶；于是这位皇帝冤大头又寄去一道实打实的问题：

“我能否打完这场仗并平安返回罗马？”

赫利奥波利斯的骗子回赠了一根切碎的老葡萄藤，意思既可以是“你将把他们砍得七零八落”，也可以是“他们将把你砍得七零八落”；图拉真就像看西洋镜时发问的小男孩——“哪一个是惠灵顿勋爵，哪一个是拿破仑皇帝？”——既然付了钱，随你自己挑吧。

有时神谕还颇为幽默。有个人问神谕该如何发财？神谕答道：“买下西锡安（Sicyon）与科林斯（Corinth）之间的一切土地。”这两座城市相距约莫十五英里。

另一个家伙问他该如何治好痛风？神谕冷冷地答道：“除了冷水，什么也别喝！”

===Original===

The Delphic oracle, and some of the others, used for a long time to give their answers in verses. At last, however, irreverent critics of the period made so much fun of the peculiarly miserable style of this poetry, that the poor oracle gave it up and came down to plain prose. Every once in a while some energetic and cunning man, of skeptical character, insisted on having just such an answer as he wanted. It was well known that Philip of Macedon bought what responses he wished at Delphi. Anybody with plenty of money, who would quietly “see” the priests, could have such a response as he chose. Or, if he was a bullheaded, hard-fisted, fighting-man, of irreligious but energetic mind, the priests gave him what he wished, out of fear. When Themistocles wanted to encourage the Greeks against the Persians, he “fixed” Delphi by bribes. When Alexander the Great came to consult the same oracle, the Pythia was disinclined to perform. But Alexander rather roughly gave her to understand that she must, and she did. The Greek and Roman oracles finally all gave out not far from the time of Christ’s coming, having gradually become more or less disreputable for many years.
All the heathen nations, as I have said, had their oracles too. The heathen Scandinavians had a famous one at Upsal. The Getae, in Scythia, had one. The Druids had them; so did the Mexican priests. The Egyptian and Syrian divinities had them; in short, oracles were quite as necessary as mysteries, and continue so in heathen religions. The only exception, I believe, is in Mohammedanism, whose votaries save themselves any trouble about the future by their thorough fatalism. They believe so fully and vividly that everything is immovably predestinated, being at the same time perfectly sure of heaven at last, that they quietly receive everything as it comes, and don’t take the least trouble to find out how it is coming.
The Sibyls were women, supposed to be inspired by some divinity, who prophesied of the future. Some say there was but one; some two, three, four, or ten. All sorts of obscure stories are told about the time and place of their activity. There was the Persian or Chaldean, who is said to have foretold with many details the coming and career of Christ; the Lybian, the Delphic, the Cumaean, much honored by the Romans, and half a dozen more. Then there was Mantho, the daughter of Tiresias, who was sent from Thebes to Delphi in a bag, seven hundred and twenty years before the destruction of Troy. These ladies lived in caves, and among them are said to have composed the Sibylline books, which contained the mysteries of religion, were carefully kept out of sight at Rome, and finally came into the hands of the Emperor Constantine. They were burned, one story has it, about fifty years after his death. But there are some Sibylline books extant, which, however, are among the most transparent of humbugs, for they are full of all sorts of extracts and statements from the Old and New Testaments. I do not believe there ever were any Sibyls. If there were any, they were probably ill-natured and desperate oldmaids, who turned so sour-tempered that their friends had to drive them off to live by themselves, and who, under these circumstances, went to work and wrote books.
I must crowd in here a word or two about the Auguries and the Augurs. These gentlemen were a sort of Roman priests, who were accustomed to foretell future events, decide on coming good or bad fortune, whether it would do to go on with the elections, to begin any enterprise or not, etc., by means of various signs. These were thunder; the way any birds happened to fly; the way that the sacred chickens ate; the appearance of the entrails of beasts sacrificed, etc., etc. These augurs were, for a long time, much respected in Rome, but, at last, the more thoughtful people lost their belief in them, and they became so ridiculous that Cicero, who was himself one of them, said he could not see how one augur could look another in the face without laughing.
It is humiliating to reflect how long and how extensively such barefaced and monstrous humbugs as these have maintained unquestioned authority over almost the whole race of man. Nor has humanity, by any means, escaped from such debasing slavery now; for millions and millions of men still believe and practice forms and ceremonies even more absurd, if possible, than the Mysteries, Oracles, and Auguries.

===Chinese===

德尔斐神谕及其他几处神谕所，在很长一段时期里向来以诗体作答。然而到头来，那个时代大不敬的批评家们把这种诗体写得极其拙劣寒酸的文风嘲笑得体无完肤，可怜的神谕只得作罢，改用平铺直叙的散文。隔三岔五，也会冒出某个精力充沛、狡黠多疑的硬汉，偏要讨出自己心仪的答案不可。世人皆知马其顿的腓力（Philip of Macedon）曾在德尔斐花钱买通自己想要的神谕。任何人只要手头阔绰、肯私下“打点”祭司，便能如愿讨得想要的签词；再不然，要是个牛脾气、拳头硬、敬神不笃却干劲十足的武夫，祭司们也会出于畏惧乖乖顺着他的意思作答。当年地米斯托克利（Themistocles）想鼓舞希腊人抗击波斯人时，便是靠贿赂“摆平”了德尔斐；而当亚历山大大帝前来向同一座神谕所问卜时，皮媞亚起初不肯开坛，亚历山大却颇为粗暴地让她明白她非说不可，于是她便乖乖说了。希腊与罗马的神谕所此前早已声名狼藉了好些年，最终大约在基督降世前后悉数歇业。

正如我所说，世上所有异教民族也都有各自的神谕。信奉异教的斯堪的纳维亚人在乌普萨拉（Upsal）有一处著名神谕；斯基泰的盖塔人（Getae）也有一处；德鲁伊教士有，墨西哥祭司也有；埃及与叙利亚诸神亦有之。简言之，神谕与秘仪一样不可或缺，且至今仍在异教宗教中延续。据我所知，唯一的例外是伊斯兰教——它的信徒凭藉彻底的宿命论省却了一切打探未来的麻烦。他们如此彻底而鲜明地笃信万事皆早已铁板钉钉地前定，同时又百分之百确信自己终将步入天堂，因此对一切遭遇皆泰然受之，压根儿懒得费神去打听未来会如何降临。

所谓西比尔（Sibyls），是指相传受神灵附体、预言未来的女性。有人说只有一位，有人说两位、三位、四位或十位。关于她们活跃的时代与地点，流传着各式各样晦涩的传说：有波斯（或迦勒底）西比尔，据说曾详尽预言了基督的降生与生平；有利比亚西比尔、德尔斐西比尔、备受罗马人尊崇的库迈西比尔，以及另外半打之多。还有提瑞西阿斯之女曼托（Mantho），据说在特洛伊毁灭前七百二十年被人装在袋子里从底比斯送往德尔斐。这些女士住在山洞里，相传她们合著了《西比尔神谕书》——书中载有宗教奥秘，在罗马被严密收藏秘不示人，最终落入君士坦丁大帝手中；另一种说法称该书在他死后约五十年被焚毁。不过眼下确有几部《西比尔神谕书》传世，然而它们却是世上最拙劣透明的骗局之一，因为书里塞满了各式各样抄自《旧约》与《新约》的段落与陈述。我压根儿不信世上曾有过什么西比尔。退一步说就算真有，多半也是些脾气乖戾、绝望透顶的老处女，性情变得如此酸腐刻薄，逼得亲友只得把她们撵出去独自过活，而在那种孤寂境遇下，她们便动手写起书来。

在此我必须再挤出几句说说占卜术（Auguries）与占卜官（Augurs）。这班绅士是罗马的一种祭司，专靠观测各种征兆来预言未来吉凶、判定眼下是否宜于继续举行选举、宜于发起某项事业等等。这些征兆包括：雷鸣、飞鸟恰好朝哪个方向飞、圣鸡啄食的吃相、献祭牲畜内脏的模样，如此等等。这些占卜官在罗马曾长期备受尊崇；但到头来，头脑更清醒的人不再相信他们，他们变得如此荒诞可笑，以至于身为占卜官之一的西塞罗（Cicero）都坦言，他真不明白两个占卜官迎面相遇时怎么能忍住不对着彼此放声大笑。

细想之下实在令人汗颜：如此明目张胆、荒谬绝伦的欺罔，竟然在漫长岁月里、在如此广袤的地域上，对几乎整个人类种族维持了不容置疑的权威。而且时至今日，人类也远未摆脱这般卑屈的奴役——毕竟仍有千千万万的人笃信并奉行着甚至比秘仪、神谕与占卜还要荒诞的仪轨与礼数。

### XLVIII / 第四十八章

===Original===

Modern heathen humbugs﻿—​Fetishism﻿—​Obi﻿—​Vaudoux﻿—​Indian powwows﻿—​Lamaism﻿—​Revolving prayers﻿—​Praying to death.
A scale of superstition and religious beliefs of today, arranged from the lowest to the highest, would show many curious coincidences with another scale, which should trace the history of superstitions and religious beliefs backward in time toward the origin of man.
