Thus, for instance, the heathen humbugs, whether revolting or ridiculous, which I am to speak of in this chapter, are in full blast today; and they furnish perfect specimens of the beliefs which prevailed among the heathen of four thousand and of eighteen hundred years ago; of the Chaldee and Canaanite superstitions, and equally of those of the Romans under Augustus Caesar.
The most dirty, vulgar, low, silly and absurd of all the superstitions in the world are, as is natural, those of the darkest minded of all the heathen, who have any superstition at all. For, as if for the humiliation of our proud human nature, there are really some human beings who seem to have too little intellect even to rise to the height of a superstition. Such are the Andaman Islanders, who crawl on all fours, wear nothing but a plaster of mud to keep the mosquitoes off, eat bugs, and grubs, and ants, and turn their children out to shift for themselves as soon as the little wretches can learn to crawl and eat bugs.
These lowest of superstitions are Fetishism and Obi, believed and practiced by negro tribes, and, remember this, even by their ignorant white mistresses in the West Indies and in the United States, today. Yes, I know where Southern refugee secessionist women are living in and about New York city at this moment, who really believe in the negro witchcraft called Obi, practiced by the slaves.
A Fetish is anything not a living being, worshiped because supposed to be inhabited by some god. In some parts of Africa the Fetishes are a sort of guardian divinity, and there is one for each district like a town constable; and sometimes one for each family. The Fetish is any stone picked up in the street﻿—a tree, a chip, a rag. It may be some stone or wooden image﻿—an old pot, a knife, a feather. Before this precious divinity the poor darkeys bow down and worship, and sometimes, sacrifice a sheep or a rooster. Each more important Fetish has a priest, and here is where the humbug comes in. This gentleman lives on the offerings made to the Fetish, and he “exploits” his god, as a Frenchman would say, with great profit.

===Chinese===

现代异教骗局——拜物教——奥比巫术——伏都教——印第安巫医法事——喇嘛教——转经祈祷——咒死人的祈祷。

倘若把当今世上的迷信与宗教信仰从最低等到最高等排成一张标尺，我们会惊奇地发现它与另一张沿时间倒溯至人类起源的迷信与宗教信仰史标尺惊人吻合。譬如，我将在本章谈到的那些或令人作呕、或荒诞可笑的异教欺罔（humbugs），眼下仍在世界各地热火朝天地盛行着；它们恰好为我们提供了四千年前与一千八百年前异教信仰的完美活标本——既有迦勒底人与迦南人的迷信，也无异于奥古斯都·恺撒治下罗马人的迷信。

世上最肮脏、粗鄙、下流、愚蠢且荒谬的迷信，自然属于那些但凡尚有一丝迷信观念的异教徒中头脑最蒙昧的一群。仿佛是为了羞辱我们骄傲的人性，世上竟真有一些人类生灵似乎智力微薄到连攀上“迷信”的高度都力有不逮。譬如安达曼群岛居民，四肢着地爬行，全身上下除了涂一层泥巴防蚊之外一丝不挂，以虫子、蛴螬与蚂蚁为食，小家伙刚学会爬行抓虫吃便被赶出门去自生自灭。

这些最低劣的迷信便是拜物教（Fetishism）与奥比巫术（Obi），至今仍为黑人部落——而且记住，甚至在今日的西印度群岛与美国，连他们那些无知的白人女主人也同样信奉并操持着。没错，我就知道眼下纽约市及周边住着一些南方逃亡分裂派妇女，她们真的笃信黑奴施展的那套名为“奥比”的黑人巫术。

所谓“拜物”（Fetish），是指任何非生物体，只因被假定有某位神灵栖居其中便受人膜拜。在非洲部分地区，拜物神算是一种守护神，每个区划都有一尊，活像镇上的治安官；有时每户人家也有一尊。这拜物神可以是街上随便捡的一块石头——一棵树、一片木屑、一块破布；也可以是某种石像或木雕——一只破罐子、一把刀、一根羽毛。在这些宝贝神明面前，可怜的黑人俯首膜拜，有时还献祭一只羊或一只公鸡。每一尊略微重要的拜物神都配有一名祭司，而欺罔的门道便在此处。这位祭司老爷专靠吃供奉给拜物神的祭品度日，照法国人的话说，他把自家神明“开发利用”得油水丰厚。

===Original===

Obi or Obeah, is the name of the witchcraft of the negro tribes; and the practitioner is termed an Obi-man or Obi-woman. They practice it at home in Africa, and carry it with them to continue it when they are made slaves in other lands. Obi is now practiced, as I have already hinted, in Cuba and in the Southern States, and is believed in by the more ignorant and foolish white people, as much as by their barbarous slaves. Obi is used only to injure, and the way to perform it upon your enemy is, to hire the Obi man or woman to concoct a charm, and then to hide this, or cause it to be hidden, in some place about the person or abode of the victim where he will find it. He is expected thereupon to fall ill, to wither and waste away, and so to die.
Absurd as it may seem, this cursing business operates with a good deal of certainty on the poor negroes, who fall sick instantly on finding the ball of Obi, two or three inches in diameter, hidden in their bed, or in the roof, or under the threshold, or in the earthen floor of their huts. The poor wretches become dejected, lose appetite, strength, and spirits, grow thin and ill, and really wither away and die. It is a curious fact, however, that if under these circumstances you can cause one of them to become converted to Christianity, or to become a Christian by profession, he becomes at once free from the witches’ dominion and quickly recovers.
The ball of Obi﻿—or, as it is called among the Brazilian negroes, Mandinga﻿—may be made of various materials, always, I believe, including some which are disgusting or horrible. Leaves of trees and scraps of rag may be used; ashes, usually from bones or flesh of some kind; pieces of cats’ bones and skulls, feathers, hair, earth, or clay, which ought to be from a grave; teeth of men and of snakes, alligators or other beasts; vegetable gum, or other sticky stuff; human blood, pieces of eggshell, etc., etc. This mixture is curiously like that in the witches’ cauldron in Macbeth, which, among other equally toothsome matters, contained frogs’ toes, bats’ wool, lizards’ legs, owlets’ wings, wolfs’ teeth, witches’ mummy, Jew’s liver, tigers’ bowels, and lastly, as a sort of thickening to the gravy, baboon’s blood.
A creole lady, now at the North, recently told a friend of mine that “the negroes can put some pieces of paper, or powder, or something or other in your shoes, that will make you sick, or make you do anything they want!” The poor foolish woman told this with a face full of awe and eyes wide open. Another lady known to me, long resident at the South, tells me that the belief in this sort of devilism is often found among the white people.

===Chinese===

奥比（Obi 或 Obeah）是黑人部落巫术的名称，施术者称为奥比男巫或奥比女巫。他们在非洲老家操持这套把戏，被贩卖到异乡沦为奴隶后也随身带着继续施展。正如我刚才暗示的，如今在古巴与美国南方各州，奥比巫术依然大行其道，而那些更无知愚蠢的白人对它的迷信程度，丝毫不亚于他们野蛮的奴隶。奥比巫术专用来害人；对仇家施法的步骤是：雇请奥比男巫或女巫配制一份符咒，然后暗中藏匿——或派人藏匿——在受害者贴身衣物或住处的某个必能被他翻出来的角落。按理说，他一发现这东西便会病倒、日渐枯槁消瘦，最终一命呜呼。

尽管听起来荒唐，这门诅咒行当在可怜的黑人身上却相当灵验：一旦在自家床上、屋顶、门槛底下或窝棚泥地里翻出那枚直径两三英寸的奥比咒球，他们立时便会病倒。这群可怜虫从此萎靡不振、食欲全无、体力与精气神尽失、骨瘦如柴，果真日渐枯萎而死。然而说来也怪，在这种节骨眼上，你若能劝得其中一人皈依基督教——哪怕只是名义上自认是基督徒——他便立刻挣脱女巫的魔掌，迅速康复。

这枚奥比咒球——巴西黑人称之为曼丁哥（Mandinga）——可用各种材料捏成，据我所知其中总少不了些令人作呕或骇人听闻的玩意儿：树叶与碎布头、骨灰或肉灰、猫骨头与猫头骨碎块、羽毛、毛发、最好取自坟头的泥土或黏土、人牙与蛇牙、鳄鱼或其他野兽的牙齿、植物胶或其他黏稠物、人血、蛋壳碎片等等。这套配方与《麦克白》里女巫大锅熬的汤药奇异地相似——那锅同样“美味”的汤里熬着青蛙脚趾、蝙蝠绒毛、蜥蜴腿、幼鸮翅膀、狼牙、女巫木乃伊、犹太人肝脏、老虎内脏，末了再添一勺狒狒血给浓汤勾芡。

一位如今身在北方的克里奥尔女士近来对我的一位朋友说：“黑人只要往你鞋子里塞几片纸、一点药粉或别的什么鬼东西，就能让你生病，或者乖乖听他们摆布！”这可怜的蠢女人说这话时满脸敬畏、瞪大双眼。我认识的另一位久居南方的女士也告诉我，白人当中信奉这种妖魔鬼道的人屡见不鲜。

===Original===

The practices called Vaudoux or Voudoux, are a sort of Obi; being, like that, an invoking of the aid of some god to do what the worshipers wish. The Vaudoux humbug is quite prevalent in Cuba, Haiti, and other West India islands, where there are wild negroes, or where they are still imported from Africa. There is also a good deal of this sort of humbug among the slaves in New Orleans, and cases arising from it have recently quite often appeared in the police reports in the newspapers of that city.
The Vaudoux worshipers assemble secretly, with a kind of chief witch or mistress of ceremonies; there is a boiling cauldron of hell-broth, à la Macbeth; the votaries dance naked around their soup; amulets and charms are made and distributed. During a quarter of a century last past, some hundreds of these orgies have been broken up by the New Orleans police, and probably as many more have come off as per program. The Vaudoux processes are most frequently appealed to for the purposes of some unsuccessful or jealous lover; and the Creole ladies believe in Vaudouxism as much as in Obi.
In the West Indies, the Vaudoux orgies are more savage than in this country. It is but a little while since in Haiti, under the energetic and sensible administration of President Geffrard, eight Vaudoux worshipers were regularly tried and executed for having murdered a young girl, the niece of two of them, by way of human sacrifice to the god. They tied the poor child tight, put her in a box called a humfort, fed her with some kind of stuff for four days, and then deliberately strangled her, beheaded her, flayed her, cooked the head with yams, ate of the soup, and then performed a solemn dance and chant around an altar with the skull on it.
The Caffres in Southern Africa have a kind of humbug somewhat like the Obi-men, who are known as rainmakers. These gentlemen furnish what blessing and cursing may be required for other purposes; but as that country is liable to tremendous droughts, their best business is to make rain. This they do by various prayers and ceremonies, of which the most important part is, receiving a large fee in advance from the customer. The rainmaking business, though very lucrative, is not without its disadvantages; for whenever Moselekatse, or Dingaan, or any other chief sets his rainmaker at work, and the rain was not forthcoming as per application, the indignant ruler caused an assegai or two to be stuck through the wizard, for the encouragement of the other wizards. This was not so unreasonable as it may seem; for if the man could not make rain when it was wanted, what was he good for?

===Chinese===

名为“伏都教”（Vaudoux 或 Voudoux）的仪式也是奥比巫术的一种变体；与后者一样，旨在祈请某位神灵相助，替信徒达成心愿。伏都教骗局在古巴、海地及其他西印度群岛相当盛行——凡是有野蛮黑人或仍在从非洲输入黑奴的地方莫不如此。新奥尔良的黑奴中间也颇流行这一套把戏，近来该市报纸的警务报道里频频出现由此引发的案件。

伏都教徒秘密聚会，由一位女巫头目兼司仪主持；现场架着一口《麦克白》式的滚沸地狱魔汤大锅；信徒们赤身裸体绕着大锅狂舞；现场制作并分发护身符与咒物。在过去四分之一个世纪里，新奥尔良警方捣毁了数百场此类狂欢邪会，而按计划顺利办完的恐怕同样多得数不清。人们求助于伏都教法术，最常是为了替失恋或妒火中烧的情人出气；克里奥尔贵妇们对伏都教的笃信程度，丝毫不亚于对奥比巫术。

在西印度群岛，伏都教的血腥狂欢较之本国更加野蛮残忍。就在前不久，在海地总统杰弗拉德（Geffrard）精力充沛而又明智的治理下，八名伏都教徒依法受审并被处决——只因他们为向神明献活人祭，竟残忍谋害了一名年幼少女（其中两人的亲侄女）。他们将可怜的孩子捆得死紧，关进一只名为“humfort”的箱子里，用某种食物喂养了四天，随后蓄意将她勒死、斩首、剥皮，把头颅与山药一道煮熟喝下汤汁，接着围着摆有头骨的祭坛跳起肃穆舞蹈并高声吟唱。

南部非洲的卡菲尔人（Caffres）也有一类酷似奥比巫师的骗子，人称“求雨师”（rainmakers）。这些先生也承接其他祈福与诅咒业务；不过鉴于当地动辄遭遇恐怖大旱，他们最红火的买卖还是求雨。求雨靠各种祈祷与仪式，其中最紧要的环节便是事先向主顾收取一笔丰厚酬金。求雨这门行当虽财源滚滚，却也并非毫无风险；因为每当莫塞莱卡策、丁甘或其他哪位酋长派自家求雨师开坛作法，而雨水却未按约定降临时，盛怒的统治者便会下令拿一两根短矛（assegai）捅穿那巫师的胸膛，以儆效尤、勉励其余巫师。这倒也不像表面听起来那么不讲理：要是该下雨的时候连雨都求不下来，留这人还有什么用呢？

===Original===

The ceremonies of the powwows or medicine-men of the North American Indians, are less brutal than the African ones. These soothsayers, like the Obi-men, prepared charms for their customers, usually, however, not so much to destroy others as to protect the wearer. These charms consist of some trifling matters tied up in a small bag, the “medicine-bag,” which is to be worn round the neck, and will, it is supposed, insure the wearer the special help and protection of the Great Spirit. The powwows sometimes do a little in the cursing line.
There is a funny story of a Puritan minister in the early times of New England, who coolly defied one of the most famous Indian magicians to play off his infernal artillery. A formal meeting was had, and the powwow rattled his traps, howled, danced, blew feathers, and vociferated jargon until he was perfectly exhausted, the old minister quietly looking at him all the time. The savage humbug was dumbfounded, but quickly recovering his presence of mind, saved his home-reputation by explaining to the red gentlemen in breechcloths and nose-rings, that the Yankee ate so much salt that curses wouldn’t take hold on him at all.
The Shamans (or Schamans) of Siberia, follow a very similar business, but are not so much priestly humbugs as mere conjurors. The Lamas, or Buddhist leaders of Central and Southern Asia are, however, regular priests, again, and may be said, with singular propriety, to “run their machine” on principles of thorough religious humbug, for they do really pray by a machine. They set up a little mill to go by water or wind, which turns a cylinder. On this cylinder is written a prayer, and every time the barrel goes round once, it counts, they say, for one prayer. It may be imagined how piety intensifies in a freshet, or in a heavy gale of wind! And there is a ludicrous notion of economy, as well as a pitiable folly in the conception of profiting by such windy supplications, and of saving all one’s time and thoughts for business, while the prayers rattle out by the hundred at home. Only imagine the pious fervor of one of these priests in a first-class Lowell mill, of say a hundred thousand spindles. Print a large edition of some good prayer and paste a copy on each spindle, and the place would seem to him the very gate of a Buddhist heaven. He would feel sure of taking heaven by storm, with a sustained fire of one hundred thousand prayers every second. His first requisite for a prosperous church would be a good waterpower for prayer-mills. And yet, absurd as these prayer-mills of the heathen really are, it may not be safe to bring them under unqualified condemnation: for who among us has not sometimes heard windy prayers even in our Christian churches? Young clergymen are especially liable and, I might say, prone to this mockery. These, however, are but exceptions to the general Christian rule, viz.: that the Omniscient careth only for heart-service; and that, before Him, all mere lip-service or machine-service, is simply an abomination.
A less innocent kind of praying is one of the religious humbugs of the bloody and cruel Sandwich Islands form of heathenism. Here a practice prevailed, and does yet, of paying money to a priest to pray your enemy to death. For cash in advance, this bargain could always be made, and so groveling was the spiritual cowardice of these poor savages, that, like the negro victim of Obi, the man prayed at seldom failed to sicken as soon as he found out what was going on, and to waste away and die.
This bit of heathen humbug now in operation, from so many distant portions of the Earth, shows how radically similar is all heathenism. It shows, too, how mean, vulgar, filthy, and altogether vile, is such religion as man, unassisted, contrives for himself. It shows, again, how sadly great is the proportion of the human race still remaining in this brutal darkness. And, by contrast, it affords us great reason for thankfulness that we live in a land of better culture, and happier hopes and practices.

===Chinese===

北美印第安巫医（powwows）的仪式较之非洲部落要温和不少。这些占卜师与奥比巫师相仿，也替主顾配制符咒，但通常与其说是为了加害他人，不如说是为了护佑佩戴者。这些符咒不过是把几件琐碎小物塞进一只小布袋——即所谓的“圣药袋”——挂在脖子上，据信便能确保佩戴者得到“伟大灵神”（Great Spirit）的特别庇佑。印第安巫医偶尔也兼做一点儿诅咒生意。

新英格兰早期有过一则趣闻：一位清教徒牧师冷静地向最负盛名的印第安大巫师下战书，邀他尽管施展地狱魔法攻过来。双方正式会面，巫医摇响法器、鬼哭狼嚎、手舞足蹈、吹起羽毛、狂吼咒语，直到筋疲力尽，而老牧师自始至终静静地冷眼旁观。那野蛮骗子傻了眼，却迅速稳住阵脚，向围观那些系腰布、戴鼻环的红皮肤绅士解释说：那扬基佬平日吃盐太多，把咒语全给腌住了，根本下不进去——好歹保住了自己在部族里的名声。

西伯利亚的萨满（Shamans）干的营生也大同小异，不过与其说是宗教祭司骗子，不如说纯粹是江湖魔术师。至于中亚与南亚的喇嘛（Lamas）或佛教领袖，则又是正儿八经的专职教士；说他们“彻底按宗教欺罔原则开动机器”可谓再贴切不过，因为他们确确实实是靠一台机器来祈祷的。他们架起一座靠水力或风力驱动的小磨坊，带动一只圆筒旋转。圆筒上写着经文，据他们说，滚筒每转一圈便算作念了一遍经。大伙儿不难想象，每逢山洪暴发或狂风大作之际，他们的虔诚度会暴涨到何等惊人的地步！企图靠这种随风空转的祈祷讨得福报，把全部时间与心思省下来去做生意，而让家里转经筒噼里啪啦一口气甩出成百上千遍祈祷——这种既荒唐可笑的精打细算与可悲愚昧，实在令人发噱。试想一下，把其中一位教士放进洛厄尔（Lowell）一座拥有十万枚纱锭的头等纺织厂里，该是何等虔诚沸腾！只要印一大批好经文，每个纱锭上贴一张，那座工厂在他眼里简直就是佛教极乐世界的南天门！每秒钟持续倾泻十万发祈祷炮火，他准保觉得自己能强行攻陷天堂。照他看来，建一座兴旺教会的头号必备条件，莫过于充沛的转经磨坊水力资源。话虽如此，异教徒的转经筒固然荒唐，咱们却也不便一棍子打死：试问我们当中谁不曾在基督教堂里听过同样空洞浮夸、随风空转的祈祷词呢？年轻牧师尤其容易——甚至可以说惯于——陷入这种虚应故事的嘲弄。好在那些终究只是基督教普遍法则的例外：全知全能的上帝只在乎发自内心的侍奉；在他面前，一切流于嘴皮或流于机器的敷衍，皆属可憎之物。

在血腥残暴的桑威奇群岛（Sandwich Islands）异教中，还流行着一种远没那么无害的祈祷骗局。当地向来盛行——至今犹存——一种做法：花钱雇祭司把你的仇敌“祈祷致死”。只要预付现钞，这桩买卖随时成交；而这群可怜野蛮人的精神怯懦竟卑微到极点，一如中了奥比巫术的黑人受害者，被诅咒的人一旦风闻有人在花钱祈祷咒死自己，十有八九立刻病倒、日渐消瘦而亡。

从地球上如此众多遥远角落仍在运作的这几宗异教骗局，足见天下所有异教在本质上是何等惊人地相似；足见人类在缺乏神恩指引时自己编造出的宗教是何等卑劣、粗俗、肮脏且一无是处；也再次显明至今仍有如此惨重比例的人类深陷在野蛮蒙昧之中。两相对照，我们生于文化更开明、怀抱更幸福希望与习俗的国度，实在有充分理由心怀感恩。

### XLIX / 第四十九章

===Original===

Ordeals﻿—​Duels﻿—​Wager of battle﻿—​Abraham Thornton﻿—​Red hot iron﻿—​Boiling water﻿—​Swimming﻿—​Swearing﻿—​Corsned﻿—​Pagan ordeals.
Ordeals belong to times and communities of rudeness, violence, materialism, ignorance, gross superstition and blind faith. The theory of ordeals is, that God will miraculously decide in the case of any accused person referred to Him. He will cause the accused to be victorious or defeated in a duel, will punish him on the spot for perjury, and if the innocent be exposed to certain physical dangers, will preserve him harmless.
The duel, for instance, used to be called the “ordeal by battle,” and was simply the commitment of the decision of a cause to God. Duels were regularly prefaced by the solemn prayer “God show the right.” Nowadays nobody believes that skill with a pistol is going to be specially bestowed by the Almighty, without diligent practice at a mark. Accordingly, the idea of a divine interposition has long ago dropped out of the question, and duelling is exclusively in the hands of the devil and his human votaries﻿—is a purely brutal absurdity. But in England, so long was this bloody, superstitious humbug kept up, that any hardened scoundrel who was a good hand at his weapon might, down to the year 1819, absolutely have committed murder under the protection of English law. Two years before that date, a country “rough” named Abraham Thornton, murdered his sweetheart, Mary Ashford, but by deficiency of proof was acquitted on trial. There was however a moral conviction that Thornton had killed the girl, and her brother, a mere lad, caused an appeal to be entered according to the English statute, and Thornton was again arraigned before the King’s Bench. In the meantime his counsel had looked up the obsolete proceedings about “assize of battle,” and when Thornton was placed at the bar he threw down his glove upon the floor according to the ancient forms, and challenged his accuser to mortal combat. In reply, the appellant, Ashford, set forth facts so clearly showingThornton’s guilt as to constitute (as he alleged,) cause for exemption from the combat, and for condemnation of the prisoner. The court, taken by surprise, spent five months in studying on the matter. At last it decided that the fighting man had the law of England on his side, admitted his demand, and further, found that the matters alleged for exemption from combat were not sufficient. On this, poor William Ashford, who was but a boy, declined the combat by reason of his youth, and the prisoner was discharged, and walked in triumph out of court, the innocent blood still unavenged upon his hands. The old fogies of Parliament were startled at finding themselves actually permitting the practice of barbarisms abolished by the Greek emperor, Michael Palaeologus, in 1259, and by the good King Louis IX of France in 1270; and two years afterwards, in 1819, the legal duel or “assize of battle” was by law abolished in England. It had been legal there for five centuries and a half, having been introduced by statute in 1261.
Before that time, the ordeals by fire and by water were the regular legal ones in England. These were known even to the Anglo Saxon law, being mentioned in the code of Ina, AD, about 700. It appears that fire was thought the most aristocratic element, for the ordeal by fire was used for nobles, and that by water for vulgarians and serfs. The operations were as follows: When one was accused of a crime, murder for instance, he had his choice whether to be tried “by God and his country,” or “by God.” If he chose the former he went before a jury. If the latter, he underwent the ordeal. Nine red hot ploughshares were laid on the ground in a row. The accused was blindfolded, and sent to walk over them. If he burnt himself he was guilty; if not, not. Sometimes, instead of this, the accused carried a piece of red hot iron of from one to three pounds’ weight in his hand for a certain distance.

===Chinese===

神明裁判——决斗——决斗裁决——亚伯拉罕·桑顿——烧红的烙铁——沸水——沉水试验——宣誓——诅咒面包——异教神明裁判。

神明裁判（Ordeals）属于粗野、暴力、唯物蒙昧、愚蠢、极端迷信与盲从的时代与社会。神明裁判的理论依据是：对于任何交由上帝裁决的被控之人，上帝必会行神迹予以断案——使被告在决斗中胜出或败北，当场惩治作伪证者，并在无辜者置身特定肉体险境时保其安然无恙。

譬如决斗，过去便称为“武斗神裁”（ordeal by battle），说白了就是把案件的裁决权托付给上帝。决斗照例总以一句庄严祷词开场：“愿上帝彰显公义。”如今再也没人相信，若不勤加打靶苦练，全能的上帝会平白无故赐下精湛的手枪枪法。于是，神意干预这一念头早已退出舞台，决斗彻头彻尾落入了魔鬼及其凡间信徒的掌心——纯粹成了一桩野蛮的荒唐事。然而在英格兰，这一血腥迷信的骗局（humbug）居然延续得如此之久，以至于直到 1819 年，任何精通兵器的铁石心肠恶棍竟仍能公然在英国法律的庇护下杀人行凶。就在那之前的两年，一名名叫亚伯拉罕·桑顿（Abraham Thornton）的乡下恶棍杀害了他的情人玛丽·阿什福德（Mary Ashford），却因证据不足在庭审中获判无罪。然而世人道义上一致认定正是桑顿杀害了姑娘，姑娘的弟弟——一个毛头少年——依英国成文法提起私人复审控告，桑顿再次被押上王座法庭受审。在此期间，桑顿的辩护律师翻出了早已废弛不用的“决斗裁决”旧案程序；当桑顿站上被告席时，他依古礼将手套往地板上一掷，向控告人发起生死决斗挑战。作为回应，上诉人阿什福德列举了足以铁证桑顿罪行的事实，主张依此应豁免决斗并直接将囚犯定罪。大吃一惊的法庭花了足足五个月研习此案。最终法庭裁定这位打手确实有英国法条撑腰，准许他的决斗要求，并进一步认定原告所提豁免决斗的理由不充分。事已至此，年少力薄的可怜威廉·阿什福德只得因年幼体弱放弃决斗；于是囚犯当庭获释，双手沾满未雪的冤血，趾高气扬地大步走出法庭。议会里的老顽固们这才吓了一大跳，猛然发觉自己竟还容许着早被希腊皇帝米海尔·帕莱奥罗格斯于 1259 年、法国贤王路易九世于 1270 年废除的野蛮恶习；两年之后，即 1819 年，英格兰终于立法废除了合法决斗即“决斗裁决”。这项自 1261 年由成文法引入的恶法，竟在英国合法存续了整整五个半世纪。

在此之前，火刑神裁与水刑神裁曾是英格兰正规的法定审判方式。此法甚至早在盎格鲁-撒克逊法律中便已有载，见于约公元 700 年的《伊纳法典》（code of Ina）。看来火被视作最具贵族气质的元素，因为火刑神裁专用于贵族，而水刑神裁则留给平民与农奴。具体操作如下：某人若被控犯罪（譬如谋杀），有权选择“由上帝与祖国审判”，还是“仅由上帝审判”。若选前者，便交由陪审团审理；若选后者，便得接受神明裁判。地上排成一列放着九块烧得通红的犁铧。被告蒙上双眼，奉命赤脚从上面走过。若脚被烫伤便是有罪；若未烫伤便无罪。有时则改为让被告赤手捧着一块重一至三磅的烧红铁块走完一段规定距离。

===Original===

The ordeal by water was, in one form at least, the same wise alternative in after years so often offered to witches. The accused was tied up in a heap, each arm to the other leg, and flung into water. If he floated he was guilty, and must be killed. If he sank and drowned, he was innocent﻿—but killed. Trial was therefore synonymous with execution. The nature of such alternatives shows how important it was to have a character above suspicion! Another mode was, for the accused to plunge his bare arm into boiling water to the elbow. The arm was then instantly sealed up in bandages under charge of the clergy for three days. If it was then found perfectly well, the accused was acquitted; if not, he was found guilty.
Another ordeal was expurgation or compurgation. It was a simple business﻿—“as easy as swearing;” very much like a “custom house oath.” It was only this: the accused made solemn oath that he was not guilty, and all the respectable men he could muster came and made their solemn oath that they believed so too. This is much like the jurisprudence of the Dutch justice of the peace in the old story, before whom two men swore that they saw the prisoner steal chickens. The thief however, getting a little time to collect testimony, brought in twelve men who swore that they did not see him take the chickens. “Balance of evidence overwhelmingly in favor of the prisoner,” said the sapient justice (in Dutch I suppose,) and finding him innocent in a ratio of six to one, he discharged him at once.
This ordeal by oath was reserved for people of eminence, whose word went for something, and who had a good many thoroughgoing friends.
Another sort of ordeal was reserved for priests. It was called “corsned.” The priest who took the ordeal by corsned received a bit of bread or a bit of cheese which was loaded heavily, by way of sauce, with curses upon whomsoever should eat it falsely. This he ate, together with the bread of the Lord’s supper. Everybody knew that if he were guilty, the sacred mouthful would choke him to death on the spot. History records no instance of the choking of any priest in this ordeal, but there is a story that the Saxon Earl Godwin of Kent took the corsned to clear himself of a charge of murder, and (being a layman) was choked. I fully believe that Earl Godwin is dead, for he was born about the year 1000. But I have not the least idea that corsned killed him.

===Chinese===

水刑神裁的其中一种形式，与后世频频赏给女巫的那套“高明二选一”如出一辙：把被告蜷成一团捆紧——左臂绑右腿、右臂绑左腿——扔进水里。若他浮起来，便证明有罪，必须处死；若他沉下去淹死了，便证明无罪——但也同样死了。因此受审几乎等同于行刑。这种二选一的性质充分说明，平日里拥有一个清白无瑕的好名声是多么要紧！另一种水刑神裁则是让被告光着胳膊直探进沸水里直到手肘，随即在教士监管下立刻用绷带封裹三天。三天后拆开若完好无损则无罪开释；若溃烂烫伤则判处有罪。

还有一种神明裁判叫做“宣誓洗冤法”（expurgation 或 compurgation）。这事儿简单得很——“像发誓一样容易”，简直跟“海关报关宣誓”没两样。无非就是：被告郑重起誓自己清白无罪，然后把他能拉拢来的所有体面人统统叫来，一同郑重起誓说他们也相信他无罪。这极像那则老故事里荷兰治安法官的判案法度：两名证人在法官面前发誓亲眼看见囚犯偷了鸡；可那贼讨了点儿宽限时间收集证词后，竟找来十二个人发誓说他们“没看见”他偷鸡。“证据天平压倒性地倾向被告一方，”这位英明的法官（我猜是用荷兰语）宣布道，随即按六比一的悬殊比例判他无罪，当场释放。

这种宣誓神裁专为地位显赫之人保留——毕竟他们的一言九鼎算点儿分量，手头又有一大帮铁杆死党。

还有另一种专为神父保留的神明裁判，叫做“诅咒面包裁判”（corsned）。接受此种裁判的神父会领到一小块面包或奶酪，上面撒满重重恶毒诅咒当作佐料，咒骂凡心怀欺诈吞食者必遭天谴。他把这东西连同圣餐面包一并吃下。人人都知道，他若真有罪，这口圣物定会当场噎死他。历史上从未记载过任何一位神父在这种裁判中被噎死；倒是有传说称，盎格鲁-撒克逊时期的肯特伯爵戈德温（Earl Godwin）为洗刷谋杀嫌疑而吞下诅咒面包，结果（大概因为他是俗人而非神父）当场噎死。我完全相信戈德温伯爵已经死了，毕竟他生于约公元 1000 年；但我可半点儿不信他是被那块诅咒面包噎死的。

===Original===

The priests had the management of ordeals, which, being appeals to God, were reckoned religious ceremonies. They of course much preferred the swearing and eating and hot iron and water ordeals, which could be kept under the regulation of clerical good sense. Not so with the ordeal by battle. No priests could do anything with the wrath of two great mad ugly brutes, hot to kill each other, and crazy to risk having their own throats cut or skulls cleft rather than not have the chance. In consequence, the whole influence of the Romish church went against the ordeal by battle, and in favor of the others. Thus the former soon lost its religious element and became the mere duel; a base indulgence of a beast’s passion for murder and revenge. The progress of enlightenment gradually pushed ordeals out of court. Mobs have however always tried the ordeal by water on witches.
Almost all the heathen ordeals have depended on fire, water, or something to eat or drink. Even in the Bible we find an ordeal prescribed to the Jews (Numbers 5) for an unfaithful wife, who is there directed to drink some water with certain ceremonies, which drink God promises shall cause a fatal disease if she be guilty, and if not, not. It is worth noticing that Moses says not a word about any “water of jealousy,” or any other ordeal, for unfaithful husbands!
This drinking or eating ordeal prevails quite extensively even now. In Hindustan, theft is often enquired into by causing the suspected party to chew some dry rice or rice flour, which has some very strong curses stirred into it, corsned fashion. After chewing, the accused spits out his mouthful, and if it is either dry or bloody, he is guilty. It is easy to see how a rascal, if as credulous as rascals often are, would be so frightened that his mouth would be dry, and would thus betray his own peccadillo. Another Hindu mode was, to give a certain quantity of poison in butter, and if it did no harm, to acquit. Here, the man who mixes the dose is evidently the important person. In Madagascar they give some tangena water. Now tangena is a fruit of which a little vomits the patient, and a good deal poisons or kills him; a quality which sufficiently explains how they manage that ordeal.
Ordeals by fire and water are still practiced, with some variations, in Hindustan, China, Pegu, Siberia, Congo, Guinea, Senegambia and other pagan nations. Some of those still in use are odd enough. A Malabar one is to swim across a certain river, which is full of crocodiles. A Hindu one is, for the two parties to an accusation to stand out doors, each with one bare leg in a hole, he to win who can longest endure the bites they are sure to get. This would be a famous method in some of the New Jersey and New York and Connecticut seashore lowlands I know of. The mosquitoes would decide cases both civil and criminal, at a speed that would make a Judge of the Supreme Court as dizzy as a humming-top. Another Hindu plan was for the accused to hold his head under water while a man walked a certain distance. If the walker chose to be lazy about it, or the prisoner had diseased lungs, this would be a rather severe method. The Wanakas in Eastern Africa, draw a red hot needle through the culprit’s lips﻿—a most judicious place to get hold of an African!﻿—and if the wound bleeds, he is guilty. In Siam, accuser and accused are put into a pen and a tiger is let loose on them. He whom the tiger kills is guilty. If he kills both, both are guilty; if neither, they try another mode.
Blackstone says that an ordeal might always be tried by attorney. I should think this would give the legal profession a very lively time whenever the courts were chiefly using tigers, poison, drowning, fire and red hot iron, but not so much so when a little swearing or eating was the only thing required.
This whole business of ordeals is a singular superstition, and the extent of its employment shows how ready the human race is to believe that God is constantly influencing even their ordinary private affairs. In other words, it is in principle like the doctrine of “special providence.” Looked at as a superstition however﻿—considered as a humbug﻿—the history of ordeals show how corrupt becomes the nuisance of religious ways of deciding secular business, and how proper is our great American principle of the separation of state and church.

===Chinese===

神明裁判历来由教士掌管，因为诉诸上帝裁决，故被视作宗教仪式。教士们自然更偏爱宣誓、吃面包、摸热铁与沸水这类神裁——毕竟这些都能稳稳控制在教士的“通情达理”之下。唯独决斗神裁却不然。面对两头怒火中烧、凶神恶煞、恨不得立刻弄死对方、宁可冒着被割喉劈脑的风险也要拼个你死我活的狂暴野兽，教士们实在无计可施。因此，罗马天主教会的全部影响力都用来反对决斗神裁，而力挺其余几种。于是决斗神裁很快剥落了宗教外衣，退化成单纯的私人决斗——一种纵容野兽般杀戮与复仇兽欲的卑劣行径。随着文明开化不断推进，神明裁判逐渐被逐出法庭。不过，暴民们却一直沿用着水刑神裁来对付女巫。

几乎所有异教神明裁判都离不开火、水，或者某种吃喝之物。甚至在《圣经》（《民数记》第 5 章）里，我们也见到为犹太人规定的一种针对不忠妻子的神裁：令她在特定仪式下饮下某种水，上帝应许她若有罪便会罹患致命恶疾，若无罪则安然无恙。值得注意的是，摩西对不忠丈夫的“疑恨之水”或其他任何神裁，却只字未提！

这种饮食神裁即便在今日依然颇为盛行。在印度斯坦，查办盗窃案时常令嫌疑人咀嚼一把干生米或米粉——照诅咒面包的套路往里拌入极猛烈的诅咒。嚼完之后，嫌疑人把嘴里的东西吐出来，若呈干燥或带血状，便定为有罪。不难看出，一个做贼心虚的无赖（往往跟所有无赖一样极度迷信）若是吓破了胆，口干舌燥在所难免，结果反倒自己招供了小偷小摸。印度教另一种做法是把一定剂量的毒药拌在黄油里喂下，若安然无恙便判无罪。显而易见，这里头负责调配药量的那位仁兄才是真正的大人物。在马达加斯加，他们让人喝“坦格纳”（tangena）毒果水；少喝一点只让人呕吐，多喝则中毒毙命——这种药性足以解释他们是如何操纵这场神裁的。

火刑与水刑神裁至今仍在印度斯坦、中国、勃固（Pegu）、西伯利亚、刚果、几内亚、塞内甘比亚及其他异教国度以各种变体沿用着。其中几项仍在实行的法子可谓奇葩至极：马拉巴尔（Malabar）有一招是游过一条鳄鱼成群的河流；印度有一种做法是让原告与被告双双站在户外，各自把一条光腿伸进土坑里，谁能忍受必至的虫咬最久谁就赢——这招放在我知道的新泽西、纽约和康涅狄格某些海滨低洼沼泽地里准保大显神通：那儿的蚊子裁决民事刑事案件的速度，快得能让最高法院大法官转得像个嗡嗡作响的陀螺！印度还有一招是让被告把脑袋按进水里，同时另有一人走完一段规定路程；要是走路那人故意磨洋工，或者囚犯肺部有疾，这法子可就够呛了。东非的瓦纳卡人（Wanakas）则用一根烧红的针穿过嫌犯的嘴唇（抓非洲人的把柄挑这儿可真够刁钻！），伤口流血便算有罪。在暹罗，原告与被告被一道关进围栏，随后放进一只老虎；老虎咬死谁，谁就有罪；若两人都咬死，两人都有罪；若一个也没咬，再换别的法子。

大法学家布莱克斯通（Blackstone）曾说，神明裁判向来可以委托代理人出庭代受。我暗想，每逢法庭主要动用老虎、毒药、溺水、烈火与烧红烙铁断案时，律师界诸公的日子定然刺激得很；不过若只需发个小誓或吃口面包，倒也没那么惊险。

整套神明裁判实乃一种奇特的迷信，其流传之广，足见人类多么乐意相信上帝连他们寻常私事都要时时插手。换言之，这在原则上与“特殊天意”学说如出一辙。然而，若将其视作一种迷信——视作一场欺罔（humbug）——神明裁判的历史便揭示了用宗教手段裁决世俗事务的流弊会腐化到何等地步，也反衬出我们美国“政教分离”这一伟大原则是何等英明正确。

### L / 第五十章

===Original===

Apollonius of Tyana.
The annals of ancient history are peculiarly rich in narratives of pretension and imposition, and either owing to the greater ignorance and credulity of mankind, or the superior skill of gifted but unscrupulous men in those days, present a few examples that even surpass the most remarkable products of the modern science of humbug.
One of their most surprising instances﻿—in fact, perhaps, absolutely the leading impostor﻿—was the sage or charlatan (for it is difficult to determine which) known as Apollonius Tyanaeus so called from Tyana, in Cappadocia, Asia Minor, his birthplace, where he first saw the light about four years earlier than Christ, and consequently more than eighteen and a half centuries ago. His arrival upon this planet was attended with some very amazing demonstrations. With his first cry, a flash of lightning darted from the heavens to the Earth and back again, dogs howled, cats mewed, roosters crowed, and flocks of swans, so say the olden chroniclers﻿—probably geese, every one of them﻿—clapped their wings in the adjacent meadows with a supernatural clatter. Ushered into the world with such surprising omens as these, young Apollonius could not fail to make a noise himself, ere long. Sent by his doting father to Tarsus, in Cilicia, to be educated, he found the dissipations of the place too much for him, and soon removed to Aegae, a smaller city, at no great distance from the other. There he adopted the doctrines of Pythagoras, and subjected himself to the regular discipline of that curious system whose first process was a sort of juvenile gag-law, the pupils being required to keep perfectly silent for a period of five years, during which time it was forbidden to utter a single word. Even in those days, few female scholars preferred this practice, and the boys had it all to themselves, nor were they by any means numerous. After this probation was over, they were enjoined to speak and argue with moderation.
At Aegae there stood a temple dedicated to Aesculapius, who figured on Earth as a great physician and compounder of simples, and after death was made a god. The edifice was much larger and more splendid than the Brandreth House on Broadway, although we have no record of Aesculapius having bestowed upon the world any such benefaction as the universal pills. However, unlike our modern M.D.s, the latter was in the habit of reappearing after death, in this temple, and there holding forth to the faithful on various topics of domestic medicine. Apollonius was allowed to take up his residence in the establishment, and, no doubt, the priests initiated him into all their dodges to impose upon the people. Another tenet of the Pythagorean faith was a total abstinence from beans, an arrangement which would be objectionable in New England and in Nassau Street eating houses.
Apollonius however, who knew nothing of Yankees or Nassau Street, manfully completed his novitiate. Restored at length to the use of beans and of his talking apparatus, he set forth upon a lecturing tour through Pamphylia and Cilicia. His themes were temperance, economy, and good behavior, and for the very novelty of the thing, crowds of disciples soon gathered about him. At the town of Aspenda he made a great hit, when he “pitched into” the corn merchants who had bought up all the grain during a period of scarcity, and sold it to the people at exorbitant prices. Of course, such things are not permitted in our day! Apollonius moved by the sufferings of women and children, took his stand in the market place, and with his stylus wrote in large characters upon a tablet the following advice to the speculators in grain:
“The Earth, the common mother of all, is just. But, ye being unjust, would make her a bountiful mother to yourselves alone. Leave off your dishonest traffic, or ye shall be no longer permitted to live.”
The grain-merchants, upon beholding this appeal, relented, for there was conscience in those days; and, moreover, the populace had prepared torches, and proposed to fry a few of the offenders, like oysters in breadcrumbs. So they yielded at once, and great was the fame of the prophet. Thus elevated in his own opinion, Apollonius, still preaching virtue by the wayside, set out for Babylon, after visiting the cities of Antioch, Ephesus, etc., always attracting immense crowds. As he penetrated further toward the remote East, his troops of followers fell off, until he was left with only three companions, who went with him to the end. One of these was a certain Damis, who wrote a description of the journey, and, by the way, tells us that his master spoke all languages, even those of the animals. We have men in our own country who can talk “horse-talk” at the races, but probably none so perfectly as this great Tyanean. The author of *The Ruined Cities of Africa*, a recent publication, informs us that at Lamba, an African village, there is a leopard who can “speak.” This would go to show that the “animals,” are aspiring in a direction directly the opposite of the acquirements of Apollonius, and I shall secure that leopard, if possible, for exhibition in the Museum, and for a fair consideration send him to any public meeting where someone is needed who will come up to the scratch!

===Chinese===

提亚纳的阿波罗尼乌斯。

古代史册向来格外富于狂妄自诩与欺诈伪饰的记载；不知是因为当年人类更加愚昧轻信，还是当时那些才华横溢却毫无底线的家伙手腕更高明，史书中竟留下过几桩甚至超越了现代“欺罔学”最杰出产物的奇例。

其中最令人惊叹的范例之一——事实上或许堪称古往今来当之无愧的头号骗子——便是那位不知该称为贤哲还是江湖术士（实在难以界定）的提亚纳的阿波罗尼乌斯（Apollonius Tyanaeus）。他因出生地——小亚细亚卡帕多西亚的提亚纳（Tyana）——而得名；他比基督早约四年降生于世，算来距今已有一千八百五十多年。他降临这颗星球时便伴随着一番极其惊人的异象：伴随他的第一声啼哭，一道闪电自天穹直劈大地又折返苍穹，狗吠、猫叫、雄鸡打鸣，而据古老编年史家记载，成群的天鹅——八成全是大鹅吧——在近旁草地上拍打翅膀，发出超自然的聒噪喧响。带着如此惊人的吉兆降临人世，年轻的阿波罗尼乌斯用不了多久自然也要自己闹出一番大动静。他溺爱的父亲送他去西里西亚的塔尔苏斯求学，他却嫌那地方太奢靡浮华，很快转往不远处的小城埃盖（Aegae）。在那里，他皈依了毕达哥拉斯学派的教义，自觉接受那套奇特体系的严格清规——其头一道入门戒律便是某种少年禁言令，学徒必须整整五年保持绝对沉默，其间严禁吐出半个字。哪怕在那个年代，也没几个女弟子受得了这等修行，于是全归男孩子们独享，而且人数也寥寥无几。熬过这段见习期后，方准许他们节制有度地开口说话与辩论。

埃盖城里矗立着一座供奉阿斯克勒庇俄斯（Aesculapius）的神庙——他生前在世间是位伟大的医者与草药师，死后被尊为神明。这座神庙比百老汇大道上的布兰德雷斯大厦还要宏伟壮丽得多，尽管史书并未记载阿斯克勒庇俄斯曾向世人赐下什么包治百病的神药丸。不过，与我们现代医学博士不同的是，这位神明习惯在死后频频在这座神庙里显灵，向虔诚的信徒高谈阔论各种家庭医术。阿波罗尼乌斯获准住进神庙，祭司们无疑将他们糊弄百姓的全套花招悉数传授给了他。毕达哥拉斯教派的另一条清规是绝对禁食豆类——这一条要是放在新英格兰和拿骚街的小饭馆里，怕是行不通的。

然而，对扬基人和拿骚街一无所知的阿波罗尼乌斯，男子汉般熬完了他的见习期。终于获准重新吃豆子、动用舌头之后，他便踏上了巡游潘菲利亚与西里西亚的讲学之旅。他宣讲的主题无非是节制、节俭与端正品行，单凭这份新奇感，便迅速聚拢了大批门徒。在阿斯彭达（Aspenda）城，他狠狠出了一次风头：当时正值粮荒，谷物商把粮食囤积居奇、抬高天价盘剥百姓，他挺身而出“痛批”这帮奸商。当然啦，这种事儿在我们今天可是绝不容许的！阿波罗尼乌斯深为妇孺的苦难所动，站在集市广场上，用铁笔在一块木板上写下大字，向粮食投机商发出如下忠告：

“大地乃众人共同之母，她公正无私。然尔等不义之徒，却欲将她变成只供尔等独享的丰饶慈母。速速停止这不义勾当，否则休想再活下去。”

粮商们见了这封通牒竟大发慈悲——毕竟那年头世上还残存着几分良心，何况百姓早已备好火把，打算把这几个罪魁祸首像裹面包屑炸牡蛎一样下锅油炸。于是他们当即屈服，这位先知的威名由此大振。自我感觉更加良好的阿波罗尼乌斯一路沿途宣讲美德，相继造访安提阿、以弗所等名城，所到之处无不人山人海，随后启程前往巴比伦。随着他不断深入遥远东方，身后的追随者队伍渐渐散去，最后只剩下三名同伴随他一路走到终点。其中一人名叫达米斯（Damis），后来写了一部游记；顺便一提，他在书里告诉我们，他的师父通晓世间一切语言，甚至包括飞禽走兽的语言。我们自己国内在赛马场上也有不少会说“马语”的行家，但恐怕没谁能像这位伟大的提亚纳人说得如此出神入化。最近出版的《非洲废墟之城》（*The Ruined Cities of Africa*）一书作者告诉我们，在非洲一个名叫兰巴的村子里有一头会“说话”的豹子。这倒说明如今的“野兽”正朝着与阿波罗尼乌斯相反的方向奋发图强；只要有可能，我定要把那头豹子弄进博物馆展览，若报酬公道，还可以租借给任何需要一位“敢抓敢挠（come up to the scratch）”的主讲人的公开集会！

===Original===

But, to resume. On his way to Babylon, Apollonius saw by the roadside a lioness and eight whelps, where they had been killed by a party of hunters, and argued from the omen that he should remain in that city just one year and eight months, which of course turned out to be exactly the case. The Babylonish monarch was so delighted with the eloquence and skill of the noted stranger, that he promised him any twelve gifts that he might choose to ask for, but Apollonius declined accepting anything but food and raiment. However, the King gave him camels and escort to assist his journey over the northern mountains of Hindustan, which he crossed, and entered the ancient city of Taxilia. On the way, he had a high time in the gorges of the hills with a horrible hobgoblin of the species called empusa by the Greeks. This demon terrified his companions half out of their wits, but Apollonius bravely assailed him with all sorts of hard words, and, to literally translate the old Greek narrative, “blackguarded” him so effectually that the poor devil fled with his tail between his legs. At Taxilia, Phraortes, the King, a lineal descendant of the famous Porus﻿—and truly a porous personage, since he was renowned for drinking﻿—gave the philosopher a grand reception, and introduced him to the chief of the Brahmins, whose temples he explored. These Hindu gentlemen opened the eyes of Apollonius wider than they had ever been before, and taught him a few things he had never dreamed of, but which served him admirably during his latter career. He returned to Europe by way of the Red Sea, passing through Ephesus, where he vehemently denounced the speculators in gold and other improper persons. As they did not heed him, he predicted the plague, and left for Smyrna. Sure enough, the pestilence broke out just after his departure, and the Ephesians telegraphed to Smyrna, by the only means in their power, for his immediate return; gold, in the meanwhile, falling at least ten percent. Apollonius reappeared in the twinkling of an eye, suddenly, in the very midst of the wailing crowd, on the market place. Pointing to a beggar, he directed the people to stone that particular unfortunate, and they obeyed so effectually, that the hapless creature was in a few moments completely buried under a huge heap of brickbats. The next morning, the philosopher commanded the throng to remove the pile of stones, and as they did so, a dog was discovered instead of the beggar. The dog sprang up, wagged his tail, and made away at “two-forty” and with him the pestilence departed. For this feat, the Ephesians called Apollonius a god, and reared a statue to his honor. The appellation of divinity he willingly accepted, declaring that it was only justice to good men. In these degenerate days, we have accorded the term to only one person, “the divine Fanny Ellsler!” That, too, was a tribute to superior understanding!
Our hero next visited Pergamus, the site of ancient Troy, where he shut himself up all night in the tomb of Achilles; and having raised the great departed, held conversation with him on a variety of military topics. Among other things, Achilles told him that the theory of his having been killed by a wound in the heel was all nonsense, as he had really died from being bitten by a puppy, in the back. If the reader does not believe me, let him consult the original MS. of Damis. The same accident has disabled several great generals in modern times.

===Chinese===

言归正传。在前往巴比伦的途中，阿波罗尼乌斯在路边看见一头母狮和八只幼狮被一队猎人杀死在那里，便从这一兆头推算出自己将在巴比伦城整整逗留一年零八个月——结果自然分毫不差。巴比伦国王对这位知名异乡客的雄辩口才与高超才智喜出望外，许诺任他挑选十二件礼物，但阿波罗尼乌斯除了食物与衣裳外一概婉拒。不过国王还是赐给他骆驼与护卫队，护送他翻越印度斯坦北部的崇山峻岭，进入古城塔克西拉（Taxilia）。途中他在山谷险隘撞见了一只希腊人称之为“恩普萨”（empusa）的可怕妖魔。这恶鬼把他的同伴吓得魂不附体，阿波罗尼乌斯却英勇地用各种恶毒粗话迎头痛击——按古希腊原文直译便是把他“痛骂得狗血淋头”——骂得那可怜魔鬼夹着尾巴抱头鼠窜。在塔克西拉，国王弗拉奥尔特斯（Phraortes）——著名君主波鲁斯（Porus）的直系后裔，名副其实一个“多孔海绵人”（porous，双关“能喝”），素以海量豪饮著称——为这位哲学家举办了盛大欢迎宴，并将他引荐给婆罗门大祭司，让他得以遍览印度神庙。这几位印度教绅士令阿波罗尼乌斯大开眼界，前所未见地长了见识，传授给他几手连做梦都想不到的绝活，在他日后的生涯里派上了绝佳大用。他取道红海返回欧洲，途经以弗所，在当地猛烈抨击黄金投机商及其他不法之徒。见众人充耳不闻，他便预言瘟疫将至，随后动身前往士麦那。果不其然，他前脚刚走，瘟疫后脚便爆发了；以弗所人急忙动用当时唯一能使上的通讯手段向士麦那拍去急电，恳请他火速归来；与此同时，金价暴跌了至少百分之十。阿波罗尼乌斯眨眼间便神出鬼没般骤然重现于集市广场哭嚎的人群正中。他伸手指向一名乞丐，命令百姓用石头砸死那个倒霉蛋；众人奉命照办得极彻底，短短片刻，那不幸的家伙便被埋在一座巨大的碎砖石堆底下。次日清晨，哲学家命人群搬开石堆，众人扒开石头一看，里头躺着的竟不是乞丐，而是一条狗！那条狗一跃而起，摇摇尾巴以“两分四十秒一英里”的飞速狂奔而去，瘟疫也随之烟消云散。凭此壮举，以弗所人尊称阿波罗尼乌斯为神明，并为他立了一座塑像。他欣然笑纳了神明之号，宣称这不过是对善人应得的公道。在咱们这个世风日下的时代，世人只把“神圣”这顶桂冠献给过一个人——“神圣的范妮·埃尔斯勒！”（Fanny Ellsler，奥地利芭蕾舞星）那倒也是对“卓越下盘（understanding，双关‘理解力’与‘双腿’）”的礼赞！

接下来，我们的英雄造访了古特洛伊遗址帕加马（Pergamus），在阿喀琉斯墓中独宿一整夜；他召出这位伟大亡灵，就诸多军事议题促膝长谈。据阿喀琉斯亲口透露，世人传说他因脚后跟中箭而死纯属胡说八道，他真正的死因其实是后背被一只小狗崽咬了一口。读者若是不信，大可去翻阅达米斯的原始手稿。近代好几位伟大将军也曾因同一宗意外而丧失战斗力呢。

===Original===

Apollonius next made a tour through Greece, visiting Athens, Sparta, Olympia, and other cities, and exhorting the dissolute Greeks to mend their evil courses. The Spartans, particularly, came in for a severe lecture on the advantages of soap and water; and, it is said, that the first clean face ever seen in that republic was the result of the great Tyanean’s teachings. At Athens, he cured a man possessed of a demon; the latter bouncing out of his victim, at length, with such fury and velocity as to dash down a neighboring marble statue.
The Isle of Crete was the next point on the journey, and an earthquake occurring at the time, Apollonius suddenly exclaimed in the streets:
“The Earth is bringing forth land.”
Folks looked as he pointed toward the sea, and there beheld a new island in the direction of Therae.
He arrived at Rome, whither his fame had preceded him, just as the Emperor Nero had issued an edict against all who dealt in magic; and, although he knew that he was included in the denunciation, he boldly went to the forum, where he restored to life the dead body of a beautiful lady, and predicted an eclipse of the sun, which shortly occurred. Nero caused him to be arrested, loaded with chains, and flung into an underground dungeon. When his jailers next made their rounds, they found the chains broken and the cell empty, but heard the chanting of invisible angels. This story would not be believed by the head jailer at Sing Sing.
Prolonging his trip as far as Spain, Apollonius there got up a sedition against the authority of Nero, and thence crossed over into Africa. This was the darkest period of his history. From Africa, he proceeded to the South of Italy and the island of Sicily, still discoursing as he went. About this time, he heard of Nero’s death, and returned to Egypt, where Vespasian was endeavoring to establish his authority. While in Egypt, he explored the supposed sources of the Nile, and learned all the lore of the Ethiopean necromancers, who could do anything, even to making a black man white; thus greatly excelling the skill of after ages.
Vespasian had immense faith in the Tyanean sage, and consulted him upon the most important matters of State. Titus, the successor of that monarch, manifested equal confidence, and regarded him absolutely as an oracle. Apollonius, who really seems to have been a most sensible politician, wrote the following brief but pithy note to Titus, when the latter modestly refused the crown of victory, after having destroyed Jerusalem.
“Apollonius to Titus, Emperor of Rome, sendeth greeting. Since you have refused to be applauded for bloodshed and victory in war, I send you the crown of moderation. You know to what kind of merit crowns are due.”
Yet Apollonius was by no means an ultra peace man, for he strongly advocated the shaving and clothing of the Ethiopians, and their thorough chastisement when they refused to be combed and purified.

===Chinese===

随后阿波罗尼乌斯游历希腊全境，遍访雅典、斯巴达、奥林匹亚等城邦，劝诫生活靡乱的希腊人改邪归正。尤其是斯巴达人，狠狠挨了一通关于肥皂与清水之益处的训斥；据说那个共和国历史上头一张洗得干干净净的脸蛋，正是拜这位伟大提亚纳人的教诲所赐。在雅典，他治愈了一名被恶魔附体的男子；那恶魔最终从受害者体内猛然蹿出，来势如此狂暴迅猛，竟把近旁一尊大理石雕像撞得粉碎。

旅程的下一站是克里特岛；恰逢当时发生地震，阿波罗尼乌斯在街上突然高呼：

“大地正在孕育新土。”

众人顺着他指向大海的手望去，果见锡拉岛（Therae）方向海面上冒出了一座新岛屿。

他的声名早已先一步传到罗马；他抵达罗马时，恰逢尼禄皇帝颁布敕令严禁一切魔法巫术；尽管明知自己也在遭斥之列，他依然大摇大摆走进罗马广场，当众救活了一名美貌贵妇的遗体，并预言一次日食，不久果真应验。尼禄下令将他逮捕，戴上沉重铁链打入地下黑牢。狱卒下次巡监时，发现铁链断裂、牢房空空如也，耳边却回荡着无形天使的赞美诗歌声。这种故事放在辛辛监狱的典狱长那儿可没人肯信。

阿波罗尼乌斯把行程一直延伸到西班牙，在那里煽动了一起反抗尼禄权威的叛乱，随后渡海进入非洲。这是他生平最晦暗的一段时期。离开非洲后，他前往意大利南部与西西里岛，沿途依旧讲学不辍。大致就在此时，他听闻尼禄死讯，于是折返埃及——彼时韦帕芗（Vespasian）正竭力在当地稳固帝位。在埃及期间，他探寻了传说中的尼罗河源头，并学尽了埃塞俄比亚通灵术士的全部秘学——那帮术士无所不能，甚至能把黑人变成白人，本事远远胜过后世一切能人。

韦帕芗对这位提亚纳贤哲深信不疑，凡遇国家军政大事必向他垂询。那位君主的继任者提图斯（Titus）同样对他信赖有加，简直将他奉若神明谕示。阿波罗尼乌斯看上去倒确实是个极其通达世故的政客：当提图斯攻陷耶路撒冷后谦逊地婉拒胜利花冠时，他给提图斯写了下面这封简短却切中肯綮的信：

“阿波罗尼乌斯致罗马皇帝提图斯问安。既因战事流血与胜利而辞谢赞颂，谨奉上‘节制之冠’。陛下深知何等功德方配受冠冕。”

然而阿波罗尼乌斯也绝非极端和平主义者，因为他曾力主替埃塞俄比亚人剃须穿衣，一旦他们抗拒梳洗净化便严加惩戒。

===Original===

When Domitian grasped at the imperial sceptre, the great Tyanean sided with his rival, Nerva, and having for this offense been seized and cast into prison, suddenly vanished from sight and reappeared on the instant at Puteoli, one hundred and fifty miles away. The distinguished Mr. Jewett, of Colorado, is the only instance of similar rapidity of locomotion known to us in this country and time.
After taking breath at Puteoli, the sage resumed his travels and revisited Greece, Asia Minor, etc. At Ephesus he established his celebrated school, and then, once more returning to Crete, happened to give his old friends, the Cretans, great offense, and was shut up in the temple Dictymna to be devoured by famished dogs; but the next morning was found perfectly unharmed in the midst of the docile animals, who had already made considerable progress in the Pythagorean philosophy, and were gathered around the philosopher, seated on their hind legs, with open mouths and lolling tongues, intently listening to him while he lectured them in the canine tongue. So devoted had they become to their eloquent instructor, and so enraged were they at the interruption when the Cretans reopened the temple, that they rushed out upon the latter and made a breakfast of a few of the leading men.
This is one of the last of the remarkable incidents that we find recorded of the mighty Apollonius. How he came to his end is quite uncertain, but some veracious chroniclers declare that he simply dried up and blew away. Others aver that he lived to the good old age of ninety-seven, and then quietly gave up the ghost at Tyana, where a temple was dedicated to his memory.
However that may be, he was subsequently worshiped with divine honors, and so highly esteemed by the greatest men of after days, that even Aurelian refused to sack Tyana, out of respect to the philosopher’s ashes.
Dion Cassius, the historian, records one of the most remarkable instances of his clairvoyance or second sight. He states that Apollonius, in the midst of a discourse at Ephesus, suddenly paused, and then in a different voice, exclaimed, to the astonishment of all:﻿—“Have courage, good Stephanus! Strike! strike! Kill the tyrant!” On that same day, the hated Domitian was assassinated at Rome by a man named Stephanus. The humdrum interpretation of this “miracle” is simply that Apollonius had a foreknowledge of the intended attempt upon the tyrant’s life.
Long afterwards, Cagliostro claimed that he had been a fellow-traveler with Apollonius, and that his mysterious companion, the sage Athlotas, was the very same personage, who, consequently, at that time, must have reached the ripe age of some 1784 years﻿—a lapse of time beyond the memory of even “the oldest inhabitant,” in these parts, at least!

===Chinese===

当图密善（Domitian）攫取帝国权杖时，伟大的提亚纳人站到了他的政敌涅尔瓦（Nerva）一边，并因这一罪名遭逮捕投入大牢；不料他当场凭空消失，瞬间现身于一百五十英里外的普特奥利（Puteoli）。在我们这个国度与时代，唯独那位大名鼎鼎的“科罗拉多”朱厄特先生曾创下过相仿的神行速度。

在普特奥利喘了口气后，贤哲重整行装再度周游希腊、小亚细亚等地。他在以弗所创办了名噪一时的学派；随后重返克里特岛时，不知怎的又大大开罪了克里特老朋友，被关进狄克提姆娜神庙（temple Dictymna）喂一群饿疯了的恶犬；然而次日清晨，人们发现他安然无恙地坐在温顺驯服的恶犬群中——那群狗在毕达哥拉斯哲学上已然大有进境，正后腿坐地围拢在哲学家身旁，张大嘴巴、吐着舌头，聚精会神地聆听他用犬语讲学。它们对这位雄辩恩师忠心耿耿，以至于当克里特人重新打开神庙门打断讲课时，群犬勃然大怒，猛扑出去把几个领头人物当场当成早饭啃了。

这便是史籍中关于神通广大的阿波罗尼乌斯的最后几桩奇事之一。他究竟如何告别人世已无可确考：有些忠实编年史家宣称他干脆风干后随风吹散了；另一些人则坚称他活到了九十七岁高龄，随后在故乡提亚纳安详辞世，当地人为纪念他建起了一座神庙。

无论真相如何，他死后确实享受了神明般的崇拜与祭祀，并深受后世伟人敬重，以至于连奥勒良皇帝（Aurelian）都因敬重这位哲学家的骨灰而下令不得劫掠提亚纳。

历史学家卡西乌斯·狄奥（Dion Cassius）记载了他“天眼通”或遥视预知最惊人的一例：据说阿波罗尼乌斯在以弗所讲学正酣之际，突然停顿下来，随后换了一种声音高呼，令全场惊愕不已：“鼓起勇气，善良的斯特法努斯！刺呀！刺呀！杀死那暴君！”就在同一天，遭人痛恨的图密善在罗马被一名叫斯特法努斯（Stephanus）的人刺杀身亡。对这一“神迹”最平淡无奇的解释，无非是阿波罗尼乌斯事先早已得知那场刺杀暴君的密谋罢了。

许多个世纪之后，卡廖斯特罗声称自己曾与阿波罗尼乌斯结伴同游，并说他那位神秘同伴贤哲阿特洛塔斯正是此人——照这么算，那位贤哲在当时怕是已达到了大约 1784 岁的“高龄”：这年岁跨度，至少在我们这一带，早已超出了连“最老住户”所能追忆的极限！
